Few days pass beneath the sun without witnessing deeds of consequence spoken by the nameless, suffered by the obscure, and endured by the forgotten. Most of these silent heroes remain buried in the dust of history until the great have fallen and the humble risen, yet many lives are lost to the world, their stories preserved only in cold, brittle epitomes that strike the heart like hailstones rather than rain. Chronicles written in the suffocating tongue of Latin often bury truth under layers of harsh brevity, leaving the facts as stark as fossils trapped in stone. It is here, in the gaps of these dry accounts, that the storyteller steps forward as an interpreter, seeking to breathe warmth into the frozen pages and grant two sorrow-scarred souls a fleeting place in the affections of the living.

Our tale unfolds in the mid-fifteenth century, a time when Louis XI ruled France, Edward IV held a contested throne in England, and Philip "the Good" reigned over Holland with a grip forged by force and guile. Having displaced his cousin Jacqueline and shattered her spirit, Philip sat undisturbed on his throne, a monarch whose tastes ran wild with the bizarre and the beautiful, from jeweled dwarfs to exotic beasts.

In the quiet burgh of Tergou, Elias and Catherine lived a life marked by both plenty and profound worry. Elias traded in cloth, silk, and curried leather—a durable material prized by the common folk for its ability to withstand a century's worth of steel knives, a necessity in an age where neighbors might carve one another at dinner over trifles. Though the couple was financially secure, nine children crowded their home, turning a once-manageable household into a sprawling fortress. When the children were young, they were heaven's playthings; but as they grew tall like olive branches, the reality of their number settled heavily on Elias and Catherine's hearts. They belonged to a prudent people, where recklessness was rare, yet the question gnawed at them constantly: *Who will find bread for us when we are gone?*

To avoid the shame of seeing their blood sink into the lower ranks of society, Elias and Catherine practiced a rigorous economy. Every penny saved, every luxury denied, flowed into a small coffer bound with steel. With this hoard, they eventually funded the futures of four sons and a daughter, sending them into the world as merchants, tailors, and robemakers. Yet, two children remained unable to walk the path of industry: Giles, a dwarf plagued by malice and physical deformity, and Little Catherine, a frail girl who moved upon crutches. Then there were Sybrandt, who preferred play to labor, and Cornelis, who waited idly for his father's death. Despite their fears, the couple clung to hope, buoyed by the presence of their youngest son, Gerard.

Gerard was a boy apart, destined for the priesthood and the altar. He possessed a mind for letters and a hand for art, though his nature was frivolous. He spent his days engrossed in penmanship and reading, often neglecting meals to chase the flicker of a candlelight. The local monks, captivated by his skill, taught him to illuminate manuscripts, praising his true eye and subtle hand. Yet, Gerard hesitated to create the glorious borders and holy images he admired. When pressed by a kindly monk, he confessed a secret shame: he lacked the funds for the expensive pigments—gold, ultramarine, and vermilion—that made such works shine. He feared asking his mother for charity, believing it would bring humiliation.

His isolation broke when an old lady named Margaret Van Eyck arrived in Tergou. Once a renowned artist from Flanders, she had fled her homeland to die in peace. Upon hearing of Gerard's talent, she invited him to her home. There, amidst drawings and sketches, she became his mentor. She gently prodded him to reveal why he refused to paint, and he finally spoke of his poverty. Moved by his modesty and despair, Margaret gifted him brushes dipped in precious colors and a sheet of fine vellum. In return for her kindness, Gerard felt a surge of gratitude, an emotion doubled by the unique spirit of the age, when artists and artisans walked side by side in love and reverence.

Back at home, the news of Gerard's ambition sparked a storm. When Gerard announced his intention to enter the great competition in Rotterdam to win prizes for painting and illumination, his mother erupted in panic. "Two gold crowns!" she shrieked, clutching her apron. "We cannot afford a single bushel of rye!" She trembled at the thought of spending a month's food on what she called "vanity." Little Catherine, however, pleaded silently for her brother, her violet eyes filled with trust. Seeing her distress, Gerard tried to explain that he could borrow vellum from the monks, reducing the cost. But his mother, fearing the loss of their savings, insisted he wait, her face flushed with anxiety.

Yet, Gerard's resolve was unyielding. The night before departing, Margaret handed him a letter addressed to Princess Marie in Rotterdam, urging him to seek her patronage. Dressed in his finest holiday garb—a silver-grey doublet, buckskin hose, and a hat tied with a purple ribbon from Little Catherine—Gerard set off for the city. Exhausted by the journey, he stumbled upon an old man and a young woman by a roadside fire. The old man wore the finery of a magistrate, yet his clothes were threadbare, and his purse empty. The young woman, dressed plainly, wore a necklace of silver cords and an expression of deep concern.

Gerard, sensing their plight, immediately lit a fire with sticks and wood, offering them bread and water from his flask. The young woman's face softened with gratitude as she watched his gentle energy. Just as Gerard prepared to gather more fuel, a magnificent figure approached on a mule draped in purple velvet. It was Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, the burgomaster of Tergou, a man of immense wealth and notorious stinginess.

Ghysbrecht froze, his face draining of color as he recognized the pair by the fire. He was accompanied by Peter, the old man he had seen earlier, whom he now realized was not his own father. The realization struck Ghysbrecht with the force of a thunderclap. Twenty years prior, he had committed a heinous act of treachery against a friend, stealing his fortune through deceit. That betrayal had ruined the man, leaving him destitute and sick. Now, seeing Peter and his daughter starving in the cold, Ghysbrecht felt the sting of guilt claw at his conscience. He reached for his purse, but his hand shook violently, paralyzed by the memory of his sin.

Ghysbrecht rode on, his wealth heavy on his person, yet his heart lighter than lead. He carried a "scorpion"—remorse—that gnawed at him, a poison that could never be cured, only borne. Meanwhile, Gerard returned to the fire, unaware of the tragedy unfolding nearby, and comforted the old man and his daughter, unknowingly paying back a debt of honor that Ghysbrecht had squandered decades ago. As Gerard prepared to continue his journey to Rotterdam, he felt a strange mix of anticipation and dread, knowing that the road ahead would test not only his skills but his faith in the justice of the world.

"The broth is piping hot," Gerard announced, his voice cutting through the quiet of the tent.

"But how shall we manage to convey it to our lips?" the elder gentleman asked, his tone heavy with despair. "My joints are stiff as iron, and kneeling over such a blazing fire with mere stalks seems impossible." Saint John the Baptist, he muttered, "what dexterity this youth possesses!"

As the old man voiced his dilemma, Gerard swiftly solved it. With a practiced flick of his wrist, he loosened the knot at his chest, removed his cap, and slipped a smooth stone into each corner. Wrapping his handkerchief around the flask, he snatched it from the heat and nestled it securely between the stones. He then lifted the cap and pressed it gently against the elder's nostrils, a mischievous grin playing on his lips. Trembling slightly, the old scholar inserted the end of the wheat straw and drew deeply. Instantly, his pale, gaunt countenance seemed to ignite, flushing with a radiant warmth. As he drew another long, sustaining breath, he exclaimed:

"Hippocrates and Galen!" he cried. "'Tis a *soupe au vin*—the panacea of panaceas. Praise God for the nation that devised it, the lady who crafted it, and the lad who delivers it to the dying. Share a sip, my daughter, whilst I recount the lore and virtues of this sovereign mixture to our young host. This restorative, young sir, was unknown to antiquity; it appears nowhere in their medical treatises nor in those popular chronicles that preserve their remedies, whether surgical or medicinal. Hector, in the *Iliad*, if my memory serves me right..."

(Margaret whispered softly, "Alas, he has begun.")

"...was summoned by one of the maidens of the tale to partake of a draught of wine. Yet he declined, citing that he was about to enter battle and must not consume anything to dull his faculties. Now, if the *soupe au vin* had been known in Troy, it is evident that in refusing plain wine on those grounds, he would have added in hexameter, 'Yet a *soupe au vin*, fair lady, I shall taste, with grateful heart.' Not only would this have been common courtesy—a virtue lacking in no perfect commander—but failing to do so would have marked him as shallow and imprudent, unfit to lead a war. For men entering combat require sustenance and every form of support, as proven by the fact that foolish generals, sending hungry troops to clash with well-fed foes, have been vanquished by inferior forces throughout the ages. The Romans suffered a crushing defeat in northern Italy at the hands of Hannibal, the Carthaginian, solely due to this oversight. Now, this celestial elixir bestows strength upon the limbs and fervor upon the spirit in a single instant; had it fallen into Hector's cup at the critical moment, aided by Apollo, Venus, and the holy saints, it would most likely have secured a victory for the Greeks. Note how faint and weary I was but a moment ago; well, I inhale this heavenly cordial, and now behold me brave as Achilles and strong as an eagle."

"Oh, father, now?" Margaret gasped. "An eagle, alas!"

"Girl, I defy thee and all creation," he declared. "Ready, I say, like a foaming charger, to devour the distance between here and Rotterdam, and mighty enough to combat the ills of life, even poverty and old age, which latter philosophers term the supreme evil. Deny me not, unless the man's life has been squandered—which, by the way, it usually has. Now, for the moderns!"

"Father! Dear father!"

"Fear me not, child; I shall be brief, unreasonably and unseasonably brief. The *soupe au vin* is absent from modern science; but this is merely further proof, if proof were required, that for the last few centuries, physicians have been fools, relying on chicken broth and decoctions of gold, thereby attributing the highest qualities to flesh with the least juice and to a metal possessing fewer chemical properties than all others; charlatans! blockheads! murderers! Since, therefore, no light is to be gleaned from these sources, let us turn to the chroniclers. First, we find Duguesclin, a French knight, preparing to engage the English—who, at that time, held sway over half of France and struck with formidable vigor by sea and land. He drank, not one, but three *soupe au vin* in honor of the Blessed Trinity. Having done so, he charged the islanders; and, as might have been predicted, slew a multitude and drove the remainder into the sea. But he was but the first of a long lineage of holy and relentless warriors who, by this divine restorative, have been sustained, fortified, strengthened, and consoled."

"My dear father, pray join yourself to that venerable company ere the soup grows cold." Holding the cap imploringly in both hands, she urged him to drink again.

This saved them from the "modern instances" and afforded Gerard an opportunity to tell Margaret how proud his mother would be if her soup had benefited a man of learning.

"Ay! but," said Margaret, "it would displease her greatly to see her son give so freely and receive nothing himself. Why did you bring but two straws?"

"Fair mistress, I hoped you would permit me to press my lips to your straw, seeing there were but two."

Margaret smiled and blushed. "Never beg that thou mayest command," she replied. "The straw is not mine, 'tis thine: thou cut it in yonder field."

"I cut it, and thus it became mine; but afterward, thy lip touched it, and thus it became thine."

"Did it? Then I shall lend it thee. Here—it is thine again; thy lip has touched it."

"No, it belongs to us both now. Let us divide it."

"By all means; thou hast a knife."

"No, I will not cut it—that would be unlucky. I shall bite it. Thus I shall keep my half; thou wilt surely burn thine, once thou returnest home, I suspect."

"You know me not. I waste naught. It is odds I fashion a hairpin thereof, or something similar."

This answer, rather than provoking Gerard, dashed his spirits, leaving him silent. And now, having disposed of the bread and soup, the old scholar prepared to resume his journey. Then arose a minor difficulty: Gerard, the adept, could not retie his ribbon as Catherine had tied it. Margaret, after watching his futile efforts with a sly, affectionate gaze, offered assistance. For at her age, girls delight in alternating between coy tenderness and saucy gentleness, and she perceived she had flustered him, only to soothe him now. A fair head, crowned with a stately mass of auburn hair, glossy and luminous amidst the silver threads, bowed sweetly toward him. As it captivated his vision, two white, supple hands played delicately upon the obstinate ribbon, molding it with soft, airy touches. A heavenly thrill coursed through the innocent young man, and vague glimpses of a new realm of feeling and sentiment opened before him. And these exquisite sensations Margaret, unknowingly, prolonged: it is not natural for her sex to hasten matters pertaining to the sacred toilet. Nay, when the slender fingers had finally subjugated the ends of the knot, her mind remained uneasy until, by a maneuver peculiar to the female hand, she had made her palm convex and applied it with gentle pressure to the center of the knot—a sweet, little coaxing kiss of the palm, as much as to say, "Now be a good knot, and remain so." The palm-kiss was bestowed upon the ribbon, but the wearer's heart leaped to meet it.

"There, that is how it ought to be," said Margaret, drawing back to inspect her work with one last keen scrutiny. Then, seeking simple approval of her skill, she met a gaze of such ardent adoration that she lowered her eyes quickly, coloring all over. An indescribable tremor seized her, and she retreated with downcast lashes and tell-tale cheeks, taking her father's arm on the opposite side. Gerard, blushing at having startled her with his gaze, took the other arm; and so the two youths departed, heads bowed and conscious, supporting the elderly scholar in silence.

They entered Rotterdam via the Schiedamse Poort. As Gerard was unfamiliar with the town, Peter directed him toward the Hooch Straet, where the Stadthouse stood. Himself, he accompanied Margaret to his cousin, located in the Ooster-Waagen Straet; thus, almost upon crossing the threshold, their paths diverged. They exchanged a friendly farewell, and Gerard plunged into the great city. A profound sense of solitude descended upon him, yet the streets teemed with life. Then he lamented, too late, that, out of delicacy, he had not inquired of his former companions who they were and where they dwelt.

"Beshrew my shamefacedness!" he murmured. "But their words and breeding surpassed their station, and something whispered me they wished to remain unknown. I shall never see her again. Oh, weary world, I hate thee and thy ways. To think I must encounter beauty, goodness, and learning—three pearls of priceless value—and never behold them again!"

Lost in this melancholy reverie, allowing his body to drift wherever it might, he momentarily lost his way

A grave, white-haired seneschal approached their table, bowing with courteous deference. "Is Gerard Eliassoen of your company?" he inquired softly. Upon receiving confirmation, the official announced, "The Princess Marie wishes to confer with you, young sir. I am to conduct you to her presence."

Instantly, every face within hearing snapped toward the speaker, etched with a mixture of sharp curiosity and jealous envy. To be summoned by a princess was a distinction that set one apart from the common herd.

Gerard rose immediately to obey.

"I wager we shall not see you again," Margaret remarked calmly, though a faint flush betrayed her composure.

"That you will," the scholar replied, leaning close to whisper, "This is my good princess; but you are my queen." Stepping back to address the company, he added, "Wait for me, I pray you. I shall return presently."

"Ay, ay!" Peter exclaimed, waking from his reverie simultaneously with the statement.

Once Gerard departed, the pair—clad in garments of simple homeliness, yet standing beside a man personally summoned by royalty—became the focal point ("the cynosure") of neighboring gazes. Observing this sudden attention, William Johnson stepped forward, feigning astonishment, and claimed kinship.

"And to think that I was standing at your backs, and you saw me not!" Johnson declared.

"Nay, cousin Johnson, I saw you long since," Margaret answered, her tone cool and distant.

"You saw me, and spoke not a word?"

"Cousin, it was for you to welcome us to Rotterdam, as it is for us to welcome you at Sevenbergen. Your servant denied us a seat in your house."

"The idiot!" Johnson muttered.

"And I had a mind to test if 'like maid, like master,'" Margaret continued, her wit cutting through the tension, "for there is often truth in bywords."

Johnson blushed a deep purple. He sensed the edge of Margaret's tongue and realized he was under scrutiny. Prudent in the face of suspicion, he chose action over explanation. He insisted that they accompany him home at once, vowing to prove whether they were truly welcome to Rotterdam or not.

"Who doubts it, cousin? Who doubts it?" the scholar interjected eagerly.

Margaret accepted his offer with gracious thanks, though she hesitated to depart immediately, citing a desire to listen to the minstrels once more. After a quarter-hour, Johnson pressed his proposal again, noting that many guests had already dispersed. Then, her genuine concern surfaced.

"It would be poor manners to abandon our friend now; he might lose us entirely. He knows not where we lodge in Rotterdam, and the city is vast. We have already partaken company once."

"Oh!" Johnson exclaimed, "We shall remedy that. My young man, ahem—I mean my secretary, shall remain seated here and await Gerard, leading him straight to my residence. He shall lodge with me and no other."

"Cousin, we shall be too burdensome."

"Nay, nay," Johnson dismissed her worry warmly. "You shall see whether you are welcome, you and your friends, and your friends' friends, if necessity demands. And I shall hear what the Princess requires of him."

A thrill of joy coursed through Margaret at the thought of Gerard lodging under the same roof as herself, yet a shadow of misgiving lingered. "But if your young man proves thoughtless, wanders off to play, and Gerard misses him?"

"He wander? Leave the spot where I placed him, and bid him stay? Ho! Stand forth, Hans Cloterman!"

A figure clad in black serge and dark violet hose materialized, stepping forward without a twitch or hesitation. He was a solemn, precise youth, the very embodiment of gravity and starched propriety. At his appearance, Margaret, overwhelmed by happiness, struggled to maintain her composure. Yet, she leaned toward Johnson and whispered, "I would wager my soul upon him. We are at your command, cousin, the moment you issue your orders."

Hans was instructed to take a seat at the table and wait for Gerard, guiding him to Ooster-Waagen Straet. He complied in silence, taking the indicated seat with mechanical exactitude. Margaret, Peter, and William Johnson then withdrew together.

"And, indeed, it is time you were abed, father, after all your travels," Margaret observed, a thought that had occupied her mind throughout.

Hans Cloterman sat, a statue of patience and business-like demeanor. Minutes ticked by, failing to stir any impatience in the perfect young man. Johnson, laughing to scorn the notion of his secretary abandoning his post for sport or youthful frivolity, proved justified.

As Gerard remained absent, the patient Hans, whose employer's gaze was no longer fixed upon him, amused the time by drinking goblets of Corsican wine. He consumed them solemnly, silently, and at intervals measured with rigorous accuracy. The wine was potent, and Cloterman's resolve was iron-clad. An hour had elapsed since Gerard's departure before the model secretary finally conceived the notion that Creation expected him to toast the health of all good fellows—and specifically the Duke of Burgundy, present in the hall. With this intention, he filled his cup nine times, rising gingerly, solemnly, and slowly. Reaching his full height, he instantly collapsed onto the grass, goblet in hand, spilling the chilled liquor over more than one ankle. His companions frisked, but Hans disturbed not a single muscle in his long, impassive face. Even in the total eclipse of reason, his countenance retained its gravity, primness, and infallible dignity.

Meanwhile, the seneschal led Gerard through winding passages until reaching the door of a pavilion. There, young noblemen, embroidered and feathered, sat sentinel around the heir-apparent, carding by the red glow of torches held by their attendants. A hush from the seneschal caused one of them to rise reluctantly, staring at Gerard with haughty surprise before entering the pavilion. He returned shortly thereafter, beckoning the pair, and guided them through additional corridors to an antechamber. Here sat three more young gentlemen, dressed in feathers and fur like works of fantasy, deeply engrossed in the instructive and edifying branch of learning known as dice.

"You cannot see the Princess," one declared, "it is too late."

Another agreed, "She passed this way but moments ago with her nurse. She is retired to bed, doll and all. Deuce—ace again!"

Prepared to withdraw, Gerard was met by the seneschal, who smiled with incredulity. "The young man is here by the Countess's orders; please conduct him to her ladies."

Upon this command, a superb Adonis rose with an injured expression and ushered Gerard into a chamber where eleven ladies sat or lounged, chatting like magpies. Two, more industrious than the rest, played cat's-cradle with fingers as nimble as their tongues. At the sight of a stranger, all tongues halted as if by some intricate machinery, and every eye swiveled toward Gerard. The young man, already uneasy, found his discomfort magnified by this battery of stares, causing his gaze to drop to the floor. Then, perceiving that they could frighten a creature, the timid ones giggled, reveling in their newfound prowess. A duenna then intervened severely, "Mesdames!" and instantly, abashed as if a string of modesty had been plucked, the chatter died. This same duenna took Gerard, marching before him in solemn silence. The young man's heart sank; he half considered turning and fleeing the place.

"What must princes be," he mused, "when their courtiers are so freezing? Doubtless they derive their breeding from him they serve."

These reflections were cut short when the duenna introduced him into a room where three ladies sat working, and a charming little girl tuned a lute. The ladies were richly, though not ostentatiously, attired. The duenza approached the one hemming a kerchief and spoke a few words in a low tone. That lady then turned toward Gerard with a smile, beckoning him closer. She did not rise, yet as she laid aside her work, her slight movement was suffused with grace, ease, and courtesy. Conversation flowed immediately.

"Margaret Van Eyck is an old friend of mine, sir, and I am right glad to receive a letter from her hand. I am also thankful to you, sir, for bringing it to me safely." Turning to the young lady, she added, "Marie, my love, this is the gentleman who delivered that pretty miniature."

"Sir, I thank you a thousand times," the young lady replied.

"I am pleased you consider yourself her debtor, sweetheart, for our friend requests a small service in return."

"I will do anything on earth for him," the young lady responded with ardor.

"Anything on earth amounts to nothing in the world," the Countess of Charolois corrected gently.

"Well, then, I will—" She paused, seeking direction. "What would you have me do, sir?"

Gerard, having just discerned the nature of high society, addressed her softly, a tremor in his voice. "My sovereign demoiselle, where no pain has been taken, no reward is needed."

"But we must obey mamma. All the world must obey."

"That is true. Then, our demoiselle, reward me, if you will, by allowing me to hear the stave you were singing before I interrupted it."

It was nearly four in the afternoon when Elias returned to his shop, finding both his eldest and youngest sons away on errands. Catherine, burdened by worry for Gerard, had ventured down the street with her small, crippled daughter, hoping to catch a glimpse of the boy returning. Leaving the children to search, Elias remained alone in the sitting room—a space defined as much by its peculiar inhabitants as by its furnishings.

The citizens of Tergou, descendants of the Dutch, prided themselves on inventing everything from printing and banking to cleanliness itself. While the local English gentry still trod upon floors matted with stale rushes and scattered with bones and debris, Elias’s parlor boasted tiles so brilliantly glazed and scrubbed that they might have served as a plate. Dominating the wall was a single large window, its central stone tracery forming a massive cross that seemed to stand in solemn relief, watched over with devotion by the family. The glass panes were tiny, diamond-shaped lozenges held together by lead strips, a rustic charm reminiscent of country cottages. Within the room stood rough, primitive chairs, save for one towering oak armchair whose back rose at right angles to the seat, forcing any sitter to stop two feet short of the top. Beside it lay a copper basin filled with holy water, attended by a besom brush, and a heavy oak table. Clinging to its edge was Giles, a dwarf whose proportions defied nature's usual balance. Where a normal child might expect symmetry, Giles was an inverted cone, his upper body disproportionately heavy compared to his legs. Yet, this very imbalance granted him supernatural agility; his light limbs allowed him to scale walls like squirrels and hang suspended by a single hand. His favorite pastime involved seizing the table with both hands, swinging wildly, and finally locking onto it with his teeth, freeing his hands to grip the wood with his jaws like a vice.

Peace was shattered when little Kate interrupted this display. Seeing her brother Sampsonet clinging to the furniture, she stood frozen, horrified not by the gymnastics, but by the damage to the wood. "Oh, Giles!" she cried, "Mother is coming! You are denting the table!"

Giles snarled, snapping his teeth shut on the oak. "Go tell your mother, little gossip. You are born to make trouble."

"Am I?" Kate asked, her voice calm and dry. "That is news to me."

"The biggest mischief-maker in all of Tergou," Giles grunted, tightening his grip.

"Oh, truly," Kate replied with a hint of sarcasm.

Having unleashed this unexpected wit without consequence, Giles settled back into his monstrous hug of the table. Kate, undeterred, sat down quietly and began to weep. Just then, Catherine entered, and Giles, startled, dove beneath the table, peering out through a gap with wide, glowing eyes.

"What is happening now?" Catherine demanded sharply. Her gaze shifted between Kate and the huddled figure below, noting the boy's sheepish expression. She raised a finger, threatening a scolding, but softened immediately.

"Nay, Mother," Kate said gently, wiping her tears. "It was merely a foolish word Giles spoke. I was tired and worried sick about Gerard, you know."

Before the mother could respond, a faint, tottering voice echoed from the doorway. "Let no one bear care for me." Gerard stepped into the room, pale, dusty, and utterly spent. Amidst a chorus of delighted cries and anxious embraces, he collapsed into the nearest chair, completely drained by his journey.

Their travels from Rotterdam, searching for Margaret, had broken him down, yet youth proved resilient. Instantly, Gerard became the center of an eager circle. Before all else, they clamored to hear about the prizes. Gerard recounted being admitted to view the competitors' works, laid out in a vast hall awaiting judgment. "Oh, Mother! Oh, Kate!" he exclaimed, his voice trembling with awe. "When I saw the goldsmiths' creations, I felt as though I should fall to the floor. I thought no smith alive possessed so much gold, silver, and jewel, nor crafted designs of such divine beauty. Truly, all art approaches the divine."

To satisfy the women, he detailed the religious treasures—reliquaries, chalices, crosses, and monstrances—and the secular wonders like goblets, clocks, chains, and brooches, causing their hearts to swell with longing. Then, his tone grew somber. "But, Kate, when I reached the illuminated manuscripts from Ghent and Bruges, my soul sank within me. Compared to them, mine was mere dust. For the first minute, I could hardly speak, but I prayed for a purer spirit. Soon, I was able to admire them, thanking God for these beautiful works and the skilled, patient artisans who created them. I realized how much I owed to them. The colored pages were so stunning I forgot the stark black-and-white sketches entirely."

"But what of the writing contest?" Elias pressed eagerly. "Whose name did the herald shout first?"

"Yours," Kate answered quickly.

The brothers scoffed, laughing her down.

"You may laugh," Gerard said firmly, "but the truth remains. Gerard Eliassoen of Tergou was the first name proclaimed. I stood stunned, dazed, until they pushed me forward. Everything swam before my eyes. I found myself kneeling on a cushion at the feet of the Duke. He spoke to me, but I was so fluttered I could not reply. Then, instead of drawing his sword to sever my dull head, he placed his hand to his side and bestowed upon me a gold medal. And here it is."

A wild cheer erupted, followed by a scramble for the prize. Gerard produced the medal and, to further delight, revealed fifteen golden angels. "I had seen one before, but I never held one until now. Here they are."

"Oh, Gerard! Oh, Gerard!" the family gasped.

He distributed the wealth among them: coins for his brothers Cornelis and Sybrandt, and for Kate and his sister Lily—"because God hath afflicted thee," he noted tenderly regarding Lily. He kept one for himself to buy colors and vellum, and set aside nine for the nurse who had risked two crowns for Gerard's sake.

The gold instantly revealed the true natures of the siblings. Cornelis and Sybrandt clutched their coins with greedy glares, their eyes darting enviously toward Kate, who held two pieces. Elias, meanwhile, seized his coin and rolled it across the floor, chasing it with gamboiling steps. Kate lowered her crutches, sat, and extended her small arms to Gerard in a gesture of pure, heavenly love. Catherine, initially stunned by the sudden shower of riches falling on her lap, eventually broke into tears. "Stop kissing him, Kate!" she cried. "He is my son, not yours. Ah, Gerard, my boy! I have not loved you as you deserved."

Gerard threw himself on his knees beside her, and she embraced him, weeping with overwhelming joy and pride. "Good lad! Good lad!" Elias cried, overcome with emotion. "I must go tell the neighbors! Lend me the medal, Gerard; I shall show it to my good friend Peter Buyskens. He is always boasting that his son Jorian won a tin mug at the shooting butts."

"Ay, do, Father," Gerard agreed, handing over the angel. "Show Peter Buyskens one of these angels. Tell him there are fourteen more where that came from. But mind you bring it back!"

"Eli," Catherine interjected softly, "stay a moment. There is better news behind."

Gerard flushed with pleasure at the anticipation. "Better? Better than this?"

Catherine nodded. Gerard then recounted his audience with the Countess, and the house rang with jubilation. "Now, God bless the kind lady, and bless Dame Van Eyck! We have secured a benefice. Our son! My cares are finally at an end. Elias, my friend and master, we two may now die happy whenever our time comes. This dear boy will take our place, and none of our loved ones will lack a home or a friend."

From that hour, Gerard became the pillar of the household, treated as a son apart, always in the right, and entitled to every kindness. Jealousy festered within Cornelis and Sybrandt; they longed for the day Gerard departed for his clerical post, dreaming that his departure would open the Reverend's purse to them. They actively cooperated with Gerard's ambition, their resentment fueled by the wound love had inflicted upon them. As Gerard's success grew, his self-regard inflated, and his bitterness toward Margaret deepened. Yet, she retained the power to chill his affections toward women generally, and now, driven by every motive, he resolved to become a priest as soon as he could advance through the orders. Already knowing the Vulgate better than most clergy, he studied the rubrics and dogmas alongside the monks. When the Bishop visited, Gerard applied to be admitted as an exorcist, the third step in holy orders. The Bishop examined him and ordained him immediately. After a brief prayer, the Bishop handed Gerard a small manuscript filled with exorcisms, commanding, "Take this, Gerard, and have power to lay hands on the possessed, whether baptized or catechumens!" Reverently receiving the book, Gerard returned home, now invested by the Church with the authority to cast out demons.

On his return from the church, little Kate met him, leaning on her crutches. "Oh, Gerard! Who do you think has come to seek you at our house? The Burg

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten was a man of guile, his mind a labyrinth where truth often hid behind a veil of plausible falsehood. To the novice standing before him, he presented a trivial task, far removed from his true intent. "The town records," he remarked, feigning concern over the ancient script, "are penned in a crabbed hand, and the ink has long since turned to rust." With a theatrical flourish, he extended an honor to Gerard: the transcription of these crumbling documents into a fair copy.

Gerard, ever practical, asked immediately for his wage.

Van Swieten named a sum so meager it covered barely the cost of pen, ink, and parchment itself.

"But, Burgomaster," Gerard protested, his voice rising slightly, "what of my labor? This is a year's work."

"Your labor?" Van Swieten scoffed, leaning back with a smirk. "Do you call the marking of parchment 'labor'? A drop of sweat falls upon it, I tell thee."

"It is labor, and skilled labor at that!" Gerard insisted, his pride stung. "In every craft, the hand of skill is honored above brute toil, regardless of the sweat. Furthermore, consider my time."

"Thy time?" The old man's eyes narrowed, fixing them sharply on Gerard to gauge the tremor of his words. "Why, what is time to thee, a boy of twenty-two? Nay, I say thou hast grown idle. Thou art in love. Thy body remains within these walls with the chanting monks, but thy heart wanders with Peter Brandt and his red-headed paramour."

"I know no Peter Brandt," Gerard denied quickly.

That denial, however, only hardened Van Swieten's suspicion. The young exorcist was playing a dangerous game, one the burgomaster intended to unravel.

"Lie!" Van Swieten roared, slamming his hand down. "Did I not find thee at her side upon the road to Rotterdam?"

"Ach!" Gerard gasped.

"Ach! And yet I saw thee at Sevenbergen but the other day."

"Was I?"

"Aye, at Peter's house."

"At Sevenbergen?"

"Yes, at Sevenbergen."

Here lay the trap, a bold assertion dressed as fact, designed to lure the youth into revealing his recent whereabouts through his own reaction. The result was immediate and startling. Gerard sprang upright, seized by a strange, electric nervousness.

"Burgomaster!" he cried, his voice trembling with a mixture of fear and defiance. "I have not set foot in Sevenbergen these three years! I know not the name of those who saw me, nor where they dwell! But as my time is precious, and yours seemingly worthless, I take leave of thee." With eyes that sparkled like flint, he darted past the door and vanished into the street.

Van Swieten leaped to his feet, a wave of furious rage washing over him, but he sat down heavily once more, his face pale.

"He fears me not," the old man muttered, his voice low. "He knows something—if not everything."

Before the silence could settle, he barked orders at his trusted servant, nearly hauling the man to the open window.

"See you yon fledgling?" Van Swieten cried, his breath coming fast. "Haste! Follow him! But ensure he sees not thy presence. He is young, yet old in cunning. Keep him under constant watch. Tell me whither he goes and what he doth."

Night fell before the servant returned, his step heavy with news.

"Well? Well?" Van Swieten demanded, springing forward.

"Master," the servant whispered, bowing low. "The young man departed thence straightway for Sevenbergen."

Van Swieten let out a long, mournful groan.

"To the house of Peter the Magician."

"Examine your own interior and record what you find!" cried Herr Cant, and the cuckoos of the earth seemed to answer his summons. Peer deeply into the Rhine, where its waters are profoundest, and into the Thames, where they flow thickest; then paint the silt beneath. Cast a bucket into a cistern of self-delusion, and whatever rises must, surely, be eternal verity, must it not? Yet, first among truths, no child of Adam ever truly reads his own heart save through the habit formed and the illumination gained by studying countless others. Even then, armed with sagacity borrowed from reflection, he can only decipher the script of his soul, never reading it with fluent ease.

Halfway to Sevenbergen, Gerard cast his gaze inward and questioned his spirit regarding his journey there. The heart responded without a moment's pause: "We depart solely out of curiosity—to learn why she spurned us, to demonstrate that her rejection has shattered none of us, and to prove that we remain entirely content with our future honors and the benefice awaiting us. We desire neither her nor any ally of her fickle sex."

Soon, Gerard located Peter Brandt's cottage. A maiden sat in the doorway, working diligently with her needle, while a burly figure leaned upon a longbow, conversing with her. A sharp, inexplicable ache seized Gerard at the sight. Yet, the man proved to be past fifty, an ancient soldier whom Gerard recalled seeing display admirable force and skill at the shooting butts. In another heartbeat, the youth stood before them. Margaret raised her eyes, dropped her work, and emitted a faint cry, her countenance alternating between pale and flushed. These tokens of emotion vanished swiftly, replaced by a chill, stony indifference far colder than if she had never betrayed any agitation.

"What! Is it you, Master Gerard?" she exclaimed. "What on earth brings you here, I wonder?"

"I chanced upon you and wished to pay respects and inquire after your father."

"My father is well. He will arrive shortly."

"Then I suppose I may wait for his return."

"As you wish. Good Martin, go into the village and tell my father a friend awaits him."

"And not a friend of yours?"

"A friend of my father is a friend of mine."

"That remains doubtful. It was unlike a friend to promise to wait, only to vanish the instant my back was turned. Cruel Margaret, you know not how I scoured the town for you; how, lacking you, nothing brought joy to me."

"These are idle words. If you had truly desired my father's or my company, you would have returned. I had prepared a bed for you at my cousin's house; he would have honored you greatly, and perhaps, who knows, I might have done likewise. That day, I was in the right humor. Do not expect me to be so again, nor should any young man dare hope for it."

"Margaret, I returned the moment the Countess released me, but you were nowhere to be seen."

"Nonsense! You did not return, or you would have seen Hans Cloterman at our table. We sent him to escort you on."

"I saw no one there, save a drunken man who had just fallen."

"At our table? Describe his attire."

"I paid little heed; he wore garments of sombre hue."

At these words, Margaret's complexion slowly warmed. Soon, however, adopting an air of incredulous severity, she posed numerous shrewd inquiries, to which Gerard replied with unwavering loyalty. Eventually, the clouds dissipated, and they deduced the origin of their misunderstanding. Followed by a sudden flood of tenderness, intensified by the wrongs they had inflicted upon one another, came mutual confessions. Neither had known peace since their parting, and neither could ever have found happiness apart from this fortunate reunion.

Gerard discovered a Latin Vulgate manuscript lying open on the table and lunged for it like a hawk. Manuscripts were his supreme delight; yet before his fingers could grasp the pages, two pale hands descended flat upon the leaf, and a crimson face emerged above them.

"No, remove those hands, Margaret, that I may see where you read, and I shall read alongside you at home; thus our souls shall meet within the sacred page. Will you not allow it?" She refused. "Then I must kiss them away." He pressed kisses upon her hands so frequently that, overcome by sheer shame, she was forced to withdraw them. And behold! The holy book lay open at the verse, "An apple of gold in a setting of silver."

"There!" she declared. "I had been searching for this line for ages, and found it only moments ago—and yet, I am caught!" With a touch of inconsistency, she indicated the passage to Gerard with her fair finger.

"Yes," he murmured, "but today, it is all concealed within that large cap."

"It is a becoming cap, I am told by some."

"Perhaps; yet what it conceals is beautiful."

"It is not; it is hideous."

"Well, it appeared beautiful in Rotterdam."

"Yes, everything was beautiful that day"—she sighed softly.

Then Peter entered, welcoming Gerard warmly and offering him supper. Margaret vanished, leaving Gerard to enjoy a pleasant, scholarly conversation with Peter. Soon, Margaret reappeared, her hair arranged in a silver net, casting a glance that was half-coquettish, half-playful. She moved about them with grace, spreading the meal, and radiated a brightness born of genuine joy and contentment. In the cooling evening, Gerard enticed her outside; she protested, yet complied. He urged her along the path toward Tergou; she demurred, yet followed. They strolled hand in hand, pausing occasionally. When he finally needed to depart, they pledged themselves never again to quarrel or misunderstand one another, sealing their vow with a long, affectionate kiss. Gerard returned home on wings.

From that day forward, Gerard devoted most of his evenings to Margaret. Their attachment deepened with every passing hour, until the time they shared became the substance of their existence, rendering the remainder of life merely endured. At first, this bond proceeded smoothly. Obstacles existed, yet to the eyes of hope, youth, and love, they appeared distant and trivial. The volatile feelings and passions of so many others, destined to thwart this union, offered no warning smoke of their volcanic nature or power. The course of true love ran placid and smooth, drawing these two young hearts into its current forever.

And then—

Upon a morning of unusual brilliance, the streets of Tergou shimmered under a canopy of velvet skies. Horses' hooves clattered rhythmically, and windows and balconies bristled with curious onlookers. The French Ambassador traversed the city en route to hunt in the adjacent woods, accompanied not merely by his own retinue but by several attendants loaned from the Duke of Burgundy to render him honor and service. Leading this procession was the Duke's jester, a figure whose grave, sedate majesty rendered his nobler companions to seem mere frivolities. Yet, whenever the atmosphere grew too thick with reverence, the jester would dismount with a clumsy, ignoble grace, provoking bursts of laughter from even the dignified Ambassador. He would then climb back onto his steed by the tail, playing his role with a provocative, comic flair. Bringing up the rear, however, drew far greater attention: the Duke's leopard.

Mounted upon a Flemish steed of colossal power, a huntsman carried a long box strapped securely to his waist. Upon this box sat a brilliant leopard, crouching low, chained firmly to the rider. The crowd marveled at her glossy coat and distinctive spots, pressing close until a few reached out to touch or pull at her tail. Then the huntsman's voice boomed, terrifying and sharp, "Beware! In Antwerp, a single handful of dust cast upon her caused the Duke to turn to dust!"

"Gramercy!" echoed a nearby spectator.

"I speak truth," the huntsman insisted. "The good Duke imprisoned a mocker in a subterranean cell where the rats stripped him clean of flesh within a single night. Justice served him right for disturbing the beast."

A ripple of fear swept through the Tergovians, shrinking them from even the thought of teasing the sovereign's leopard. Yet, their spirits lifted shortly thereafter with the arrival of the Duke's giant, a Hungarian towering seven feet and four inches. Like other giants of legend, he possessed a treble, flute-like voice lacking in power, yet he was utterly unaware of this flaw, consumed by vanity. As the pageant wound down, the giant spotted Giles perched atop a distant balcony. Stopping abruptly, he addressed the diminutive figure.

"Hallo! Brother!" he squeaked, his voice thin and airy. "I nearly passed without spotting thee."

"You are plain enough to see," Giles bellowed back, his bass tones resonating like a deep drum.

"Come on my shoulder, brother!" squeaked Titan, extending a massive arm as an invitation.

"If I do, I'll cuff your ears," roared the dwarf.

Recognizing the homunculus's irascibility, the giant pounced upon him, encouraged by the surrounding chorus of laughter. However, the spectators laughed not at the giant's wit, but at the absurd juxtaposition of the two voices—the gigantic body emitting a feeble whistle, and the tiny mound belching thunder. The incongruity created a singular duet that ended as uniquely as it began. Giles, losing all patience and self-command, seized the moment. Devoid of fear and driven to boot, he leaped from the balcony, dropping directly onto the giant's neck. With one hand, he gripped the giant's hair; with the other, he rained punches upon his head.

The giant's initial impulse to laugh vanished under the weight and speed of the blows. "He! He! Ah! Ha! Hallo! Oh! Oh! Holy Saints! Here! Help! Or I must throttle the imp! I can't! I'll split your skull against the—" he screamed, making a wild, stumbling run backward toward the balcony. Giles, sensing imminent disaster, grabbed the railing and whirled over it just as the giant's head collided with it with a stunning crack.

The populace roared with laughter and exultation at the little champion's acrobatics. Indignant, the giant seized two laughing bystanders, knocking them together like dumbbells before shaking them flat onto the cobblestones. Catherine shrieked, throwing her apron over Giles, before the enraged giant strode wrathfully away to join his master. Though no one present foresaw the long-term consequences, the immediate effect was agreeable: the Tergovians felt a surge of pride in Giles, listening with newfound affability to his pleas for parchment. Indeed, he conducted a regular trade with his brother Gerard, begging sheets freely while Gerard supplied copper coins.

Later that afternoon, Catherine and her daughter Kate sat chatting of their favorite topic: Gerard, his kindness, his benefice, and the brightened prospects of their entire household. Their fortune had arrived precisely as they wished. Beyond the material advantage of a clerical income—such as Countess Charolois would surely grant—they shared a profound feminine delight in having a holy man within their own circle. "He will marry Cornelis and Sybrandt," Kate declared enthusiastically, noting that the girls were now of age. "Gerard will tend to you and Giles when we depart."

"Yes, Mother," Kate agreed, adding, "And we may confess to him rather than a stranger."

"Aye, girl! And he may administer the sacred oil to your father and me, closing our eyes when our time comes."

"Oh, Mother!" Kate interjected softly, her expression shifting. "Not for many, many years, I pray Heaven. Pray speak not of that; it always saddens me. I hope to go before you, Mother dear. No, let us be gay today. I am free of all pain, Mother, quite out of it all; it seems so strange. I feel so bright and happy that..." She paused, lowering her voice. "Can you keep a secret?"

"Better than anyone, child. Why, you know I can."

"Then I will show you something so beautiful. You have never seen its like, I trow. Only Gerard must never know; surely he intends to surprise us with it. He hides it so carefully, and sometimes carries it away entirely."

Taking her crutches, Kate rose and moved slowly away, leaving her mother in a state of heightened curiosity. Soon she returned, bearing something wrapped in cloth. Unwrapping it, she revealed a lovely image of the Virgin Mary, adorned with all her insignias, her tiara resting above a cascade of magnificent, flowing hair. Catherine was immediately struck with awe.

"It is herself," she cried. "It is the Queen of Heaven. I have never beheld one like her before."

"And her eyes, Mother," Kate observed, pointing upward. "Lifted to the sky, as if they belong there, not to a mortal creature. And her beautiful hair, burning gold."

"To think I possess a son capable of making saints live again upon wood!" Catherine marveled.

"The reason is simple, Mother," Kate whispered reverently. "He is a young saint himself. He is too pure for this world; he is here to portray the blessed, and then he will depart to be with them forever."

Before they could finish their adoration, a strange voice intruded from the door. Driven by instinct, the women hastily concealed the picture. Moments later, Ghysbrecht Van Swieten entered, casting furtive glances around the room—a man who had not set foot in the house for ten years.

Startled, the women stared at him, exchanging a look. "The burgomaster!" they murmured. The tone compelled Ghysbrecht to acknowledge it.

"Yes! I admit the last time I visited was not on a friendly errand. Men love their own interests—Elias's and mine were opposed. Let this visit atone for the last. Today, I come solely for your business, not mine."

Catherine and Kate exchanged a swift glance of contemptuous incredulity. They knew the man far better than he imagined.

"It concerns your son, Gerard," Ghysbrecht stated.

"Ay! Ay! You wish him to labor for the town gratis. He told us."

"I come on no such errand," Ghysbrecht countered, his demeanor shifting. "I am here to warn you: he has fallen into bad hands."

"Now, Heaven and the saints forbid!" Catherine cried, clutching her hands. "Man, torture not a mother! Speak out, quickly! Speak before you have time to coin falsehood; we know you!"

Ghysbrecht turned pale at the affront, spite mingling with his motives. "Thus it stands," he ground out, speaking rapidly. "Your son Gerard is more likely to become a father of a family than a priest. He is constantly with Margaret, Peter Brandt's red-haired girl, and loves her like a cow her calf."

Both mother and daughter burst into laughter. Ghysbrecht stared, bewildered.

"What! Did you know it?"

"Carry this tale to those who do not know my son, Gerard," Kate declared. "Women are naught to him."

"Other women, perhaps. But this one is the apple of his eye, or soon will be, if you do not part them. Come, Dame, do not waste my time and friendly counsel. My servant has witnessed them together dozens of times, gazing into one another's eyes like... you know, Dame—you have been young, too."

"Girl, I am uneasy. Yes, I have been young and know how blind and foolish youth can be. My heart! He has turned me sick in a moment." Turning to Kate, she asked, "If it should be true?"

"Nay, nay!" Kate cried eagerly. "Gerard might love a young woman; all young men do. I

While the burgomaster of Tergou exposed Gerard to the town's scorn, a quieter, yet deeper torment beset Margaret at Sevenbergen. Her sorrow was born of a housewife's despair, a hunger that gnawed harder than any physical want. She knelt before Martin Wittenhaagen, the aged soldier, her eyes wet with unshed tears.

"Martin," she whispered, "there is naught in the larder, yet Gerard returns tonight. His heart is kind, though his mind is scattered; he forgets to dine at home. When his labors cease, he rushes to me, often so weak with fatigue that he nearly faints. And what cruel irony—that I possess no bread to feed the servant who loves me so dearly!"

Martin scratched his chin, his brow furrowed. "Pray tell, what hope remains?"

"It is Thursday," she replied, her voice trembling. "By custom, it is your day to hunt. I had pinned my hopes upon your return today."

"Nay," the soldier grunted, shaking his head. "The Duke hunts today, and his friends. To venture forth would be madness. Read this." He produced a parchment sealed with a grand crest. It bore the King's command: a stipend of four merks annually, granted to Martin Wittenhaagen for service in the Duke's wars and a wound sustained at the Duke's side, alongside a license to shoot three arrows weekly—only on Thursdays—in the Duke's forests of Holland. The law was strict: he might only take a seven-year-old buck or a doe with a fawn, and never if the Duke himself hunted. To break this rule meant forfeiting his salary, facing a heavy fine, and risking his life.

Margaret let out a long, weary sigh and fell silent.

"Cheer up, mistress," Martin said gently, softening his tone. "For your sake, I will risk my carcass. I have done so for many who were not worthy of a forefinger. Is there not a hare or a young fawn within reach?"

"If I permit you to go," she insisted, her voice breaking, "promise me you will not stray far, nor be seen. Far better for Gerard to sleep without supper than for harm to come to you, faithful Martin."

Her plea moved him. With a solemn vow, Martin took his bow and three arrows, slipping quietly into the woods that bordered the estate. The air hummed with the distant, faint notes of horns; every beast was on the move. *Here,* Martin thought, *I shall fill the pot unseen.*

He positioned himself behind a massive oak overlooking a sunlit glade, drawing his formidable weapon. It was crafted from English yew, towering six feet two inches, thick and powerful. Martin, a man of iron arms and cord-like muscles, had drawn it since boyhood. He could pull a three-foot arrow to its limit, and when released, the string sang like a harp string plucked by a master, the flight swift enough to blur the eye. This bow had felled countless soldiers in the wars of the Hoeskens and Cabbel-jaws, where battles were fought not in clouds of smoke, but with stark, visible brutality.

First, a hare cantered into view, stopping with ears pricked forward in a perfect 'V'. Martin leveled his aim. The arrow flew, the string twanged, but in his haste, he missed by mere inches. The shaft struck the earth beside the fleeing animal, passing beneath her belly like a ghost before vanishing into the grass. The hare leaped three feet upward and vanished.

"Bungler!" Martin muttered to himself. A true proof of his skill was that he blamed the hare, not his hand. Before he could fit another arrow, a wood-pigeon landed softly upon the branch above him. *Small, but dainty,* he thought. This time, he took care. He drew slowly, loosed smoothly, and watched the arrow pierce the bird's breast. Yet, instead of falling, the bird fluttered feebly, rising with a great effort only to fall dead fifty yards away, hidden by the thick foliage.

"Fortune turns against me," he murmured, spirits sinking. He fitted another arrow and scanned the glade. Suddenly, a commotion erupted behind him. Turning, he saw a magnificent stag crossing the open space, but it was too late; the animal had already slipped past. Frustrated, Martin slammed his bow down in anger. Just then, a long-spotted beast glided swiftly across the clearing after the deer—a leopard. Its belly brushed the earth as it moved.

Martin snatched up his bow instantly. Recognizing the Duke's leopard, he knew the hunters would be close. "Gerard must go supperless tonight," he resolved grimly.

He plunged back into the woods, following the tracks of the stag and the leopard. Soon, the sounds grew louder: leaves rustled violently, and the ground trembled under hooves. Rushing forward, he found the scene of chaos: the leopard, mounted upon the stag's back, tearing flesh with teeth and claws. The stag ran in a convulsive circle, blood streaming down its hide.

Desperation sparked a new resolve. For Margaret, he would secure the meat. Drawing his arrow to full length, he buried it deep in the stag's flank. Despite the beast atop him, the stag bounded high into the air and collapsed, dead. The leopard continued to tear at the carcass, undeterred.

Martin hoped the predator would gorge itself until it slept, allowing him to claim the kill. Minutes passed. He stepped forward, placing a hand on the stag's leg. The leopard emitted a terrible growl, pausing her feeding. She regarded him with a sullen, wary gaze. How to proceed? He recalled the old lore: wild creatures fled before the steady human eye. Standing tall, he fixed his gaze upon the beast. She returned the stare, unblinking. Then, the leopard, ignorant of nature's laws, lunged at his head with a roar, jaws gaping wide, eyes aflame.

Time froze. Martin caught her by the throat just as her teeth sought to crush his face. One claw raked his shoulder, tearing through leather and skin; the other, aimed at his cheek, snagged on the loose leather of his scapulary, which he wore like a hood over his jerkin. The brute's claw caught, and the struggle began.

Martin held her teeth back with iron strength, squeezing her throat while she ripped at his shoulder. It felt like blunt reaping-hooks grinding together. The pain was excruciating, yet it fired his blood rather than cowed him. He gnashed his teeth in a rage nearly equal to hers, and with a grip of steel, he maintained the pressure. Their eyes flared in a deadly duel of wills. Realizing she was being strangled, she made a wild, frantic lunge, dragging his hood across his face and blinding him, tearing the claw free from his shoulder, flesh and bone. Yet, he did not falter; he throttled her with hand and arm.

Suddenly, her tail, raised high, dropped. "Aha!" Martin cried joyfully, gripping her like death itself. Next, her body lost all elasticity. He held a choked, powerless thing, maintaining his hold until all motion ceased. Only then did he dash her to the earth, panting heavily, and remove his hood.

At his feet lay the leopard, silent, tongue protruding, a bloody paw twitching. For the first time, terror seized Martin. "I am a dead man," he whispered. "I have slain the Duke's leopard."

Hastily, he gathered handfuls of dry leaves and threw them over the corpse, concealing the evidence. Shouldering the heavy carcass, he staggered away, leaving a trail of mixed blood—the stag's and his own.

He burst into Peter's house, a horrifying figure, bleeding and stained. "There!" he roared, casting the deer down. "Broil me a steak, and ask no questions. I am faint."

Margaret, seeing the blood, assumed it came solely from the deer. She busied herself at the fire while the sturdy soldier bound his wounds. Soon, Gerard joined them, bringing a flask of Schiedam wine. Under its warmth, Martin recovered, recounting the tale of the hunt. They ate royally on the broiled venison, their mirth ringing with the memory of the exploit.

Then, silence fell. Margaret's eyes widened, fixed and transfixed, her cheeks draining of color. She gasped, unable to speak, pointing a trembling finger toward the window.

All turned to look. There, in the deepening twilight, crouched a dark, hunched form. Two eyes burned with the cold, green fire of glowworms.

It was the leopard.

Petrified, they stared as the beast sat unmoving. From the woods outside, a single, deep bay echoed, followed immediately by the chorus of many more.

"They have lost her," Martin trembled, the realization chilling his blood. "Muzzled bloodhounds are on her scent. They will find her here, and the venison. Good-bye, friends. Martin Wittenhaagen ends here."

Gerard grabbed his bow and thrust it into the soldier's hands. "Be a man!" he cried. "Shoot her and cast her into the wood before they arrive. Who knows?"

Voices of hounds grew louder, closer, their bays a terrifying crescendo.

"Curse her!" Martin screamed, his face contorted. "Once I spared her;

Margaret severed a generous portion of venison and hurled it through the open window, where it landed squarely in the emerald gaze of the fire-leopards. With a savage, guttural snarl, the beasts lunged forward, tearing and crunching the meat until a sudden, piercing bay from a hound echoed through the hall, causing the trio at the sill to recoil in terror. Realizing her supper was compromised, the leopard slipped away with furtive grace into the treeline. Moments later, the clamor of horses, men, and curs erupted past the window as they pursued the beast across the countryside. Safe in the knowledge that the animal was too swift to be caught within a league of their home, Martin and his companions exhaled, clasping hands in relief. Seizing the brief respite, Margaret allowed herself a quiet sob, which Gerard gently wiped away with a kiss.

Back at the table, Gerard raised his cup to toast "Woman's Wit," declaring it far superior to raw masculine strength. "Only when those she loves are in peril," Margaret replied softly. That night, Gerard lingered beside her longer than custom allowed, returning home buoyed by a pride that felt akin to royalty. Yet, scarcely a mile from home, beneath the canopy of trees, two figures materialized, blocking his path. It was his father and mother. A sudden chill seized him. They stood immovable, grim and silent. Stammering inquiries died on his lips.

"Why ask?" his father growled. "You know why we follow."

"Oh, Gerard!" his mother interjected, her voice a tangled weave of reproach and tenderness.

Gerard's heart hammered against his ribs, silence falling over him like a shroud. His father, however, softened the blow, chiding him gently: "Do not bow your head like a guilty fool. You are not the first young man ensnared by a rosy cheek and azure eyes." Catherine added quickly, attributing their union to witchcraft, citing the fame of Peter the Magician. Her husband, Elias, pressed for a promise that Gerard would cease visiting Sevenbergen, offering leniency for one fault.

"I cannot promise that, Father," Gerard whispered.

"Not promise it, you young hypocrite!" Elias snapped.

"Nay, Father, do not misname me," Gerard pleaded, his voice breaking. "I lacked the courage to speak of what I feared would grieve you. I am grateful to the friend who revealed your knowledge. It is a heavy burden lifted. Yes, Father, I love Margaret. Call me not a priest, for I shall never be one. I would sooner die."

"That we shall see," Elias muttered, his patience fraying. "Come, do not defy me further. You shall learn the price of disrespect."

The return walk was a somber procession, broken only by Catherine's occasional, heavy sigh. From that moment, the tranquil sanctuary of the Tergou house dissolved into a battlefield. The following day, Gerard was summoned before the entire family, assailed by a chorus of condemnation save for little Kate and the dwarf, whose loyalty stemmed from Kate's influence. Cornelis and Sybrandt, his cousins, were particularly venomous. Gerard, stunned by the sudden shift in alliances, cast a desperate glance at his younger sister. Her eyes, usually warm, filled with tears at the harsh words directed at him, who had been the household's favorite just weeks prior. Yet, she offered no solace, turning her face away to mutter, "Pray to Heaven to cure you of this madness!"

"What, am I your enemy too?" Gerard asked, his voice thick with sorrow. Rising with a deep, weary sigh, he departed the house and headed toward Sevenbergen.

Conflict between those bound by affection yet divided by opposing interests begins innocently, often with both parties claiming moral superiority. However, as discord endures, it widens and deepens, fueled by human fallibility and irritability, inevitably leading both sides to error. Gerard, possessing a broader and more cultivated mind, perceived nuances that his hot-tempered kin missed. While Elias raged and the cousins plotted with malice, Gerard suffered in quiet unhappiness, plagued by indecision and a profound loneliness. He had no confidant. Margaret, spirited and proud, he dared not burden with his domestic torment; she might, to save her own future, side with his relatives and abandon him. Though she had been a beacon of comfort in the past, her own history—a lifelong sacrifice of marriage and motherhood for the sake of painting—made him hesitate. Could he ask her to choose between him and the artistic solitude she had chosen?

Just as Gerard teetered on the brink of surrender, a formidable adversary emerged: Catherine Senior. Good-hearted yet unrefined, she lacked the steadiness of her daughter, oscillating wildly between irritation and self-serving attacks. On one occasion, Cornelis suggested that Gerard wed Margaret merely to satisfy hunger. Catherine retorted fiercely, asking what provision the lazy cousins had made for themselves, accusing them of fearing Gerard would claim their inheritance. These moments sparked fleeting, slinking smiles in Gerard, momentarily confusing Catherine's allies. But after six months of relentless friction, the pressure reached a breaking point.

In front of the assembled family, Elias announced that he had ordered the Burgomaster to imprison Gerard in the Stadthouse, preventing his marriage to Margaret. Gerard paled, his blood running cold, yet he forced himself to remain silent. His father continued, decreeing that Gerard would be ordained a priest within the year, regardless of his will.

"Is this so?" Gerard cried, his voice ringing with sudden, fierce resolve. "Hear me, all! By God and Saint Bavon, I swear I will never wear the habit of a priest so long as Margaret lives. If force decides this, then let it be tried, Father! But know this: the day the Burgomaster arrives to drag me away, I shall leave Tergou forever, forsake Holland, and abandon your house, where I have been valued not for myself, but for what you can extract from me."

Spurning the room, he fled, his face white with anguish and despair. Outside, Catherine lamented the cruelty of driving young hearts too hard, fearing he would leave them unmarried or married alike.

Emerging from the house, Gerard met Reicht Heynes, who bore a message from Margaret Van Eyck requesting an audience. Finding the elderly woman sitting sternly, Reicht Heynes questioned why Gerard had neglected her lately. Before Gerard could respond, Reicht accused him in a sneering tone of failing to confess his love.

"It is because you never told her," Reicht cooed, feigning pity.

Gerard, confused and stammering, admitted his fear of being called a fool. Reicht scoffed at the notion, recounting her own youthful rejection of love for art, leaving her a childless widow surrounded by grandchildren while her former lovers thrived. "I look upon you as my son," she declared warmly. "You are good, handsome, and a painter. I will not let you waste your youth as I did yours. Marry this Margaret. I have inquired; she is a dutiful daughter."

Moved by her wisdom, Gerard poured out his heart, and Reicht and Margaret both shed tears of empathy. Women possessed a unique, vicarious courage, unshaken by the tremors that paralyzed men. Through their support, Gerard realized he must seize the initiative. He resolved to marry Margaret immediately, hoping the deed would settle the matter. Yet, listening to their counsel, he hesitated. Fear of his father's wrath, not imprisonment, haunted him. If he defied his father openly, Elias would disown him entirely, condemning Margaret to a lifetime of poverty under a father's curse.

Could he propose a different path? He imagined eloping with Margaret to a foreign land where his art was highly valued, perhaps Italy. After a year or two, free from his family's shadow and financially secure, he could return, begging forgiveness not for rebellion, but for survival, offering only his love and labor, not their wealth.

"The idea is sound," Reicht whispered, her eyes gleaming. "There lies a realm where fortunes await you. Here, art freezes; there, it flourishes like never before."

"Italy!" Gerard exclaimed. "Italy!"

"Yes, Italy!" Reicht affirmed. "Where painters are honored as princes and scribes earn three hundred crowns for a single copy. The Pope seeks skilled hands to transcribe the treasures fleeing Constantinople."

"But the journey costs a fortune," Gerard protested.

"I shall find the means," Reicht promised. "Ten golden angels to buy you passage to Rome. And bring the girl with you, if she loves you truly."

For hours, they sat discussing the plan, and afterward, Gerard carried a secret talisman against the storms of home. Beyond the funds, Margaret Van Eyck gifted him invaluable technical secrets. She spoke of Jan van Eyck's mastery, revealing that the artist's longevity was due to his refusal to rush. He ground his own pigments, preparing panels a year in advance. She warned him against boiling oils, explaining that heat melted vegetable impurities into the heart of the medium, killing the color. Instead, she taught him to wash oil in bottles, decanting the muddy water, and then exposing it to sunlight in a glass trough to clarify it further without turning it to varnish. "Jan never hurried," she concluded, "and neither will the world forget him."

Armed with money and knowledge, Gerard decided to flee with his bride. He traveled to Sevenbergen to announce his resolution to Margaret Brandt and publish the banns. However, when he proposed

The legal formalities of matrimony required the reading of the banns three times, a custom unchanged from modern days, save for a local variation wherein the curate, newly arrived in the parish, was readily swayed by the earnest entreaties of the young pair to compress these readings into a single twenty-four-hour span. Consequently, the announcements were proclaimed on Monday at both Matins and Vespers. To the profound relief of the lovers, the church remained empty of the townsfolk of Tergou; the next morning, however, the atmosphere shifted from anticipation to dread. As Gerard and Margaret entered, trembling with anxiety, a stranger rose in the midst of the congregation and, to their horror, interdicted the union. His grounds were twofold: the alleged minority of the parties and the purported lack of parental consent.

Outside, amidst the shadows of the nave, Margaret and Gerard engaged in a desperate, quivering consultation, their hopes seemingly extinguished. Before they could formulate a defense, the officious interloper approached them. With feigned regret, he explained that while his heart inclined toward their happiness, his livelihood depended entirely upon the prohibition of illicit matches. "A crown," he offered, "shall suffice to unravel this tangle; inform the priest that I was mistaken, and the ceremony proceeds as planned."

"A golden angel!" Gerard exclaimed, his spirit igniting at the prospect. The man, equally eager, accepted the sum and presented himself to the curate, confessing a ludicrous error corrected by a mere glimpse of the couple. The priest, relieved, scheduled the wedding for the following day at ten. The professional impediment departed with his payment, a fool indeed among knaves, and sought solace in a local tavern known for its green dedicated to archery and rustic sports. Intoxicated by his success, he boasted of his exploit to the patrons, specifically a frequent visitor named Sybrandt, a man of dubious repute. Sybrandt, absorbing every detail, fled home to deliver the news to his father, who was away in Rotterdam procuring cloth. Sybrandt signaled his elder brother, confiding the tale, and together they became the "black sheep" of a large family—men whose idleness had festered into a venomous obsession with inheritance, transforming their parents' prudence into avarice.

Conspiring in secrecy, the brothers resolved not to burden their mother, whose temperament was volatile, but to seek the Burgomaster, Ghysbrecht Van Swieten. Though they suspected his opposition, they could not discern the motive. Swieten, a man of sharp intellect, saw through their scheme immediately, masking his insight behind a veneer of magisterial indifference. "Since the father is absent, the duty falls to me, the father of the town," he declared, dismissing them with a supercilious air, ashamed of his unwitting accomplices.

Upon returning home, the brothers discovered Gerard seated at his mother's knee, receiving tender affection and promises of support. Yet, the catalyst for her sudden reversal was a moment of female fury: having torn Margaret's portrait to shreds in a fit of irritation, she had witnessed Gerard collapse into a state of ghastly stillness, his eyes fixed on the fragments until tears blinded him. Terror-stricken by her cruelty, her conscience had softened, and she vowed to repair the breach. Unaware of the immediate cause, Gerard, inexperienced in feminine capriciousness, interpreted her kindness as a restoration of maternal warmth, kissing her repeatedly and retreating to his chamber a happy prince, oblivious to the storm brewing within his own household.

The following morning, at ten, Gerard and Margaret stood in the church of Sevenbergen, radiant with joy, flanked only by Peter and Martin Wittenhaagen. Secrecy was paramount; Margaret had refused Italy to remain near her helpless, scholarly father, settling instead on a brief exile in Flanders. As the curate prepared to speak, a harsh voice shattered the sanctity of the moment. "Forbear!" cried a constable of Tergou, seizing Gerard in the name of the law. Martin's long knife flashed, prompting the priest to denounce the impiety of drawing weapons within holy bounds. The burgomaster's servant, however, asserted that the youth married against his father's will and that justice demanded his removal to Rotterdam to await the Duke's sentence.

Margaret screamed in despair, and the lovers collapsed into each other's arms, weeping piteously. One of the constables, stepping forward under the guise of separation, whispered to Margaret, "Rotterdam? 'Tis a lie. We merely escort him to the Stadthouse." True to his word, Gerard was removed on horseback, subjected to a dozen halts and sly detours, until, just outside Tergou, he was placed in a crude canvas-covered cart. By five in the evening, he was clandestinely conveyed to the prison of the Stadthouse, thrust up several flights of stairs into a cramped cell illuminated only by a narrow window barred with vertical iron. The sole furniture was a massive oak chest. Imprisonment in that era was a swift highway to death—a combination of cold, solitude, torture, and starvation. Gerard, realizing he held the grasp of an implacable enemy, knelt to commend his soul to God, fearing he would never see daylight again.

Yet, hope sparked anew when he leaped to the iron bar, clutching it and pressing his knees against the wall to gaze outward. For a fleeting minute, he beheld Martin Wittenhaagen, quietly fishing in the brook beside the Stadthouse. Gerard whistled; Martin instantly abandoned his rod, turning with alacrity to acknowledge the prisoner before vanishing quickly. Gerard, comforted by the sight of his friend, waited, knowing they were not idle. Soon, the burgomaster, Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, appeared stealthily, bringing bread and water. Gerard's initial impulse was to strike the intruder with the iron bar, but Swieten's cough summoned three armed guards. "My orders," Swieten intoned, "are to detain you until you swear an oath to abandon Margaret Brandt and return to the Church." "Death sooner!" Gerard replied. Swieten bowed and withdrew.

Meanwhile, Martin hastened to Sevenbergen, finding Margaret agitated yet resolute, penning a letter to Countess Charolois accusing Swieten of treachery. Upon Martin's entrance, he revealed that he had located Swichten in the haunted tower atop the Stadthouse—a grim place where many had ascended only to descend feet foremost. He described seeing Gerard's face at a slit-like window, noting a pallor that suggested doom. Margaret, questioning whether Gerard looked anxious, was answered by Martin's observation that he appeared as bright as a pewter pot. "He counts on us," Margaret realized. She entrusted Martin with her letter to Rotterdam, but Peter, the elderly scholar, intervened. Having sat silently, observing the turmoil, he proposed a solution born of knowledge rather than wealth. "A Florentine knight," Peter recounted, "was imprisoned in a tower higher than Gerard's, yet his squire freed him using nothing but a bow and arrows."

Dismissing the notion of bribery, Peter outlined a plan relying on wit and dexterity. That same night, under a clear moon, a mysterious figure—Giles, the dwarf—emerged from the nursery, followed by his sister Kate. They had overheard Cornelis and Sybrandt discussing Gerard's incarceration and deduced that their father, currently in Rotterdam, was not responsible. Convinced of treachery, Kate urged Giles to accompany her to the foot of the haunted tower to comfort Gerard and assure him of his father's absence. Carrying a lantern, they ventured forth, the girl seeking courage against the rumored spirits.

Inside the cell, Gerard's hope waned as twilight faded, leaving him famished and despondent. Suddenly, a sharp strike echoed against the wall, followed by the clatter of an object at his feet. An arrow lay there, devoid of a head. Seizing it, Gerard lit a lamp with his tinder-box and discovered a skein of silk tied to the shaft, bearing a message: *"Well-beloved, make fast the silk to thy knife and lower to us: but hold thine end fast: then count an hundred and draw up."*

Seizing the oak chest, Gerard dragged it to the window, standing upon it to survey the scene below. Indistinct figures clustered at the base of the tower.
