He secured the silk to his knife, lowered it, and counted a hundred beats of his heart before pulling it up. It emerged heavy, revealing a stout whip-cord, which yielded further to a thicker rope as he hauled. The truth dawned on him: a great mass was descending, a "snake" emerging from the shadows. With a shout of joy, he hauled the rope until it reached his hand, threading it through the handles of the chest and securing it. Realizing the chest concealed a secret mechanism, he tested it by leaping upon it. The side burst open, spilling a cascade of parchments, but he quickly reinforced the ropes, bracing the iron bar against the window frame.

Mounting the chest, Gerard positioned himself astride, one leg inside, one outside, and began the perilous descent. The air was thin, the drop immense. He clung to the rope, his hands burning, sliding down stone after stone. Below, thirty feet beneath him, Margaret and Martin stretched their arms, their faces illuminated by the moon, mouths open in a silent plea. "Take care, Gerard!" they cried. "Fear me not!" he rejoined, accelerating his fall. They caught him moments before he touched earth, and the three embraced in

A look of venomous hatred from the burgomaster, coupled with his brutal incarceration, had convinced Gerard that Ghysbrecht sought nothing less than his death. He slipped around the corner of the tower, bracing himself not for any divine intervention, but for some cruel, treacherous trap devised by a wicked man to destroy him within those walls, an escape from which would remain a mystery.

Yet, as he moved forward, a soft yet courageous hand slipped into his own. Margaret stood beside him, ready to share this newfound peril.

Before they could reach the base of the haunted tower, a vision froze them in their tracks. Halfway up the stone spiral, a creature resembling a giant glow-worm climbed steadily. Its head blazed with a fiery light, illuminating its dark form, which stretched nearly four feet in length. At the foot of the tower stood a figure dressed in white, indistinguishable from a living woman. Gerard and Margaret quaked with awe.

"The rope!" Gerard gasped, his breath catching in his throat. "The rope! It ascends the ladder."

As they stared, the luminous creature vanished into Gerard's former prison, its light flooding the cell and turning the windowpane a deep, bloody red. The white-clad figure remained motionless below.

Those who survive the initial shock of the supernatural often succumb to a terrifying, primal urge to rush toward the horror, just as a bird might fly blindly at a serpent. This fascination had gripped Margaret. She gently released Gerard's hand, standing bewildered for a heartbeat, before suddenly letting out a wild cry and darting toward the specter. Gerard, unaware of this natural instinct, instantly believed the devil was leading her to ruin. He fell to his knees.

"Exorcizo vos! In the name of Blessed Mary, I exorcise you!"

Though the exorcist screamed his invocations in sheer terror, he was relieved to hear the specter emit a weak, frightened cry. Discovering that hell, too, possessed vulnerabilities offered a glimmer of hope. Redoubling his prayers, Gerard soon saw the ghastly shape kneeling at Margaret's feet, pleading pitifully for mercy.

Soon after, Kate and Giles arrived at the scene. Their surprise was immense when they discovered a new rope dangling from the prisoner's window down to the earth.

"I understand now," declared the younger brother, accepting reality as it presented itself. "Our Gerard descended this rope. He is free. I shall ascend and verify."

"No, Giles, no!" cried the older brother, his judgment clouded by superstition. "Do you not see this is sorcery? This rope is a line cast by the Evil One to lure you to destruction. He knows every weakness of our hearts; he sees how fond you are of climbing. How could Gerard procure such a rope? How could it be fastened in the sky like this? It defies nature. Holy saints guard us tonight, for hell is loose upon the world."

"Nonsense!" retorted the dwarf. "Hell is downward, and this rope goes upward. I have never had the fortune to climb a rope of such length. It may be years before I encounter one prepared for me. Better to be knocked senseless at once than to wait forever for happiness."

With a cry of pure delight, akin to a cat leaping onto a table laden with fish, Giles sprang onto the rope. His athletic vigor burned brightly, and the only concession Kate could extract from him was permission to attach a lantern around his neck.

"A light frightens evil spirits," she explained.

Thus, with massive arms and legs moving with feather-like agility, Giles ascended the rope faster than Gerard had descended. The lantern strapped to the back of his neck transformed him into a glowing beacon, a living glow-worm. His sister watched his progress with trembling anxiety. Suddenly, a female figure burst from the solid masonry, charging at her with superhuman speed.

Kate emitted a faint, strangled cry—the limit of her vocal cords under the weight of terror. Her crutches clattered to the ground, and she sank to her knees, burying her face and moaning, "Take my body, but spare my soul!"

"By heavens, it is a woman!" Margaret exclaimed, panting.

"Why, it is a woman!" Kate stammered, quivering.

"How terrified you make me!" Margaret cried.

"And I am terrified enough!" Kate sobbed. "Oh! Oh! Oh!"

"This is bizarre!" Margaret continued, regaining her composure slightly. "But the fiery-headed thing? It was with you, yet you are unharmed! Why are you here at this hour?"

"Nay, tell me why *you* are here?"

"Perhaps we share the same mission? Ah! You are his dear sister, Kate!"

"And you are Margaret Brandt."

"Yes."

"All the better. You love him; you are here. Then Giles was correct. He has gained his freedom."

Gerard stepped forward to confirm the truth, but their conversation was abruptly interrupted by a horrific, unearthly sound, echoing as if from a tomb: "PARCHMENT!—PARCHMENT!—PARCHMENT!"

With each repetition, the volume swelled. They looked up to see the dwarf, clutching handfuls of parchments, his face alight with fiendish joy and burning with a diabolical fire. The lantern around his neck rendered him a translucent nightmare, startingling the mortals below. As he spoke the word, he hurled the ancient documents at their stunned heads. The papers rained down like wounded ducks; some collapsed immediately, others fluttered helplessly, while still others spiraled slowly in wide, airy circles until they settled on the grass. Hardly had these fallen when the sepulchral roar returned—"Parchment! Parchment!"—and another flock of documents descended, followed by another, blanketing the ground in white.

Finally, the fire-headed imp, with his luminous body and horned hands, slid down the rope like a falling star. Prioritizing business over sentiment, he proposed an immediate settlement for the merchandise he had just delivered to his rescued brother.

"Hush!" Gerard warned sharply. "Speak too loudly. Collect them, and follow us to a safer place than this."

"Will you return home with me, Gerard?" asked little Kate.

"I have no home."

"You shall not say so. After such cruelty, who could be more welcome to your father's house?"

"Father!" Gerard replied sternly. "I have no father. He who was my father has become my jailer. I have escaped from his grasp; I shall never enter his reach again."

"An enemy did this, not your father," Kate insisted, recounting what she had overheard Cornelis and Sybrandt say. Yet, the injury was too fresh to be soothed. A bitterness of indignation, previously unseen in Gerard, surged to the surface.

"Cornelis and Sybrandt are two vile curs who have bared their teeth and hearts to me for a long time, yet they could achieve no more. My father is the culprit. He granted the burgomaster his authority, or the man would not dare touch me, a free burgher of this town. So be it. I was his son; I am now his prisoner. He has played his part; I shall play mine. Farewell to the borough where I was born, lived honestly, and was imprisoned. Until there remains another town in existence, I shall never trouble you again, Tergou."

"Oh! Gerard! Gerard!" Kate cried.

Margaret whispered urgently, "Do not oppose him now. Allow his anger to cool."

Kate turned quickly to her. "May I look upon your face?" The inspection seemed favorable, prompting her to whisper, "It is a comely countenance, and not that of a mischief-maker."

"Fear me not," Margaret said in the same gentle tone. "I could not find happiness without your love, as well as Gerard's."

"These are comforting words," Kate sobbed. Then, lifting her gaze, she added, "Little did I think I would like you so well. My heart is willing, but my infirmity forbids me to embrace you."

At this, Margaret gently wound her arm around Gerard's sister and kissed her tenderly.

"He has often spoken of you to me, Kate," Margaret murmured. "And often I longed for this moment."

"You, too, Gerard," Kate said, "Kiss me before you depart; for my heart grows heavy at the thought of leaving you this night."

Gerard kissed her, and she departed on her crutches toward home. The final sound they heard from her direction was a small, patient sigh. Then, tears welled in Margaret's eyes, though Gerard, a man, paid them no mind.

As they turned toward Sevenbergen, the dwarf nudged Gerard with his bundle of parchments and extended a curved claw.

Margaret dissuaded him. "Why accept what belongs to none of us?"

"Oh, spoil an enemy however you can."

"But might they not use this as a pretext for renewed violence?"

"How could they? Do you truly believe I will remain in Tergou after this? The burgomaster has stolen my liberty; I suspect I would not hesitate to take his life for it, if I could."

"Oh, fie, Gerard!"

"What! Is life worth more than liberty? Well, I cannot take his life, so I shall take the first opportunity that presents itself."

He handed Giles a few small coins, bringing the boy great joy, who then shuffled after

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten clutched the key to Gerard's prison within the folds of his tunic, waiting until the clock tolled ten before making his visit. "A touch of hunger serves to humble them," he muttered to himself, steeling his resolve. At the appointed hour, he ascended the creaking stairs, carrying a loaf and a pitcher, trailed closely by his loyal servant, who bore a weapon at his hip. Ghysbrecht pressed his ear to the heavy wooden door. Silence answered him—a profound, unnerving stillness. A grim satisfaction curled his lips. "By this time, he will be as despondent as Albert Koestein," he mused. With a slow turn of the lock, he pushed the door open.

But the cell was empty.

Ghysbrecht stood frozen, his breath hitching in his throat. Though his face remained obscured, his entire form seemed to drain of vitality, sinking into a stupor. Moments later, however, he collapsed to his knees, his legs giving way under him. His servant, seeing the tremors shake his master's frame, stepped forward and peered past the staggering old man. Upon beholding the vacant cot, the severed rope, and the missing iron bar, the servant gasped aloud, a cry of sheer disbelief. Yet, his astonishment deepened when he watched his master, ignoring everything save the hollow space, throw himself down before the empty chest. Ghysbrecht's hands, trembling violently, raked frantically over the wood, as though he dared not trust his own eyes to confirm the truth.

The servant stared, bewildered. "Mistress... Master, pray, what troubles you?"

Ghysbrecht's pale lips moved, forming words that escaped no sound. His hands dropped to his sides, and he fixed his gaze upon the void where the contents once lay. "Why stare into that emptiness?" the servant urged gently. "The boy is not there. Observe the cunning of the rogue; he removed the bar—"

"GONE!" Ghysbrecht screamed, the word tearing from his throat like a beast's roar. "GONE! GONE!"

"What is gone, holy saints? Is he stricken mad?"

"STOP THEEF!" shrieked Ghysbrecht, spinning around to seize his servant by the collar, shaking him with a fury born of sudden madness. "Do you stand idly by and watch your master robbed? Fly! Run! Offer a hundred crowns to anyone who brings back the box. Nay, it is futile! Oh, fool! Foolish me, to leave it unguarded in that same room. Yet, none before discovered the hidden spring. None could have, save him. It was meant to be. It *must* be. They are lost! Lost!" The weight of his age and failing health soon overpowered his brief frenzy, and he sank back against the chest, muttering brokenly, "Lost! Lost!"

"What precisely is lost, sir?" the servant asked, his voice kind.

"My house! My lands! My good name!" Ghysbrecht groaned, wringing his hands with feeble, frantic motions.

"WHA-AT?" the servant cried, his tone shifting instantly to eager curiosity.

That sharp emphasis, coupled with the master's look of desperate hope, reignited Ghysbrecht's natural cunning. "I have lost the town records," he stammered, glancing sideways at the man like a fox cornered near a henhouse.

"Oh, is that all?"

"Not enough! What shall the burghers say? What will the magistrates do?" Suddenly, Ghysbrecht erupted again. "A hundred crowns to whoever recovers them! Every single item that sat in this box. If even one piece is missing, I pay naught."

"'Tis a bargain, master," the servant replied quickly, patting his pouch. "See, those sheets of sheepskin you prize so highly are indeed there, right beside Gerard Eliassoen."

"That is true," Ghysbrecht whispered, relief washing over his features. "Good, faithful Dierich. All of it, mind you, every scrap."

"Sir," the servant said, straightening his stance, "I shall take the constables directly to Gerard's dwelling and seize him for theft."

"Theft? Ay, most certainly! Good! Very good. It is theft. I had forgotten that detail. Since he is now a thief, we shall cast him into the dungeons below, where the toads and rats dwell. Dierich, let this man never see the light of day again. It is his own doing; he was prying. Haste, haste! Before he finds time to speak, you understand how swiftly words can undo us."

Within the hour, Dierich Brower and four constables arrived at the hosier's home, demanding young Gerard from the terrified Catherine. "Alas, what mischief has he wrought now?" she cried, tears streaming down her cheeks. "That boy will break my heart."

"Nay, dame, 'twas merely youthful folly," Dierich smoothed, his voice dripping with false concern. "He has simply absconded with certain parchment skins. Doubtless a frolic, yet the Burgomaster is answerable to the citizens for their safekeeping, and thus he worries greatly. As for the youth, he shall likely receive a mere reprimand."

These smooth words lulled Catherine into a false sense of security, but her daughter, Kate, remained wary. Her suspicions grew sharper when Dierich learned that Gerard was nowhere to be found, nor had he returned that night. His disproportionate anger and disappointment masked his true intentions.

"Then depart," Dierich commanded roughly, turning on his heel. "Time is wasted. I shall locate him if he walks the earth."

"And what need did he have to steal?" Kate asked quietly as he prepared to leave.

Dierich paused, his brow furrowing. "What need?" he retorted bitterly.

"Gerard did not steal the trash," Kate insisted, her voice steady despite her fear. "He took it solely to spite the Burgomaster, who stole his freedom. Yet he shall answer to the Duke for his actions. And regarding these parchment skins you cherish so—look in the nearest stream or pigsty, and chances are you shall find them there."

"Think you so, mistress? Think you so?" Dierich's eyes flashed with suspicion. "Perhaps you know the truth?"

"I know this," Kate declared firmly, "that Gerard is too virtuous to steal, and too wise to burden himself with rubbish while traveling. Give you good day, then."

"And the sheepskin you scorn?" Dierich snapped, his composure cracking. "I value it more than the hide of any creature in Tergou."

With that, he hurried away, chasing a phantom trail.

Kate retreated to the inner chamber and called Giles aside. "Giles, my heart forebodes ill. Speak not a word of what I tell you to any soul. I told Dirk Brower that Gerard has fled Holland, but I strongly suspect he is not a league from Tergou."

"Why, where is he, then?" Giles asked, leaning closer.

"With whom he loves," Kate whispered urgently. "But if so, he must not linger. These men are deep, dark, and wicked, seeking him. Look into Dirk Brower's eye, Giles, and you see why I tremble. Oh, why cannot I flee to Sevenbergen myself and command him away? Why am I not strong and agile like other girls? God forgive me for fretting at His will, but I have never felt so helpless, so lame and weak and useless. But you are strong, dear Giles," she added, clutching his arm. "You are very strong."

"Yes, I am strong," Giles boomed, though his expression softened as he grasped her meaning. "But I detest walking on foot."

"But if you refuse to help..." Kate pleaded. "Dear Giles, do you not love Gerard?"

"Yes, I hold him in higher esteem than any other," Giles assured her. "I shall ride to Sevenbergen on Peter Buyskens' mule. Ask him, for he will surely not lend it to me otherwise."

Kate protested, warning that the whole town would follow, revealing Gerard's whereabouts and placing him in greater peril. Giles deflected her fears by vowing to ride out of town in the opposite direction, ensuring the mule did not turn toward Sevenbergen until they had successfully evaded the curious eyes of the crowd.

Kate finally relented, borrowing the mule. She entrusted Giles with a short, weighty message, drilling it into his memory until he could recite it word for word. As Giles mounted the animal, Kate withdrew, performing the final act within her power for her beloved brother: kneeling in prayer, pleading earnestly for his safety.

Gerard and Margaret hastened to Sevenbergen with a buoyant spirit, buoyed by the fresh air of regained freedom and the thrill of their recent escapades. Yet, this exhilaration quickly curdled into somber reflections. Gerard, though free, remained a fugitive, perpetually under the shadow of recapture and punishment. Consequently, separation became inevitable. Furthermore, Gerard had cultivated a profound loathing for his homeland. Margaret desired his departure, yet the mere mention of Italy caused her heart to falter. Conversely, Gerard's willingness to abandon her stemmed solely from his fervent wish to journey southward; he was convinced that Italy offered the wealth, renown, and legal sanctuary necessary to overcome every barrier standing between them and marriage. Having confided in Margaret the warnings of the demoiselle Van Eyck, he reiterated that the gold pieces bestowed upon him were specifically intended for this Italian venture. As a craftsman and artist stifled in this uncouth town, he believed his talents would finally be recognized across the sea. It was on this selfless, pragmatic ground that the gentle girl granted her consent, though her acquiescence was accompanied by a torrent of tears. Young and loving as she was, Gerard wept bitterly alongside her, frequently questioning what sin had forged such a united front of enemies against them, seemingly conspiring to tear them apart.

Hand in hand, they sat until the clock struck midnight, oscillating between lamentations of their cruel destiny and painting luminous, hopeful visions of a future reunited in foreign lands. Amidst these reveries, Margaret's sorrow would well up anew, causing poor Gerard's eloquent comfort to dissolve into a weary sigh. By dawn, they had reached a state of resignation regarding their imminent parting, though neither possessed the courage to specify the hour; indeed, it is doubtful whether the moment of separation would ever have arrived without external intervention.

However, around three o'clock in the afternoon, Giles returned, having traversed miles on circuitous paths to evade detection. Both lovers rushed to the threshold, driven by intense curiosity. "Brother Gerard!" Giles cried, his voice booming with its customary intensity. "Lady Kate commands you to flee for your life! They accuse you of theft; you have handed them evidence. Do not attempt explanation; seek no justice in Tergou. The parchments you seized were merely a ruse. She has seen the verdict in the eyes of the men—a price hangs over your head. Fly! For Margaret's sake, and for every soul who loves you, do not waste a single moment of life, but run!"

It was a thunderclap that left two pale, terrified faces staring at one another, fixed upon the ominous messenger. Then, Giles, breaking from his script of Catherine's orders, spoke a word of his own accord. "All the constables descended upon our dwelling in search of you, and so did Dirk Brower himself. Kate is astute, Gerard. Listen to her counsel and flee!"

"Oh, yes, Gerard!" cried Margaret in a frenzy. "Escape immediately! Ah, those parchments! My conscience warned me; why did I permit you to take them?"

"Margaret, they are but a deception," Giles insisted. "Let that pass. That wretch shall never behold them again. I will not depart until I have concealed his treasure in a place where he can never discover it." After offering Giles a warm thanksgiving, Gerard bade him farewell, instructing him to return and tell Kate that he was already gone—"For I shall be gone before you reach home," he declared. He then shouted for Martin, relaying the dire news and begging the young archer to travel a short distance toward Tergou and keep vigil along the road.

"Aye!" replied Martin. "Should I sight Dirk Brower or any of his men, I shall shoot an arrow into the oak tree within our garden. Upon seeing it, you must dash into the nearby forest and meet me at the Hunter's Spring. There, I shall guide you safely through the woods."

With this precaution in place, Gerard drew a breath he had not known he needed. Accompanied by Margaret, while she stood guard near the oak tree, trembling lest an arrow strike the branches, Gerard began digging a deep pit to inter the stolen documents. One by one, he cast them into the earth. Most were charters and records of the burgh, but one document appeared to be a private indenture between Floris Brandt, Peter's father, and Ghysbrecht.

"Why, this belongs just as much to you as to him," Gerard observed, reaching to read it. "Let me examine this."

"Oh, not now, Gerard, not now!" Margaret implored, panic rising in her voice. "Every second you delay fills me with dread; look! Large drops of rain begin to fall, and the clouds darken ominously."

Yielding to her urgent plea, Gerard withdrew the deed, tucking it securely into his bosom. He then covered the remaining parchments with soil, trampling the earth down firmly. As he worked, a jagged bolt of lightning pierced the sky, followed instantly by a roll of distant thunder, and soon the heavens opened in a deluge. Margaret and Gerard fled indoors, swiftly joined by Martin.

"The road ahead is clear," Martin reported, "and a violent storm is approaching." His prophecy proved accurate. The thunder grew louder, crashing overhead like a giant's drum, while flashes of lightning strobed continuously, resembling the strokes of a whip, as rain poured down in torrents. Margaret buried her face, unable to bear the spectacle of the lightning. Inside, Gerard raised the rough shutters and lit a candle. The lovers conferred quietly, and Gerard offered thanks to the tempest, blessing the storm for granting them a few fleeting, precious hours together. Unnoticed by the world outside, the sun dipped below the horizon, yet the thunder continued to rumble and the lightning to flash, drenching the land. Supper was laid out, yet Gerard and Margaret could not eat; the knowledge that this might be their final supper together choked them with grief.

As the storm gradually subsided, Peter retired to his chamber for rest. However, Gerard was to depart at first light, and neither lover could afford even an hour of slumber. Martin lingered for a while, carefully fitting a new string to his bow, a task requiring his utmost precision. Beside him, the lovers whispered their sorrows and their enduring love.

Suddenly, the old man raised a hand, signaling silence. The room fell quiet, and they strained their ears, hearing nothing at first. But moments later, a soft, dry *crack* resonated—the sound of footsteps crunching faintly upon the autumn leaves scattered across the garden behind the house. To those living without enemies, such a step might have been ignored; but to those hunted, it was a terrifying signal. It was a foot attempting to move silently.

Martin swiftly nocked an arrow to his string and extinguished the candle with a sharp blow of his hand. Immediately, to their mounting horror, they heard additional footsteps approaching the side door of the cottage. These steps were less silent than the first, yet executed with equal stealth—and then, a dead, agonizing pause.

Their blood seemed to freeze within their veins.

"Oh, Kate, oh, Kate!" Margaret sobbed, wringing her hands in anguish, terror, and wild remorse for having delayed Gerard's escape. "You said to fly at once!"

"Hush, girl!" Martin whispered, his tone stern and commanding.

A heavy, authoritative knock shook the doorframe, sending a tremor through the hearts of those within.

The back door gave way with a violent crash, the sound echoing through the quiet house like a gunshot. Immediately, heavy blows battered against the front entry, sealing them inside. Yet, amidst the chaos, Margaret found a fragment of composure. With a whispered command—"Tell them he was here, but he is gone"—she seized Gerard and nearly dragged him up the rough stone steps leading to her father's chamber. Her own room lay just beyond.

"Who disturbs this hour?" her father's voice called out from within, thick with sleep.

"Open, or you shall answer for it!" the intruders shouted.

"I open not to thieves! Honest folk are all asleep!"

"Open to the law, Martin Wittenhaagen, or you will regret it!"

"That is Dirk Brower's voice," Martin muttered, recognizing the tone. "What brings you so far from Tergou?"

"Open, and you will know!"

Slowly, deliberately, Martin drew the bolt. In rushed Dierich and four burly companions, letting in their leader who had slipped through the rear entrance.

"Now, Martin, where is Gerard Eliassoen?" Dierich demanded, his face grim.

"Gerrard Eliassoen?" Martin feigned surprise. "Why, he was here but moments ago!"

"Here?" Dierich's expression darkened. "And where is he now?"

"The rumor is he fled to Italy," Martin lied smoothly. "But why the alarm?"

"It matters not. When did he leave? Do not tell me he departed in such a storm!"

A sneer curled Martin's lips. "There is quite a fuss over Gerard Eliassoen." He lit a candle and sat calmly by the fire, taking a piece of fine silk and whittling it around his bowstring where the arrow notch fretted it. "Let me inform you," he said casually. "Do you know his brother, Giles? A little malformed imp, all head and limbs? Well, he tore over here on a mule, screaming something I could not hear—only the noise. Whatever he said, he frightened Gerard. Before Giles vanished, there was much weeping and kissing, and then Gerard left. They say he has gone to Italy—you may know where that is, though I do not."

Dierich's countenance sank deeper with every word. Martin's lie was flawless; a less cunning man might have spun too many threads, raising suspicion, but Martin adhered strictly to his orders, inventing nothing of his own.

"Folks," Dierich murmured, "I fear he tells the truth. I warned the burgomaster how this would play out. He spotted the dwarf galloping Peter Buyskens's mule from Sevenbergen. 'They sent that imp to Gerard,' he said, 'so Gerard must be at Sevenbergen.' 'Ah, master!' I replied, 'too late now. We wasted time hunting corners of Tergou for parchments we shall never find without the man who holds them. If he was at Sevenbergen and they sent the dwarf, it was to warn him. He is leagues away.'" He spat on the floor. "Confuse that chalk-faced girl! She has outwitted us, bearded men that we are. So I told the burgomaster, but he would not listen. A wet jerkin apiece, that is all we gain from this job."

Martin returned a cool grin to Dierich.

"However," Dierich added, "to satisfy the magistrate, we shall search the house."

Martin's demeanor shifted instantly to gravity. Dierich noticed the change immediately. After a brief reflection, he ordered, "Two of you watch outside, flanking the house, lest anyone jump from the upper windows. The rest follow me." Taking the candle, he ascended the stairs, followed by three comrades, leaving Martin alone.

The sturdy soldier bowed his head. It had begun so well, and now this fatal twist. Suddenly, hope sparked. Gerard must be in Peter's room or Margaret's; neither was high enough to preclude a leap. Gerard would jump. Dierich had left a guard below, but what then? For thirty seconds, Gerard and Martin would stand two to one, and in that fleeting span, anything might happen.

Martin propped the back door ajar and peered into Peter's room. Light spilled from within. "Curse the fool!" he cursed. "Will he let them capture him like a damsel?" As the light shifted to Margaret's chamber, it revealed no open window. If Gerard intended flight that way, he would not wait until men crowded the room. Seeing this, Martin retreated from the door, climbed down to the foot of the stairs, and listened.

Silence stretched, growing heavier with each passing second. Martin grew convinced Gerard had already slipped out the window while the men occupied the rooms. Then, the illusion shattered. Faint, desperate cries drifted from the inner bedroom—Margaret's.

"They have taken him," Martin groaned. "They've caught him."

Instantly, a terrible resolve crystallized in his mind. If they removed Gerard, his life would be forfeit, and Margaret's heart would shatter. His gaze darted wildly around the room, like a wild beast seeking an exit, before forming a plan characteristic of iron times and a soldier backed into a corner. He stepped to each door, mimicking Dierich's voice to shout sharply, "Watch the window!" Then, quietly, he locked and bolted both doors. He grasped his bow and six arrows, fitting one to the string and stowing the rest in his quiver. He laid his knife on a chair behind him, handle facing inward, and settled at the foot of the stairs. His eye burned with a cold, steady light, prepared to slaughter four men or fall by their hands. He calculated that two could be dispatched with arrows before they reached him; Gerard and he would face the remaining pair hand-to-hand. Moreover, he knew men panicked easily at a sudden ambush. If Brower hesitated even an instant, Martin would loose three shafts, tipping the odds.

He need not wait long. Heavy footsteps echoed in Margaret's room, approaching steadily. The light followed, accompanied by voices.

Martin's heart, though iron-forged, hammered violently. To hear men marching to their deaths—and perhaps to his own? More likely the latter, given four versus one. Gerard might be bound, powerless to aid. Yet, the man who had trembled at the mere mention of Giles-o'-lantern stood now, muscles coiled, breath held, equally ready to give and receive death. A desperate gamble! Victory meant instant exile for life; defeat meant immediate death upon the floor beneath his feet.

Dierich Brower and his men entered Peter's chamber, finding him in deep slumber. They ransacked his cupboards, slashed at an alligator skin he had nailed to the wall, and lifted the mattress from his bed. It was a spacious room, full of potential hiding spots, yet Gerard was nowhere to be found.

Next, they entered Margaret's room. The sight of it was disheartening—it was small, stark, devoid of furniture save for a large fireplace and chimney. Dierich's gaze fixed immediately on the chimney. There, lying upon an old chest barely a foot high, uncovered and unhidden, slept the beauty of Sevenbergen. Her sheets were snow-white, matching her own linens. She lay there, looking like a lily dropped into a muddy ditch.

Soon, she awoke, sitting up in bewildered amazement. Upon seeing the men, she began to scream faint prayers for mercy.

Her plight humiliated Dierich Brower. "Here is a mess," he stammered, slightly flustered. "We mean no harm, fair maiden. Lie still, close your eyes, and dream of your wedding night while I inspect the chimney for Master Gerard."

"In my room?" she gasped.

"Why not? Rumors say you and he..."

"Cruel! You know they have driven him away, exiled him from his native land! This is a trick. You are thieves, wicked men, not of Sevenbergen. If you truly knew Margaret Brandt, you would not seek her lover in this room above all others in the world. Oh, brave! Four great hulking men, armed to the teeth, to insult one poor, honest girl! The women in your own homes must be nothing, or you would hold them in too high regard to defile a maiden of good standing."

"There! Depart, before we hear further nonsense!" Dierich urged hurriedly. "He is not in the chimney. Plaster can repair what a cudgel breaks, but a woman's tongue is a double-edged dagger, and a girl remains a girl with her mother's milk still warm." Beating a hasty retreat, he muttered to his men, "I told the burgomaster precisely how this would unfold."

Where is the woman who cannot feign a role? Where is she who refuses to play the part, yet plays it so well that she saves the man she adores? In moments of supreme crisis, nature itself seems to intervene on behalf of the humblest souls, teaching them to cast dust into the eyes of Solomon. The moment the men had withdrawn, Margaret rose from her bed. Clad in her nightgown, skirt, petticoats, and stockings, she dragged the heavy chest—upon which she had been reclining—to the side of the room and propped its lid firmly against the wall. With silent, gliding steps, she moved to the threshold and pressed her ear to the wood. Through the silence of the house, the fading echoes of footsteps traveled past her father's chamber and descended the stairs until nothing remained but a hushed void.

Within that chest lay a secret, a peculiarity so subtle that even a stranger might easily miss it. Some years prior, when the floorboards of the room had been damaged, Gerard, lacking proper timber, had devised an ingenious solution. He had cut away a precise section of the flooring, just large enough to accommodate what appeared to be a small, shallow box, and used those planks to repair the surrounding area. To the untrained eye, the room looked sound; the "bed" seemed merely a low, flat container resting directly upon the floor. Yet, in truth, the chest sat suspended beneath the original joists, two feet deep, concealed by the illusion of a mere foot of depth.

Absolute silence reigned. Margaret knelt briefly, offering a prayer of gratitude to Heaven for the men's departure. Rising again, she approached the chest and leaned over the lid, whispering softly, "Gerard!"

There was no response.

Encouraged by the lack of movement, she increased her volume slightly. "Gerard, all is safe, thank God! You may rise now—but beware!"

Silence answered her.

Desperate, she placed her hand upon his shoulder. "Gerard!"

Still, no sound.

"Oh, what is wrong?" she cried, panic seizing her throat. Her hands flew wildly across his face and bosom, searching for any sign of life. She gripped him by the shoulders, shaking him violently, lifting him up. But he slipped from her trembling grasp, falling back not with the weight of a living man, but with the terrible, final heaviness of a corpse. A cold dread, sharper than ice, froze her blood. The lid had been lowered. She had lain upon him. The intruders must have been in the room for some time already. With the frenzied strength of madness, she tore him from the chest, bearing him in her arms to the window. She flung the casement wide, and the cool, sweet night air rushed in, mingling with the silver glow of the moon. She laid him upon the sill, bathed in pale light. His face was the color of ashes, his limbs utterly limp and motionless. Reaching for his heart, she found it still as stone. Horror! A horror beyond measure! She had suffocated him with her own body.

Human minds cannot instantly accept such a sudden, catastrophic, and absurd tragedy. Gerard, who had barely crawled into that chest five minutes prior, how could he be dead?

She called upon every endearing name her heart could conjure, every phrase her tongue could form. She kissed him, caressed him, coaxed him, imploring him to speak.

Yet, no answer returned to the words of love she had never dared utter before, nor believed herself capable of speaking. Trembling from head to toe, the poor girl began to mutter foolish, terrible, and pitiable things over his ashen countenance.

"Oh, Gerard! I am so sorry you are dead. I am so sorry I have killed you. Forgive me for not letting the men take you; it would have been kinder than this. Oh, Gerard! I am so, so sorry for what I have done."

Suddenly, her voice broke into a ravaging frenzy.

"No! No! This cannot be! If this is true, there is no God! It is monstrous! How can my Gerard be dead? How could I have killed my Gerard? I love him! Oh, God, you know how I love him! He does not know. I never told him. If he truly knew the depths of my heart, he would speak to me, he would not remain so deaf to his poor Margaret. Is it all a trick to make me scream and betray him? But no! I love him too deeply for that deception. I will strangle myself rather than yield." And she clutched her own throat, stifling the wild urge to shriek in her terror and agony.

"If he would but say one word... Oh, Gerard! Do not die without a single word. Have mercy on me and scold me, but speak to me! If you are angry, curse me! I deserve it! I am the fool who slew the man she loved more than her own life. Ah, I am a murderer. The worst criminal in all the world. Help! Help! I have murdered him. Ah! Ah! Ah! Ah! Ah!"

She tore at her hair, emitting shrieks so wild and piercing that they rang like a funeral knell through the house. They reached the ears of Dierich Brower and his men, who were lingering nearby. Startled from their vigil, they leaped to their feet, eyes wide with alarm, gazing at one another as the sounds of wailing and despair echoed into the night.

Martin Wittenhaagen stood rigid at the stairwell's base, his yew bow drawn taut until the string hummed near his ear, a steel knife gripped in his other hand. A jolt of pure astonishment froze him when he saw the men return empty-handed, Gerard nowhere in sight. His aim faltered, the weapon slipping down as his mouth gaped in disbelief. The intruders, however, stared back with equal bewilderment, their faces twisted by the shock of catching him poised for slaughter.

"Why, mates," Dierich barked, breaking the silence, "was the old fellow sizing us up for an arrow?"

"Rubbish!" Martin roared, forcing his stony demeanor back into place. "I was merely testing this new string. See? If you doubt me, I will unloose it right now."

"Hmph!" Dierich grunted, eyeing him with deep suspicion. "There is more in you than meets the eye, friend. Put a log on the fire, and let us warm our skins before we descend further."

A roaring hearth was kindled almost instantly, casting dancing shadows against the stone walls. The men clustered around the flames, stripping off their heavy cloaks and letting the heat singe the ends of their matted hair. It was then, amidst the crackling wood and rising smoke, that the shrill, piercing cries erupted from Margaret's chamber.

Every muscle coiled tight. One of the men snatched up a tallow candle and bolted up the steep steps toward the bedrooms.

Martin surged upward in haste, his heart hammering against his ribs. Confused by the sudden wails and terrified by the stranger's intent to pry, he lunged to block the path.

In a flash, Dierich shot out from behind, seizing Martin's arm with iron grip and shouting for the others to hold him fast. The candle-bearer slipped past the chaos, but the remaining men swarmed Martin like wolves. A brutal, tumbling fight ensued, sending the three men sprawling across the floorboards repeatedly, Martin pinned beneath their combined weight. Finally, they subdued the ancient giant, binding his hands and feet with the very rope they had intended for Gerard.

Martin let out a guttural groan. Through the haze of the struggle, he realized the intruder had already breached the sanctum of Margaret's room. Now, helpless and bound, he could only watch.

"Aye, gnash those teeth, you old rascal," Dierich panted, loosening his grip. "You shall not use them again."

"I am convinced," another man muttered, wiping blood from his brow, "that our lives hung by a thread while this brute remained loose."

"He makes me suspect Gerard is not far behind," someone else chimed in.

"No such fortune," Dierich retorted, shaking out his limbs. "Listen! Jorian Ketel has been loitering in that girl's chamber far too long. Some of you, best go check on him."

The crude laughter that followed the command died abruptly as the sound of frantic footsteps thundered overhead.

"Oh, look who returns at last!" Dierich shouted, his voice echoing through the stairwell. "Well, Jorian, pray tell us what mischief requires attention up above!"

Jorian Ketel pressed directly into Margaret's chamber, expecting silence, but instead encountered a scene of suspended animation. In the center of the room sat the man he sought, Gerard, pale and utterly still, his head cradled in Margaret's lap. She knelt over him, her countenance a mask of petrified grief; her eyes were wide yet glassy, fixed on the white visage above her, blind to the candlelight, deaf to Jorian's approach, and devoid of any care for the world save for this dying figure.

Jorian stood frozen, the flickering flame of his taper trembling in his grip, awestruck by the stillness around him. "Where was he, then, all this time?" he whispered, his voice breaking the heavy silence.

Margaret offered no answer, her spirit locked in a trance of despair. Jorian stepped forward to inspect the empty chest where the stolen goods had supposedly been hidden. As he looked, the girl's silent, paralyzing agony began to weigh heavily upon his heart. "A pitiful sight," he murmured, his tone shifting from curiosity to compassion. "A black night's work, all for a few skins! Had it been better to let us go with them." He paused, looking at the unresponsive girl, then back at Gerard. "She is past answering me, poor wretch. Stay!"

With sudden resolve, Jorian reached into his tunic and produced a small, circular mirror, no larger than his palm. He pressed it gently against Gerard's lips and nose, holding it steady. When he drew it away, the surface remained dull, reflecting no breath.

"There is life in him!" Jorian exclaimed to himself, a spark igniting within his chest.

The word struck Margaret like a thunderclap. Though spoken softly, it shattered her stupor. Like a statue brought suddenly to motion, she sprang up, throwing her arms around Jorian's neck with a fervor born of desperate relief. "Oh, bless the tongue that speaks this truth!" she cried, clutching the burly stranger tightly, her embrace fierce and trembling.

"Here, here! We must lay him warm," Jorian urged, gently disentangling her. With practiced ease, he lifted Gerard and laid him carefully upon the bedclothes. From a flask at his belt, he poured a generous measure of Schiedamse brandy into his open hand and dashed it sharply onto Gerard's face. The sharp, burning liquor seemed to wake the dormant spirit within; Gerard let out a faint, ragged sigh. To the ears of those listening, it was a sound of profound, miraculous joy. Margaret rushed toward him, but halted abruptly, her body quivering with a fresh wave of terror, afraid that even her touch might harm him. She had lost all faith in her own strength.

"That is right—leave him alone," Jorian advised kindly. "Do not cling to him as you clung to me, or you will drive the breath back into the earth. Let him rest; he will surely recover. He is no old man."

Deep within Gerard's chest, a sigh resonated, and a faint flush crept into his lips, returning color to his wan cheeks. Seizing the moment, Jorian turned toward the door. Hardly had he crossed the threshold when hands seized his legs from behind.

It was Margaret. She coiled around his ankles like a frightened serpent, kissing his hand and fawning upon him with tear-stained eyes. "You will not speak? You have saved his life! You lack the heart to cast him back into the grave, to undo your own noble deed?"

"No, no!" Jorian assured her, his voice firm. "It is not the first time I have served you both well; I told you in the church where we intended to take him. Besides, what concern does Dierich Brower hold for me? I would see him hang before I reveal his secret. Yet, I pray you tell me where the parchments lie! A hundred crowns are offered for them—a fortune for Joan and the children, you understand."

"They shall have those hundred crowns," Margaret promised, her voice thick with emotion. "Are the items in the house?" she asked eagerly.

"No," Jorian said, leaning closer. "But I know where they are. By God and Saint Bavon, I swear you shall possess them tomorrow. Come to me whenever you wish, but come alone."

"I am bound to do so," Jorian declared, his conscience clear. "What! Share the windfall with Dirk Brower? And may my bones rot in my skin if I allow a soul to learn the boy is here."

Before lingering further and risking suspicion, Jorian fled the room. Behind him, Margaret knelt, her frame shaking from head to toe, praying fervently beside Gerard and *for* Gerard.

Outside, Jorian met Dierich Brower, dismissing the urgency of the situation. "What is to be done?" Jorian asked casually. "We have simply scared the girl into a fit. She has been in a state."

"We ought to visit her and tend to her," Dierich suggested.

"Oh, yes!" Jorian laughed lightly, his offhand manner disarming all suspicion. "And frighten her further into the churchyard? Her father is a physician, and I have already roused him to bring her back to sense. Shall we view the fire?"

Soon the group agreed that the kitchen of the "Three Kings" offered far more warmth than Peter's dwelling, and they departed, first releasing Martin from his bonds. At the door, Dierich chuckled, nodding at Jorian. "Note well, friend, that I was correct and the Burgomaster mistaken. I said we were too late to catch him, and indeed, we were."

Thus, Gerard escaped the jaws of prison and the shadow of the grave, not through cunning concealment or the girl's quick wit, but through a sudden impulse of mercy in the heart of one of his captors, a reckless fellow whose greed for gold had been tempered by a sliver of humanity. How strangely intertwined are human motives, how short-sighted the sharpest strategies! Those whose gentle fates spare them the fierce extremes of joy and anguish may be the happiest, yet to them, I cannot fully convey the unspeakable bliss that descended upon these persecuted lovers.

To watch a beloved face return from the abyss to health and beauty, to see the roses bloom once more on a pale cheek, to hear the words of love return to the lips—this was Margaret's joy, balancing a lifetime of sorrow. For Gerard, it was awakening from a trance to find himself cradled in the arms of the woman he adored, hearing her murmur words of eloquent devotion, showering him with tears and tender kisses. Only in this sweet hour did he truly understand the depth of her love. He thanked his enemies, for they had led him here. Their arms wound around each other, and the terrors of danger and imprisonment seemed worlds away, an ancient dream. They called themselves husband and wife; were they not solemnly betrothed? Had they not stood before the altar, blessed by the Holy Church, cursed by it all who would divide them?

Yet, as no woman's nerves could withstand such terrible strain without cost, Margaret presently grew faint and sank onto Gerard's shoulder, her smile feeble, her spirit unstrung. Gerard felt immediate anxiety and sought to call for aid, but she held him fast with a gentle grasp, imploring him not to leave her side for even a moment. "As long as I may lay my hand upon you, I know you are safe," she whispered, her voice trembling. "Foolish Gerard! Nothing ails me. I am weak, dearest, but so happy!"

Now it was Gerard's turn to support that fragile head, with its waves of hair flowing loosely over his shoulder, to nurse and soothe her quivering form with soft, encouraging words and murmurs of affection. The sweetness of a woman's weakness to a manly heart knows no equal. Poor souls! They were happy. Tomorrow, however, they must part. Yet, compared to the glimpse of death they had shared, separation for a year or two was nothing. While there was life, there was hope; while there was hope, there was joy. The future shone bright, the present was heaven, and so passed these blissful hours.

Alas! Their innocence faced dangers beyond the prison walls or the grave. Now that visible peril had vanished, they were in utmost jeopardy from their own hearts and their youth, from the fires of passion and the naivety of love.

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten lay awake until the dawn, his mind a tempest of dread far worse than any storm. Though he claimed fear of thunder and lightning kept him from joining the night watch, the truth was a cold knot of suspicion twisted in his gut. The moment the skies cleared, he slipped down the back stairs, mounted his mule, and galloped to the "Three Kings" tavern in Sevenbergen. Inside, his men slumbered in a chaotic mix of postures—some sprawled over chairs, others collapsed upon the tables, a few curled up on the bare floorboards. Ghysbrecht roared like a beast, shaking them from their stupor, and demanded the tale of their failed vigilance, punctuated by their own boasts of unyielding zeal.

"Fool!" the burgomaster barked, his face flushed with indignation. "To send you away without me! My life depends on whether he is still there. Did you look beneath the girl's bed?"

"No," admitted Dierich Brower, rubbing his eyes. "There was simply no space for a man to hide there."

"How can you know that unless you looked?" Ghysbrecht snarled, his voice rising. "You must have peered under the mattress and probed every panel with your knives. Rise! Now I will teach you the art of searching."

Dierich Brower sat up, shaking dust from his tunic. "If you manage to find him, then I am a horse and not a man."

Within moments, Peter's house stood once more encircled by the burghers' party. Ghysbrecht tethered his mule to a post outside, entrusting it briefly to Jorian Ketel before leading the charge inside with Dierich and the rest. They moved through the silent rooms, but the house was utterly deserted. Not a soul remained within its walls—not even Peter. They ascended the stairs, expecting to find the fugitive hiding above, yet silence greeted them. Then, abruptly, one of the men threw up his hand, pointing a trembling finger toward Peter's window, which stood wide open to the morning air.

Beyond the glass, the others saw him. At a moderate distance, walking calmly across the open fields, were the three figures they had hunted: the man himself, accompanied by Margaret and Martin. A cry of triumph tore from Ghysbrecht's throat. With an exultant yell, he leaped from the stairs, scrambled onto his waiting mule, and spurred it forward. Behind him, his men shouted and followed, setting off in hot pursuit of the fleeing trio.

Before the first ray of dawn pierced the gloom, Gerard rose, his spirit hardened by the terror of the night. He roused Martin, the old soldier, who stared at him in bewilderment. "Did you not slip out by the window?" Martin asked, incredulous. "I thought you vanished into the dark."

Gerard, however, spoke of necessity. "We must flee," he urged, "but not blindly. There is but one safe path. I must guide you through the Great Forest to a bridle-road I know. From there, a hostelry awaits a swift horse, and within a day's gallop, we shall be free of Holland." Before the villagers stirred from their slumber, they had to move.

Their home lay a mere half-furlong from the timberline. Martin drew his bow, fletching three arrows, though it was already Thursday—a day marked for departure. For Gerard, Peter offered only a sturdy oak staff, a weapon of simple wood for a journey fraught with danger. Margaret, practical and quick, pinned up her kirtle and farthingale against the wet roads. Peter walked them to the edge of his garden, his face etched with a gravity rarely seen, and bestowed his blessing upon the young man.

As the sun crested the eastern horizon, bathing the stony fields in pale gold, they pushed toward the wood. Halfway across, Margaret froze. Her eyes darted backward, then widened in horror. With a strangled cry, she turned and sprinted toward the tree line, her screams cutting through the morning air. Behind them, the heavy tread of Ghysbrecht and his men pounded relentlessly.

To resist would have been suicide. Martin and Gerard followed Margaret's lead. Though the pursuers closed the gap, Martin's voice rang out, a beacon of desperate hope: "Only win the wood! Only win the wood!"

With their fresh start, the fugitives surged ahead, their hearts leaping. To them, the towering oaks stretched like benevolent guardians, their canopy weaving a screen against the world behind. Yet, a shadow emerged from the periphery. The fiery old burgomaster, Ghysbrecht, flung himself onto his mule, spurring it into a wild gallop. He did not merely chase his men; he aimed to cut off the fugitives entirely. Galloping in a wide semicircle, the burgher positioned himself squarely between Gerard and the open space, forcing the young man into a corner.

Margaret screamed, clutching at Gerard in a futile attempt to shield him, but he brushed her hands away with rough urgency. In his feverish state, Ghysbrecht forgot the primal truth of the hunt: that prey often turns upon the hunter, and hatred breeds a ferocity that transcends reason. Two men who hated one another could find a motive to kill.

Instead of dodging as Ghysbrecht anticipated, Gerard launched himself forward, a savage, exulting cry tearing from his throat. He swung the oak staff with every ounce of his soul, striking the burgomaster's face with a bone-shattering crash. Ghysbrecht tumbled beneath his mount, beaten by the beast's hooves, his face streaming with blood, his collar spattered red.

In an instant, the trio dove into the treeline. The yells of dismay and vengeful fury erupting from Ghysbrecht's men signaled that the game had shifted to a race for survival.

"Why run?" Gerard gasped, chest heaving, shaking the bloody staff. "You have your bow, and I have this!"

"Boy!" Martin roared, his voice raw. "The gallows! Follow me!" He plunged deeper into the woods. Soon, the baying of hounds echoed through the trees, a chorus of hunters closing in. Margaret moaned, her breath coming in ragged gasps, while Gerard gritted his teeth, gripping his staff until his knuckles turned white. They reached a dense thicket of hazel, where Martin shoved aside the young saplings as if they were dry stalks of grain.

Within fifty paces, four blind paths converged. Martin chose one, signaling them to bend low. They moved as ghosts, gliding along twisting trails that seemed to loop back on themselves, until finally, they entered a thick pine grove. Here, the ground was hard, the air stifling, and the young firs grew so close together that visibility dropped to mere yards.

After some distance, Martin halted. Having learned in war to separate the sensation of danger from the reality of it, he calmly unpacked a piece of bread and a slice of ham from his wallet, beginning to eat. The younger pair stared at him in stunned disbelief.

"All Sevenbergen could not find you now," Martin said, chewing slowly. "You will lose your purse, Gerard, long before you reach Italy. Is that the way to carry a purse?"

Gerard looked down. Attached to his wallet's buckle and strap was a large, triangular purse, entangled by chains. "This is not mine," he whispered, trying to free it. But the straps had tightened around it during the flight; it was impossible to remove without cutting. "It seems loath to leave me," Gerard murmured, drawing his knife to sever the bonds.

Upon examining the contents, Gerard found silver coins of various sizes, but the bag was swollen with crumpled brown paper. A sudden realization struck him. "It must belong to that old thief. See how it is stuffed with paper to fool the world!"

The mystery solved itself quickly. The purse had hung at Ghysbrecht's saddlebow. As Gerard charged his enemy, he had unconsciously torn it away, felling the burgomaster and stripping him in a single, fatal motion.

Gerard beamed with delight at his accidental triumph, but Margaret's face remained troubled. "Throw it away, Gerard, or let Martin take it back. They will call you a thief already. I cannot bear the stain."

"Throw it away? Give it back?" Gerard recoiled. "Not a stiver! This is spoil won lawfully in battle from an enemy. Is it not, Martin?"

"Why, of course," Martin agreed easily. "Send him back the brown paper, but keep the purse or the coin—it is a sin to return it."

"Oh, Gerard!" Margaret pleaded, tears welling. "You are going to a distant land. We need the goodwill of Heaven. How can we hope for grace if we take what is not ours?"

But Gerard saw the world through a lens of divine providence. "It is Heaven that gives it to me by a miracle, and I shall cherish it accordingly," he declared, echoing the scriptural tale of Israel spoiling Egypt. "Thus, the favored people were blessed."

"Take your own way," Margaret yielded humbly, lowering her gaze. "You are wiser than I. You are my husband..." Her voice trailed off, soft and submissive.

"These humble words," Martin noted internally, "from a woman who usually holds the whip-hand... they surprise me."

Gerard kissed her gently, grateful for her yielding nature, and they pressed onward, hand in hand, led by Martin into the vast, silent depths of the forest. As they ventured further, the sense of security grew absolute. Townsmen dared not venture so far into the trackless wilds.

Yet, impetuous spirits repent quickly. As soon as the immediate threat faded, Gerard's conscience pricked him. "Martin, would I had not struck quite so hard."

"Whom? Let that pass; he was cheap served."

"I saw his grey hairs as my stick fell," Gerard confessed, trembling. "I doubt I shall see them again."

Martin grunted with disdain. "Who spares a badger for his age? The greyer your enemy, the older he is; the older, the craftier. And the craftier, the better for a little killing."

"Killing? Killing, Martin? Speak not of killing!" Gerard shuddered, the word seeming to haunt him.

"I am much mistaken if you haven't killed," Martin said cheerfully.

"Now Heaven forbid!"

"The old vagabond's skull cracked like a walnut. Aha!" Martin grinned ruthlessly. "He rolled off his mule like a stone shot from a cart. I thought to myself, 'There is one wiped out.'"

Gerard fell to his knees, begging for mercy for his enemy's soul.

Martin's patience snapped. "Here's mummery! You, who set yourself up for learning, do you not know that a wise man never strikes unless to kill? What is this fuss over killing an old man? If it were a youth, full of wine, women, and life's pleasures, I might hesitate. But an old fellow at the edge of the grave? Why not shove him in? He goes today or tomorrow, and what better place for a greybeard? If I ever live to be so ancient as Ghysbrecht, walking on a mule's legs with a back like this—" he tapped his bow "—and a string like this, I'd thank and bless any young fellow who knocked me on the head, just as you did that shopkeeper. May his memory be cursed!"

"Oh, *culpa mea*! *Culpa mea*!" Gerard cried, smiting his breast in penitence.

"Look there!" Martin scoffed at Margaret. "He is a priest at heart even now—and when he isn't angry, St. Paul, what a milksop!"

"Tush, Martin!" Margaret chided reproachfully. Then, she

Common courage, much like common talent, is confined to a narrow channel. Step an ordinary man but an inch outside his accustomed bounds, and he falls. Martin's bravery was absolute within the limits of his experience; having weathered countless perils in his rugged life, he faced known threats with Spartan calm, bordering on indifference. Yet, he had never been hunted by a bloodhound. He had never witnessed that beast's unerring instinct thwarted by human guile. Now, a sense of the supernatural, compounded by sheer novelty, shattered his resolve. After mere steps, he leaned heavily upon his bow, his spirit draining away like water through cracked earth. Gerard, whose peril seemed diminished by distance, pressed him to flee.

"What profit is there?" Martin murmured, his voice heavy with sorrow. "If we escape the forest, we shall buy our lives dearly enough; but here, close at hand, I know a spot where we may sell them well."

"Alas, good Martin!" cried Gerard, "do not despair so swiftly! Surely some path remains for escape."

"Oh, Martin!" sobbed Margaret, "what if we separate? Gerard's life alone stands forfeit. Can we not lure the pack onto both of us, leaving him free?"

"Child, you know not the nature of the bloodhound," Martin replied grimly. "It does not track this man or that; it follows the trail of blood. They have taken him to where Ghysbrecht fell, and from the corpse's gore to the killer's hand, this cursed beast will lead them. Let Gerard run through an army or swim the Meuse itself, the hound finds him." Again he rested on his bow, his head sinking low.

Through the trees rang out the hound's mellow cry.

Strange indeed that beauty could be so terrible and deadly. The eye of the boa constrictor, while entrancing its prey, possesses a loveliness that mirrors the ruby crowned with emerald's green light. Yet the deer beholding it loses all motion, trembling in anticipation of death. So, too, did this sweet, mellifluous sound bewilder Martin Wittenhaagen. He stood frozen, confused, and utterly unnerved. Gerard, scarcely better, felt his spirits crushed. Martin's final words had daunted him. Having struck down an elder and spilled his blood, the four-legged avenger of kinship now tracked him. Was not the finger of God pointing this way?

While the men were numbed by dread, the woman's mind remained a whirlwind of action. Her lover was in mortal danger.

"Lend me your knife," she said to Martin. He handed it over.

"But 'tis little use in your hands," he warned.

Then Margaret performed a crafty deed. Stepping behind Gerard, she drew the blade across her forearm, causing it to bleed freely. Stooping, she smeared her hose and shoes with the dark fluid, doing so with such dexterity that neither Gerard nor Martin perceived it. As the blood trickled, she continued to coat her garments, creating a false trail. Then she seized the soldier's arm.

"Be a man!" she commanded, "and let this end. Lead us to some thick cover where numbers will not save our enemies."

"I am going," Martin grumbled sullenly. "Rushing helps naught; we cannot outrun the hound, and the cover lies nearby." Turning left, he led the way, as those do who march to execution.

Soon he brought them to a dense hazel coppice, reminiscent of the one that had aided their escape that morning.

"There," he declared, "though but a furlong wide, it will serve."

"What shall we do?"

"Pass through this and wait on the other side. As they emerge straggling, loose three arrows, smash two skulls, and the rest will fall upon us."

"That is all you have devised?" asked Gerard.

"That is all."

"Then, Martin Wittenhaagen, I take the lead, for you have lost your wits. Come, can you obey a youth like me?"

"Oh, yes, Martin," cried Margaret, "do not deny Gerard! He is wiser than his years."

Martin offered a reluctant assent.

"Do as I do," Gerard ordered. Drawing his massive knife, he hacked at hazel shoots close to the ground, twisting them upright behind him to form a wall among the standing stems. Martin mimicked the act, but with a dogged, hopeless demeanor. As they painfully traversed the majority of the copse, the bloodhound's deep bay grew louder, less melodic, and sterner.

Margaret trembled.

Martin dropped to his knees and listened.

"I hear horses' hooves," he whispered.

"No," corrected Gerard. "I suspect a mule. This cursed Ghysbrecht must still live; no other would pursue me so fiercely."

"Never strike your foe unless to slay him," Martin growled gloomily.

"I shall deliver a heavier blow this time, if Heaven grants me the chance," Gerard vowed.

Finally, they emerged into an open woodland. The trees were vast but widely spaced, offering no avenue for escape. Soon, mingled with the hound's bay, rose a chorus of twenty voices hooping and hallooing.

"The entire village hunts us," Martin observed.

"I care not," Gerard replied calmly. "Listen. I have smoothed the path for the dog, yet roughened it for the men, ensuring we fight them separately. Thus, the hound will gain on the footmen, and the moment he exits the copse, we kill him."

"The hound? There are many!"

"I hear but one."

"Aye! One speaks, the others run silent; but if the leader loses the scent, another takes up the cry. There will be two dogs, at least—devils in canine skin."

"Then we must slay two instead of one. Once they fall, back into the copse immediately and reverse course."

"A sound plan, Gerard," Martin admitted, regaining some heart.

"Hush! The men enter the wood."

Gerard whispered his orders. "Stand by the side of the copse, in the ditch. I shall move but a few yards to that oak and hide behind it. The dogs will follow me. Shoot as many as you can as they exit; I shall finish the rest as they circle the tree."

Martin's eyes flamed. They took their posts.

The hooping and hallooing drew nearer. Soon the rustling of the undergrowth became audible, punctuated periodically by the unerring bark of the bloodhound. It was a nightmare: the branches brushing closer, the inevitable struggle for life approaching minute by minute, the death knell ringing. A trembling hand touched Gerard's shoulder, startling him violently.

"Martin says if we must part, flee to that high ash-tree we entered by," the hand indicated.

"Yes! Yes! But return, for heaven's sake! Do not stay out in the open!"

She retreated toward Martin, but before reaching him, a huge dog burst from the copse, standing erect for a fleeting moment. Margaret cowered, but the beast ignored her; scent guided him, not sight. Lowering his nose briefly, he unleashed an awful yell, springing straight for Gerard's hiding tree. He rolled headlong over, dead as a stone, impaled by an arrow from Martin's bow, which twanged silently beside the copse. Simultaneously, a second hound emerged, sniffing at his fallen comrade. Gerard lunged at him, but before he could swing his cudgel, a flash of white lightning seemed to strike the beast. It collapsed into the dust, mortally wounded yet not dead, howling pitifully.

Gerard lacked the time to dispatch it. The copse rustled dangerously close, seeming to breathe. Pointing wildly at Martin to retreat, Gerard dashed a few yards to the right, crawling cautiously back into the thick brush just as three men burst forth. These had gained considerable ground on their fellows, while the rest trailed at varying intervals. Gerard scrambled backward almost on all fours. Instinct taught Martin and Margaret to mimic his movements. Thus, within mere yards, pursuers and pursued passed one another on diverging tracks.

A shrill cry announced the discovery of the dead and dying hounds. Followed by a babble of voices, swelling as fresh hunters arrived, the scene unfolded. The hunters, prone to squandering time on surprises, wasted precious moments, while the hunted maximized theirs.

"I hear no more hounds," Martin whispered to Margaret, feeling himself return to normalcy.

Yet it was Margaret's turn to tremble and despair.

"Oh, why did we leave Gerard? They will kill him, and I am not near to protect him."

"Nay, nay! Their heads are not on those shoulders. Did you not bid him meet us at the ash-tree?"

"And so I did. Praise God, Martin, for thinking of that. To the ash-tree!"

"Aye! But with silence."

As they neared the copse's edge, hooping and hallooing echoed behind them. The villagers, convinced the fugitives lay within the thicket, beat back into the woods.

"It matters not," Martin whispered to his shaking companion. "Time enough to break clear and slip back unseen by hard running. Ah!"

Suddenly, he stooped. Just as he prepared

Speech is often the vessel for thought, yet certain emotions are so primal, so overwhelming, that they escape the tongue and erupt instead as raw, eloquent cries. In those moments, man sheds his civilized veneer and becomes little more than a beast driven by instinct. There was a terrifying, feral quality to the scene unfolding in the woods: the guttural roar of triumph from the pursuers bursting forth from the thickets, clashing violently against the piercing shriek of terror as their prey scattered into the undergrowth. Hands clawed at empty air mere feet away, grasping for ghosts as Gerard and the mule surged forward, fleeing for their lives. For a heartbeat, the hunters stood stunned, confused like lions whose springs have gone wide, allowing the fugitives to gain a ten-yard advantage. But the confusion vanished instantly. With weapons raised high, Dierich and his men launched themselves anew. They were confident in victory; they knew these runners well. Earlier that morning, in the flat lands, the trio had been steadily gaining ground, but now, in the open, the contest would be decided solely by sheer velocity. A hundred yards vanished in the blink of an eye, yet that fatal gap of ten yards remained.

Dierich Brower frowned, troubled by a sudden, unexplained shift in the fugitives' stamina. It defied logic. Normally, when three flee together, they move at the pace of the slowest link. From Peter's house to the tree line, Gerard had dragged himself along at Margaret's weary rhythm. Now, however, he moved with a fierce, singular urgency. The mule beside him was a wind beneath their wings, capable of outrunning them all, and Gerard seemed to draw strength from it. Furthermore, the effects of youth and a life of chastity began to manifest. As daylight slowly crept back into the world ahead of them, leaving the hunters in the gloom, Dierich threw himself into a final, desperate sprint, closing the distance by two yards. But within seconds, Gerard stole those precious steps back, silent and sure. Frustration bubbled over the pursuers' lips.

Farther back, Martin heard the grumbling and a glimmer of hope lit his face. "Hold fast, Gerard! Courage, brave lad!" he shouted, his voice cutting through the din. "Look at them! They are breaking formation." And indeed, order was fracturing. Dierich was now flanked by only one man, while another lagged dangerously near the rear.

They ascended a long, gentle rise, and here the virtues of youth, grit, and sobriety proved superior to age and experience. Before Gerard could reach the summit, the weight of forty years crushed Dierich like forty heavy stones. "Our prize turns to dust," he gasped, breathless. "Seize him dead if you cannot catch him alive!" He abandoned the run, falling into a clumsy walk, trailing behind. Jorian Ketel soon followed suit, dropping further back, until finally, save for one, the entire party stood still. Only one man pressed on, relentless as a bloodhound, though he lost ground with every step. If memory serves me right, his name was Eric Wouverman. Guided by this lone survivor, they approached a second rise in the wood—shorter, but far steeper than the first.

"Grab the reins!" Martin cried urgently.

Gerard obeyed instantly, and the mule's powerful legs carried them up the slope with a speed that outstripped Gerard's previous running pace. Witnessing this maneuver, Eric Wouverman's spirit crumbled. Realizing he was now nearly eighty yards behind the fugitives and significantly distant from his nearest comrade, he halted abruptly. Obedient to Dierich's prior command, he lowered his crossbow, aimed it with deliberate care, and, just as the trio dipped below the crest of the hill and vanished from sight, loosed a whistling bolt into the air.

A scream of horror tore through the silence. The next instant, as if struck by a lightning rod, the entire group collapsed onto the earth, mule and all, brought low by the unseen arrow.

The effect of the shot was instantaneous and bewildering, leaving the marksman himself momentarily stupefied. Before he could fully recover, he called upon his comrades to press the attack, sprinting upward along the slope. Yet, scarcely halfway up, the silhouette of Martin Wittenhaagen emerged against the sky, drawing a heavy, bent bow. Seeing this, Eric Wouverman, whose prowess with the crossbow was legendary and who still bore the memory of the fallen hound, instantly dove behind a thick trunk, shrinking into the shadows as best he could.

Peering anxiously around the rough bark, Eric saw the tip of an arrow still leveled directly at his hiding place, gleaming in the sun. Paralyzed by fear, he dared not shift even an inch. Minutes passed in tense silence before his allies arrived in full force. With a derisive chuckle, Martin vanished, soon heard riding away on his mule, leaving the hunters baffled.

A surge of men rushed forward to the high ground, which overlooked a narrow, seemingly endless glade. From afar, they spotted Gerard and Margaret fleeing at a frantic pace, appearing no larger than gnats. Hot on their heels, Martin spurred his mount, racing *ventre à terre*. The pursuers found themselves both outsmarted and outpaced. To understand Martin's strategy, one must look closer at the circumstances. Though coincidences often reveal cause, they can sometimes be deceptive illusions. Much like how a hare might tumble over a thicket precisely when a gunshot rings out, raising hopes only to dash them by escaping unharmed, so too did fate intervene here. At the very moment the harmless bolt whistled overhead, the mule stepped into a rabbit hole, stumbled, and threw both rider and passenger. Gerard managed to catch Margaret, but her weight and momentum dragged him down with her. In an instant, the stage was littered with dramatic figures.

The docile beast rose immediately, standing trembling amidst the dust. Martin was next to stir, scanning the scene until he realized only a single pursuer remained. Seizing the opportunity, he urged the young lovers to flee on foot, checking his own enemy as previously recorded. He then galloped after them once more. After a grueling chase, when he finally caught up, he swiftly hoisted Gerard and Margaret onto the mule. Running alongside them until his lungs burned, he then swapped places, taking the reins while the others rested. By rotating duties, he ensured a runner was always fresh. Long before their exhausted footsteps slowed, every trace of them was hopelessly lost to Dierich and his men, who returned crestfallen to attend to their leader and their winged bloodhound.

Life and freedom, when untroubled, pass unnoticed like the steady hum of a loom until the thread snaps; then, their worth becomes stark and absolute. It is akin to sunlight, unseen at high noon when it burns brightest, but cherished in the fading hour when it fractures into shards of topaz beneath the boughs of elm. Yet, in that twilight, the light grows dimmer, shadowed by the encroaching gloom, making the warmth all the more precious by contrast. Thus, Gerard and Margaret, trembling at every rustling leaf that sounded too loudly, burned with a radiant, feverish gratitude as they slipped through the gentle woods, the echoes of baying hounds and savage shouts still ringing in their ears.

Soon, however, Gerard's gaze fell upon dark smears on Margaret's ankles.

"Martin! Martin, help! They have hurt her!" he cried, his voice cracking with panic. "A crossbow bolt!"

"No, no!" Margaret laughed softly, a sound meant to soothe his terror, though her eyes betrayed her fear. "I am unhurt, truly."

"But what is this?" Gerard demanded, agitated, pointing to the red stains. "In Heaven's name, what is it?"

"Do not scold me," she murmured, flushing deeply.

"I have never scolded you?"

"Not a word, dear Gerard." Her tone shifted, becoming defensive yet tender. "Well, Martin told me those cruel dogs followed the scent of blood. So, thinking to save you, I wished to draw their attention away. If I could but make them think I was bleeding, perhaps they would chase me, leaving you free. So, I used Martin's knife to scratch my arm—forgive me! Whose blade should I have taken? Yours, Gerard? Ah, no. You forgive me?" she pleaded, her expression a mixture of guilt, love, and devotion.

"Let me see the wound first," Gerard gasped, his throat tight with emotion. "There... I thought so. A mere scratch? To me, it looks like a deep, terrible, cruel gash."

Gerard shuddered as he stared at the blood.

"She could have pierced herself with her bodkin," the soldier grumbled, stepping forward. "What a milksop! Sickened at the sight of a scratch and a drop of blood."

"No, no!" Gerard protested fiercely. "I could face a sea of crimson, but not hers. Oh, Margaret, how could you be so cruel?"

Margaret smiled, a smile of ineffable, quiet love. "Foolish Gerard," she whispered, "to make such a fuss of nothing." She threw her guilty arm around his neck. "As if I would not give every drop of blood in my veins for you, let alone a few from my arm." And under the weight of their shared danger, she buried her face against his shoulder, weeping for pity and love; he wept with her.

"And I must leave her," he sobbed, the reality crashing down. "We two who love so dearly—one bound for Holland, the other for Italy. Ah me! Ah me!"

Margaret wept anew, but her tears were silent and patient. Nature never sleeps, and within her selflessness lay an instinct for survival. To speak her true thoughts now would only deepen Gerard's agony at their separation, so she kept her grief locked inside.

Suddenly, they burst onto a worn, beaten track, and Martin halted.

"This is the bridle-path I mentioned," he said, bowing his head. "And there, lying ahead, is the inn."

Gerard and Margaret exchanged a terrified glance.

"Follow me a step, Martin," Gerard whispered urgently. Drawing the soldier aside, he spoke in a broken voice, "Good Martin, keep watch over her for me! She is my wife, yet I must leave her. Look, Martin! Here is gold—it was saved for my journey. It would shame me to ask her to take it, for she would refuse; but you will take it for her, will you not? Oh, Heaven! Is this all I can offer? Money? Poverty is a curse. You will ensure she wants for nothing, dear Martin? The burgomaster's silver is sufficient for me."

"You are a good lad, Gerard," the soldier replied warmly. "Neither lack nor harm shall touch her.
