I care for the tip of her little finger more than for all the world; and were she nothing to me, I would still be a father to her for your sake. Go with a stout heart, and may God walk with you." With unusual tenderness, the rough warrior squeezed Gerard's hand and turned his gaze away.

For a moment, silence hung between them. Martin prepared to return, but Gerard stopped him. "No, good Martin; pray, remain hidden behind this thicket, and turn your face away from us, while I..."

"Martin!" he choked out.

Through this ruse, Gerard avoided having his anguish witnessed, and Martin spared himself a heartbreaking view. He saw neither the pair kneeling before Heaven to renew those holy vows shattered by cruel men, nor did he see them clasp together like drowning souls, trying and failing to part, before returning to embrace again. He heard only Gerard's sobs and Margaret's low, mournful cries.

Then, a hoarse shout tore through the air, and feet pounded on the hard earth.

Martin sprang up. There stood Gerard, running wildly, both hands clasped high above his head in desperate prayer. Behind him, Margaret tottered backward, palms stretched out in pitiful supplication, her cheeks ash-gray, her eyes fixed on some invisible horror.

He caught her in his arms, speaking words of comfort, but her mind refused to hear them. At the sound of his voice, she only moaned, clutching him tightly, her body trembling with violent shivers.

He lifted her onto the mule, wrapping his arm around her frame. Once strong as a boy, she had now grown limp and powerless in his hold. Supporting her, he guided her slowly and sorrowfully toward home.

She shed not a single tear, uttered not a single word.

At the tree line, he helped her down and bade her cross to her father's house. She obeyed silently.

Martin returned to Rotterdam, finding Sevenbergen far too hot for his temper.

Gerard, severed from the woman he loved, walked as if in a trance. At the roadside inn, he hired a horse and a guide, urging his steed swiftly toward the German border. Yet, everything was mechanical; his senses felt dulled, as if viewed through a thick veil. Trees, houses, and men drifted past him like phantoms. His companions spoke to him twice, receiving no answer. Once, in a savage outburst, he cried, "Shall we never escape this hateful land?"

Hours later, they reached the crest of a steep hill, overlooking a narrow brook at the bottom.

"Halt!" the guide shouted, pointing across the valley. "That is Germany."

"Where?" Gerard asked, bewildered.

"On the other side of the stream. I doubt we need to ride down the hill."

Gerard dismounted without a word, drawing the burgomaster's purse from his belt. As he opened it, the guide muttered sullenly, "You'll soon leave this cursed country. Perhaps the place you're heading to won't treat you kindly either. Still, once you cross that water, they cannot arrest you, even if you've robbed a church."

Such words might have earned the guide a blow, but now they fell on Gerard like empty air. He paid the boy in silence and descended the slope alone. The brook sparkled silver, murmuring over pebbles varnished by the clear current. Gerard sat down, staring blankly at the stones. Then he drank, washing his burning hands and feet in the icy water. It woke him.

Rising, he took a run and leaped across the stream into Germany. The instant his boots touched the foreign soil, he whirled around and looked back.

"Farewell, ungrateful country!" he cried. "But for her, it would cost me naught to leave you forever—to abandon all my kin, and the mother who bore me, and my playmates, and my little native town. Farewell, fatherland—welcome, the wide world! *Omne solum fortipatrat*."

With those brave words on his lips, he collapsed suddenly, limbs weak, and sat down to weep bitterly upon the alien earth.

After a while, the young exile rose, wiping his tears away as a man might shake off dust. He cast not one final glance behind him, lest it weaken his resolve, and stepped forth into the vast unknown.

His love and heavy sorrow left no room for petty fears. Compared to tearing himself from Margaret, walking barefoot to Italy in this rough age seemed trivial.

"All nations meet in a convent," he thought, recalling the monks who had aided him. Thanks to their hospitality and his own insatiable thirst for learning, he could speak most of the tongues needed for this long road. "I will reach Rome soon," he resolved. "The sooner, the better."

After walking a league, he arrived where four winding country roads intersected, confusing many a traveler lost between villages. Gerard took out the small dial Peter had gifted him, setting it in the autumn sun. By this compass, he steered unhesitatingly toward Rome, inexperienced yet determined as a young swallow flying south—but unlike the bird, he flew alone.

Gerard encountered a small party resting by the roadside. Two servants in somber attire leaned idly against a horse, chatting, while their master, a knight clad in a silk doublet and a vibrant green jerkin, lay prone in the dust like a sluggish lizard. His velvet cloak, a blazing yellow, was draped neatly over the horse's flank.

"Is anything amiss?" Gerard asked.

"Not that I know of," replied one servant. "But your master lies as if dead. Are you not ashamed to let him sprawl there?"

"The bare earth is the finest remedy for his malady," the other scoffed. "If you become sober in bed, you wake with a splitting headache, but rising from hard ground makes you jump up like a lark in spring. Does not Ulric speak truth, boy?"

"He speaks plainly, young sir," Ulric agreed warmly.

"What!" Gerard exclaimed, "Is the gentleman drunk?"

The servants erupted in a rough laughter at his naivety. Suddenly, Ulric halted, scanning Gerard with piercing intensity. "Who are you, and where born, that you know not the Count is always drunk at this hour?" Gerard instantly felt the weight of suspicion.

"I am a stranger," Gerard stammered, "but a truthful man who loves learning; therefore I ask questions, not out of prying."

"If you be a true man," Ulric said shrewdly, "give us a trinket for the knowledge we have shared."

Gerard looked confused but forced a smile. "A trinket you shall receive, whatever my lean purse can spare, if you tell me why you have taken his cloak from the man and placed it on the beast."

Inspired by the prospect of payment, the servants immediately proposed two theories. First, that if the Count regained consciousness and found his horse sweating in the chill while his cloak lay discarded, he would certainly curse and perhaps lash out. Second, more philosophically, that a horse is a simpleton requiring warmth and care, whereas a master, constantly filled with good beer, possesses an internal heat sufficient to ward off the cold, rendering the cloak a mere vanity. Both men passionately embraced their respective arguments until their voices rose so high that the green-clad sot began to growl in his sleep.

Their debate quickly soured. Hans, holding the bridle, cuffed Ulric soundly; Ulric retaliated with equal fervor. They exchanged blows over the horse's mane, battering the poor animal until it reared backward and stepped heavily onto the green lord's foot. The knight, like a frog stung by a spear, leaped up howling, clutching his ankle and drawing his sword. Terrified, the servants released the reins. The horse galloped away, the men pursued in panic, and the enraged knight chased them down the lane, his sword drawn and his body bouncing wildly from hedge to hedge.

"In which hurry," Gerard muttered, turning his back on the chaos, he walked calmly south. He was relieved to have saved the four tin farthings he had intended to offer as a bribe, yet his heart remained heavy at their drunken folly.

The sun was nearing its zenith, dipping low in the western sky. Gerard, who had hoped in vain for an inn, grew increasingly uneasy. Black clouds gathered overhead, and he quickened his pace to a run. Rain fell in torrents, soaking the bewildered traveler and seemingly extinguishing the fading sunlight.

Gerard trudged on, dark, wet, and lost in an unfamiliar region. "Fool," he muttered to himself, "to leave Margaret."

Darkness thickened as he entered a vast wood. Huge branches blocked the narrow path, and the benighted stranger groped through what felt like an endless, ink-black cavern with a rugged floor. He stumbled repeatedly, his limbs shivering, his stomach empty, and his heart faint. Suddenly, wolves rose from their dens, bayed around the trees, and filled the air with their harsh cries.

Gerard's hair bristled, but he gripped his cudgel, preparing to sell his life dearly. With no wind, his heightened senses caught the patter of light feet over fallen leaves and the rustle of low branches as creatures glided swiftly past.

Amidst the sea of darkness, a great fiery star appeared close to the ground. He hailed it as his patron saint, shouting, "CANDLE! A CANDLE!" and attempting to run toward it. However, the terrain was too treacherous, and the light proved distant. Finally, deep within the forest, he spotted a house with lit windows and voices rising inside. Looking for a signboard, he found none. "Not an inn, after all!" he sighed. "Yet, what Christian would turn a dog out into this wood tonight?"

Making for the door, he opened it slowly and peered inside timidly. Abruptly, he recoiled as if slapped, returning to the rain and darkness. Inside lay a large, low room dominated by a huge round clay stove reaching to the ceiling. Wet clothes hung everywhere—some on lines, others on rustic poles. These garments, soaked with the day's rain and stained by a life of labor, emitted rank vapors and indescribable odors.

In one corner sat a large traveling family, bringing with them the peculiar, sickly smell of neglected children. Garlic permeated the air, filling every space. With windows closed and an intense heat radiating from the central furnace, the breath of at least forty persons created a stifling atmosphere. They had just finished supper.

Like many artists, Gerard possessed sensitive organs, and the potent effluvia struck him with dismay. Yet, the relentless rain outside and the temptation of the light and fire drew him back again and again, like a moth to a flame. Upon closer inspection, he realized the various smells did not fully blend. An odor of family prevailed in two corners; stewed rustic foods reigned supreme in the center; and garlic, emanating from a noisy group by the window, occupied the smallest orbital space. The scent of the reeking rustic foods, however, drifted farthest—a flavor reminiscent of ancient goats or the ancestors of all foxes, dried by Nebuchadnezzar.

Creeping into a corner near the door, Gerard found himself besieged. Though the solid fetors isolated him slightly, the circulating heat and reeking vapors made the walls drip. He felt as though a cold snake wound about his legs, his head turning to a heavy lump of lead. Next, he felt the urge to choke, to sweetly slumber, and to die, all in one motion. Within an ace of swooning, he recovered with a deep sense of disgust and discouragement, resolving to return to Holland at first light. Gathering a modicum of courage, he approached a man steeped in garlic and asked if this was truly an inn.

"Whence come you, who know not 'The Star of the Forest'?" the man replied.

"I am a stranger; in my country, inns always have signs."

"Droll country yours! What need of a sign to a public-house, a place known to every soul?"

Too tired for argument, Gerard asked where he could find the landlord. This display of ignorance sparked the native's contempt. He pointed to a middle-aged woman seated opposite the oven and signaled to his companions, who fell silent as the news spread, their eyes turning upon Gerard like pivots following a zoo exhibit.

The landlady sat on a chair slightly elevated above the others, between two bundles of feathers. She was diligently plucking downy plumes from quills, filling a second bundle ankle-deep with the debris and contributing a stuffy, malaria-like aroma to the room. Gerard asked if he could have something to eat.

She opened her eyes in astonishment. "Supper is over an hour ago."

"But I had none, good dame."

"Is that my fault? You were welcome to your share."

"But I was benighted, a stranger, and sore delayed against my will."

"What have I to do with that? Everyone knows 'The Star of the Forest' serves from six to eight. Come before six, you sup well; come before eight, as Heaven pleases; come after eight, you get a clean bed and a horn of milk at dawn."

Gerard looked blank. "May I go to bed, then, dame?" he asked sulkily. "For it is ill sitting up wet and fasting, and the proverb says, 'He sups who sleeps.'"

"The beds have not yet arrived," she replied. "You will sleep when the rest do. Inns are not built for one."

It was Gerard's turn to be astounded. "The beds were not come! What, in Heaven's name, did she mean?" Afraid to question her further, sensing the zoological scrutiny of the room, he leaned against the wall and sighed.

A ripple of amusement went through the watchful company. "So this is Germany," Gerard thought, "and Germany is a great country by Holland. Small nations for me." He consoled himself, knowing it would be his last and first night in the land.

His reverie broke as an elbow drove into his ribs. Turning sharply, he saw a woman in the corner beckoning. Approaching gingerly, surprised and irresolute, he stopped beside her. "Hold the child," she said in a hearty voice. In a moment, she plumped the infant into Gerard's arms.

He stood transfixed, his hands feeling like jelly of lead, sudden horror widening his features.

At this expression, the lynx-eyed assembly laughed loudly and for a long time.

"Never heed them," the woman said cheerfully.

Denys reached out, catching Gerard by the shoulder to stem his downward plunge. Yet, the rescue was precarious; Denys feared that saving his friend's neck might still result in broken limbs. The only solid anchor beneath Denys was the carcass of the dying bear, upon whom Denys's head and shoulders crashed. With frantic strength, Denys tore himself off the beast. It was a futile gesture. The bear, panting heavily with quivering limbs, was already succumbing to exhaustion, yet a hare was not so innocuous. Soon, the animal breathed her last. The practical Denys immediately propped Gerard upright against the soft, warm flesh of the corpse, fanning him gently until consciousness returned.

Gerard revived gradually, though confused and disoriented by the sensation of the bear surrounding him. Startled, he rolled away, letting out a piercing yell.

"Courage!" cried Denys, pointing to the motionless form. "Le diable est mort."

"Is it truly dead?" Gerard questioned, hiding partially behind a tree. His courage burned with a feverish intensity, and the chill of the mountain air had settled deep within him.

"Behold," Denys replied, pulling the brute's ear playfully. He opened the jaw wide, inserting his head inside to perform various insulting antics. In the middle of this grotesque display, Gerard was violently overcome with nausea.

Denys laughed at his companion's distress.

"What troubles you now?" the soldier asked. "Also, why did you tumble from your perch just as we secured victory?"

"I swooned," Gerard admitted.

"But why?"

Receiving no immediate answer, Denys pressed on. "Green girls faint merely by looking at you, yet they choose their time and place wisely. What woman has ever fainted atop a tree?"

"She sent her filthy blood all over me," Gerard retched, wiping his brow. "I believe the stench must have overwhelmed me! Faugh! I detest blood."

"I suspect it did potent work."

"Look at the mess she has made of me."

"But with her blood, not yours," Denys countered, showing unexpected sympathy. "I pity the enemy who strove to satisfy you."

"You need not boast, Master Denys," Gerard accused, glancing at the soldier. "I saw you beneath the tree, your shirt stained with color."

"Let us distinguish," Denys blushed slightly. "It is permissible to tremble for a friend."

As proof, Gerard threw his arms around Denys's neck in a silent embrace.

"Look here," whined the stout soldier, moved by this sudden outpouring of youth and affection. "Was anything ever so reminiscent of a woman? I love thee, little milksop—go on. Good! Behold him on his knees now. What new caprice is this?"

"Oh, Denys, ought we not to return thanks to Him who has saved both our lives against such fearful odds?" Gerard knelt, praying aloud. Shortly thereafter, he found Denys kneeling quietly beside him, hands crossed over his chest in the fashion of his nation, wearing a face as long as his arm. When they rose, Gerard's countenance was radiant.

"Good Denys," he said, "Heaven will reward thy piety."

"Ah, bah!" the Frenchman scoffed. "I did it merely out of politeness. It was to please thee, little one. *C'est égal*, the prayer was well and orderly, and it edified me to the core while it lasted. A bishop could scarcely have handled the matter better. Now that our vespers are sung and the saints enlisted with us, *marchons*."

Before taking two steps, Denys paused. "By-the-by, the cub!"

"Oh, no, no!" cried Gerard.

"You are right. It is late. We squandered time climbing trees, tumbling off them, swooning, vomiting, and praying. Furthermore, the brute is heavy to carry. And now I recall, we shall likely have Papa hunting for it next; these bears create quite a commotion over an odd cub."

Suddenly, Denys noticed something wrong. "What is this? You are wounded! You are wounded!"

"Not I."

"He is wounded; miserable that I am!"

"Be calm, Denys. I am not touched; I feel no pain anywhere."

"You? You only feel when another is hurt!" Denys cried, thrown into great emotion. Throwing himself to his knees, he examined Gerard's leg with glistening eyes.

"Quick! Quick! Before it stiffens!" he urged, hurrying him forward.

"Who makes the commotion about nothing now?" Gerard inquired composedly.

Denys's reply was indirect. "Be pleased to note," he said, "that I have a bad heart. You possessed the fortitude to save my life, yet I must sneer at you, a novice in war. Was I not a novice once myself? Then you fainted from a wound, and I mistook it for fear, calling you a milksop. Briefly, I possess a bad tongue and a bad heart."

"Denys!"

"Plait-il?"

"You lie."

"You are very kind to say so, little one, and I am eternally obliged to you," mumbled the remorseful Denys.

Before they had traveled many furlongs, the muscles of Gerard's wounded leg contracted and stiffened. Eventually, he could barely plant his toe upon the ground, doing so with excruciating pain. At last, he could endure it no longer.

"Let me lie down and die," he groaned. "This is intolerable."

Denys argued that it was afternoon and the nights were becoming frosty; cold and hunger were terrible companions, especially with a notorious figure already proven dead. Thus, Gerard leaned upon his axe, hobbling on. But soon, he gave in all at once, sinking helpless into the road.

Denys drew him aside into the woods. To his surprise, the soldier handed Gerard his crossbow and bolts, strictly ordering him to remain quiet. If any suspicious individuals discovered him, Gerard was to command them to keep aloof; if they refused, he was to shoot them dead at twenty paces. "Honest men keep the paths, and knaves in the woods are only fools who attempt to parley." With this, Denys snatched up Gerard's axe and set off running—not toward Düsseldorf as Gerard expected, but back along the road they had come.

Gerard lay aching, the journey to Rome seeming impossibly distant despite having covered two hundred miles. Soon, however, his thoughts drifted toward Sevenbergen. How sweet it would be one day to hold Margaret's hand and recount everything he had endured for her! The mere thought of her soothed his pain, and amidst the irritation of his nerves, he lay resigned, offering a faint, sweet smile.

More than two hours later, shouts erupted. Suddenly, an object struck a nearby tree with a sharp thud, vibrating within its branches. Gerard looked up to see an arrow. Starting to his feet, he watched as several missiles rattled through the boughs, and the wood echoed with battle cries. Unable to determine their origin, as the vast forests reverberated sound, it seemed the air itself filled with noise. Presently, there was a lull, followed by the fierce gallop of hooves. Louder shouts and cries arose, mingled with shrieks and groans. Above all, strange and terrible sounds, like fierce claps of thunder, bellowed loudly before dying off in cracking echoes. Red tongues of flame shot intermittently among the trees, and clouds of sulfurous smoke drifted overhead. Then, all was still.

Struck with awe, Gerard cried, "What will become of Denys?" "Oh, why did you leave me? Oh, Denys, my friend! My friend!"

Just before sunset, Denys returned, almost collapsing under a hairy bundle—the bear's skin. Gerard welcomed him with a burst of joy that surprised him.

"I thought never to see you again, dear Denys. Were you in the battle?"

"No. What battle?"

"The bloody fray of men or fiends that raged in the wood a while ago!" Gerard described it vividly.

Denys patted him indulgently on the back. "It is well," he said. "Thou art a good painter; and fever is a great spur to the imagination. Once, I lay in a cart-shed with a cracked skull and saw two hosts maneuver and fight for a good hour on eight feet square, which I described fairly to my comrade, though not so gorgeously as thou, for lack of book learning."

"What, then, you believe me not?" Gerard insisted. "When I tell you the arrows whizzed over my head..."

"May the foul fiends fly away with me if I believe a word of it."

Gerard took his arm and quietly pointed to a tree close by. "Why, it looks like—it is—a broad arrow, as I live!" He approached closely to inspect it.

"It came from the battle. I heard it, and saw it."

"An English arrow."

"How know you that?"

"Marry, by its length. The English bowmen draw the bow to the ear; others only to the right breast. Hence, the English loose a three-foot shaft, and this is one of them, perdition seize them! Well, if this is not glamour, there has been a trifle of a battle. And if there has been a battle in such a ridiculous place for a

The afternoon following Gerard's injury found him no longer dizzy, but plagued by a restless irritability and a flood of peculiar fancies. In one such whimsy, he entreated Denys to procure a lemon for sucking. Denys, transformed from a rough soldier into a figure resembling a grandfather by the tenderness of his friendship, rose hastily. "Lemons?" he exclaimed, assuring Gerard he would have them "in the twinkling of a quart pot," and vanished to scour the local markets. They were rare luxuries in the north, and Denys was gone a considerable time. Gerard grew increasingly impatient until the shop door finally creaked open.

However, it was not Denys who entered. Instead, a towering, imposing figure glided softly inside. He was an elderly gentleman clad in a long, sober gown trimmed with rich fur, wearing cherry-colored hose and pointed shoes. A sword rested at his side within a fine Morocco scabbard, and around his neck hung a severe, starched ruff, treacherously stiffened into deep furrows by hidden wooden frameworks known as rebatoes. Upon his head sat a four-cornered cap bordered with fur, framing a majestic white beard that cascaded over his bosom. Gerard instantly recognized the vocation of this visitor; barring the weapon, this attire was the full regalia of a physician. Following closely at the elder's heels was a small boy carrying a basket brimming with phials, lint, and surgical instruments, all seemingly inviting scrutiny.

The old gentleman approached the bedside with soft steps and asked quietly, "How fares it with thee, my son?"

Gerard answered with gratitude that his wound caused him little pain, though his throat felt parched and his head heavy.

"A wound!" the doctor interrupted, his tone shifting to professional curiosity. "They told me not of that. Let me see it. Ay, ay, a good, clean bite. The mastiff had sound teeth that took this out, I warrant me;" and the physician's sympathy seemed to drain entirely toward the canine predator he had conjured, his imagined jackal. "This must be cauterized forthwith, or we shall find you starting back from water, turning somersaults in bed under our hands. 'Tis the season for raving curs, and one hath done your business; but we will baffle him yet. Urchin, go heat thine iron."

"But, sir," Gerard protested gently, "'twas no dog, but a bear."

"A bear!" the senior physician remonstrated severely. "Think what you say; 'tis ill jesting with the man of art who brings his grey hairs and long study to heal you. A bear, quotha! Had you dissected as many bears as I, or even a tenth thereof, and drawn their teeth to keep your hand in, you would know that no bear's jaw ever made this foolish trifling wound. I tell you 'twas a dog, and since you put me to it, I even deny that it was a dog of magnitude, but neither more nor less than one of these little furious curs that are so rife, running devious paths, biting every manly leg, and laying its wearer low—but for me and my learned brethren, who still stay the mischief with knife and cautery."

"Alas, sir!" Gerard hastened to correct him. "When did I say 'twas a bear's jaw?' I said, 'A bear;' it was his paw, now."

"And why didst not tell me that at once?"

"Because you kept telling me instead."

"Never conceal aught from your leech, young man," the senior continued. He was a good talker, albeit one of the worst listeners in Europe. "Well, it is an ill business. All the horny excrescences of animals—to wit, claws of tigers, panthers, badgers, cats, bears, and the like—and the horn of deer, and the nails of humans, especially children, are imbued with direst poison. You'd better have been bitten by a cur, whatever you may say, than gored by a bull or stag, or scratched by a bear. However, you shall have a good biting cataplasm for thy leg; meantime, let us keep the body cool: put out thy tongue!—Good!—Fever. Let me feel thy pulse: Good!—Fever. I ordain flebotomy, and on the instant."

"Flebotomy! That is bloodletting," Gerard murmured. "Humph! Well, no matter, if 'tis sure to cure me, for I will not lie idle here." The doctor assured him that flebotomy was infallible, particularly in this case.

"Hans, go fetch the things needful," commanded the man of art, "and I will entertain the patient meantime with reasons."

The physician then began to explain to Gerard that in disease the blood becomes hot, distempered, and increasingly poisonous. By removing a portion of this unhealthy liquid, nature would be compelled to create a purer fluid to fill the void. Therefore, bleeding served as both a cooler and a purifier, acting as a specific cure for all diseases, for all ailments were essentially febrile, regardless of what empirics might claim.

"But think not," he said warmly, "that it suffices merely to bleed. Any paltry barber can open a vein (though not all possess the skill to close it again). The art lies in knowing precisely which vein to empty for a specific disease. The other day, they brought me a man tormented with earache. I let him blood in the right thigh, and away flew his earache. By-the-by, he has died since then. Another came with a toothache. I bled him behind the ear, and relieved him in a jiffy. He, too, is now dead, as it happens. I bled our bailiff between the thumb and forefinger for rheumatism. Presently, he returned with a headache and drumming in his ears, holding out his hand over the basin; but I smiled at his folly, bled him in the left ankle sore against his will, and made his head as light as a nut."

Diverging from the immediate medical theme in the manner of an enthusiast, the reverend teacher proceeded to lecture further: "Know, young man, that two schools of art contend at this moment throughout Europe. There is the Arabian school, whose ancient oracles are Avicenna, Rhazes, and Albucasis; its revivers include Chauliac and Lanfranc. Then there is the Greek school, whose modern champions are Bessarion, Platinus, and Marsilius Ficinus, but whose pristine doctors were medicine's very oracles: Phoebus, Chiron, Aesculapius, and his sons Podalinus and Machaon, along with Pythagoras, Democritus, and Praxagoras, who invented the arteries, and Dioctes, 'qui primus urinae animum dedit.' All these taught orally. Then came Hippocrates, the eighteenth descendant of Aesculapius, of whom we possess manuscripts. To him we owe 'the vital principle.' He also invented the bandage and tapped for water on the chest; and above all, he dissected—yet only quadrupeds, for the brutal prejudices of the pagan vulgar withheld the human body from the knife of science. Him followed Aristotle, who gave us the aorta, the largest blood-vessel in the human body."

"Surely, sir," objected Gerard humbly, "the Almighty gave us all that is in our bodies, and not Aristotle, nor any Grecian man."

"Child! Of course He gave us the thing," the doctor retorted, his color rising. "But Aristotle did more; he gave us the *name* of the thing. But young men will still be talking. The next great light was Galen, who studied at Alexandria, then the home of science. He, justly malcontent with quadrupeds, dissected apes, as coming nearer to man, and bled like a Trojan. Then came Theophilus, who gave us the nerves, the lacteal vessels, and the pia mater."

This worried Gerard. "I cannot lie still and hear it said that mortal man bestowed the parts which Adam our father took from Him, who made him of the clay, and us His sons."

"Was ever such perversity?" said the doctor, indignant. "Who is the real donor of a thing to man? Is it he who plants it secretly in the dark recesses of man's body, or the learned wight who reveals it to his intelligence, thereby enriching his mind with knowledge? Comprehension is your only true possession. Are you answered?"

"I am put to silence, sir."

"And that is better still; for garrulous patients are ill to cure, especially in fever. I say, then, that Eristratus gave us the cerebral nerves and the milk vessels; nay, more, he was the inventor of lithotomy, whatever you may say. Then came another whom I forget; you do somewhat perturb me with your petty exceptions. Then came Ammonius, the author of lithotrity, and here comes Hans with the basin—to stay your volubility. Blow thy chafer, boy, and hand me the basin; 'tis well. Arabs, quotha! What are they but a sect of yesterday who, about the year 1000, fell in with the writings of those very Greeks and read them awry, having no concurrent light of their own? For their demigod, and camel-driver, Mah

The physician returned home, his brow furrowed with a pain he termed "a bad burn." In the economy of human affairs, one often cauterizes the neighbor's finger while singering one's own. His housekeeper, a woman of practical wisdom, applied a mild salve of ancient recipe. He submitted to her ministrations with the patience of a lamb, his sole ambition being swift recovery, while simultaneously drafting a bill for shattered vials and arranging for the immediate imprisonment of his traveling guests. Before a magistrate, he swore an oath that these strangers, owing him money, plotted to flee the town instantly.

Alas, it proved his unluckiest day. His earnest, albeit dishonest, endeavor to commit perjury was thwarted by a circumstance he had never anticipated. He had, in fact, spoken the truth. And yet, in an affidavit! The officers, arriving at "The Silver Lion," discovered that the travelers had already taken wing.

Turning to the riverbank for intelligence, they learned the friends were heading upstream in hot pursuit. This fleeting escape owed everything to Denys's acute sense and observation. After a burst of laughter—so hearty it served as a tonic—he sobered abruptly. "Gerard Dusseldorf," he declared, "is no place for us. That old fellow departed with unnatural silence for such a chatterbox. We are strangers here; the bailiff is his friend. Within five minutes, we shall lie in a dungeon for assaulting a Dusseldorf dignitary. Are you strong enough to hobble to the water's edge?"

"The odds, Denys?" Gerard asked, his voice trembling with fever. "Untold, and entirely in favor of the boat. I pine for Rome; Rome is my path to Sevenbergen. There, we shall lie in a boat, but *upon* the Rhine—the famous, cool, refreshing Rhine. I feel its breezes already. The mere sight will cure a hop-and-a-thimbler's fever like mine. Away!"

Seeing his companion in this agitated state, Denys hastily settled accounts with the landlord, and they hurried to the river. Inquiry revealed that the public boat had departed half an hour prior, and no others would launch until evening. However, by questioning numerous locals and gathering a crowd, they secured an offer from an elderly man and his two sons to provide a private craft.

A bystander chuckled at this arrangement. "The current is far too strong for three oars."

"Then my comrade and I shall assist with the rowing," offered the invalid.

"No need," the old man replied kindly. "Bless your silly heart, he owns the other boat."

A powerful breeze filled a broad sail, and the men, rowing alongside, propelled the vessel at a spanking pace.

"Are ye better, lad, for the river breeze?"

"Much better. But truly, the doctor did me good."

"The doctor? You refused his remedies!"

"No, but I mean... you'll say I'm nothing—but knocking the old fool down somehow soothed me."

"Amiable dove! How thy little character unfolds daily, like a rosebud. I misread thee at first."

"Nay, Denys, take me not wrongly. I trust I bore with his idle threats, though his voice pierced my poor ears. Yet he was an infidel, or next to one, and such I am taught to abhor. Did he not imply we owe our inward parts to men with long Greek names, and not to Him, whose name is but a syllable, yet whose hand governs all the earth? Pagan!"

"So you struck him down forthright—as a true Christian."

"Now, Denys, cease your jesting. Do not take the part of an ill man. Had it been a thunderbolt from Heaven, he would have met his due; yet he took but a sorry bolster from this weak arm."

"What weak arm?" Denys inquired, his eyes twinkling. "I have lived among arms, and by Samson's hairy paw, I have never seen one more like a catapult. The bolster wrapped around his nose, and the two ends kissed behind his head; his forehead resonated. Had he been Goliath, or Julius Caesar, instead of an old quack, down he would have gone. Saint Denys guard me from such feeble opposites as thou! And above all, from their weak arms—you diabolical young hypocrite."

The river wound through many turns, shifting the wind from behind them to the side. They adjusted course to keep the boat from heeling, save for a five-year-old boy, the boatman's grandson and darling. This urchin had slipped aboard at the start; being too light to disturb the trim, he existed above, or rather below, the laws of navigation.

They sailed merrily, unaware that a whole posse of constables, armed with the bailiff's writ, pursued them. Though the wind gusted, the current ran deep and fast.

Suddenly, Gerard recalled that this route led to Rome, not Burgundy. "Oh, Denys," he said, his face pale, "this is not your road."

"I know it," Denys replied quietly. "But what can I do? I cannot leave thee till the fever breaks. It lingers still; thou art red and white by turns. I have watched thee. I must e'en proceed to Cologne, I fear, and then strike across."

"Thank Heaven," Gerard breathed, a touch of self-deception coloring his eagerness. "'Twere a sin to be so near Cologne and not behold it. Oh, man, it is a vast, ancient city of my dreams, which fortune denied me. Alas, by what cruel chance do I arrive now? Well then, Denys," he continued, less warmly, "it is old enough to have been founded by a Roman lady in the first century, sacked by Attila, and later marred by Norman Lothaire. It possesses a church for every week of the year, besides countless chapels, convents, and nunneries. Above all, there stands the stupendous Minster, unfinished, wherein rest the Three Kings who brought gifts to our Lord: Melchior with gold, Gaspar with frankincense, and Balthazar, the Black King, who carried myrrh. Over their bones rises the Shrine, the wonder of the world—a masterpiece of ever-shining brass brighter than gold, studded with exquisite imagery and brave colors. Two broad stripes run to and fro, adorned with jewels so great and rare that each could ransom a crown-wearer. Upon it stand the three kings curiously counterfeited; two in solid silver, richly gilt, bareheaded; but the Ethiopian king, ebony-skinned, bears a golden crown. In the midst sits our Blessed Lady, in virgin silver, holding Christ, flanked at the corners by four goodly waxen tapers that burn night and day. Holy eyes have watched and renewed that light unceasingly for ages, and holy eyes shall watch them in eternity. Legend says they burned when first found, and they shall light the earth unto its grave. There is also St. Ursula's Church, where lies the saint herself and her eleven thousand companions, virgins put to the sword by barbarous Moors on the twenty-third day of October, two hundred and thirty-eight. Their bones are piled in the vaults, many of their skulls displayed in the church. St. Ursula rests in a thin golden case upon the high altar, shown to humble Christians only on solemn days."

"Eleven thousand virgins!" cried Denys. "What babes German men must have been in days of yore. Would that all their bones might turn to flesh again, their skulls to sweet faces, as we pass through the gates. Odds are some are weary of their estate by this time."

"Tush, Denys!" Gerard chided gently. "Why wilt thou, being good, make thyself seem evil? If thy wishing-cap be on, pray that we meet the meanest of those wise virgins in the next world, and to that end, let us reverence their holy dust in this one. And there is the Church of the Maccabees, with the cauldron in which they and their mother Solomon were boiled by a wicked king for refusing swine's flesh."

"Oh, peremptory king! And pig-headed Maccabees! I would have eaten bacon with my pork-liver rather than change places at the fire with my meat."

"What scurvy words? It was their faith."

"Nay, bridle thy choler. Tell me, are there naught but churches in this thy vaunted city? For I affect Sir Knight rather than Sir Priest."

"Ay, marry. There is a university nearly a hundred years old. There is a marketplace, fairest in the world, surrounded by houses great as palaces. There stands a stupendous Senate-House covered with images, and at its head stands one of stout Herman Grin, a soldier like thyself, lad."

"Aye. Tell me of him! What feat of arms earned him his niche?"

"A rare one. He slew a lion in fair combat, with nothing but his cloak and a short sword. He thrust the cloak into the brute's mouth and cut its spine in twain. There stands the man's effigy, and beside it the lion's, to prove it. The like was never done but by three others, I ween: Samson, Lysimachus of Macedon, and Benaiah, captain of David's host."

"Marry! Three tall fellows. I would like well to sup with them all tonight."

A curious equilibrium governs the world, balancing good against evil with a singular, almost universal precision. It was the steel bow strapped to the arbalestrier's back—a burden that had nearly claimed his life—that now served as Gerard's salvation. Suspended above the water, the weapon had drawn the drowning soldier down, yet its position rendered it impossible for anyone else to grasp. Gerard seized it instantly, one hand clutching the shaft, the other wedging firmly between the bowstring and the limb, securing his grip as surely as any master craftsman. With a desperate, jerking motion, Denys was hauled upward, half his torso breaking the surface before the current could claim him again.

"Now, grip me not!" Gerard cried, his voice trembling with the terror of having made a fatal error. "Grip me not!"

"Pas si bete," Denys gurgled, the words bubbling through his gasping lungs.

Instantly, however, Gerard's countenance shifted from panic to a steely resolve. "Lie flat upon thy back," he commanded sharply. Seizing the heavy arbalest, he used it to pull himself forward, creating a bridge for his companion. "Place thy hand upon my shoulder! Beat the water with the other—no, strike downwards! Do exactly as I bid thee." Grasping Denys's long, sodden hair, Gerard twisted it tightly, biting down on the strands to anchor himself, while the powerful muscles of his youth strained against the weight. He propelled them both, striking out vigorously against the torrent. For a fleeting moment, hesitation plagued him regarding which bank to aim for; he knew little of the terrible consequences of such a choice. Yet, spotting the western shore slightly closer, he directed his course toward it rather than toward prison, sparing himself a scandalous capture. Driven by the fierce current, they angled downstream, covering nearly a hundred yards until Denys, buoyed by exhaustion, muttered uneasily, "How much longer?"

"Hold fast," Gerard mumbled, his voice steady despite the strain. "Whatever a duck knows, a Dutchman knows; thou art safe as in thy bed."

Suddenly, the depths yielded to shallow mud, and they waded onto the firm ground of the bank. Upon stepping onto terra firma, they stared at one another from head to toe, eyes wide with shock, as if trying to devour each other with a single gaze. Then, by a shared, instinctive impulse, they flung arms around each other's necks, panting heavily, hearts overflowing with a silence too profound for speech. In this sacred moment, life tasted sweeter than heaven, especially for the exiled lover who had just saved the soul of his friend. What heights of joy have poets yet ascended, or dared attempt? To save a human life—and specifically, a beloved friend—is a reward worth three score years and ten. Calmer now, they clasped hands, strolling along the riverbank like a pair of sweethearts, scarcely aware of their destination or the path beneath their feet.

Meanwhile, the boatmen remained safe upon their vessel, Herr Turnip-face, the "Inverter of things," sitting centrally. The chaos that had erupted on the water had failed to disturb his habitual composure. As he watched them shoot back into the Rhine, and later greet them upon their return, he maintained an air of detached amusement.

"Shall we wait for them to right the boat?" Denys asked.

"No," Gerard replied briskly. "Our fare is paid; we owe them naught. Let us depart."

Denys assented, noting that they could traverse the entire distance to Cologne along this bank.

"I fare not to Cologne," Gerard answered calmly.

"Why, whither then?"

"To Burgundy."

"To Burgundy? Ah, no! That is too good to be true."

"It is truth, coupled with sense. What matters to me the route to Rome?"

"Nay, nay," Denys protested gently. "Thou speakest thus merely to please me. The change is too sudden. Think me not so ill-hearted as to accept thy word blindly. Did I not see thine eyes sparkle at the wonders of Cologne? The churches, the images, the relics..."

"How dull art thou, Denys," Gerard interrupted with a smile. "That was when we were meant to enjoy them together. Churches! I shall see plenty, wherever I go toward Rome. There are bones of saints and martyrs aplenty; alas, the world is full of them. But where on earth's face shall I find another friend like thee? Nay, I will not drive thee further from the road leading to thy dear home, nor will she pine in vain. Nor will I deprive myself of thy company by sending thee away. Since I drew thee from the Rhine, I love thee more deeply than before. Thou art my pearl; I fished thee, and I must keep thee. Gainsay me not, lest thou bring back my fever. Cry courage, lead on, and hey for Burgundy!"

Denys threw up his arms in a joyful caper. "Courage! Va pour la Bourgogne! Oh, soyes tranquille! Cette fois, il est bien décidément mort, ce coquin-là." And turning their backs upon the Rhine, they marched away.

As this decision crystallized, a commotion stirred among the small group watching from the opposite bank. They had not taken many steps before a voice boomed across the water. "HALT!"

Gerard turned, recognizing one of the four figures holding aloft a badge of office and a parchment scroll. His heart sank; he was a citizen of good standing, accustomed to yielding to the voice of the Law that now commanded him to return to Düsseldorf.

Denys, however, shared none of his scruples. A Frenchman by birth, he despised the nations, laws, inhabitants, and customs of others. A soldier by trade, he viewed the situation through a martial lens: superior force opposed, a river as a barrier, and the rear unguarded. Retreat was easily made. Seeing at a glance that the boat still drifted in mid-stream and that no ferry existed nearer than Düsseldorf, he declared, "I shall make a quick retreat to that hill, and once out of sight, I shall move with speed."

They sauntered off casually.

"Halt! In the bailiff's name!" cried a voice from the shore.

Denys spun around, ostentatiously snapping his fingers at the bailiff, and continued his pace.

"Halt! In the archbishop's name!"

Denys snapped his fingers at the prelate and moved on.

"Halt! In the emperor's name!"

With a final, defiant snap of his fingers addressed to the sovereign, Denys proceeded.

Gerard watched this unnecessary pantomime with regret. As soon as they passed the brow of the hill, he declared, "There is now but one course; we must run to Burgundy rather than walk," and he broke into a sprint, maintaining his pace for nearly a league without faltering.

Denys, thoroughly winded, inquired what had become of Gerard's fever. "I begin to miss it sadly," he remarked dryly.

"I dropped it in the Rhine, I trow," Gerard replied.

Soon, they reached a small village, where Denys procured a loaf of bread and a massive bottle of Rhenish wine. "We must sleep in some hollow or corner," he explained. "If we lodge at an inn, we shall be discovered in our beds." This was sound prudence for an old soldier in those times. The official net for capturing lawbreakers, particularly the plebeians, was tight, although the cooperation of the general public on the Continent was nearly non-existent. Innkeepers were subject to intense surveillance regarding their travelers, bearing responsibility not only for their guests' actions but seemingly for their sufferings as well.

Both friends breathed a sigh of relief as the sun dipped below the horizon, and their spirits lifted when, after a long trudge under the starlight—for the moon, if memory serves, did not rise until approximately three in the morning—they arrived at a large barn belonging to a dwelling some distance away. A recent harvest had left the interior filled with threshed grain; a mountain of straw piled on one side of the floor stood nearly as high as the unthreshed corn on the other.

"Here be two royal beds," Denys observed. "Which shall we occupy, the mow or the straw?"

"The straw for me," Gerard decided.

Seated on the heap, they devoured their brown bread and drank their wine. Then Denys covered his friend in layers of straw, piling it high above him, leaving only a narrow aperture for breathing. "Water is said to be death to fevered men," he noted. "Yet, I shall make warm water available, anyhow."

Gerard assured him to ease his mind. "These few drops from the Rhine cannot chill me. I feel sufficient heat within my body now to parch a kennel or boil a cloud if I were situated in one." With this epigram, his consciousness faded rapidly, allowing him to fall swiftly into sleep.

Denys, remaining awake for a time, heard sounds that caused him to draw closer. Hooves rang out from Düsseldorf, approaching the wooden barn. The structure vibrated violently as they rattled past, emitting a howl familiar to the fifteenth century but alien to modern Europe, akin to a Red Indian's war-whoop. Denys shuddered where he lay. Gerard slept soundly, like a spinning top. The storm of horses and howls swept past and died away.

The sturdy soldier drew a long breath,

Gerard stood stunned, the news striking him like a blow that left his complexion pale, then flushed, then drained again. Rage tightened his jaw, grinding down upon the thought of pursuit. Men of spirit, he realized, behave much like wild boars; they may flee a superior force initially, driven by instinct rather than folly, but if the hunters press too closely and never let go, the beast turns. It lashes out, charging blindly through a thicket of enemies, seeking only space to breathe. Gerard braced himself for this counter-charge, his eyes hardening into battle-readiness. Yet, the resolve shattered instantly, leaving his face fallen and voice trembling with despair. "But my axe remains behind," he whispered, the weight of his abandoned tools echoing in the silence. "It lies in the Rhine."

Together, they weighed their options. Prudence offered counsel, urging caution against the village where enemies might lie in wait. Hunger, however, roared its command to seek sustenance. In the quiet struggle between prudence and necessity, the stomach won the day. They decided to cut across the open fields, away from the watchful eyes of the hamlet.

They paused beside a great haystack, borrowing two bundles to create a makeshift bed. Lying there, hidden beneath the straw yet prickled by stinging nettles, they rested in the damp earth. One by one, they ventured forth, returning with handfuls of turnips. They gnawed these meager roots at intervals throughout their slow retreat, savoring the sweet relief until the sun dipped below the horizon.

Then, the world turned gray and wet. Shivering, they slipped out into the driving rain and encroaching dark, emerging onto the far side of the village. The night was terrible, a blanket of pitch-blackness swept over by a gale that howled with ferocity. Neither sight nor sound held sway; they moved unseen and unheard, invisible phantoms drifting past their foes who slept unawares. In the natural terrors of such a storm, human enemies faded from their minds, replaced by the sheer, blinding horror of the tempest. It seemed as though they were groping and hacking their way through blocks of black marble, blind and deaf. When the moon finally rose, casting a wan light upon the landscape, they had traveled leagues beyond Düsseldorf. Still, they pressed onward, trudging through the mud.

Suddenly, looming before them, stood a massive edifice.

"Take heart!" cried Denys, his voice cutting through the wind. "I believe I recognize this place. Yes, it must be so. We have entered the diocese of Jülich. Here, Cologne holds no sway."

With those words, the fear began to recede. The next moment, they crossed the threshold and found themselves secure within the sanctuary of the walls.

In the quiet sanctuary of the cloister, Gerard found solace in the company of a monk renowned for his ingenuity. This brother, a master craftsman of the divine arts, had erected a grand sundial in the prior's garden, fashioned wheels to lift water from the deep, and devised machines to winnow grain. His workshop brimmed with wonders: he had carved several psalteries and two dulcimers, and was currently striving to construct a set of regales, small organs intended for the choir.

Gerard, playing his humble psaltery with tentative grace, listened as the monk touched the strings with a divine fluency, instantly revealing the novice's lack of experience. While the monk shone brightly in illumination, Gerard surpassed him in the beauty of his script. In an age where accomplishment reflected a genuine spiritual bent, their differences melted away. Youth and Age stood side by side like brothers, and Gerard was warmly invited to remain that night. He sought counsel from Denys, who, with a rueful shrug, granted permission.

That evening, Gerard shared his destination and recounted their recent trials, carefully softening the harsher truths of their journey. "Alas!" sighed the elderly monk, "though I have traveled widely in my time, I was never molested." He warned Gerard against inns, describing them as haunts of rogues and rioters where the soul itself might fall prey to evil, urging him instead to seek refuge in peaceful monasteries for every stage of his travels. Suddenly, his demeanor shifted, his eyes sharpening like needles as he asked, "How long hast thou been shriven?" Gerard blushed, offering a feeble reply, "More than a fortnight." "And thou an exorcist!" the monk exclaimed, alarmed. "No marvel then that perils have beset thee. Thou must be absolved at once." "Yes, Father," Gerard knelt, joining his hands, but the monk held him back with a fretful gesture. "Not unto me! Not unto me! I am as saturated with worldly cares as any living soul. My entire being rests upon these wooden pipes and leather stops, destined to rot alongside those whose hearts are fixed upon such vanities." "Most holy Father," Gerard pleaded gently, "these instruments serve the Church, and surely that sanctifies the labor bestowed upon them?" "Precisely what the Devil whispers in my ear," the monk retorted, placing a hand behind his back and pointing a finger that danced between threat and playfulness. "He reminds me that Solomon built the Lord a house adorned with rare hangings, counting it gracious and sinless. Oh, he quotes Scripture with startling frequency! Yet I am no simpleton, my lad," he declared, turning his gaze upward with sudden defiance, addressing the empty air rather than Gerard. "One toy completed, vigils, fasts, and prayers await me—prayers standing, prostrate upon the chapel floor, and immersed in a basin of icy water." He nudged Gerard, winking knowingly. "Nothing terrifies me more than witnessing monks lost in prayer up to our chins in cold water, for *corpus domat aqua*. Go now, confess thy trifling, pardonable sins of youth and secular life, and leave me to mine, sweet as honey and demanding equal measure of expiation."

Gerard bowed his head, unable to resist murmuring, "Where shall I find a confessor holier and more merciful?" "Within each of these cells," the monk replied simply, guiding him down the corridor, "seek Brother Anselm, yonder." Gerard approached the indicated door, but the cells were clustered so closely together that he feared a mistake. Just as he raised his hand to knock, he heard his old friend calling out in a frantic whisper, "Nay! Nay! Nay!" Turning quickly, he found the monk pacing anxiously outside his own door, beating the air with doubled hands like a woodcutter swinging an axe. Gerard, convinced the adjacent cell harbored some ferocious beast, hesitated, glanced back, and moved to the next door. Immediately, the monk nodded vigorously, his face transforming from agitated distress to a blissful radiance, before darting back into his chamber. He retrieved an hourglass, flipped it, and resumed his work on the regales, muttering to himself, "Well-a-day, how swiftly the sands flow when a man bends over earthly toys."

Brother Anselm was a venerable figure, his ample head crowned with dignity and his countenance radiating a profound, gentle love. As Gerard confessed, answering Anselm's tender yet penetrating inquiries, a thought crossed his mind: "Behold a head! Oh, dearest Father, I wonder if you will permit me to sketch it when our conversation ends." So engrossed was he in admiration that his own thoughts grew hazy, and he momentarily forgot certain crimes. Nevertheless, he offered no false comfort nor diminished the gravity of his past errors. The penance imposed was severe yet symbolic: Gerard was to enter the church, prostrate himself, and kiss the lowest step of the altar three times; then, kneeling, recite three Pater Nosters and a Creed. Upon completion, he was to return immediately.

Having fulfilled this brief mortification, Gerard returned to find Father Anselm applying plaster to his injured leg. "First the soul, then the body," the monk explained. "I am the surgeon here, lacking a better. This is applied to cool the flesh, lest it burn. The Saints forbid otherwise." During this treatment, the monastic physician, having shown interest in Gerard's account of his injuries in Düsseldorf, leaned toward Denys's theory regarding bleeding. "We Dominicans seldom bleed patients nowadays," he mused. "Lay physicians claim it stems from timidity or lack of skill, yet we have long known that simples alone can cure most ailments. Furthermore, remedies never kill in capable hands, whereas others strike like thunderbolts. Regarding blood, the Vulgate explicitly states it is the life of man. To be wiser than the All-Wise in medicine or law is to be a fool. Moreover, simples are mighty. Consider the four-legged creature that slays the poisonous snake; if bitten itself, it finds an herb potent enough to quell the venom, superior in strength and speed to any mortal remedy. Taught by this wisdom and guided by tradition, we still search for the virtues of the plants God scatters across the earth, some to nourish, others to heal. Only in desperate cases do we mingle heavenly with earthly virtue, steeping the hair or bones of a departed saint within the medicine to effect marvellous cures."

"Pray tell, Father, is it truly due to relics?" Gerard asked. "For Peter a Floris, a learned physician and no pagan, denies it stoutly." "What does Peter a Floris know? And what do I know?" Anselm countered gently. "I dare not claim we command the saints, nor can we dictate whether their blessed remains draw forth physical virtue. Yet I observe that a patient receiving such treatment with faith is often restored, as by a charm. Undoubtedly, faith plays a vast role in these healings. Thus it has always been: a sick woman whom Jewish physicians could not save merely touched Christ's garment and was healed instantly. Had she not touched that sacred cloth, she would never have recovered. If she had worn it without faith to her grave, I doubt she would have benefited at all. Yet we do ill to probe these mysteries too curiously. Everything around us demands faith. Exercise a little patience. Soon we shall understand everything. Meanwhile, I, your temporary confessor, strictly forbid you, under penalty of your soul's salvation, to listen to learned laymen on matters of religion. Their arrogance is their ruin; with it, they shut heaven's open door in their own faces. Be mindful, I speak of the learned laic. The unlearned have often been my teachers in humility and may become yours. Your wound is tended; in three days, it shall be but a scar." With words of blessing, wishing God speed and that the saints render Gerard as virtuous and joyful as he was thoughtful and kind, Father Anselm dismissed him.

Gerard lingered, hoping to delay departure for dinner in the refectory, but Anselm, with a faint trace of regret, informed him that he remained in penitential seclusion within his cell for the week. Before closing the door, Anselm held Gerard's head delicately in both hands and kissed his brow. Almost before the cell door swung shut, the monk retreated to his devotions. Gerard stepped out, chilled to the core by the stark solitude of monastic life, his heart heavy with sadness. "Alas!" he thought, "Here lies a kindly face I shall never behold again upon this earth; a gentle voice forever silenced from my ears and my heart. There is nothing but meeting and parting in this sorrowful world. Well-a-day! Well-a-day!" This melancholy reverie was broken by the arrival of a young monk who escorted him to the refectory. There, Gerard found several brethren seated at a table, while Denys stood rigid as a poker, scrutinized regarding the towns he must traverse. The friars pooled their knowledge, charting a safe route and marking every religious house along the path, which they presented to Gerard. After supper, the old monk carried Gerard to his cell, where they engaged in an eager conversation. Incidentally, Denys revealed the cause of his earlier theatrical display in the corridor. "You nearly fell into Brother Jerome's grasp. That was his cell." "Is Father Jerome a wicked man, then?" "A wicked man!" Denys crossed himself, shocked. "A saint, an anchor

Gerard felt a hunger for this promised land nearly as keen as Denys's. His companion, however, was a man whose patriotism bordered on the absurd; he sang the praises of Burgundy at every turn, using minor inconveniences to prove that "things were done better" across the border. Above all, he exploited Gerard's vanity regarding hygiene, promising the spotless linens of a polished nation. "Ask no more!" the Hollander would declare, his face contorted with disgust at the idea of bare bedsheets. "To think I have not slept in a naked bed since leaving home! When I look at their linen, I wish my eyes and nose were closed forever."

Denys carried this love of country to extremes. He once walked twenty leagues in shoes that had long since disintegrated rather than accept hospitality from a German peasant who might offer anything from poor civility to a subpar meal.

As the sun began to dip below the horizon, they stood equidistant between a roadside tavern and a monastery standing slightly off the path. Denys favored the inn; Gerard preferred the convent. Denys yielded, but on the strict condition that whenever they entered Burgundy again, they would stay exclusively at inns. Gerard agreed, for his chart listing the local monasteries had reached its final page. Turning off the main road, Gerard asked in genuine surprise why Denys suddenly despised the very houses that had fed and sheltered them without charge.

The soldier hesitated, stammering at first, before the dam broke. It transpired that this was not a sudden dislike, but an ancient, deep-seated horror he had suppressed until now, largely out of politeness. "I suspected they had put poison in your drink," Denys explained, his voice rising with indignation. "So I avoided them. But since this is our last stop, why not clear my conscience? I have lived like a fish out of water in all those great dungeons. You rise early with some old friar, so you never witness my purgatory."

"I beg forgiveness," Gerard said humbly. "I have been selfish."

"Nonsense, little one! Forgive me, not you. Art thou not the first fool to be rid of a priest and hit by a monk? But I shall not forgive *them* their misery." With the gravity of a prosecutor centuries before Henry VIII's commissioners, Denys laid out his indictment. These gloomy structures were all identical monuments to boredom. Inns varied, but here everything was a monotonous loop: a great gate, a small gate, endless steps leading to a shadowy cloister. There lay the dormitory, followed by the vast, freezing refectory where one sat in silence, forced to mumble words or risk speaking aloud.

"And then," Denys continued, pacing, "no one is truly human there; everyone is a slave to a petty, tinkling bell that never sleeps. If it were a trumpet blaring alarms, it might not freeze a man's heart quite so. *Tinkle, tinkle,* and you must sit to eat with perhaps no appetite. Before the food settles, *tinkle, tinkle!* and you must march to church with perhaps no spirit for devotion. I am not a hog at prayers, take my word for it. *Tinkle, tinkle,* and now you must lie to bed with your eyes wide open. By the time you manage to close them, some restless imp of darkness pulls the bell-rope, and *tinkle, tinkle!* forcing you to pray in the dark, knowing nothing of the liturgy. If they could hear the gibberish I muttered when they shattered my rest with those bells! Then you drift off, gaining perhaps a wink of sleep... and lo, *tinkle, tinkle* for matins."

"And the only clapper you respect is a woman's?" Gerard interjected, half-contemptuous.

"Because there is some music in that, even when she scolds," Denys retorted stoutly. "But worse is the constant checking. If I merely dip my finger in the salt-cellars, immediately I hear, 'Hast thou no knife wherein to touch the salt?' And if I dare wipe my blade on the cloth to save time, the accusation follows: 'Thou wipest thy knife dirty on the bread, and clean upon the cloth!' Oh, souls so small! These trivial pedantries fall like chilling iceicles upon good fellowship, melting slowly from strawen eaves."

"I hold cleanliness to be virtue, not pedantry," Gerard argued gently. "Thou shouldst learn better manners once and for all."

"Nay! It is they who lack manners. They stop a fellow's tongue at every breath."

"At every other word, you mean; specifically every obscene or blasphemous utterance."

"An exaggerator, go to! At the very last of those dungeons, I found poor travelers sitting chilled and silent around a single friar, looking like rogues awaiting execution. To lift their spirits, I simply shouted, 'Courage, *tout le monde*, *le diable*—"

"*Connu!* What happened next?" Gerard interrupted sharply.

"Marry, this. '*Blaspheme not!*' quoth the executioner. '*Plait-il?*' I asked. Did he not wheel and weep at me in some sort of Alsatian French, twisting every 'P' into a 'B'? I had much ado not to laugh right in his face."

"Being yourself unable to speak ten words of his tongue without a fault," Gerard teased.

"Well, all the world ought to speak French. What use are so many jargons except to build walls between men's hearts?"

"But what did he say?"

"What matters it what a fool speaks?"

"Oh, not all the words of a fool constitute folly, or I should not listen to thee."

"Well, then, he declared: 'Those who begin by playing freely with the Devil's name, yea, end by doing so with all the names in Heaven.' 'Father,' said I, 'I am a soldier, and this is but my *consigne*, my watchword.' 'Oh, then, it is but a custom?' he asked. Not seeing through the old fox, thinking to clear myself, I said, 'Yea, it was.' 'Then that is ten times worse,' he replied. 'It will bring about thine ears one of these days. He still comes where he hears his name often called.' Mark this! No gratitude for the tidings which neither his missals nor his breviary had ever taught him. Then, with a courtesy that belied his nature, he informed me that soldiers commonly commit the crimes for which all other men are broken on the wheel: murder, rape, and pillage."

"And is it not true?"

"Truth or falsehood, it was bad manners," Denys replied guardedly. "And so says this courteous host of mine: 'Being foes of mankind, why make enemies of good spirits too, by still shouting the names of evil ones?' and a host of similar drivel."

"Well, Denys, whether he heed thy advice or scorn it, why blame a man for raising his voice to save thy soul?"

"How can his voice save my soul when he keeps turning his Ps into Bs?"

Gerard was stunned. Before he could recover from this thunderbolt of Gallicism, Denys spun triumphantly off on another tangent, labeling all monks as hypocrites. "Look at them," he sneered. "How they creep about, unable to eye thee like honest men."

"Nay," Gerard countered eagerly, "that modest, downcast gaze is part of their discipline, known as *custodia oculorum*."

"Cursed toads eating *hoc hac horum*? Nonsense! That is how a cutpurse looks. A true man cannot meet thy eye. Doff thy cowl, monk, and behold a thief; don thy cowl, thief, and lo, a monk. Tell me not they will ever be able to look God Almighty in the face when they cannot even look a true man in the face down here."

He paused, staring into the distance as if hearing something invisible. "Ah, here it is, black as ink! Into the well we go, comrade. *Misericorde*, there goes the tinkle already. 'Tis the best of tinkles, though; 'tis for dinner: stay, listen! I thought so: the wolf in my stomach cried 'Amen!'" He confirmed this last statement with two oaths and marched forward like a victorious gamecock into the convent gates, believing by Gerard's silence that he had won the argument, entirely unaware of how profoundly he had repelled him.

Within the quiet sanctity of the refectory, conversation drifted inevitably toward the grave, specifically regarding the sudden demise of the monk tasked with transcribing the monastery's charters and rules. Seizing the moment with cautious humility, Gerard proffered his services. A hesitation rippled through the table, a subtle pause he misinterpreted as skepticism. "Nay, let it not be for hire, my lords," he hastened to assure them, "but for love alone. As a trifling return for countless nights of shelter bestowed upon this poor wayfarer by the brethren of your order."

A monk, pleased by the sentiment, smiled but gently interjected that the departed brother was a master of the pen, a craftsman whose skill could not simply be inherited or imitated by a novice. Undeterred, though his heart beat faster, Gerard produced from his tunic a vellum deed. He had carefully cleaned its reverse side and used it as a canvas for a sample of his script. When the monk laid eyes upon it, he leapt to his feet with a start, rushing up the long hall to the high table. Bending low, he touched the hem of his robe to the fifth step, then the tenth, presenting the document to the Prior with breathless enthusiasm.

Instantly, a dozen discerning eyes locked onto the page, and a hum of appreciation rose from the assembly. Soon, Gerard saw the Prior nodding repeatedly, pointing out features of the writing. The monk returned, his face glowing with pride, carrying a savory *crustade royale*—a game pie heavy with spices—and a silver grace cup brimming with rich pimentum for Gerard. Accepting the offerings, Gerard bowed deeply, first to the distant Prior, then to his companions, raised the cup, and circulated it around the table.

Before the liquid had settled, the entire table hailed him as a brother. "Art convent-bred?" they asked in unison. "Deny it not!" Gerard, relieved, acknowledged the truth, offering thanks to Heaven. Without this lineage, he felt he would have been as coarse and ignorant as Sybrandt and Cornelis. "But 'tis passing strange how you could know," one monk remarked. Two others spoke simultaneously, explaining the trick: "You drank with the cup in both hands."

Their voices, previously subdued, swelled into a roar of mirth so obstreperous and prolonged that the Prior dispatched the Sub-Prior down the hall to quell the disturbance and impose penance on the riotous monks. Gerard's cheeks burned with shame; amidst the chaotic laughter, his ear had caught the distinct word "courage!" echoing alongside the trumpet-like tones of Denys of Burgundy.

Later, Gerard was installed in Feu Werter's cell, illuminated by wax lights, equipped with a tilting frame and a full array of caligraphic tools. Yet the work proved too vast for a single evening. The dilemma arose: how to ask Denys, the sworn enemy of monks, to remain longer? Gerard approached him, submitting to whatever decision Denys wished. To his delight, Denys replied graciously, "A day's rest will harm neither of us. Thou shalt write, and I will pass the time as I may."

Gerard's labor was vastly admired. They concurred that the monastery's records had been grievously impoverished by poor Werter's untimely death. The Sub-Prior pressed a rix-dollar into Gerard's hand, along with several brushes and colors from the convent's ample stock. Warm-hearted and buoyed by this omen, Gerard resolved that his pen alone must forge his fortune and help him recover his beloved. "Come, Denys," he said, his tone good-natured, "see what the good monks have given me. Now, strive to be fairer to them. For, to speak plainly, it chilled my friendship for a fleeting moment to hear thee call my benefactors 'hypocrites.'"

"I recant," Denys declared instantly.

"Thank you! Thank you, good Denys!" Gerard exclaimed.

"I was a scurrilous vagabond."

"Nay, nay, say not so!" Gerard protested warmly.

"But we soldiers are rude and hasty. I give myself the lie, and I offer those I misunderstood all my esteem. 'Tis unjust that thousands should be defamed for the hypocrisy of a few."

"Now thou art reasonable," Gerard observed. "Thou hast pondered what I said?"

"Nay, it is their own doing," Denys shrugged.

Gerard chuckled, for men delight in being proven right, and listened intently as Denys recounted the genesis of his conversion. "Well then, at dinner on the first day, a young monk beside me opened his jaws and laughed right out, a musical, boisterous sound. 'Good,' said I, 'at last I have fallen on a man and not a shorn ape.' To test him further, I slapped his broad back and delivered my salute. 'Heaven forbid!' cries he. I stared. For the fellow looked as sad as Solomon; a better mime hath never been seen, even at a Mystery play. 'I see war is no sharpener of the wits,' said he. 'What are the clergy for but to fight the foul fiend? And what else are the monks for?'

'The fiend being dead,
The friars are sped.'

'Thou mayst plough up the convents,' he continued, 'and we poor monks shall have naught to do—but turn soldiers, and so bring him to life again.' Then there was a great laugh at my expense. 'Well, thou art the monk for me,' said I. 'And thou art the crossbowman for me,' quoth he. 'And I'll be bound thou couldst tell us tales of the war that should make our hair stand on end.' 'Excusez! The barber hath put that out of the question,' quoth I, and then I had the laugh."

"What wretched ribaldry!" Gerard murmured pensively.

The candid Denys immediately admitted he had witnessed merrier jests hatched with less cacophony. "'Twas a great matter to have got rid of hypocrisy," he recalled. "'So,' said I, 'I can give you the chair of the rooster, if that may content thee.' 'That we will see,' was the cry, and a signal went round."

Denys then burst forth, glee shining in his eyes, describing how at bedtime he had been ushered not into the great dormitory but into a private cell, strictly forbidden to sleep. To aid his vigil, a book of pictures depicting a hundred merry adventures of monks pursuing the female laity was lent to him. In due course, he was led barefoot down to the parlour, where a supper fit for a Duke awaited—a feast of twelve jolly friars, the roaringest youths he had ever met in peace or war. How the stories, toasts, jests, and wine cups circulated! Some played cards with a gorgeous pack wherein Saint Theresa and Saint Catherine, adorned in gold, served as the four queens; black, white, grey, and crutched friars acted as the four knaves. They staked their very rosaries, swearing like troopers when they lost.

At midnight, a sly monk stole out, but he was cunningly followed by others into the garden, where he was seen thrusting his hand into the ivy and drawing out a rope-ladder. With this, he scaled the ten-foot wall, aided by a russet kirtle popping up from the outside world as quickly as he ascended. Thus began billing and cooing—a situation Denys found far more feline than ecclesiastical, eliciting from him the appropriate comment of a "mew!" The monks joined the musical chorus, and the lay visitor shrieked, greatly discomforted; but Abelard merely cried, "What, are ye there, ye jealous miauling knaves? Ye shall caterwaul to some tune tomorrow night. I'll fit every man-jack of ye with a fardingale!" It was this brutal threat that reconciled Denys to staying another day—at Gerard's request.

Gerard groaned.

Meanwhile, unable to disconcert such a brazen monk, and the damsel beginning to whimper, they danced caterwauling in a circle, bestowing a solemn benediction on the two wall-flowers before retreating to the parlour. There they found a pair lying dead drunk, and two others affectionate to tears. They promptly carried off the inanimate ones, dragged the loving and lachrymose ones kicking and screaming into their cells.

"And so, shut up in measureless content," Denys concluded.

Gerard was disgusted and voiced his displeasure.

Denys chuckled and proceeded to recount how the following day he and the young monks had drained the fish ponds, secreting immense quantities of pike, carp, tench, and eel for their own use. Furthermore, in the dead of night, he had been taken shoeless by crooked paths into the chapel, a ghost-like, dark place, and then down steps into a crypt beneath the floor. Suddenly, paradise had burst upon him.

"'Tis there the holy fathers retire to pray," Gerard inserted solemnly.

"Not always," Denys corrected. "Wax candles by the dozen were lit, and princely cheer! Fifteen *soups maigre*, with marvelous twangs of venison, grouse, and hare within them, and twenty different fishes—for it was Friday—cooked with wondrous art. Each dish flanked by two buxom lasses, and each

They encountered the host in the hallway.

"Welcome, gentlemen," he said, removing his cap with a low bow.

"Come, we are not in Germany," Gerard remarked.

Inside the public room, they found the proprietress, a robust woman of forty. She curtsied to them and smiled warmly. "Take the trouble to sit, fair sir," she said to Gerard, dusting two chairs with her apron—a gesture unnecessary given the state of the furniture.

"Thank you, dame," Gerard replied. Internally, however, he thought, "This is a polite nation. The trouble of sitting down? I shall endure it with singular patience. And shortly thereafter, the labor of eating, the toil of digestion, and finally, by Hercules's aid, the strain of retiring to bed, and the struggle of sinking fast asleep."

"Why, Denys, what are you doing? Ordering supper for only two?"

"Why not?"

"What? Can we dine without waiting for forty others? Burgundy forever!"

"Aha! Courage, camarade. Le dia—"

"C'est convenu."

Salic law seemed to have missed the French inns entirely. In this establishment, wimples and kirtles reigned supreme; doublets and hose were few and feeble. The landlord himself wandered aimlessly, eternally tipping his cap to patrons out of absent-minded habit, while the women, as they passed, shoved him aside silently, as one brushes aside a live twig in a rushing forest.

A maid brought in supper, and the mistress followed her, empty-handed.

"Fall to, my masters," she said cheerily. "You have but one enemy here; and he lies under your knife." (I suspect this was a formula.)

They ate. The mistress drew her chair closer to the table, providing company as well as food. She gossiped genially with them like old acquaintances. Once this formality was complete, the busy dame departed, sending in her daughter, a beautiful young woman of about twenty, who took the vacant seat. She lacked the elder's breadth and geniality but possessed a gentle, cheerful nature. Upon learning the distance the poor boy had traveled and the journey he must undertake, she displayed a womanly tenderness for Gerard. She lingered nearly half an hour. When she left, Gerard observed, "Is this an inn? Why, it is like home."

"Qui fit Francois il fit courtois," said Denys, bursting with gratified pride.

"Courteous? Nay, Christian; to welcome us like home-guests and old friends—us vagrants, here today and gone tomorrow. But indeed, who deserves pity and kindness more than the weary traveler far from his folk? Hola! Here is another."

The newcomer was the chambermaid, a woman of about twenty-five, with a cocked nose, a large laughing mouth, and a sparkling black eye. Her bare arm was stout, if not particularly shapely.
