The moment she entered, one of the travelers made a somewhat free jest at her expense; instantly, the entire company roared at his cost, so swiftly had her practiced tongue accomplished its business. Just as, in a fencing match between a novice and a master, foils clash—the novice is pinked. On this, another, then another, must break lance with her. But Marion planted her great arms upon her haunches and held the whole room in play. This country girl possessed in perfection that rude, ready humor which looks mean and vulgar on paper but carries all before it when spoken: not wit's rapier, but its bludgeon. Nature had done much for her, and daily practice in an inn completed the rest.

Yet shall she not be photographed by me, but feebly indicated: for it was just four hundred years ago, the raillery was coarse, she returned every stroke in kind, and though a virtuous woman, said things without winking which no decent man of our day would utter even among men.

Gerard sat gaping with astonishment. This was to him almost a new variety of *homo*. He whispered, "Denys, now I see why you Frenchmen say 'a woman's tongue is her sword,'" just then as she levelled another assailant. The chivalrous Denys, to console and support "the weaker vessel"—the iron kettle among the clay pots—administered his consigne, "Courage, ma mie, le—" etc.

She turned on him directly. "How can he be dead as long as there is an archer left alive?" (General laughter at her ally's expense.)

"It is 'washing day,' my masters," she said, with sudden gravity.

"After?" a peevish old fellow by the fireside objected. "We travellers cannot strip and go bare while you wash our clothes." He had kept mum until now but crept out into the sunshine of commonplaces.

"I aimed not your way, ancient man," replied Marion superciliously. "But since you ask me"—here she scanned him slowly from head to foot—"I trow you might take a turn in the tub, clothes and all, and no harm done" (laughter). "But what I spoke for, I thought this young sire might like his beard starched."

Poor Gerard's turn had come; his chin crop was thin and silky.

The loudest of all the laughers this time was the traitor Denys, whose beard was of a good length, and singularly stiff and bristly; so that Shakespeare, though he never saw him, hit him in the bull's eye.

"Full of strange oaths, and bearded like the pard."

Gerard bore the Amazonian satire mighty calmly. He had little personal vanity. "Nay, 'chambriere,'" said he, with a smile, "mine is all unworthy your pains; take you this fair growth in hand!" and he pointed to Denys's vegetable.

"Oh, time for that, when I starch the besoms."

Whilst they were all shouting over this palpable hit, the mistress returned, and in no more time than it took her to cross the threshold did our Amazon turn to a seeming Madonna, meek and mild.

Mistresses are wonderful subjugators. Their like, I think, breathes not on the globe. Housemaids, decide! It was a waste of histrionic ability, though; for the landlady had heard, and did not at heart disapprove, the peals of laughter.

"Ah, Marion, lass," said she good-humouredly, "if you laid me an egg every time you cackle, 'L'es Trois Poissons' would never lack an omelet."

"Now, dame," said Gerard, "what is to pay?"

"What for?"

"Our supper."

"Where is the hurry? Cannot you be content to pay when you go? Lose the guest, find the money, is the rule of 'The Three Fish.'"

"But, dame, outside 'The Three Fish' it is thus written--'Ici-on ne loge'"

"Bah! Let that flea stick on the wall! Look hither," and she pointed to the smoky ceiling, which was covered with hieroglyphics. These were accounts, vulgo scores; intelligible to this dame and her daughter, who wrote them at need by simply mounting a low stool and scratching with a knife so as to show lines of ceiling through the deposit of smoke. The dame explained that the writing on the wall was put there to frighten moneyless folk from the inn altogether, or to be acted on at odd times when a non-paying face should come in and insist on being served. "We can't refuse them plump, you know. The law forbids us."

"And how know you mine is not such a face?"

"Out fie! It is the best face that has entered 'The Three Fish' this autumn."

"And mine, dame?" said Denys; "dost see no knavery here?"

She eyed him calmly. "Not such a good one as the lad's; nor ever will be. But it is the face of a true man. For all that," added she drily, "an I were ten years younger, I'd as lieve not meet that face on a dark night too far from home."

Gerard stared. Denys laughed. "Why, dame, I would but sip the night dew off the flower; and you needn't take ten years off, nor ten days, to be worth risking a scratched face for."

"There, our mistress," said Marion, who had just come in, "said I not t'other day you could make a fool of them still, an if you were properly minded?"

"I dare say ye did; it sounds like some daft wench's speech."

"Dame," said Gerard, "this is wonderful."

"What? Oh! No, no, that is no wonder at all. Why, I have been here all my life; and reading faces is the first thing a girl picks up in an inn."

Marion. "And frying eggs the second; no, telling lies; frying eggs is the third, though."

The Mistress. "And holding her tongue the last, and modesty the day after never at all."

Marion. "Alack! Talk of my tongue. But I say no more. She under whose wing I live now deals the blow. I'm sped--'tis but a chambermaid gone. Catch what's left on't!" and she staggered and sank backwards onto the handsomest fellow in the room, which happened to be Gerard.

"Tic! tic!" cried he

"Where are the righteous men?"

"Here stand we. God bless ye all!"

A sudden rush surged toward the staircase, where six sturdy, yet kindly, hands extended to grasp the rescuers warmly.

"You've saved our lives, lads," cried Denys, his voice thick with emotion. "You've saved us tonight."

Before the eyes of the rescued pair, the scene unfolded as a vision from hell. The chamber blazed with torchlight, illuminating the gleaming breastplates of the archers and their bronze-complexioned faces. Beside them lay the pale, bound forms of the thieves and the massive corpse of the giant, spilled in its own gore.

Gerard moved slowly around the line of soldiers, shaking hands with each one, his eyes glistening with unshed tears. In return, they all bowed their heads to kiss him. Turning to a handsome archer of similar age, Gerard spoke softly, "Pray, good soldier, watch over me. A heavy drowsiness overtakes me. See that no one cuts my throat while I sleep—for pity's sake."

The archer laughed, assuming Gerard jest, and promised faithfully. Almost immediately, Gerard sank into a deep slumber.

Denys observed this silence with surprise but refrained from interference; it served his immediate purpose. Nearby, two archers were examining the Abbot's lifeless form, kicking it gently aside to inquire, "Which of the two cast this enormous rogue from the upper story?" They sought to understand the mechanics of the fall.

At first, Denys scoffed, unwilling to boast. He feared that the young vagabond would later claim it was the finger of Heaven, making him look a fool. But seeing Gerard unconscious, Denys decided to speak.

"Well, comrades, you see," Denys began, his tone shifting to a rough, storytelling cadence, "my sword pierced this one right through to the hilt. Others swarmed about me, so I needed my blade or I perished. Thus, I planted my foot against his belly, gave a sharp tug with my hand, leaped, and sent him flying to kingdom come! He dropped in mid-air, and his carcass rolled onto you without ceremony—making quite a jump, I warrant. But pikewives and pillage! What use is chatter once the spoils are gone? Let us drink, friends! *Buvons*, camarades!"

The archers noted that saying "drink" was easy where no liquor existed, though doing so was harder.

"Nay, I shall find you drink quickly," Denys declared. "My nose has a natural alacrity for scenting wine. Follow me, and I shall lead the way with this torch!"

They departed the room, descending a short flight of stone steps into a vast, low cellar. It smelled close and dank. The walls were encrusted here and there with what appeared to be cobwebs, but proved to be saltpeter oozing from the damp stones and crystallizing.

"Oh! the fine moldy smell," murmured Denys. "In such places, good wine still lurks. Advance your torch."

"Diable!" exclaimed Denys suddenly. "What is that in the corner? A pile of rags?"

"No," replied the torch-bearer, stepping closer. "'Tis a man."

They gathered around the flickering flame. Lo! a figure crouched on a heap in the corner, pale as ash and shivering violently.

"Why, it is the landlord," Denys stated.

"Rise, thou craven heart!" shouted an archer.

"Why, men, the thieves are bound, and we who bound them are thirsty. Rise! Show us your wine; I see no bottles here."

"What, are the rascals bound?" the pale landlord stammered. "Good news... Wine? Yes, I will, honest sirs."

He rose with unsteady joints, offering to guide them to the wine cellar. But Denys interposed sharply. "You are all in the dark, comrades. This man is in league with the thieves."

"Alack, good soldier, I in league with the accursed robbers! Is that reasonable?"

"The girl said so anyway."

"The girl! What girl? Ah! Curse her, traitress!"

"Well," interrupted another archer, "the girl is not here; she fled to the bailiff. So let the burghers decide if this coward is guilty or not. We caught him not in the act, so let him draw us our wine."

"One moment," said Denys shrewdly. "Why did he curse the girl? If he were a true man, he would bless her as we do."

"Alas, sir!" sobbed the landlord. "I live only by my good name. I cursed her because you said she had lied about me."

"Humph! I trow thou art a thief, and where is the thief who cannot lie with a smooth face?" Denys commanded. "Seize him, comrades. A prisoner may draw wine even if his hands are bound."

The landlord offered no objection, instead hoping they would pay for whatever they drank, noting his rent was overdue by two months.

The archers smiled grimly at his simplicity. One laid a quiet, firm hand on his shoulder, while the other, bearing the torch, stepped forward.

As they reached the threshold, Denys cried, "Halt!"

"What is it?"

"Here be bottles in this corner. Advance your light."

The torch-bearer approached. Having just removed his scabbard, he probed the heap where the landlord had crouched.

"Nay, nay!" cried the landlord. "The wine is in the next cellar. There is nothing here."

"Nothing is mighty hard, then," said Denys. Drawing his hand from the heap, he produced something.

It was merely a bone.

Denys tossed it onto the floor; it rattled harshly.

"There is naught there but the bones of the house," insisted the landlord.

"Just now 'twas nothing," Denys countered. "Now that we have found something, 'tis nothing but bones. Here is another. Humph? Look at this, comrade, and you, bring the smooth knave along."

Philippe, the torch-bearer, held the bone to the light, turning it over and over.

"Well?" asked Denys.

"Well, if this were a battlefield, I'd say 'twas a man's shankbone. No more, no less. But 'tis not a battlefield, nor a churchyard; 'tis an inn."

"True, mate; but that knave's ashen face shines brighter than any field of battle. I read the truth by it. Bring that face nearer, I say. When the chine is missing and the house dog cannot look at you without his tail creeping between his legs—who was the thief? Good brothers, my mind misgives me. The deeper I dig, the more I find. Mayhap if these bones could speak, true men's flesh would creep."

"Alas! Young man, what hideous fancies are these!" protested the landlord. "These are bones of cattle, sheep, and kids, not men and women. Holy saints preserve us!"

"Hold thy peace!" Denys roared. "Thy words are air. Thou hast not got burghers by the ear, those who know not a veal knuckle from their grandsire's ribs. These are soldiers-men seeking their fallen comrades, only to find their bones picked clean by crows—and perhaps by thee and thy mates. Men and women, saidst thou? And prithee, when did I speak of women's bones? Thou wouldst make a child suspect thee. Field of battle, comrade! Was not this house a field of battle half an hour ago? Drag him close, let me read his face."

Denys thrust a small object suddenly into the landlord's face.

"Alas! I know not."

"I would not swear either—but it looks too like the thumb bone of a man's hand. Mates, my flesh creeps. Churchyard! How do I know this is not one?"

Denys drew his sword and began raking the heap of earth, broken crockery, and bones across the floor.

The landlord assured him he wasted time. "We poor innkeepers are sinners," he pleaded. "We give short measure and baptize the wine; we are fain to do these things because the laws are so unjust. But we are not assassins. How could we afford to kill our customers? May Heaven's lightning strike me dead if there be any bones save those used for meat. 'Tis the kitchen wench who flings them here. I swear by God's holy mother, by Saint Paul, by Saint Dominic, and by Saint Denys, my patron saint—"

"Ach!"

Denys held a bone steadily before the landlord's eye in dead silence. It was a bone no man, however ignorant or lying, could confuse with those of sheep or oxen. The sight silenced the lying lips and paralyzed the heartless heart.

The landlord's hair rose on his scalp like spikes, and his knees buckled as if his limbs had been severed. The archers dragged him upright under the glare of the torch, staring fascinated at the dead skull, white as the living cheek opposite it, glaring back at its murderer. The assassin's pale lips opened and closed, but no sound escaped.

"Oh!" said Denys solemnly, trembling with rage. "Look upon the sockets from which thou hast plucked the eyes, and let them blast

Gerard opened his eyes to find Denys gazing upon him with a mixture of relief and lingering worry. "It is you still asleep!" Denys exclaimed, glancing toward the window where the midday sun beat fiercely against the glass. "'Tis already high noon."

"It was a benediction," Gerard replied softly, pressing a hand to his brow. "Methinks Heaven itself bestowed this slumber upon me. It has drawn a curtain, thick and white, between me and that dreadful nightmare. To sit here, breathing freely, you and I, safe and sound—it feels like a miracle. The vision I saw... it was so terrible."

"Ay, lad, that is the proper way to view such terrors once they have passed," Denys advised gently, pouring water from a pitcher. "They are but shadows, fleeting phantoms. Eat thy breakfast, and soon enough, thou shalt speak of naught else. Furthermore, I promised to escort thee into the town before the hour strikes, to present thyself to His Worship."

Gerard dipped a slice of coarse rye bread into a bowl of deep red wine, consuming it with a grateful fervor to banish the last dregs of his hunger. Then, under Denys's guidance, they traversed the battlefield where the struggle had unfolded. As they walked, Gerard shivered, his gaze darting nervously through the hedgerows, imagining ambushes where there were none, until they finally reached the outskirts of the little town.

Denys steered him into an inn known as "The White Hart."

"No need to fear the knife-wielders here," Denys assured him, leaning against the doorframe. "I have known this host for many a year. He is a burgess, likely the bailiff himself. I intended to seek shelter here last night, but time slipped away, and darkness overtook us before I could secure a room—and..."

"And yet, a woman stood at the threshold," Gerard interrupted, his voice rising with emotion. "She proved far wiser than any Jeanneton; she told us the danger was a lie."

"Why, what other salvation did we have if not her?" Denys pressed, his eyes wide. "Yes, she risked her own life to save ours."

"That is truth, Denys," Gerard affirmed, clutching his heart. "Though I hold women in little regard usually, I long to thank this poor soul, to offer her a reward, nay, I would give all I possess, every penny in my purse, just to see her face again. Dost thou not feel the same?"

"By God's bones, yes," Denys muttered.

"But where can we find her?"

"Perhaps the alderman knows," Denys suggested. "We must pay him a visit first."

The alderman received them with a countenance so peculiar and unreadable that it seemed almost theatrical. Yet, after a brief pause, his features softened into a grim, knowing smile, and he began to question Gerard in detail, committing every answer to memory. Once the interrogation concluded, he declared that they must remain within the town walls until the thieves were brought to trial, warning them that failure to appear would incur heavy fines and imprisonment.

Both men stared at him, bewildered by the severity of the order.

"However," the alderman continued, "time shall be short. The culprits were caught red-handed." He added casually, "And let me remind thee, in any event, thou couldst not depart this place this week."

Denys gazed at the old man with a furrowed brow, while Gerard smiled inwardly, amused by what he perceived as the antiquated gentleman's naive belief that a provincial town in Burgundy possessed any charm capable of detaining a pilgrim bound for Rome and destined for Margaret.

Now, driven by a singular impulse, Gerard turned his attention to the matter nearest both their hearts.

"Your Worship," he said earnestly, "we cannot locate our benefactress within these streets."

"Nay, but who is thy benefactress?" the alderman asked, his tone shifting from official to curious.

"The good maiden who approached thee by night," Gerard answered, his voice trembling slightly with gratitude. "She saved our lives at the peril of her own. Sir, our hearts burn within us to thank and bless her. Where might we find her?"

"In prison, sir?" The constable seemed surprised, scratching at his chin. "Good lack, for what misdeed?"

"A witness, and a necessary one," the alderman replied, his voice carrying a distinct satisfaction.

"Why, Messire Bailiff," interjected Denys, stepping forward, "you lay not all your witnesses by the heels, I trow."

Pleased by the title, the alderman grew communicative. "In cases of blood, we detain all testimony likely to give us leg bail and thus defeat justice. That is why we still keep the women folk. A man may hide in one mind for a week, but a woman, if she does her duty to the realm on Friday, shall undo it before Sunday comes around. Could you see that wench now, you would find her a-blubbering at having betrayed five males to the gallows. If they had been females, we might have trusted to a subpoena; they despise one another and show some sense. But now, thinking on it, there were other reasons for laying this one aside. Hand me those depositions, young sir." He adjusted his spectacles. "Ay! She was implicated; she was one of the band."

A loud denial erupted simultaneously from Denys and Gerard.

"No need to deafen me," said the alderman, waving a hand dismissively. "It is written in black and white. 'Jean Hardy (who is one of the thieves), being questioned, confessed that—humph? Ay, here it is. And that the girl Manon was the decoy, and her sweetheart was Georges Vipont, one of the band, and hanged last month. And that she had been dejected ever since, and openly blamed the band for his death, saying if they had not been rank cowards, he had never been taken, and it is his opinion she did but betray them out of very spite.' "

"His opinion!" cried Gerard indignantly. "What signifies the opinion of a cutthroat, burning to be revenged on her who has delivered him to justice? And if you follow that line, what avails his testimony? Is a thief never a liar? Is he not always a liar? And here is a motive to lie! Revenge—it is the strongest of all passions. Oh, sir, what madness to question a detected felon and listen to him lying away an honest life—as if he were a true man swearing in open daylight with his true hand on the Gospel!"

"Young man," said the alderman, his tone hardening. "Restrain thy heat in presence of authority! I find by your tone you are a stranger. Know then that in this land we question all the world. We are not so weak as to hope to get at the truth by shutting either our left ear or our right."

"And so you would listen to Satan belying the saints!"

"Ta! Ta! The law meddles but with men and women, and these cannot utter a story of nothing but lies, let them try ever so. Wherefore we shut not the barn-door against any man's grain. Only having taken it in, we do winnow and sift it. And who told you I had swallowed the thief's story whole like fair water? Not so. I did but credit so much of it as was borne out by better proof."

"Better proof?" Gerard looked blank. "Why, who but the thieves would breathe a word against her?"

"Marry, herself."

"Herself, sir? Did you question her too?"

"I tell you we question all the world. Here is her deposition; can you read? Read it yourself, then."

Gerard glanced at Denys and read him Manon's deposition.

"I am a native of Epinal. I left my native place two years ago because I was unfortunate: I could not like the man they bade me. So my father beat me. I ran away from my father. I went to service. I left service because the mistress was jealous of me. The reason she gave for turning me off was because I was saucy. Last year I stood in the marketplace to be hired with other girls. The landlord of 'The Fair Star' hired me. I was eleven months with him. A young man courted me. I loved him. I found out that travelers came and never went away again. I told my lover. He bade me hold my peace. He threatened me. I found my lover was one of a band of thieves. When travelers were to be robbed, the landlord went out and told the band to come. Then I wept and prayed for the travelers' souls. I never told. A month ago my lover died.

"The soldier put me in mind of my lover. He was bearded like him I had lost. I cannot tell whether I should have interfered, if he had had no beard. I am sorry I told now."

The paper almost slipped from Gerard's hands. For the first time, he saw clearly that Manon's life hung by a thread. He knew the doggedness of the law and the men who executed it. With sudden desperation, he threw himself on his knees before the alderman. "Oh, sir! Think of the difference between those cruel men and this poor, weak woman! Could you have the heart to send her to the same death with them? Could you have the heart to condemn us to look on and see her slaughtered, who, but for risking her life for ours, had not now been in jeopardy? Alas, sir! Show me and my comrade some pity, if you have none for her, poor soul. Denys and I be true men, and you will rend our hearts if you kill that poor, simple girl. What can we do? What is left for us to do then but cut our throats at her gallows' foot?"

The alderman was tough, yet mortal. The prayers and agitation of Gerard first astounded him, then touched a hidden cord. He showed it in a peculiar manner, becoming peevish and fretful. "There, get up, do," he said. "I doubt whether anybody would say as many words for me. What ho, Daniel! Go fetch the town clerk."

On the functionary entering from an adjoining room, the alderman continued, "Here is a foolish lad fretting about yon girl. Can we stretch a point? Say we admit her to bear witness, and question her favorably."

The town clerk, a man of "impossibility," shook his head. "Nay, sir, we cannot do that. She was not concerned in this business. Had she been accessory, we might have offered her a pardon to bear witness."

Gerard burst in. "But she did better! Instead of being accessory, she stayed the crime; and she proffered herself as witness by running hither with the tale."

"Tush, young man, 'tis a matter of law."

The alderman and the clerk then engaged in a long discussion, the one maintaining, the other denying, that she stood as fair in law as if she had been accessory to the attempt on the travelers' lives. This proved lucky for Manon. Irritated by the clerk reiterating his impossibilities, the alderman declared he would show him he could do anything he chose. He summoned Manon. Upon the landlord of "The White Hart" acting as her bondsman, and Denys depositing five gold pieces, and the girl promising, not without some coaxing from Denys, to attend as a witness, he liberated her. To ease his conscience, however, he explained his leniency in his own terms.

"The town had to buy a new rope for everybody hanged, and present it to the bourreau, or compound with him in money: and she was not, in his opinion, worth this municipal expense, whereas decided characters like her late confederates were."

Thus, Denys and Gerard carried her off. Gerard danced round her for joy, Denys keeping up her spirits by assuring her of the demise of a troublesome personage, and she wept inauspiciously. On the road to "The White Hart," the public found her out. Having heard the whole story from the archers, who naturally told it warmly in her favor, they followed, hurrahing and encouraging her until, backed by numbers, she plucked up her heart. The landlord, seeing at a glance that her presence would draw custom, received her politely and assigned her an upper chamber. Here she buried herself, and being alone, rained tears again.

Poor little mind, it was like a ripple, up and down, down and up, up and down. Bidding the landlord be very kind to her and keep her a prisoner without letting her feel it, the friends went out. Lo! As they stepped into the street, they saw two processions coming towards them from opposite sides. One was a large one, attended with noise and howls and those indescribable cries by which rude natures reveal at odd times that relationship to the beasts of the field and forest which at other times we succeed in hiding. The other, very thinly attended by a few nuns and friars, came slow and silent.

These were the prisoners going to exposure in the marketplace, and the gathered bones of the victims coming to the churchyard.

And the two met in the narrow street nearly at the inn door, and could not pass each other for a long time. The bier, that bore the relics of mortality, got wedged against the cart that carried the men who had made those bones what they were, and in a few hours must die for it themselves. The mob had not the quick intelligence to be at once

The condemned criminals were ordered to stand pinioned in the marketplace for two hours, exposed to the gaze of the populace, so that any who recognized them for other crimes might testify against them at their trial. They endured the full duration, yet no fresh accusation arose against them. This was unsurprising, for they were creatures of the night—vampires preying in the shadows upon weary travelers, mostly strangers.

However, as they were being removed, a terrifying scream pierced the crowd. A woman pointed a trembling finger at one of the men, her eyes wide with horror. Before she could speak, she collapsed, fainting instantly. Men and women surged around her, torn between aiding her and satisfying a morbid curiosity. As consciousness returned to her, frantic conjectures erupted among the onlookers. "It was him!" "No, it was this one!" "Nay, nay, it was the hangdog with the hair hanging round his neck!"

These speculations were cut short. The poor creature, recovering her senses, lunged at the man identified as the culprit like a lioness. "My child! Man! Man! Give me back my child!" she cried, seizing the glossy golden hair the officers had tied around his neck. With desperate strength, she tore it from him and buried her face in it, kissing it feverishly. Then, as her confused mind cleared, she realized the tragic truth: the girl was dead. She sank to the ground, shrieking and sobbing over the severed locks, until the crowd swarmed toward the assassin with a savage, unified growl.

His life had ended swiftly; in those days, everyone carried a weapon at their girdle. But Denys drew his sword immediately, shouting, "A moi, camarades!" (To me, comrades!), and held the mob at bay. "Who touches him dies!" he commanded. Other archers backed him, and with difficulty they protected him from the frenzied throng, while Denys appealed to the rioters for restraint.

"What sort of vengeance is this?" he asked, his voice ringing above the din. "Would you be so mad as to rob justice, to give this vermin an easy death?"

Though the mob remained passive due to the archers' steel rather than Denys's words, they growled with flashing eyes. Seeing this, the municipal officers gathered, formed a guard with the archers, and prudently escorted the accused back to jail. The mob hooted at them and the prisoners indiscriminately. Once the inmates were safely lodged, Denys headed for "The White Hart," where he expected to find Gerard.

On the way, he spotted two girls working at a first-floor window. He saluted them, and they smiled. Conversation ensued; their manners were easy, their complexions high. He invited them to dinner at the inn. They initially objected, then acquiesced. In this charming society, Denys momentarily forgot his concern for poor Gerard, who, meanwhile, had been arrested.

On the way to jail, Gerard suddenly stopped, regaining his presence of mind. He demanded to know by whose authority he was detained. "By the vice-baillie's," replied the constable.

"The vice-baillie? Alas! What have I, a stranger, done to offend a vice-baillie? For this charge of sorcery must be a blind. No sorcerer am I; I am a poor, true lad far from home."

This vagueness disgusted the officer. "Show him the *capias*, Jacques," he ordered. Jacques held the writ out, about a yard and a half from Gerard's eye, while the large constable pinned Gerard down. Both officers were on tenterhooks, fearing the prisoner might snatch the document, to which they attached superstitious importance.

But the poor prisoner had no such thought. He craned his neck, reading it, and to his infinite surprise discovered that the vice-bailiff who had signed the writ was the friendly alderman. Taking courage, he assured his captor there must be an error and demanded to be taken before the alderman.

"What say you to that, Jacques?"

"Impossible. We have no orders to take him before his worship. Read the writ!"

"Nay, but good kind fellows, what harm can it be? I will give you each an ecu."

"Jacques, what say you to that?"

"Humph! I say we have no orders *not* to take him to his worship. Read the writ!"

"Then say we take him to prison round by his worship."

Agreed. They accepted the money and bade Gerard observe they were doing him a favor. He saw they desired gratitude as much as silver. He tried to satisfy their cupidity, but feigning was not his forte.

Entering the alderman's presence, Gerard's heart hammered in his throat. He begged with a faltering voice to know what offense he had committed since leaving the room with Manon and Denys. "Nought that I know of," said the alderman. Upon seeing the writ, he explained he had signed it at daybreak. "I am growing old, and my memory fails me: I forgot your offense entirely, thinking only of the girl... but I remember now. All is well. You are the one I committed for sorcery. Stay! Ere you go to gaol, you shall hear what your accuser says: run and fetch him."

The man could not find the accuser immediately. Impatient, the alderman told Gerard the main charge was that he had set a dead body ablaze with diabolical fire that flamed but did not consume. "And if 'tis true, young man, I'm sorry for thee, for thou wilt assuredly burn with fire of good pine logs in the market-place of Neufchâteau."

"Oh, sir, for pity's sake let me have speech with his reverence the cure."

The alderman advised against it. "The Church was harder upon sorcerers than was the corporation."

"But, sir, I am innocent," Gerard insisted, his voice shifting between snarling and whining.

"Oh, if you think you are innocent..." The officer was dismissed to escort Gerard to the cure, but instructed to ensure he did not escape.

They found the cure in his doublet, repairing a wheelbarrow. Gerard told him everything, appealing piteously. "Just for using a little phosphorus in self-defense against cut-throats, they are going to hang."

Lucky for the magician, he had already told his tale fully to the cure, who thus held the stick at the right end while the corporation held it by the ferule. The cure looked exceedingly grave, stating, "I must question you privately on this untoward business." He took Gerard into a private room, bidding the officer stand outside and guard the door. The big constable stood quaking, expecting the room to vanish in a stink of brimstone.

Instantly alone, the cure's countenance unlocked, and he became himself again. "Show me the trick on't," he said, his curiosity piqued.

"I cannot, sir, unless the room be darkened."

The cure quickly closed out the light with a wooden shutter. "Now, then."

"But on what shall I put it?" Gerard asked. "Here is no dead face. 'Twas that made it look so dire." The cure groped about the room. "Good; here is an image: 'tis my patron saint."

"Heaven forbid! That were profanation."

"Pshaw! 'twill rub off, will't not?"

"Ay, but it goes against me to take such liberty with a saint," objected the sorcerer.

"Fiddlestick!" said the divine.

"To be sure, by putting it on his holiness, it will show your reverence it is no Satanic art."

"Mayhap 'twas for that I did propose it," said the cure subtly.

Encouraged, Gerard fired the eyes and nostrils of the image, making the cure jump. Then he lit up the hair in patches and set the whole face shining like a glow-worm's.

"By'r Lady," shouted the cure, " 'tis strange, and small my wonder that they took you for a magician, seeing a dead face thus fired. Now come thy ways with me!"

He put on his grey gown and great hat, and in a few minutes they found themselves before the alderman. By his side stood the accuser, a singular figure in red hose and shoes, a black gown with blue bands, and a cocked hat. After saluting the alderman, the cure turned to the accuser, Mangis, saying good-humoredly, "So, Mangis, at thy work again, babbling away honest men's lives! Come, your worship, this is the old tale! Two of a trade can ne'er agree. Here is Mangis, who professes sorcery, and would sell himself to Satan tonight, but that Satan is not so weak as to buy what he can have gratis. This Mangis, who would be a sorcerer, but is only a quacksalver, accuses of magic a true lad, who did but use in self-defense a secret of chemistry well-known to me and all churchmen."

"But he is no churchman, to dabble in such mysteries," objected the alderman.

"He is more churchman than layman, being convent-bred, and in the lesser orders," said the ready cure. "Therefore, sorcerer, withdraw thy plaint without more words!"

"That I will not, your reverence," replied

Outside the town, the snow lay fresh and untouched save where countless wolves had trampled paths through it. Every foot of the road bore witness to their passage, a chaotic trail of destruction.

"We did well to heed the old man's counsel, Denys."

"Aye, indeed. As I recall now, I heard them last night scurrying beneath our window, howling and whining for man's flesh in that market square. Yet not a single fat burgher showed pity, nor dropped a crust to the poor vagabonds."

Gerard offered a smile, though it carried an air of distant abstraction. They trudged forward in silence, the crunch of snow the only sound accompanying their steps.

"What dost thou muse upon so deeply?" Denys asked eventually, breaking the quiet.

"Upon thy goodness."

Denys bristled immediately. Despite his usual good humor and capacity for bearing impertinence, he was prone to flare up where no cause for anger truly existed. "A civil inquiry deserves a civil response," he said dryly.

"Alas, I meant no offense," Gerard replied gently.

"Then why feign concern for my virtue when you know I possess no goodness beneath my skin?" Denys pressed, his tone sharp.

"Had anyone else spoken thus, I would have retorted, 'Thou liest.' But to thee, I say, 'Thou hast no eye for men's qualities, save for those of women.'" Gerard raised his chin, defying the warrior's unreasoning choler. "I was thinking of thy kindness shown last night. Thou wouldst have married me to the 'Tête d'Or,' or rather to the 'Golden Head,' and left thyself solitary."

"Oh, art thou there, lad?" Denys recovered his geniality in a heartbeat. "Truly, I spoke not of goodness, but of friendship and true fellowship. Let me confess, my young master, that my conscience pricks me even now for allowing thee to turn thy back upon fortune and peaceful days. A truer friend might have steered thee differently. If I had acted sooner, thou mightest have lain smarting at the 'Tête d'Or' for a month or two; that skittish lass would have nursed thee tenderly, and all would have been well. I had the blade in hand to perform this act, but remembering how thou hatest pain, even a mere scratch, my craven heart failed me at the critical moment." Denys wore a look of humble apology for his lack of virtuous resolve when the path of duty had lain so clear.

Gerard arched his brows in astonishment at this monstrous yet thoroughly characteristic revelation. This new, delicate point of friendship—whether one ought to sever the heel-tendon of a beloved friend in times of crisis—was never to be discussed. An incident intervened before the debate could deepen.

"Behold one riding behind us upon a neighbor's mule!" Denys shouted.

Gerard turned. "And how knowest it is not his own?"

"Oh, blind fool! Because he rides it with no discretion."

In truth, the man approached galloping like a fury. But what startled the friends most was that upon reaching them, the rustic rider's eyes widened saucer-like. He jerked the reins so suddenly and powerfully that the mule stumbled, extending her forelegs and sliding between the pedestrians like a four-legged table on castors.

"I trow ye are from the 'Tête d'Or,'" the stranger called out. They assented. "Which of ye is the younger?"

"He that was born later," Denys winked at his companion.

"Glad to hear the news."

"Pray, divine then!"

"And I shall. Thy beard is ripe, thy fellow's is green; he shall be the younger. Here, youngster." He extended a paper packet. "Ye left this at the 'Tête d'Or,' and our mistress sends it unto thee."

"Nay, good fellow, methinks I left naught." Gerard felt within his pouch, finding it empty.

"Wouldest thou make our burgess a liar?" the rustic reproached, his voice dripping with indignation. "Shall I receive no *pourboire*?" He demanded it again, even more sharply, noting how he had arrived *ventre à terre*.

"Nay, thou shalt have *pourboire*," Gerard said, producing a small coin.

"*À la bonne heure*!" cried the clown, his features beaming with disproportionate joy. "May the Virgin go with ye! Come up, Jenny!" With a final "stomach to earth," he departed.

Gerard untied the packet. It measured roughly six inches square. Inside, he discovered another packet, which contained yet another, and so on. By the fourth layer, frustration overwhelmed him, and he hurled the entire bundle into the snow. Denys retrieved it, rebuking his petulance. Gerard excused himself on the grounds of hating affectation.

Denys attested, "'The great toe of the little daughter of Herodias'—there was no affectation here, but only woman's good wit. Doubtless the wraps concealed something which, out of delicacy or her sex's lovely cunning, she would not wish her hind to see bestowed upon a young man; thy garter, to wit."

"I wear none."

"Her own, then? Or a lock of her hair? What is this? A scrap of raw silk, fresh from the worm. Well, of all the love tokens!"

"Now who but thee ever dreamed she was so base as to send me love tokens? I saw no harm in her—save for her hands."

"Wait, here lies something hard lurking in this soft nest. Come forth, I say, little nestling! Saints and pikestaves! Look at this!"

It was a gold ring featuring a great amethyst, glowing and sparkling, fully colored yet pure as crystal.

"How lovely!" Gerard exclaimed innocently.

"And here is writing; read it, for I do not read as glibly as some when I know not the matter beforehand."

Gerard took the slip of paper. "'Tis a posy, and fairly written." He read the lines, blushing like a schoolgirl. They were naive, perhaps thus translated into English:

*Come back to 'the Hedde of gold.'"

"The little dove!" Denys cooed.

"The great owl! To risk her good name thus. However, thank Heaven she has played this prank with an honest lad who will never expose her folly. But oh, the perverseness! Could she not bestow her nauseousness upon thee?" Denys sighed and shrugged. "Upon thee that art as ripe for folly as herself?"

Denys confessed that his young friend had echoed his very thoughts. It was passing strange to him that a damsel with eyes in her head should pass by a man to bestow her affections upon a boy. Still, he could not but recognize in this the bounty of Nature. Boys were human beings after all; but for this occasional caprice of women, their lot would be too terrible—they would remain outside the sun, blighted and never coming to anything. Since only the fair could fashion a man out of such unpromising materials as a boy. Gerard interrupted this flattering discourse to beg the warrior-philosopher's acceptance of the lady's ring. Denys refused it flatly, insisting instead that Gerard return to the "Tête d'Or" at once, ring and all, like a man, and not allow a poor girl to hold out her arms in vain.

"Her hands, you mean."

"Her hand, holding the 'Tête d'Or.'"

Failing to persuade him otherwise, Denys considered placing the ring upon his friend's finger. Gerard declined. "I wear a ring already."

"What, that sorry gimcrack? 'Tis pewter, or tin at best: and this virgin gold, besides the jewel."

"Aye, but 'twas Margaret gave me this one; and I value it above rubies. I'll neither part with it nor give it a rival," he declared, kissing the base metal and bidding it fear naught.

"I see the owl hath sent her ring to a goose," Denys murmured sorrowfully. Nevertheless, he prevailed upon Gerard to fasten it inside his bonnet. To this, indeed, the lad consented very readily. For sovereign qualities were universally ascribed to certain jewels, and the amethyst ranked high among these precious talismans.

Once this matter was settled, Gerard earnestly requested his friend to let the subject drop. Speaking of the other sex to him made him pine so desperately for Margaret, and nearly unmanned him with the thought that each step took him farther from her. "I am no general lover, Denys. There is room in my heart for one sweetheart, and for one friend. I am far from my dear mistress; and my friend, but a few leagues hence, and I must lose him too. Oh, let me drink thy friendship pure while I may, and not dilute it with any of these stupid females."

"And shalt, honey-pot, and shalt," Denys said kindly. "But as to leaving thee at Remiremont, reckon thou not on that! For—" (three consecutive oaths)—"if I do. Nay,

Travel holds a peculiar allure for the English soul, regardless of whether the wanderer seeks holy shrines or mere adventure. Even ancient friendships possess a strange fascination, akin to fossils preserved in stone. Yet, since the true heart of our tale beats within Holland, it is time to return there—to those ordinary lives and everyday struggles that have shaped humanity across the ages.

Jorian Ketel arrived at Peter's house determined to claim Margaret's promise, only to find her confined to a fever-stricken bed. Upon learning his purpose, Peter, inflamed by public sentiment, rebuffed him with sharp disdain and ordered him from the premises. Those standing nearby, swayed by the crowd's undiscriminating fury, threatened to dunk the intruder. Both father and daughter were beloved figures, and the entire town buzzed with gossip regarding their plight. Thus, Jorian departed in a sullen mood, regretting his previous kindness. Such repentance, born of anger rather than genuine remorse, is not uncommon.

Meanwhile, Dierich Brower, caught attempting to buy information about Martin Wittenhaagen from a drunken man at "The Three Kings," was seized and hurled into a horse pond. Threatened with further punishment if he ever returned, he was effectively silenced. Simultaneously, municipal jealousy flared in Sevenbergen. The burgomaster dispatched a formal letter to his counterpart in Tergou, accusing Gerard of trespassing and demanding any future complaints be lodged locally.

Ghybrecht Van Swieten, the cunning mastermind behind these machinations, suppressed his rage. He replied politely, claiming the fugitive was merely a traveler named Gerard from Tergou who had stolen town records before fleeing abroad. Having lost track of Gerard, Ghybrecht feigned closure, masking his growing unease. However, his composure was a thin veil over mounting dread. Baffled by the failure to locate Gerard in Sevenbergen, he secretly dispatched emissaries to Tergou to trace the boy's movements. To his infinite frustration, they returned empty-handed. This silence terrified him; he feared Gerard was lurking nearby, waiting for the perfect moment to strike back. Consequently, Ghybrecht transformed into a moody, irritable man, his eyes darting nervously as if expecting an unseen blow. The misery he had inflicted upon others offered him no solace.

Back at the modest household in Tergou, the atmosphere had shifted irrevocably. Though outward appearances remained unchanged, the simple happiness that once filled the home had vanished. Little Kate knew the extent of Cornelis and Sybrandt's role in banishing Gerard. While she held her tongue to prevent further discord, she often shuddered at their presence, wincing at their hypocritical expressions of regret. Catherine, exercising a mother's vigilance, observed Kate's distress without speaking aloud, silently piecing together the truth. The "black sheep" themselves, acting out of guilt, succeeded in convincing Father Giles and their father of their contrition, yet the demure satisfaction hidden beneath their feigned sorrow betrayed them to the keen feminine insight of the women.

"Their actions, though seemingly innocent, spoke volumes," Catherine reflected internally.

Mistrust and suspicion now dominated the dinner table, serving as grim substitutes for Gerard's vibrant presence, which had once illuminated the circle. The old hosier, wounded by his son's disobedience, maintained a stoic exterior, refusing to utter Gerard's name. Yet, beneath his Spartan facade, Nature pulled relentlessly at his heartstrings. His greatest anxiety was not his pride, but Gerard's safety. "If I but knew where the boy is, and that his life and health are secure, my cares would be few," he would sigh deeply, his voice trembling with unspoken love. Perhaps, if Gerard had simply walked through the door at that moment, the old man would have embraced him with tears, offering forgiveness rather than reproach.

Publicity, however, proved to be the most surprising development. Within a week of Gerard's departure, his exploits became the talk of every street corner. Popular sympathy rallied fiercely behind the lovers, condemning Gerard's "cruel" parents and the meddling burgomaster who dared interfere in matters that concerned no one.

"Mother," Kate whispered, "everyone knows Margaret is down with a severe fever. Her father is terrified."

"Margaret?" Catherine asked, feigning calm. "Which Margaret?"

"Oh, Mother!" Kate exclaimed. "Gerard's Margaret."

"Gerard's Margaret!" Catherine shrieked, the color draining from her face. "How dare you utter that name in this house? That hussy has ruined everything. She is the cause of my boy's exile, preventing him from becoming a priest among us. She has left me a desolate, broken-hearted widow. Do not weep, my child; I speak harshly because I bear this grief alone. At night, I dream of him, plagued by horrors—wild beasts tearing at him, robbers raising their cruel knives to strike his pale face. If I recall correctly, while I sit here in relative comfort, perhaps he lies dead in some savage land, all because of that girl. Her name is poison to me; I tremble whenever I hear it."

"I shall say nothing to hurt you further, Mother," Kate murmured tenderly, though a sigh escaped her lips.

Outside, the world treated Margaret with pity. Young men and maids exchanged sympathetic glances as they passed her window, whispering of the "beauty of the village dying for love." They underestimated her unyielding spirit and selflessness. Gerard lived, and she trusted implicitly in his constancy. Her father was helpless, and her love for him compelled her to fulfill her duties despite her frailty, though she performed them with a bowed head and a heavy heart. Physical exhaustion had triggered a violent fever, leaving her utterly debilitated. Though the illness eventually receded, it left her in a terrifying state of weakness, where life felt like a burdensome weight.

In moments of such fragility, love and friendship become angelic presences, bringing comfort through gentle voices and healing touches. For many days, Margaret lay alone, the hours rolling over her like leaden waves. Existence seemed a futile struggle, a shadow of what it had been. Lacking Gerard, she had no reason to strive for recovery. She would lie motionless for hours, tears streaming silently down her cheeks.

One morning, awakening from a restless sleep, she found two visitors in her chamber. One was a servant; the other, distinguished by thick fur collars and sleeves, was clearly a woman of status. A narrow strand of silvery hair escaping her coiffure revealed she was well past the age when sensible women hid their years. Their expressions were kind and warm. Margaret attempted to rise, but the older lady gently pressed a hand to stop her.

"Lie still, sweetheart. We have not come to tire you, but to comfort you, God willing. Cheer up, and tell us, who do you think we are?"

"Nay, madam," Margaret replied, her voice weak. "Though I have never met you, you are the Demoiselle Van Eyck, and this is Reicht Heynes. Gerard spoke often of you and your kindness. Madam, he has no friend like you near him now." As the thought crossed her mind, she sank back into the pillows, fresh tears filling her eyes.

The kindly Reicht Heynes began to weep for company, but her mistress chided her softly. "You are a charming companion for a sickroom," she remarked, employing innocent wit to lift the patient's spirits. With remarkable skill, the elderly woman, who had witnessed life's full spectrum of trials, soothed and engaged Margaret. Within an hour, Margaret rested her head on the stranger's shoulder, her eyes reflecting gentle gratitude.

"Alice!" the old lady exclaimed, tracing the strands of hair. "Look at this, Reicht!"

Reicht approached, admiring the locks sincerely. The poor girl, hardly able to endure flattery, blushed. "Truly, madam, I used to find it hideous. But he praised it, and ever since, I have been almost vain of it, saints forgive me. You know how foolish those who love become."

"They are greater fools than those who do not," the old lady countered sharply.

Margaret lifted her eyes, searching for the meaning behind the words. This visit marked the beginning of a series of daily calls from Margaret or Reicht, which continued until the patient fully recovered. Under their tender care, Margaret improved rapidly. Reicht credited the progress to nutritious dishes prepared in Peter's kitchen, but Margaret herself recognized the deeper healing power of the kind words and encouraging gazes that reminded her she had friends to live for.

Elsewhere, Martin Wittenhaagen marched directly to Rotterdam, intent on confronting the issue head-on. His adversary was a bipedal figure crowned with royal horns: Philip the Good, Duke of Burgundy. Finding the court currently seated at Ghent, Martin sought an audience but was initially dismissed by pages and lackeys. Undeterred, he intercepted the Duke while hunting, boldly breaking court protocol to initiate a conversation by loudly begging for mercy.

"Why, where is the peril, man?" the Duke laughed, surveying his surroundings.

"For mercy from an old soldier hunted down by burghers!" Martin cried.

Kings, like common folk, vary in temperament, yet they share a tendency to be affable toward those of low station. These subjects pose no threat of rivalry, amusing the monarchs with their bluntness and novelty, refreshing the court with a touch of raw nature rarely seen in palaces. Philip the Good reined in his horse, granting Martin a private audience. Reminded of a

Some grow weary of the world before their fortieth year, while others retain the vigor of youth until eighty. Yet Margaret Van Eyck's heart remained perpetually green, burning with a youthful fervor for her young namesake. This affection was no fleeting whim; Margaret Van Eyck possessed a sharp insight into character, recognizing in Margaret Brandt a quality rare among women: sincerity. Beyond this inherent virtue, the younger girl enjoyed a powerful ally within the older artist's bosom.

Human nature operates on a peculiar paradox, one that puzzled the ancient Greeks but which the bard of Britain immortalized in verse. We warm to those whom we have served rather than to those who serve us. Consider the memories that haunt the warrior's mind: standing shoulder to shoulder against the tide of battle, shielding a friend's breast; sharing the bleak deserts of Darien where death stalks the evening wind; throwing one's cloak over a fallen companion to face the deadly dew alone; rescuing a friend from a foundering ship amidst breaking waves, only to suck the venom from an arrow wound. These recollections do not stem from gratitude for past kindnesses received, but from the indelible imprint of duties fulfilled.

Margaret Van Eyck had indeed been extraordinary in her kindness. She had broken through her solitary habits to visit the sickbed, nursing and soothing the invalid with a tenderness that surpassed all earthly medicines. Consequently, her heart opened fully to the recipient of her charity. Though her initial visits were motivated by love for Gerard, her devotion to Margaret now eclipsed even that, surpassing the depth of feeling she had ever harbored for Gerard himself.

When Margaret Brandt presented the roses blooming on her cheeks and the slip of parchment that caused them, the elder lady yielded her original plans without a murmur. "Sweetheart," she said gently, "I wished him to remain in Italy for five or six years, returning wealthy and a master artist. But your happiness comes first. Since you cannot live without him, we must hasten his return."

"Oh, madam!" the young woman replied, hanging her head and blushing. "But how shall I let him know? My skill fails me. He has departed for Italy, though I know not his destination. Wait! He listed the cities he would visit—Florence and Rome." Then, sensing the impracticality of sending a vague letter labeled "To Gerard, Italy," which might easily go astray, she cast an imploring gaze at her friend.

"You have arrived at the right place and time," the old lady declared. "Hans Memling was here today; he departs for Italy no later than next week, ostensibly to 'improve his hand.' I assure you, it is not premature."

"But how shall he locate Gerard?"

"Why, he knows Gerard intimately," the old artist explained. "They have dined together more than once, bound as hand and glove. Since their trade is identical, they will traverse the same paths and inevitably meet. Therefore, compose a long letter, copy this pardon into it, and I vouch for the messenger. Within six months, at the latest, Gerard shall receive it. Upon receipt, he will kiss it, press it to his heart, and fly home. What is this smile? And why do your cheeks burn? Why do you clutch me? Ah, yes! Happy days approach for my little pearl."

Meanwhile, in the kitchen, Martin sat before his black-jack, Reicht Heynes spinning beside him. The night proved eventful for Reicht; she interrogated him relentlessly.

Hans Memling, an old pupil of Jan Van Eyck and his sister, was a painter by profession—a fact Margaret's sneer did not negate—and a man of considerable integrity, save for one flaw. His passion for ale, wine, and strong beer exceeded all reason. This singular predilection prevented him from accumulating wealth and frequently compelled him to seek sustenance from Margaret Van Eyck, occasionally even a groat. Yet, this dependence forged a bond; she knew he would honor any commission she entrusted to him.

The letter was penned and delivered to Margaret Van Eyck the following week, alongside a piece of gold for Hans's travel expenses. He appeared eager to depart.

"All the better," remarked the old artist. "He will reach Italy the sooner."

However, just as horses may burn with restless energy to start, only to require a whip after the first few yards, so Hans's urgency cooled into inertia once he reached the principal inn at Tergou. There, two of his boon companions sat in the bay window. Entering for a farewell drink, Hans initially insisted on paying, but they refused, declaring they would treat him; everyone must treat him, including the landlord.

The result of this hospitality loosened Hans's tongue as if filled with oil. Amidst boasts of showing the Italians how to paint, tales of his martial exploits with a pike, and exaggerated accounts of his romantic conquests, he inadvertently revealed the contents of his mission. He confessed he carried a letter to a townsman of theirs, Gerard, calling him a good fellow. "You are all good fellows," he added, slapping Sybrandt on the back with such force as to nearly expel his breath.

Sybrandt, circling the table to evade this muscular greeting, listened intently, learning for the first time that Gerard had traveled to Italy. Seeking certainty, he feigned disbelief. "My brother Gerard is never in Italy."

"Ye lie, ye cur!" Hans roared, instantly turning irascible and failing to recognize that his opponent was the same man he had just praised and struck. "If he is ten times your brother, he is in Italy. Read the superscription!" He flung the letter onto the table.

Sybrandt examined it gravely but claimed inability to read it. However, a member of the company, boasting a rare accomplishment, took the document and read aloud: "To Gerard Eliassoen, of Tergou. These by the hand of the trusty Hans Memling, with all speed."

"'Tis excellently well writ," observed the reader, scrutinizing every character.

"Ay!" Hans boomed. "Small wonder! It is written by a famous hand—that of Margaret, sister of Jan Van Eyck. Blessed and honored be her memory! She is an old friend of mine." Hans then diverged into forty disparate topics.

Sybrandt slipped away to join Cornelis. Together, they pondered the news. Italy was an immense distance. Could they keep him there? "Keep him there?" Cornelis scoffed. "Nothing would keep him long from his Margaret." "Curse her!" Sybrandt cried. "Why didn't she die when she was about it?" "She die? She would outlive the plague to vex us," Cornelis retorted, furious at her refusal to succumb to death for their benefit.

These two black sheep, infecting one another with malice, eventually hatched a scheme to keep Gerard in Italy forever, thereby securing his share of their father's inheritance. Yet, planning required no talent, execution demanded it. Iniquity stalled until, as if Satan whispered into their ears simultaneously, they both shouted, "THE BURGOMASTER!"

They approached Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, who received them eagerly. Suspense, he knew, was intolerable. "You have news of Gerard?" he asked. They recounted the letter and Hans Memling. He listened with restless eyes. "Who wrote the letter?" "Margaret Van Eyck," they replied, assuming the content matched the signature. "Are you sure?" Ghysbrecht moved to a drawer, producing a paper written by Margaret Van Eyck during negotiations for her house. "Did she write like this?" "Yes," Sybrandt affirmed boldly.

"Good. Now, what would you have me do?" Ghysbrecht asked, his heart beating beneath a mask of feigned carelessness that bewildered them. They fumbled with their bonnets, stammering, hesitating, beating about the bush, gradually revealing their desire for a letter to detain Gerard in Italy—one to be substituted for the real one in Hans's wallet. As these rogues vacillated between respect for the burgomaster and suspicion that he was as crooked as they, a storm raged within Ghysbrecht. Vengeance urged Gerard to return and taste the law's weight. Prudence argued for keeping him a thousand miles away. But prudence also questioned why perform dirty work on a doubtful chance? Why empower these knaves to tarnish his name? Furthermore, Ghysbrecht suspected Gerard held a secret that could wound him deeply. Caution and vindictiveness shaped his response: "It is my duty to aid citizens who cannot write. But for the matter itself, I assume no responsibility. Tell me, then, what I shall write."

"Something concerning this Margaret," Sybrandt suggested.

"Ay, ay!" Ghysbrecht declared. "That she is false, that she is married to another—I will bail."

"Nay, burgomaster, nay!" Sybrandt cried. "Gerard would not believe it, or only half-believe it, and then he would return to verify. Say instead that she is dead."

"Dead! What, at her age, will he credit that?"

"Sooner than the other. She was nearly dead; thus, it is not a downright lie."

"Humph! And you think this will keep him in Italy?"

"We are sure of it, are we not, Cornelis?"

"Ay," Cornelis agreed. "Our Gerard will never leave Italy now that he is there. It was always his dream to get there. He would return for his Margaret

Denys, positioned squarely between his companions to prevent any desperate flight, was borne away in a torment of grief and regret. Because of him, Gerard had forsaken the direct German road to Rome; and what recompense awaited him? To be stranded utterly alone in the heart of Burgundy. This crushing realization drove Denys to madness, causing him to rage against the heavens and the earth, alternating with a silence so bleak and ominous that his captors felt compelled to subdue him. They seized their leader just beyond the town limits, bringing the entire cavalcade to a halt at the "Tête d'Or" inn.

Though the young landlady was deeply preoccupied with the Count and far more so with the Bastard, her gaze fell upon Denys, and she asked anxiously what had become of his youthful companion. With a sudden outburst of sorrow, Denys recounted everything, imploring her to dispatch someone after Gerard. "Now that he is severed from me, he may perhaps heed my counsel," he pleaded. "Poor wretch, he cannot bear solitude."

The landlady gave a sharp shake of her head. "I believe I have already shown you kindness enough," she replied, flushing slightly.

"You will not?"

"Not I."

"And then"—and he unleashed a torrent of curses and vituperation upon her.

She turned her back on him, departing with whimpers and complaints that she was unaccustomed to such treatment, before ordering her servant to saddle two mules. Denys rode north with his troop, silent and dejected over his saddle, unaware that this truthful young woman had transformed herself into a rider within merely forty minutes. Driven by genuine urgency, she spurred away with her servant in the exact opposite direction.

By nightfall, after a grueling march, the Bastard and his men arrived at "The White Hart." Their entrance stirred a tremendous commotion, and it took some time before Manon spotted her old friend amidst the throng. When she finally did, her reaction betrayed itself only through flushed cheeks. She did not acknowledge the acquaintance openly. The poor soul seemed already to be hardening his heart, scorning the connection.

Denys saw her but could not smile. The inn brought memories of Gerard too vividly to allow for cheer. Before the night fully closed, the wind shifted. Manon entered the room and beckoned to him with a finger. He rose sullenly, followed by his guards.

"Nay, I wish to speak a word to thee in private."

She led him to a shadowed corner of the chamber and whispered urgently if he would perform her a kindness. He answered aloud, "No, I will not; I am not in the mood to bestow kindness upon man or woman. If I do grant a favor, it shall be to a dog—and not even that, unless necessity demands it."

"Alas, good Archer, I once aided thee and thy fair comrade," said Manon humbly.

"You did, Dame, you did; well then, for his sake—what is required?"

"Thou knowest my tale. I have known better fortune. Now I am honored, yet a woman has hurled insults at me today regarding him, and thus it shall remain until he hangs there. I desire him removed; alas!"

"With all my heart."

"And no other dares I beseech save thee. Wilt thou do it?"

"Not I, even were I not a captive."

Upon this stern refusal, the gentle Manon sighed, pressing her palms together in despair. Denys assured her not to worry. There were soldiers of a lesser caliber who would not hesitate to take two pieces of silver. It was a simple transaction of coin; if she could procure two angels, he would locate two men to perform the dirty work of "The White Hart."

This proposition was not entirely palatable, yet Manon reflected that soldiers were transient birds, drinking deeply tonight to be silenced tomorrow. Softly, she agreed, "Send them to me. But pray, instruct them that this is for a friend of mine; let them not believe it is for me. I would sink into the earth; times have changed."

Denys secured warriors eager to win an angel apiece with such ease. He dispatched them and immediately dismissed the subject with contempt, sitting instead to brood over his lost friend. Soon, Manon and the warriors reached a mutual understanding regarding the operation. However, a question lingered: What of the body once removed? She murmured, "The river runs near—the spot."

"Fling him in, then?"

"Nay, nay; be not so cruel! Might ye not lay him gently—with some heavy stones?"

She must have pondered the logistics in depth, for she was not a mind given to swift ideas. All arrangements were swiftly concluded, barring the timing of payment. The armored men itched for their due and sought it in advance. Manon hesitated at this demand. Did she doubt their word? Then let her accompany them, or watch from afar.

"Me?" cried Manon in horror. "I would sooner die than witness it."

"Which yet thou wouldst have done."

"Ay, for sore is my need. Times have changed."

She had already forgotten her earlier admonition to Denys. An hour later, the disagreeable remnant of their caterpillar-like existence ceased to mar the respectable matron's public life, and the grim eyes of the past stopped casting malignant glares downward into the white hind's clover field. Her total tally stood at a sound average: she became the landlord's wife, a devoted house-hound, and outlived everyone around her.

Her troops, returning from executing the medieval axiom, "Away with the old love," received a jarring shock. They found the marketplace black with clusters of people, whereas it had been vacant an hour prior. Conscience struck them. Such trouble stemmed from meddling with the dead. Nevertheless, the bolder of the pair, emboldened by the darkness, slipped forward alone and stealthily merged into a crowd. He soon returned to his companion, speaking in a tone of reproach that was hardly reasonable:

"You born fool, 'twas nothing but a miracle."

The letters of flame scrawled across the church wall had asked, with an earnest and curious fervor, why no mass was being held for the Duke amidst this time of trouble. Many had witnessed this supernatural protestation before it slowly faded, leaving the crowd staring in stunned silence. Ultimately, the city council, unwilling to lag behind divine power in their loyalty to a temporal ruler, generously funded the required services. From this transaction, our old friend the Curé derived a handsome sum in hard coin—a fact he certainly appreciated given his discerning palate for wealth. Yet, I shall not detain you further with this trivial miracle.

Denys pleaded for his weapons. "Do not dishonor him as you break his heart?"

"Swear then, upon the cross of thy sword, to remain in the service of the bastard until the sedition is crushed." Yielding to necessity, he swore three solemn vows, reclaiming both his arms and his liberty.

The troops paused at "The Three Fish," where Marion, spotting him, exclaimed, "I am ruined! Who would have thought to see you again?" Seeing his somber countenance and noting that he appeared more pained than amused by her bluntness, she inquired what troubled him. He confided in her, and she adopted a bright, optimistic outlook. Gerard, she reasoned, was too handsome and well-mannered to suffer harm; moreover, the women would always support him. Things must eventually resolve themselves; if fortune smiled, he would join the companies bound for Rome, and if ill, he would return home, perhaps arriving before his friend, for he had fighting to do in Flanders by all accounts. She produced her gold pieces, steadfastly refusing to accept a single coin despite his repeated urging. Denys found her honesty refreshing, convincing him slightly of the honor of that world where his poor friend wandered alone.

Small detachments of foot soldiers, reduced to pairs or trios, were already straggling toward Flanders, many penniless, passing from town to town under the strict orders of bailiffs demanding food and lodging from innkeepers. Anthony of Burgundy overtook numerous stragglers, gathering them beneath his banner until he marched into Flanders at the head of six hundred men. Upon crossing the frontier, he was joined by his brother Baldwyn, bringing men, arms, and provisions. Anthony organized his forces and marched through several towns in full battle array, encountering not impediments but great acclamations. Such unbridled loyalty drew comments that were hardly gracious.

"This rebellion of ours is a joke," growled a soldier named Simon, who had chosen to be Denys's companion.

Denys remained silent, yet he made a private vow to Saint Mars to slay Anthony of Burgundy if the rebellion, having already cost him Gerard, proved to be anything less than genuine.

That afternoon, the vanguard spotted a heavily fortified town. A whisper rippled through the small army identifying it as a disaffected stronghold. However, when they drew near, the great gate stood wide open, and the flanking towers were guarded by but a single sentinel apiece. The advancing force, lulled by carelessness, broke formation. Within a furlong, however, the drawbridge across the moat rose slowly and groaning until it stood vertical against the fort. The precise moment it settled into this warlike posture, the portcullis crashed down, and the towers and curtains bristled with lances and crossbows.

A grim hum swept through the bastard's front rank and echoed to the rear.

"Halt!" cried Denys. The command traveled down the line, and they stopped. "Herald to the gate!" A pursuivant spurred forward, halted twenty yards from the entrance, raised his bugle, and blew a summons. A towering figure in brazen armor emerged over the gate. Fiery words, inaudible yet clearly understood, passed between him and the herald. The herald responded with a single, piercing, threatening note, galloped back with war in his eyes, and returned. The bastard stepped out to meet him, and within two seconds, he wheeled in his saddle and shouted, "Pioneers, to the van!" Instantly, hedges were leveled, the force cleared the field, and encamped just outside the range of the walls. Mounted officers scattered south, east, and west toward friendly towns, gathering catapults, palisades, mantelets, raw hides, tar barrels, carpenters, provisions, and every material necessary for a siege.

This bright prospect greatly cheered a despondent soldier. At the first clanging of the portcullis, his eyes lit up and his temples flushed. When the herald returned with battle in his gaze, the soldier realized it instantly. For the first time in many days, he cried, "Courage, everyone, the devil is dead." If the great warrior heard, he surely would have grinned.

The besiegers encamped a furlong from the walls and laid out roads. They kept their pikemen in camp, ready for assault, while sending forward their sappers, pioneers, catapultiers, and crossbowmen. These engineers opened the siege by filling the moat, mining, and breaching the walls. Since much of this labor had to be performed under heavy fire from arrows, quarrels, bolts, stones, and rocks, these artisans required a hundred eyes. They acted in concert with a vigilance, individual intelligence, daring, and skill that made a siege not merely a military endeavor, but a spectacle of interest, if not amusement, for observers.

Their first task was to advance their carpenters behind rolling mantelets to erect a high, strong stockade on the very edge of the moat. Some lives were lost, but not many; a strong contingent of crossbowmen, including Denys, rolled their mantelets forward and showered the workers with arrows whenever a besieged man showed his nose or exposed a loophole commanding their position. Protected by this condensed fire, they soon raised a high palisade shielding them from missiles fired from the pierced masonry.

Yet the besieged anticipated this stratagem. At night, they extended boards or wooden penthouses atop their curtains. These curtains featured square holes near the summit designed to receive supporting beams—the true defense of medieval forts. From these positions, the defenders delivered missiles with far greater freedom and variety of range than the besiegers could achieve through the oblique, immovable loopholes of the curtain or the sloping crenelets of the higher towers. In response, the besiegers brought up mangonels, hurling huge stones at these woodworks and battering them to ruins. Simultaneously, they constructed a triangular wooden tower as high as the curtain, keeping it ready for use just out of effective shot.

To the besieged, this was a terrifying sight. Wooden towers had captured many towns. They began mining beneath the section of the moat where the tower loomed, making other preparations for a fierce reception. The besiegers also mined at another point, aiming to undermine the square barbican and bring it crashing down. Throughout this operation, Denys remained behind his mantelet alongside another arbalestrier, protecting the workmen and delivering excellent shots. Eventually, these deeds earned him the esteem of an unseen archer who periodically sent a winged compliment quivering into his mantelet. One arrow struck within an inch of the narrow slit through which Denys was currently squinting.

"Peste!" cried Denys. "You shoot well, my friend. Come forth and receive my congratulations! Shall merit such as thine hide its head? Comrade, it is one of those cursed Englishmen, wielding his half-ell shaft. I will not die until I have had a shot at London Wall."

On the side of the besieged, a figure quickly attracted great attention by promenading under fire. It was a tall knight, clad in complete brass, carrying a light but prodigiously long lance, with which he directed the movements of the defenders. Whenever disaster befell the besiegers, this tall knight and his long lance were almost certain to be implicated.

"Young reader," you might ask, "why did not Denys shoot him?" Denys did shoot him; every day of his life, other arbalestriers shot him, archers shot him. Everyone shot him. He was there to be shot, apparently. The abomination was that he did not mind being shot. Worse still, he became so demoralized by the constant barrage that he seemed unaware when he was hit. He paced his battlements under fire, like a sturdy skipper walking his deck in a suit of Flushing, calmly oblivious to the April rains falling upon his woollen armor. Finally, the besiegers grew spiteful and refused to waste any more good steel on him, cursing him and his impenetrable coat of mail. He took those missiles like the rest.

Gunpowder has since spoiled war. War has always been detrimental to the solid interests of mankind, but in ancient times, it painted well, sang divinely, and furnished Iliads. Yet, invisible butchery under a pall of smoke a furlong thick leaves no one the better for it. A poet with his notebook may repeat, "Suave etiam belli certamina magna tueri"; but the sentiment is hollow and savors of a cuckoo. One cannot "view" anything but a horrid row. He did not say, "Suave etiam ingentem caliginem tueri per campos instructam."

The Middle Ages managed better.

This siege was a small affair, yet a writer or minstrel could witness it and earn an honest penny by singing it; thus, so far, the sport was reasonable and served an end.

It was a bright, clear day,

A profound silence settled over Margaret Brandt, transforming her spirit into something spectral. Her domestic labors were performed with the mechanical daze of a sleepwalker, yet beneath the surface, a storm raged. If Peter so much as raised his voice slightly, she would start violently, her gaze locking onto him with wide, terrified eyes. She retreated from the warmth of her friendship with Margaret Van Eyck, finding the old lady's affection overwhelming; instead of leaning into those embraces, Margaret shrank back, trembling and passive. Most striking of all was her refusal to venture outdoors during daylight. She only ventured forth after dusk, returning before dawn, avoiding even the early prayers at matins.

Peter, though typically oblivious, finally perceived the shift. "Why do you avoid the light, child?" he asked, sensing the weight behind her stillness.

"Methinks the folk all look at me," she whispered, the words hanging heavy in the air.

One afternoon, Margaret Van Eyck pressed gently, asking what burden weighed upon her heart. A look of sheer dread and a sudden flood of tears were her only response. The older woman, softening with compassion, urged her to share her sorrow.

"I have no sorrows, madam, but ones of my own making," Margaret stammered, her voice thin. "I am treated with kindness I do not deserve, especially within these walls."

"Then why not visit me more often, my dear?"

"I come oftener than I deserve," Margaret sighed, the sound breaking like dry leaves. "Reicht is crying for you," Mrs. Van Eyck interjected, pointing toward the nursery. "Go, child—what is troubling you?"

Mrs. Van Eyck, piecing together the fragments, suspected Margaret felt humiliated by the disdain Gerard's family showed her. Resolving to intervene, she donned her hood and cloak, taking Reicht with her, and marched directly to the hosier's house. Catherine received her with tearful gratitude for past kindnesses toward Gerard. However, when Mrs. Van Eyck attempted to pivot the conversation to explain Margaret's plight, Catherine's demeanor shifted instantly. Her eyes hardened, her lips compressed until they resembled a tight, unyielding line, and her expression took on the stubborn, ignorant cast of a brick wall. Despite Mrs. Van Eyck's gentle efforts, the barrier remained impenetrable. Provoked by this resistance, the old lady abruptly curtseied—a gesture that felt more like a dismissal than an honor—and turned away.
