Outside the door, Reicht encountered a pale girl seated on crutches. As Mrs. Van Eyck pushed past them, intending to send a scornful glance at the entire household, a small, delicate hand suddenly glided into hers. Turning, she met two eyes like doves', filled with water and seeking both gratitude and plea. In that complex, luminous gaze, Mrs. Van Eyck's anger dissolved. She knelt and kissed Kate's brow. "I see," she murmured. "Leave this to me."

Returning home, she recounted her journey to herself. "I visited a house today," she reflected, "where I witnessed a common stupidity and an uncommon grace. I saw an obstinate woman, and I saw an angel in the flesh. If I had my paints here, I would take down my brushes again to try and capture her face."

Kate did not betray the high regard her mother held for her. Waiting for the right moment, she revealed what she had learned from Reicht Heynes: that Margaret had given her very blood for Gerard in the woods.

"Look, Mother, how she loves him!" Kate insisted.

"And who would not love him?" Margaret replied coolly, recoiling from the emotional intensity.

"Oh, Mother, think of it! Poor thing."

"Ay, wench. She has her own troubles, just as we do. I cannot stand the sight of blood, let alone someone else's."

Though this point was conceded, whenever Kate pressed further, her mother cut her off. Then, nearly a month later, a soldier of the Dalgetty tribe returned from service in Burgundy, carrying a letter to the hosier's house. He delivered it to Catherine as the family sat at supper, declining any reward before departing for Sevenbergen.

The letter was unfolded. Though none of them possessed the skill to decipher the script, they all sensed immediately that it was Gerard's handwriting.

"And Father must be away," Catherine cried, her voice rising. "Are you not ashamed? Not one among you can read your brother's letter!"

Yet, though the words were hieroglyphics to their uneducated eyes, the meaning transcended language. There exists an art that speaks without ink, free from the limits of the pen, stealing straight into the heart and brain of the learned and the unlearned alike. It crosses seas and frontiers, losing nothing in translation, for it writes a universal tongue.

When they beheld this, a simple sketch drawn by Gerard between two short paragraphs, the truth leaped into view. It depicted two hands clasped together.

Gerard was bidding them farewell.

As they gazed upon the drawing, recognizing the familiar curve of his lines and the gentleness of his touch, Gerard seemed to stand before them, saying goodbye. The women wept openly until the vision blurred. Giles spoke softly, "Poor Gerard!" Even Cornelis and Sybrandt fell silent, overcome by a fleeting, crushing remorse.

They were desperate to understand the words, loath to display their ignorance or emotion to a stranger.

"The Dame Van Eyck?" Kate suggested timidly.

"And so I shall, Kate," Catherine agreed, her voice regaining strength. "She has a kind heart. She loved Gerard. She will be glad to hear news of him. I was sharp with her when she first came, but I will humble myself, and then she may tell me what my poor child wrote."

Kate hurried to Margaret Van Eyck's house. Reicht led her into a chamber, announcing briefly, "Wait a moment; she is at prayer."

Inside, a young woman sat pensively by the stove. Upon seeing the visitors, she rose gracefully to make way. "Thank you, young lady," Catherine said, warming her hands and casting a furtive, assessing glance from head to toe. The youth wore a plain wimple, yet her gown was trimmed with fur—a rare luxury in those times, signaling superior rank or wealth. What struck Catherine most, however, was the candor and modesty of the girl's face. She felt an immediate bond with such goodness and began to chatter.

"Now, what brings me here, young lady? A letter! A letter from my poor boy, far away in some savage land. I take shame to confess that none of us can read it. Pray, can you?"

"Yes," the girl replied simply.

"It is a great credit to you, my dear. I fear it will not be long before the post arrives, but every minute feels like an hour to a mother."

"I will read it to you."

"Bless you, my dear! Bless you!"

Driven by unfeigned urgency, Catherine failed to notice the suppressed, trembling eagerness with which the hand extended to take the letter, nor the slight shake of the fingers closing around it.

"Read it to me, pray," Catherine urged, her voice thick with anticipation. "I am wearying for it."

"The first words are, 'To my honoured parents,'" Kate read aloud.

"Ay! And he always did honour us, poor soul."

"'God and the saints have you in His holy keeping, and bless you by night and by day. Your one harsh deed is forgotten; your years of love remembered.'"

Catherine laid her hand upon her bosom and sank back into her chair, letting out a long, rending sob.

"Then comes this, madam. It speaks for itself: 'a long farewell.'"

"Ay, go on. You give me sorry comfort, yet it is comfort."

"'To my brothers Cornelis and Sybrandt—Be content; you will see me no more!'"

"What does that mean? Oh!"

"'To my sister Kate. Little angel of my father's house. Be kind to her—for poor Gerard's sake: since grief to her is death to me.'"

Kate choked on the final words. "That is Margaret Brandt, my dear... his sweetheart, poor soul. I have not been kind to her, my dear. Forgive me, Gerard!"

Nature, resenting the girl's struggle to maintain a facade of composure, suddenly broke the dam. She collapsed from her chair, falling insensible, the letter clutched in her hand, her head resting heavily against Catherine's knees.

Experience teaches a woman not to tremble when another falls, nor to rush to supernatural conclusions for a faint, but to treat it merely as a failure of the body, something she has witnessed often. Catherine moved with brisk efficiency. She laid the girl flat upon the floor, her head resting squarely on the boards, flung open the casement to let the raw air in, and loosened the bodice where the girl lay helpless. Only after these tasks were complete did she turn to the door and call out, her voice ringing with command:

"Come here, if you please."

Margaret Van Eyck and Reicht entered, finding their mistress prostrate and Catherine striking the girl's hands together.

"Oh, my poor soul! What mischief have you wrought?" cried Margaret, her face flushing with sudden anger.

"Myself?" Catherine replied, her tone sharp and defensive. "What is the matter, then?"

"Nothing, Madam," Catherine answered, though her eyes burned. "Nothing save what nature dictates in her present state."

"You speak with too much composure!" Margaret retorted, stepping forward. "And you, who are the architect of this wretched plight."

"I am not," Catherine declared bluntly. "Nor is any woman born."

"But surely it was you and your husband who drove them apart? Now he has fled to Italy alone. A 'situation' indeed! You have shattered her heart within your own walls."

"Why, Madam? Who else could it be? By Heaven's mercy! If I heard you right, I'd swear this was Gerard's own girl. But that cannot be. Look at this fur; it costs no less than five crowns per ell. Besides, this young lady is a wife, or ought to be."

"Certainly she ought. And who, pray, is the cause that she remains unmarried? Who stood before them at the very altar?"

"God forgive whomever it may be," Catherine said solemnly, her expression grave. "It was neither I nor my man."

"Well," the other woman said, her fury softening slightly, "now that you see her, perhaps you will not hold such bitterness against her, Madam. She is waking, thank God."

"Bitterness against her?" Catherine laughed softly. "That is long past. Poor creature! Trouble behind her, trouble ahead of her; yet to think I set her, of all living women, to read Gerard's letter aloud. Yes, that was why she fainted away, I swear it. She is coming to. What, sweetheart? Fear not; there is no enemy here, only friends."

They raised her gently into an easy chair. As color returned to her cheeks and lips, Catherine drew Margaret Van Eyck aside into a corner.

"Does she stay with you, if you please?"

"No, Madam."

"I would not have her return to Sevenbergen tonight, then."

"That rests upon her wish. She still refuses to spend the night."

"Ay, but you are older; you can persuade her. See, she begins to take notice."

Catherine leaned close, pressing her lips to Margaret Van Eyck's ear for a fleeting moment. It seemed insufficient time to whisper a single word, far less a sentence. Yet between women, secrets travel like lightning, carried by thought alone.

The old lady started back, her eyes wide.

"It is a lie! A slander! Monstrous! Look at her face. It is blasphemy to cast suspicion on such countenance."

"Tut! Tut! Tut!" Margaret snapped. "You might as well deny that hand is yours. I must know better, and I tell you it is true."

Then, to Margaret Van Eyck's utter shock, she rose and took the girl in both arms, kissing her fervently.

"I suffered for Gerard," Margaret wept, her voice breaking. "And you have shed your blood for him, I hear; his own words confess that I have been wrong, the very words you read to me. Ay, Gerard, my child, I have stood aloof from her, but I shall make it right the moment I begin. You are my daughter from this hour."

A second, warmer embrace sealed this impromptu pact, and the woman of impulse vanished.

Margaret sank back into her chair, a faint, weary smile touching her lips. Gerard's mother had kissed her and named her daughter. But the next instant, she saw her old friend regarding her with a troubled, vexed gaze.

"I wonder you let that woman kiss you."

"His mother!" Margaret murmured, half-reproachful.

"Mother, or no mother, you would not have allowed her to touch you if you had known what she whispered in my ear concerning you."

"Concerning me?" Margaret asked faintly.

"Aye, concerning you, whom she had never seen until tonight." The old lady hesitated, choosing her words carefully, preparing to share her indignation, when an unexpected interruption silenced her.

The young woman slid from the chair to her knees and began to pray piteously for forgiveness. In the cadence of her penitence and the humility of her posture, any bystander would have guessed that she had inflicted some cruel wrong, some intolerable insult, upon her venerable friend.

The room at the hosier's house, previously filled with the low murmur of a family gathering, fell abruptly silent as the mother entered. Her return carried a weight that silenced the men immediately seated around the table. With a gaze that pierced through the pretense of the gathering, she placed a single sheet of paper flat upon the wood. Though illiterate, she recited the contents of the letter from memory, tracing the ink with a finger that trembled slightly, feigning the ability to read the very words she had just heard spoken aloud. Then, her head snapped up, and a second, sharper scrutiny landed upon Cornelis and Sybrandt. Their eyes dropped instantly to the floor.

In that instant, the tempest which had been gathering within the household for weeks broke loose, drowning everything in its fury. Catherine, the matriarch, seemed to inflate before them like an enraged hen fluffing her plumage, her voice rising into a torrent of wisdom and nonsense, a chaotic mix of high-minded invectives and petty scolding that no man could possibly sustain in a single flow, let alone most women.

"I have harbored suspicions for years," she cried, her breath coming in ragged, rapid bursts, "that you kindled the fire between Gerard and your father, and set that old knave, Ghysbrecht, to stoke it. And now, here stands Gerard's own handwriting to confirm your treachery. You have driven your own flesh and blood across seas, stripping me of the darling who was the pride of my eye and the joy of my heart. Yet, you remain a perfect unit from stem to stern. When we were all lads, my other children brought comfort, but you were a plague—mischievous and cunning. It took a woman half a day merely to mend your garments, for why? Labor wears down cloth, but play tears it apart. Wisdom arrives with the beard, but it never reached you; you were still the last to retire and the last to rise. Why? Because honesty sleeps early, and industry wakes before dawn, whereas two idlers in a house breed a pair of failures. I have gazed upon your conduct and marveled at your origin; you bear neither your father's likeness nor mine—not even as a wasp resembles an ant. Surely you were swapped in the cradle, or perhaps a cuckoo bird deposited you upon my floor, for you possess neither our hands nor our hearts. Of all my offspring, none but you have mocked those whom God afflicted. While I turned my back, I heard you sneer at Giles for lacking stature, and at my flower-like daughter Kate for not being strong as a Flemish mare. After that, robbing a church would seem easy to you! In the sight of Him who created Kate and Giles, and in the sight of Mine, who suffered for them, you are nothing but paltry, unfeeling, treasonous curs!"

She paused, breathing heavily, then continued with relentless intensity. "No, I shall not stop, my daughter. The cup has been filled to the brim. It takes a mountain of stone to turn a mother's heart against the sons she has nurtured upon her knees. Many times I have closed my eyes and seen too much, biting back my tongue so that your father might not discover the truth and cast you forth like dogs. But now, the measure is full. And whence did you acquire this fortune? For the past month, you have rolled in wealth. You earned not a penny of it. May I never hear from another lip how you procured it! It began the night you stayed out so late, returning with a swollen head, Cornelis. Sloth and greed are a fatal pairing, my masters. Those who love money must either sweat or steal. If you plundered any poor soul, it must have been a woman, for I'd lay my life on it a man would drive you off with a bare hand. Be that as it may, it serves one purpose. It reveals how you would manage affairs if they ever passed into your keeping. I have watched you closely, my lads. Today, you squandered a groat between you, while I, who keep you all, good and bad, scarcely spend a groat per week. I abandon hope of seeing the shoes that might follow your coffins. This shop and this home shall never belong to you. Gerard is our heir—the poor Gerard whom you have exiled and done your utmost to destroy. From this hour, never address me as 'mother' again! But you have made him ten times dearer to me. My lost boy! Soon I shall behold him once more, hold him in my embrace, and seat him upon my knees. Yes, you may stare! You are too shrewd, yet not shrewd enough. You severed the stalk, but you left the seed—a seed that shall outgrow you and outlive you. Margaret Brandt is swift, and the child within her is Gerard's, and whatever belongs to Gerard is mine. I have prayed the saints for a son, and it shall be so—it must be. When I learned the truth, my bowels yearned over her unborn child as if it were my own. He is our heir. He will survive us. You will not; for a wicked heart within a rotting carcass is like the worm in the nut, swiftly bringing the body to dust. So, Kate, fetch down Gerard's bib and tucker from the drawer you know of. One day, we shall carry them to Sevenbergen. We will borrow Peter Buyskens' cart and go to comfort Gerard's wife beneath her burden. She is his wife. What power does Ghysbrecht Van Swieten hold? Can he stand between a couple and the altar, and sever those united by God and the priest? She is my daughter, and I am as proud of her as I am of you, Kate. As for you, keep clear of my sight for a time, for you are like the black dog that haunts my vision."

Cornelis and Sybrandt seized the subtle warning, slipping quietly from the room, their steps heavy with a mixture of remorse, impotence, and hatred. They avoided her gaze as diligently as ever. For many days thereafter, she said not a single word—neither kind nor cruel nor indifferent—to either of them. Her soul, finally, was free.

Catherine was a model of domestic industry, a woman who rarely ventured beyond her walls; yet whenever she did, chaos invariably ensued. Acutely aware of this pattern, she had postponed her trip to Sevenbergen for weeks, waiting for a break in her household duties. Finally, one afternoon, when Peter Buyskens' mule happened to be away, she resolved to go.

Before departing, however, Eli summoned the whole family. "Tell me truthfully," he asked, "have you considered visiting Margaret Brandt?"
"Ay, my man," Catherine admitted.
"Then I forbid you."
"Oh, do you?" she challenged, flushing.
"I do forbid it," Eli repeated sternly.
"There is nothing more to say, then," she murmured, her defiance curdling into shame.

Once alone with her daughter, Kate, Catherine berated herself furiously, not Eli. "I acted like a cat cornered by a robin," she lamented, pacing. "I am a silly hen that cannot lay an egg without cackling and gathering the whole flock to watch me. Next time either of us desires something, let us do it immediately, without thought or speech. If questioned later, we can claim ignorance, say *'Alack, we knew naught'*—who would suspect us? For two pins, I would have gone to Sevenbergen despite their teeth!"

Such wild defiance staggered Kate. "Nay, Mother, Father will come around with patience."
"And so will Michaelmas," Catherine retorted, her tone softening slightly. "But when? I was so set on you seeing the girl. Perhaps then we might consult. Say what they will, one judges a beast or human only by the eye. If I had fifty more sons, I would never thwart one's fancy until I had seen the object and verified it. Life is a school, and the lessons never end; we discard one error only to commit another, stumbling blindly until we reach our graves."

Kate stepped forward timidly. "Mother?"
"Well, what brings this look? No good tidings, I fear."
Kate faltered. "An avowal she hath to make."
"Now, there is a noble word," Catherine declared proudly. "Gerard taught you that, I swear. Speak it."
"Well, Mother, sooth to say, I have seen her."
"And?"
"And spoken with her."
"And you never told me? After this, miracles are dust."
"Mother, you were so angry against her. I waited until I could speak without further provoking you."
"Ay," Catherine sighed, half sadly, half bitterly. "Like mother, like daughter; cowardice is our ruin. I buffeted the others, or how would the house fare? Did you, Kate, ever receive a harsh word or look from your poor mother? Nay, I will not have you weep, girl. Ten to one you used sound judgment. Rise, brave heart, and tell me all. Better late than never. First and foremost, how did you manage to visit Sevenbergen with your crutches, and I not knowing?"

"I never was there in my life," Kate stammered. "And, Mammy dear, to say that I never wished to see her... but I never sought her out."
"There now," Catherine disputed, "did I not say 'twas unlike you to seek her secretly? Come, I am all agog."
"Thus it befell. Some manner of ear caught wind, and prithee, good mother, on my knees, ask me not how, that Gerard was a prisoner in the Stadthouse tower."
"Catechism," Catherine murmured, her sigh turning to a moan. "Blacker than I thought."
"Giles and I went out at night to encourage him. There, at the foot of the tower, stood a brave maiden, utterly strange to me. At first, we frightened one another through the gloom of his bad reputation and our fearful imaginations, paling in the moonlight. But instantly, she cried, 'You are Margaret,' and I, 'And you are Kate.' Think on it!"
"Treason!" Catherine exclaimed. "He would have talked back and forth of you to her, and her to you."
In return, Kate bestowed upon Catherine a gift of supreme value—the composite kiss. She pressed a single kiss to her cheek, conveying four distinct messages simultaneously:
1. Quite correct.
2. Good, clever mother, for guessing so quickly.
3. How sweet it is to be of one mind again after discord.
4. And many others.

"Now, speak thy mind, child," Catherine urged gently. "Gerard is not here. Alas, what am I saying? Would to Heaven he were."
"Well, Mother, she is comely and resembles her portrait but little."
"Eh, dear! Hark to the youth! I care for good deeds, not mere looks. Does she love my boy as he deserves?"
"Sevenbergen lies farther from the Stadthouse than does our home," Kate reflected. "Yet she was there before I arrived."
Catherine nodded knowingly.
"Nay, more," Kate continued. "She had freed him before I came. Yes, down from the captive's tower."
Catherine shook her head in disbelief. "The highest tower for miles! It is not feasible."
"'Tis sooth," Kate insisted. "She and an old man brought found means and wit to send him up a rope. There he dangled from his prison, and our Giles ascended it. When I first saw the rope hanging, I thought, 'This is glamour.' But when the frank lass's arms enfolded me, and her bosom beat against mine, and her cheeks grew wet, I realized, 'Tis not glamour: 'tis love.' She is not like me, but sturdy and capable. Even I, poor frail creature, feel sometimes that I could move the world for those I love. I love you, Mother. And she loves Gerard."
"God bless her for it! God bless her!" Catherine cried.
"But..."
"But what, lamb?"
"Is her love truly honest? 'Tis strange. Yet that same quality which warms your heart has somewhat cooled mine towards her. Poor soul. She is no wife, you know, Mother, when all is done."
"Humph! They stood at the altar together."
"Ay, but they departed as they entered, maid and bachelor."
"The parson says so?"
"Nay, for that I know not."
"Then I will take no man's word but his own in such a tangled skein." After a pause, she added, "Nevertheless, you are right, girl. I shall go to Sevenbergen alone. A wife I am, but not a slave. All are in darkness here. She holds the key. I must question her, and no one beside me—not you, lest you interfere. I will take no lily to a house with a stain, no, not to a palace of gold and silver."

As Catherine pondered these words, her resolve hardened. Curiosity, a potent and nearly omnipotent passion, began to drive her. One fine afternoon, after dinner, she whispered to Kate, "Keep the house from falling to pieces, if you can." Donning her finest kirtle and hood, her scarlet clocked hose, and brand-new shoes, she marched briskly toward Sevenbergen, troubling no man's mule.

Upon arrival, she inquired for Margaret Brandt. The first person she asked shook his head. "The name is strange to me." Moving slightly, she approached a girl of fifteen standing at a door. "Father," the girl called inside, "another seeker comes after the magician's daughter." The man emerged, informing Catherine that Peter Brandt's cottage lay just outside town on the east side. "You may see the chimney from hence," he pointed out. "But you will find neither father nor daughter; they fled this week, bless you."
"Do not deceive me, good man," Catherine protested, noting the distance from Tergou. "From Tergou? Then you must have met the soldier."
"What soldier?"
"Ay, I did meet a soldier."
"Well, then, that soldier was here seeking that very Margaret."
"Ay," the girl interjected, "and he was frantic with us because she was gone. His long beard and her cheek are no strangers, I warrant."
"Says no more than you know," Catherine snapped sharply. "You are too young to slander your elders. Stay! Tell us more about this soldier, good man."
"Nay, I know no more than that he sought Margaret Brandt. I told him she and her father had vanished in a moonlight flight this past fortnight. Some thought the devil had whisked them away, being magicians. 'And,' says he, 'the devil fly away with thee for thy ill news!' That was my thanks. 'But I doubt 'tis a lie,' said he. 'If you think so,' I replied, 'go and see.' 'I will,' he said, and burst forth with a handful of gibberish. My wife thought 'twas curses; he hurried to the cottage. Soon he returned, singing another tune. 'You were right, and I was wrong,' he said, pushing a silver coin into my hand. Show it to your wife, some of you, and she will believe me; I have been called a liar twice today."
"It needs not," Catherine said, examining the coin, "though I inspected it anyway."
"He seemed quiet and sad, didn't he now, wench?"

The following morning, Denys seized an opportunity to reveal the remainder of his tale to Mother and Daughter, carefully shielding Cornelis and Sybrandt from the knowledge. "I cannot stain their names," he explained, his characteristic candor tempered by military discretion. "They are of honorable blood. Yet Gerard views them as enemies in this affair. Since I am Gerard's comrade, and since it is a law among soldiers to deny truth to foes, I am bound to withhold the facts—but I shall speak the lies necessary to preserve honor."

Catherine sighed, offering no rebuttal. The adventures Denys recounted stirred within them a tempest of agitation and sorrow. They wept bitterly at the thought of the friends' separation, a scene Denys could not describe without stumbling. Yet, eventually, their grief coalesced into a radiant hope: the anticipation of Gerard's swift return. Denys shared this confidence, yet gently cautioned them against neglecting his friend's dying wishes. "If Gerard returns next week and finds no Margaret," he mused, "what manner of man shall I prove?"

For the first time, Catherine felt a profound tenderness toward the absent Margaret. Her anxiety mirrored Denys's, yet she refused to agree with his rash plan to scour the entirety of Holland. "She must have confided in someone," she reasoned. "These are not fugitives fleeing debt; they owe naught in Sevenbergen. Dearest Denys, you cannot hunt the breadth of the Netherlands for a single girl."

"Can I not?" Denys asked grimly, his jaw set. "We shall see." After a moment's reflection, he proposed a division of labor: Catherine remained behind, her senses sharpened to catch any whisper, while he would ransack every town if necessity demanded. "She will not stray many leagues," he declared firmly. "Three do not flee as swiftly as one."

"That is sound counsel," Catherine admitted. However, she insisted he visit the Demoiselle Van Eyck first. "She and Margaret were bosom companions. If anyone knows the girl's whereabouts, it is she." Denys, eager to depart instantly, allowed Catherine to guide him. With a dexterous turn of her hand, she smoothed her gown, making herself appear a shade more composed, and escorted him forth.

They were ushered into a sumptuously furnished chamber where the elderly lady sat. Upon hearing their errand, the tapestry slipped from Margaret Van Eyck's hands. "Gone? Departed Sevenbergen without telling me? The ungrateful child!"

This reaction stunned the visitors. "You know nothing? When was she last seen?"

"Perhaps ten days ago," the old lady murmured. "I had taken out my brushes after so many years to paint her portrait. Yet I did not proceed, for reasons."

Catherine noted the strangeness of such a departure—"bag and baggage"—without permission or explanation. "Was ever anything so untoward? Just as our hearts grew warm toward her, here comes Gerard's companion from afar with news of his love, yet she remains missing, and Gerard awaits. What am I to do? Surely she has not fled without cause. Pray, give us the clue, my good demoiselle."

"I possess no clue to offer," the elder lady replied, her tone tinged with petulance.

"Then I shall provide one," interjected Reicht Heynes, stepping into the doorway, her cheeks flushed with sudden animation.

"So you have been listening, have you?"

"What are my ears for, Mistress?" Reicht countered sharply.

"Precisely. Then illuminate this darkness with your wisdom."

"There is no darkness," Reicht declared. "And if the clue is a two-stranded cord, with both ends lying right here in this room, you will not be far astray. Oh, Mistress, I marvel at you pretending ignorance!"

"Marry, draw near," the hostess said, her own complexion flushing nearly as crimson as the maid's. "So it was I who drove the foolish girl away."

"You played your part, Mistress. How did you greet her on her final visit? Did she fail to notice the coldness, especially when she stood friendless? You said, 'I have changed my mind regarding your portrait,' lifting your nose high."

"I did not lift my nose. It is not shaped like yours for gazing heavenward."

"Oh, all noses follow the heart's inclination, regardless of shape. Poor soul! She came to my kitchen. 'I am not to be painted now,' she whispered, tears streaming down. She said no more, yet I understood perfectly. I had seen you."

"Well," admitted Margaret Van Eyck, "I confess as much, and I submit judgment to you, Madam. These young girls can accomplish nothing of their own volition; they merely mimic their sweethearts. Your Gerard is remarkably adept at many trades, including the illuminator's craft. Margaret is his pupil, a patient one. Is there not a natural affinity between a woman's eye for color and a lover to emulate? 'Tis a trick I despise in my heart. By it, the grand art of color—which should be regal, aspiring, and free—degenerates into a servile slave to petty crafts, fettered and imprisoned to match the triviality of books, destined to adorn a rich fool's pocket. Nevertheless, affection governs us all. When the poor wench presented me with thorn-leaves, lilies, ivy, and dewberries, along with butterflies and beetle larvae—all nature's refuse stuck fast in gold leaf like wasps trapped in a honey-pot—and her daily book revealing she had labored hundreds of hours over each minute detail, I was rightfully wrathful. An immortal soul, countless hours of toil, and exquisite manual skill cast away upon nature's trash? Yet, possessing compassion, I restrained myself, damming up my better feelings, and feigned kindness, offering suggestions to improve the work. I told her, 'Seeing as Heaven has doomed us to squander time, soul, and color upon great letters and tiny beetles, omitting saints and heroes, why not present the scum naturally?' I reminded her that grapes hang in the air, not stuck to walls, and that insects do not live miserably wedged in metal prisons but roam freely, infesting the air."

"'Ah, my dear friend,' she replied, 'I see now whither you drive; but this ground is gold; we may not shade it.' 'Who said so?' I asked. 'All teachers of this craft,' she answered—and perhaps to conclude me immediately—'Gerard himself!' 'That for Gerard himself,' I retorted, 'and the entire gang; give me a brush!'"

Consequently, I chose to shade her fruit and reptiles with a hue false to nature but true relative to that monstrous background of glaring gold. Within five minutes, a cluster of raspberries, stalk and all, appeared, almost ready to fly into one's mouth; likewise, a butterfly larva rendered so realistically it might turn the stoutest stomach. My lady threw her arms around my neck, exclaiming, 'Oh!'"

"Did she?"

"The little love!" observed Denys, finally managing to insert a word.

Margaret Van Eyck stared at him, then smiled. She continued, recounting how she had been led step by step to promise resuming the art she had abandoned with a sigh upon her brothers' death—to paint the Madonna anew, using Margaret as a model. Incidentally, she revealed the mechanics of turning girls into saints. "'Thy hair is adorable,'" I told her. "'Why, 'tis red,'" she replied. "'Ay,'" I responded, "but what a red! How brown! How glossy! Most hair is not worth a straw to us painters; thine is the artist's very hue. But thy violet eyes, which currently reflect the earth through languor for lack of Gerard, soon shall blaze with hope; these I shall lift to heaven in fixed, holy meditation. Thy nose, which already aspires upward (though not so piously as Reicht's), I shall modestly lower, and somewhat soften thy chin.'"

"Softening the chin?" Reicht exclaimed. "Alack! What does that signify? You surpass me, Mistress."

"'Tis a resolute chin. Not a jot too firm for this wicked world; but when dealing with a Madonna? No, thank you."

"Well, I never. A resolute chin."

"Denys," Catherine interjected softly. "The darling!"

"And now comes the rub," Margaret Van Eyck continued. "When you informed me of her condition—the way she stands—it gave me a shock; I dropped my brushes. Am I to transform a girl, unable to keep her lover at bay, into the Virgin Mary at my age? I love the poor ninny still. But I adore our Blessed Lady. Say, 'must not a painter be peevish in such matters'? Well, most painters are men, and men are fine fellows. They can do anything. Their saints and virgins are no more nor less than their mistresses, save for the sanctification. Yet, for this very reason, half their craft escapes me; I discover beneath their angels' white wings the very trollops I have seen flaunting themselves on the streets, bedizened like pagan idols, worn like queens in a deck of cards. And I am not a fine fellow, but merely a woman. My painting is but half craft; the other half is devotion. Thus, you may read me. 'Twas foolish, perhaps, but I could not help it; yet I am sorrowful." The old lady concluded despond

Catherine sat in a quiet, heavy dismay, her mind racing with the terrifying arithmetic of time. Gerard would return within a month, perhaps sooner, yet she had not once looked upon his betrothed. A shadow fell over her soul regarding the girl's fate, an uncertainty that curdled into something sickening whenever she let it linger. "Oh, Kate," she whispered, her voice trembling, "what if she has already gone and ruined everything?" Her daughter, Little Kate, protested gently, insisting that Margaret would never be so wicked. But Catherine shook her head, her eyes clouded with a deep, maternal sorrow. "Ah, my lass, you know not the madness that grips young hearts unmoored from a mother's hand," she murmured, recounting stories of girls who plunged into rivers for lovers they would despise a week later, their emotions as volatile as the weather, jumping from despair to hope with a terrifying speed. To her, men were monsters who built their traps with cruel intent, knowing full well that drowning oneself was no different than sleeping, a final act where the water washed away the sin entirely. She spoke of her own near-fatal mistake years past, when a quarrel with Eli had driven her to the edge of a canal, tempted by the promise of dramatic reconciliation and heavenly reward, only to stop because she had nearly finished sewing a new gown. That moment, she explained, proved that life itself was the true treasure, superior to any fleeting passion or grievance. Little Kate clung to the hope that Margaret loved her father too deeply to abandon him, reminding her mother that he was everything to her. Yet Catherine remained unconvinced, placing her faith not in the wisdom of the aged, but in the innocence of the unborn. "Keep your heart high, dear mother," Kate urged, trying to soothe the tempest within her. She finally turned to a solemn entreaty, begging her mother to cease predicting the sex of Margaret's baby, arguing that nature offered equal chances for boy or girl. This plea, however reasonable, was met with immediate and fierce clamor. "Do you dare threaten me with a daughter!" Catherine cried out, her voice rising in indignation. "While I have you here, I need no more girls! Where is the joy in setting a lass upon my knee when I cannot see Gerard again? I tell you it is settled."

Margaret Brandt had always stood apart from the clamor of Sevenbergen, a solitary figure whose reserve the town misread as arrogance. To her, however, it was merely a shield against the swelling tides of jealousy and malice that already churned beneath the surface. Now, with her condition growing impossible to conceal, she feared the moment when those hidden resentments would burst forth. This terror gnawed at her through the nights, driving her to lock herself within the walls of her home, to shun the sunlight whenever she ventured out.

Yet, in the quiet chambers of her conscience, Margaret possessed a clear, unclouded vision. She understood, with the practical wisdom of her sex, that betrothal was a binding contract, as legal as any signed document, and that breaking it carried heavy spiritual and social weight. She knew the laws of the land and the teachings of the Church regarding annulment and legitimacy. But what paralyzed her simple medieval mind was not the law itself, but the absence of proof. While she believed herself faithful, Gerard held the "marriage lines"—the physical token of their union—and he was far away, lost to her sight.

Her intellect, sharp and unblinded by emotion, calculated the odds with chilling precision. Without those lines to produce, her visible pregnancy offered no defense against the inevitable rumors. The world would see a woman alone with a child, not a bride awaiting her groom. "I have lost my tokens, but I cannot show them," would become her confession, a statement that no sensible woman would accept as truth. Margaret, naturally modest, sought the approval of women, not men, and she realized too late that slander attacking her sex held a terrifying power, especially when it wore a mask of apparent truth. In an age before the "strong-minded" woman, she was vulnerable. Though a capable mistress of a household, she trembled like a dry leaf before a storm, unable to withstand the gaze of public opinion.

The agony deepened when she returned from Margaret Van Eyck's house that evening. There, among friends, her condition had been exposed—not by a man, but by women, by neighbors, by the gossips who thrived on scandal. Her father, the old artist, was sequestered and silent, bound by honor not to speak. But Catherine, the center of the household, a woman of loose tongue and natural inclination for chatter, posed a different threat. A single spark of kindness from Catherine could ignite a blaze of exposure. Margaret watched the silence stretch, hoping for a sign of loyalty, but found none. Hope died, replaced by a cold certainty that Catherine would reveal everything.

A wild, desperate impulse seized her: flight. The thought of running away from Sevenbergen grew until it consumed her, a frantic desire to vanish before the storm broke. Yet, how could she persuade her father to leave? Men, particularly old ones, clung to their roots. To drag him along would require making him her confidant, a step she dreaded more than anything. She swung between the urge to flee and the inability to speak the truth to a man, vibrating in a suspense that felt like torture. Finally, amidst this turmoil, a dark idea formed. The city's canals, usually mere obstacles, now tempted her. Standing by the water's edge one afternoon, watching the deep, calm surface, she saw an image of eternal rest. *No more trouble,* she thought. *No more fear.* If Gerard had not loved her, she might have jumped then and there.

But as it was, she knelt by the bank and prayed with fervent desperation for strength. "Selfish wretch!" she cried aloud to the sky. "To abandon my father! To end the life of the child who makes Gerard suffer the pain he endured for my sake!" Tears streamed down her face as she vowed, "I will tell Father all. Yes, before this sun sets." She hurried home, eager to secure her resolve before it slipped away.

Inside, the dynamic of the house shifted. Peter, the learned master who was simple in domestic matters, had lived under Margaret's gentle but absolute rule since her sixteenth year. It was strange indeed to see the young mistress, usually decisive, faltering before him.

"Father, I would speak to thee."

"Speak on, girl."

"Wilt you listen?" she asked, her voice trembling. "And... and try not to excuse my faults?"

"We all have faults, Margaret," Peter said softly, his tone kind. "Fewer, I think, than most, unless parental affection clouds my judgment."

"No, Father," she whispered, tears welling. "I am a foolish girl who meant well but has done ill, terribly ill. Now I must bear the shame. But I love you, Father, with all my faults. Will you not forgive my folly and still love your motherless daughter?"

"That you may count upon," Peter replied cheerfully.

"Oh, well, do not smile," she urged, trying to stifle a sob. "How can I speak and make you sad?"

"Why, what troubles you?"

"Father, disgrace is knocking at this door. And I am the culprit. Oh, Father, turn your head away. I—I have let Gerard take away my marriage lines."

"Is that all?" Peter interrupted gently. "'Twas an oversight."

"It was the act of a madwoman!" Margaret cried, her composure breaking. "But woe is me! That is not the worst."

Peter paused, sensing the gravity behind her words. "The youth is honest, and he loves you dearly. You are young. What is a year or two? Gerard will surely return and keep his troth."

"And meanwhile, do you know what comes?" she asked, her voice rising.

"Not I, save that I shall depart before the storm breaks."

"There is worse pain than death!" she insisted, pressing closer. "Nay, for pity's sake, turn away, Father."

"Foolish wench!" he muttered, turning his face aside as requested.

She trembled violently, her cheeks burning like coals. "I looked upon Gerard as my husband—we were betrothed—and he was in such dire danger. I thought I had killed him. And I..." She stopped, overwhelmed. "If you were but my mother, I might find the courage. You say not a word."

"Why, Margaret, what is this fuss? Why are thy cheeks crimson, speaking to no stranger, but to thy ancient father?"

"Why do my cheeks burn?" she begged, falling to her knees. "Because—because Father, kill me! Send me to Heaven! Bid Martin shoot me with his arrow! Then the gossips will come and tell you why I blush. And when I am dead, I pray you will love your girl again for her mother's sake."

Peter reached out, his expression stern yet filled with care, and took her hand. She extended it to him, shaking. He grasped it gently and pressed his fingers to her wrist, checking her pulse with anxious concentration.

"Alas, nay," he said, releasing her. "'Tis not my blood that burns, but my soul, fevered with grief." She wrung her hands impatiently. "I cannot, I will not stay in Sevenbergen."

"Be calm now," the old man soothed. "Do not torment thyself for nought. Stay not a day longer if it vexes thee and brings on this fever."

"What!" Margaret cried, throwing herself upon her knees beside him, clutching his sleeve, then his arm, then his shoulder. "Would you yield to go hence, asking no reason but my longing to depart? Alas, what need? You will soon see. I could never say it. I would rather die."

"Foolish child, who seeks thy girlish secrets?" Peter asked, smiling faintly. "Is it I, whose life has been spent studying Nature, who would bind you? As for leaving Sevenbergen, what keeps me here? Respect? Gratitude? Are they not called quack-salvers by those who avoid me, and wizards by those I heal? Do they not give gold and honor to the charlatans who slay them? Besides, does it matter where we sojourn? Choose thou, as thy mother did before thee."

Margaret embraced him tenderly, weeping softly upon his shoulder.

For the moment, she was spared. Yet, as the tears flowed and the reprieve settled, she almost wished she had possessed the fortitude to confess everything.

Later, Peter prepared a medicament, bringing it to her himself. She took it submissively, not because she needed it, but to please him. It was a soothing draught, administered under a slight misconception, yet it did her more good than harm. A long, deep sleep calmed her agitated spirit. And when she awoke, the preparations for departure had begun.

Within the week, they left Sevenbergen, bag and baggage, lodging above a tailor's shop in the Brede-Kirk Straet of Rotterdam. Only one person in Tergou knew of their destination: the Burgomaster. He locked the knowledge safely within his own breast, hiding its meaning from himself as much as from others. Time, it seemed, would soon reveal the use he intended to make of this secret, though neither he nor anyone else could fully divine it yet.

Among strangers, Margaret Brandt found a comparative happiness, until a new and unexpected source of contentment arose. A civic dignitary, plagued by an illness and fanciful in equal measure, sought physician after physician in desperation. Having reached the gates of death, he summoned Peter. Finding the patient reduced to nothing by excessive bleeding and purging, Peter flung a battalion of bottles from the window and left them standing wide open. Instead, he beat egg yolks in neat Schiedam wine, administered them in small doses, followed by meat stewed in red water infused with mild febrifugal herbs. Slowly, the patient recovered, moving about looking somewhat like a man encased in a pillowcase. Being a voluble man, the dignitary spread Peter's fame through every street, granting the artist, who had long deserved a reputation in vain, a rapid rise by sheer luck.

Things appeared bright. The old man's pride was cheered, and his purse began to fill. Yet he squandered much of his gain on sovereign herbs and choice drugs, intending to invest them all in medicine. It was Margaret who intercepted a portion, effectively "white-mailing" it from his grasp. However, the victory came too late; the happy excitement proved fatal.

One evening, bidding her good night, his voice sounded strangely inarticulate. By the next morning, he lay speechless, barely sensible. Margaret, who had been her father's attentive pupil for years, immediately recognized the symptoms of a paralytic stroke. Trusting no one, she ran for a doctor. One who had previously been obstructed by Peter came, cheerfully confirming her fears. He advocated for bleeding the patient, a procedure she firmly declined. "He was always against bleeding," she argued, "especially the elderly." Peter survived, but he was never the same man again. His memory became severely impaired, rendering him untrustworthy to prescribe, leaving several patients awaiting his convalescence with uncertainty. Misery stared her in the face. Resolved to seek advice and comfort, she turned to her cousin, William Johnson, from whom she had hitherto kept aloof due to pride and poverty.

She found William and his servant sitting in the same room, neither the better for liquor. Mastering her surprise, she greeted them and announced her intention to discuss a family matter, glancing quietly at the servant to signal the sensitivity of the topic. The woman took the hint, but not as expected.

"Oh, you can speak before me, can she not, my old man?"

At this familiarity, Margaret turned crimson. "I cry you mercy, mistress. I knew not my cousin had fallen into the customs of this town. Well, I must take a fitter opportunity," she said, rising to depart.

"I wot not what ye mean by custom o' the town," the woman replied, bouncing up. "But this I know; 'tis the part of a faithful servant to keep her master from being preyed on by his beggarly kin."

Margaret retorted sharply, "Ye are too modest, mistress. Ye are no servant. Your speech betrays you. 'Tis not till the ape hath mounted the tree that she shows her tail so plain. Nay, there sits the servant; God help him! And while so it is, fear not thou his kin will ever be so poor in spirit as to come where the likes of you can flout their dole." Casting a look of mute reproach at her cousin for remaining passive and silent, she turned and walked out haughtily, refusing to shed a single tear until she reached home.

Now, two men needed lodging and feeding by one pregnant girl, with another mouth destined to enter the world. Yet, though the unborn child was the most helpless of all, it would prove their best friend. Nature was strong in Margaret Brandt—that same instinct which makes beasts, birds, and insects so cunning in providing food and shelter for their progeny. Stimulated by nature, she sat and brooded, thinking intensely on how to forestall destitution. Though she still possessed five gold pieces, she foresaw starvation approaching with inevitable footsteps.

Her sex, when deviating from custom to think with male intensity, reasoned with equal purpose. She rose, ordered Martin to move Peter to another room, tidied her own chamber with meticulous care, polished a glass globe, suspended it from the ceiling, dusted a crocodile figure, and nailed it to the exterior wall. After instructing Martin, she set him to act as a lounging sentinel at the street door, telling the "crocodile-bitten" visitors that a great, aged, and learned alchemist resided within, who, in his moments of recreation, sometimes amused himself by curing mortal diseases.

Patients soon arrived, demanding to see the leech. "That might not be," Margaret declared. "He is deep in his studies, searching for the grand elixir, and not even princes have speech of him. You must tell me your symptoms, and return in two hours." With mysterious powers, when they returned, the drug or draught was invariably ready. For worshipful patients, she would scan their faces, feel their pulses, and listen to their stories, then retreat to Peter's room to consider how her father, whose system she had long observed, would treat the case. She would write an illegible scrawl with cabalistic letters, bring it down reverently, and ask, "Could you read that?" Usually, the answer was, "I am no reader," or, with admiration, "Nay, mistress, nought can I make of it."

"Ay, but I can. 'Tis sovereign. Look on thyself as cured!" If she had the materials, she would sometimes let the patient see her compound the mixture, anxiously consulting a sacred prescription lest science suffer in her hands. Thus, she sent them away relieved of cash but filled with medicine and faith, humbugged to their hearts' content. *Populus vult decipi.* When they departed, she would take down two little boxes Gerard had made her. On one she wrote "To-day," and on the other "To-morrow," placing smaller coins in the former and larger sums in the latter, alongside whatever gold pieces had survived the journey from Sevenbergen and the expenses of housekeeping in a strange land. In this manner, she met current expenses and laid by for the rainy day she saw coming, mixing drugs with simples and vice with virtue. On this last score, her conscience pricked her sore. After each day's comedy, she knelt and prayed God to forgive her "for the sake of her child." But lo and behold, cures were reported to her; thus, her conscience began to harden.

Martin Wittenhaagen had lately become a dead weight on her hands. Like most men who had endured great hardships, he had stiffened suddenly. Though less supple, he was as strong as ever, capable of carrying the doctor herself around Rotterdam at his own pace. He carried her slops instead. In this new business, he displayed the qualities of a soldier: unreasoning obedience, punctuality, accuracy, dispatch, and drunkenness. He fell among "good fellows"; the blackguards plied him with Schiedam, and he babbled and bragged.

Doctor Margaret had risen very high in his estimation. All this brandishing of a crocodile for a standard, ambushing a dotard, disposing of slops, and taking good money in exchange struck him not as science but as strategy. He boasted in his cups and before a mixed company how "me and my General we are a biting of the burghers."

When this revelation had time to leaven the city, his General, Doctor Margaret, received a summons from the constables. They took her, trembling and begging subordinate machines to forgive her before the burgomaster. Beside him stood real physicians, a terrible row, in long robes and square caps, accusing her of practicing unlawfully on the bodies of the Duke's lieges. At first, she was too frightened to speak. A novice like herself, the mere name of "Law" paralyzed her. But questioned closely, though not so harshly as if she had been ugly, she told the truth. She had long been her father's pupil and had merely followed his system, curing many. "And it is not for myself, in very deed, sirs," she pleaded, "but I have two poor helpless honest men at home upon my hands, and how else can I keep them? Ah, good sirs, let a poor girl make her bread honestly; ye hinder them not to make it idly and shamefully. And oh, sirs, ye are husbands, ye are fathers; ye cannot but see I have reason to work and provide as best I may." Ere this woman's appeal had left her lips, she would have given the world to recall it, standing with one hand upon her heart and one before her face, hiding it, but not the tears that trickled beneath. All went to the wrong address. Perhaps a female bailiff might have yielded to such arguments, permitting her to practice medicine and break the law until her child was weaned.

"What have we to do with that," said the burgomaster, "save and except that if thou wilt pledge thyself to break the law no more, I will remit the imprisonment, and exact but the fine?"

On this, Doctor Margaret clasped her hands together and vowed most penitently never, never, never to cure body or beast again. Dismissed with the constables to pay the fine, she turned at the door, curtsied, and thanked the gentlemen for their forbearance.

To pay the fine, the "To-morrow box" must be opened instantly. With excess of caution

For Margaret, the day was a paradox of sweetness and sorrow. It brought her a loyal companion, yet it delivered the crushing truth that Gerard stood utterly alone in that distant, strange land. Her hope, which Denys had nurtured, shattered instantly; she realized with a jolt that he would not return, nor had he ever truly intended to. The silence of his absence filled the room, heavy and absolute.

Yet, amidst this grief, Denys proved to be a balm. He embraced her with the warmth of a brother, calling her his "she-comrade" and filling the air with his characteristic formulas and boisterous cheerfulness. Margaret indulged him completely, treating him with a gentle, almost doting affection that she also reserved for Martin. She became a strict guardian of his manners, refusing to let him take a single meal in her house unless he observed proper decorum, and fiercely forbidding any coarse language. "It spoils you, Denys!" she would chide, her voice softening despite her sternness. "Good lack, to hear such ugly words issuing from so comely a head! Forbear, or I shall be angry, so be civil." Under this pressure, Denys would switch from his natural, rough politeness to a ludicrous display of affected refinement, the struggle between his innate nature and his adopted civility often ending in a comedic pantomime.

However, as the old saying goes, when it rains, it pours. Soon, others sought to court Margaret's friendship. She had sent a humble letter to Margaret Van Eyck, acknowledging her diminished status and promising to keep her distance, yet she could not erase the memory of her former benefactor's kindness. In her note, she recounted the myriad struggles she had endured to survive, and concluded with a desperate plea: "Do not betray my residence, least of all to his people. I loathe them; they drove him from me. Even when he is gone, their hearts turn away from me as though repenting their cruelty were impossible."

The Van Eycks were thrown into perplexity. Eventually, they made Reicht a confidante, and like water passing through a pipe, the secret flowed from Reicht to Catherine.

"Ay, and has she turned so bitter against us?" asked the good woman, Catherine, her voice tinged with regret. "She stole our son from us, and now she hates us for failing to rush into her embrace. Yet, surely it is a blessing she lives and remains no further than Rotterdam."

Meanwhile, across the water, the English Princess, now known as Countess Charolois, moved with stately grandeur through the northern duchy, accompanied by her stepdaughter, the young heiress of Burgundy, Marie de Bourgogne. The Old Duke, the most magnificent prince in Europe, poured out his splendor to honor them. Troops of dazzling knights and battalions of fair ladies, gorgeously attired, formed a retinue around the princesses. Minstrels, jongleurs, storytellers, bards, musicians, actors, and tumblers trailed behind, creating a carnival of noise and color. Fencing matches, dances, and unending merriment lit up every town they visited. Richart issued an invitation to all his people to gather in Rotterdam to witness the pageantry.

Days passed in Rotterdam, and eventually, Denys encountered Catherine accidentally on the street. After exchanging warm greetings, he crowed triumphantly about having found her "she-comrade." But Catherine quickly cooled his enthusiasm, pointing out that he would have found her far sooner by staying quietly at Tergou rather than wandering aimlessly through Holland. "And now that we are found, what is gained?" she asked sharply. "Her heart is set dead against us now."

"Oh, let that fly buzz around its ear," Denys insisted, undeterred. "Come with me to her house."

"No," Catherine refused firmly. "I am certain of an ill welcome. Those who come unbidden sit unseated." Instead, she suggested that Denys act as mediator, bringing the parties together for a reconciliation. He accepted the task with immense pomp and confidence, trotting off to Margaret.

"She-comrade," he announced breathlessly, "I met a friend of yours today."

"You looked into Rotterdam, and saw yourself?" Margaret teased, though her eyes betrayed a flicker of hope.

"Nay, 'twas a female, and one who seeks your regard. 'Twas Catherine, Gerard's mother."

"Oh, was it?" Margaret replied, her tone shifting instantly from curiosity to cold resolve. "Then you may tell her she comes too late. There was a time I longed and longed for her, but she held aloof in my hour of greatest need. So now, we shall remain as we have been."

Denys attempted to shake this resolution, coaxing her gently. But she grew bitter and sullen, impenetrable to his charm. He then resorted to scolding, and suddenly, she burst into hysterics.

Startled by this emotional collapse, Denys, off guard, allowed himself to be trapped into a solemn promise never to mention the subject again. Crestfallen, he returned to Catherine and relayed the reception of his overture. Catherine flared with anger and informed the family how poorly his efforts had been received. The family erupted, and what began as a misunderstanding quickly ignited into a fierce, burning feud. Only little Kate offered any excuse for Margaret, trying to bridge the widening gap.

The following day, another visitor arrived at Margaret's door. Inside, the household appeared military yet enslaved and degraded: Martin was elbow-deep in soapsuds, Denys ironing with clumsy ineptitude, and Margaret herself was plaiting ruffs. Yet, above the mundane labor, her gaze remained sharp, a mistress's eye surveying her raw recruits. Just then, an old man entered. He appeared venerable at first glance, but closer inspection revealed a face keen and wizened.

"Oh!" cried Margaret. With swift, instinctive motion, she turned her back on him, hiding her face with an invincible repugnance. "Oh, that man! That man!"

"Nay, fear me not," said Ghysbrecht calmly. "I come on a friend's errand. I bring you a letter from foreign parts."

"Do not mock me, old man," she murmured, turning slowly back to face him.

"Nay, see;" and he held out an enormous, crinkled envelope.

Margaret darted forward, snatching the letter. She clutched it with trembling hands, her eyes glistening with sudden, overwhelming emotion. It was Gerard's handwriting.

"Oh, thank you, sir, bless you for this!" she gasped, her voice breaking. "I forgive you all the ill you ever wrought me."

Pressing the letter tightly to her bosom with one hand, she glided swiftly from the room, leaving Ghysbrecht standing there. As she did not return, the old man departed, casting a final, unreadable scowl at Martha. For hours afterward, Margaret sat alone, clutching the letter, the weight of his absence momentarily lifted by the tangible proof of his existence.

Margaret descended the stairs a transformed figure. The storm of bitterness and wild emotion that had churned within her moments before had settled into a profound, quiet calm. Though her eyes held traces of unshed tears, they were dry now, clear, and steady.

"Denys," she whispered, her voice soft yet firm, "I have received my orders. I am to read my lover's letter to his family."

"You will not do that?" Denys asked, disbelief coloring his tone.

"I shall," she replied without hesitation.

Denys studied her face, searching for a crack in her resolve. "I see some word in that letter has softened your heart toward them."

"Not a single jot, Denys," she corrected gently, a sad smile touching her lips. "Even if I loathed them as one hates poison, I could not disobey the command of my love. It is so sweet to obey, Denys. And infinitely sweeter to obey a soul who stands afar, unable to force my hand, trusting entirely in the fidelity of my love. Ah, Gerard, my darling, were you standing beside me, close enough to touch, I might well have slighted your wishes, repelled by the grudge you bear against them. But distance makes the bond unbreakable. If you had ordered me to wade into a raging sea and read your sweet letter to the sharks, I would have done so. Therefore, Denys, go to his mother. Tell her I have the letter. If she and her kin wish to hear it, I am their servant. Let them set the hour, and I will sit them where they please, and welcome them with every courtesy I possess."

Denys departed with this hopeful tidings. He found the household gathered around the dinner table. Announcing the arrival of a long letter from Gerard, he watched the joy ripple through the room. Yet, amidst the celebration, he added, "Her heart has been softened; she intends to read it to you personally. You may choose whatever time suits you."

"What notion is this?" Catherine asked, her brow furrowing. "That no one else can read it save her?" "Let her send it here, and we shall read it together."

"Nay, Mother," interjected little Kate, her voice small but earnest. "Perhaps she cannot bear to part with it. She loves him so dearly."

"Do you think she fears we shall steal it?"

Cornelis, ever the pragmatist, suggested she sought merely to wedge herself into the family circle through this token of affection. Denys shot a look of utter scorn at the suggestion. "A foul heart speaks there," he declared. "La camarade hates you all with the venom of a serpent. Do not mistake me, Dame; I defend her not—she is full of spleen, and Gerard's mere mention of them stirs her wrath. Yet, despite her hatred, she offers to read his letter with her own lips, and her voice sounds like honey—it is a fair offer."

"'Tis true," the father, Eli, admitted gravely. "It merits a civil response. Hold your tongues, you women, and let me answer. Tell her that I, her husband's father, setting aside all past grievances, thank her for this grace. And if she desires double thanks, let her send my son's letter via your faithful hands. I shall read it to his flesh and blood, and I shall return to her with such surety and faithfulness as Eli a Dierich, a William a Luke, a free burgher of Tergou, bound by the honor of my ancestors. A man of his word."

"Ay, and a man who keeps his word better than most," Catherine cried, tears glistening. "He is the only one I have ever known."

"Hold thy peace, wife!" Eli commanded sternly.

"Art a man of sense, Eli? Art a dirk? Art a chose?" Denys shouted, laughing. "The she-comrade will be delighted to obey Gerard, yet she dares not face you, whom she hates as wormwood, unless you are present. Blessed be, the world has changed. She is wholly submissive today. 'Obedience is honey,' she quoth. And truly, it is a sweetmeat she cannot resist, tasting so little of it. With her fair face and mellow tongue, coupled with her fitful flights that terrify us soldiers to death when we thwart her, and her alternating chidings and pettings that turn us, two honest swashbucklers, into crawling slaves, she has achieved wonders. I am the ironing ruffian; the other knave washes. What next? Why, whenever the brat enters the world, I shall rock the cradle, and the other scoundrel shall wash the tucker and bib. So, I shall fetch the letter instantly. Will you let me stay and hear it read?"

"Else our hearts were black as coal," Catherine sighed.

Denys hastened forth. He returned, however, crestfallen. "She will not let it leave her hand—not to me, nor to you, nor to any living soul."

"I knew she would not," Cornelis observed quietly.

"Hush!" Eli interrupted. "Let Denys speak his tale."

"Nay," Denys protested, recounting the scene. "'Be ruled by me,' I said. 'Not I,' she retorted. 'But consider the honey of Obedience!' 'You are a fool,' she answered. 'Obedience to Gerard is sweet, but obedience to any other body?'"

"At length, she seemed to yield slightly," Denys continued, holding up a piece of paper. "And here, Mademoiselle, is a written note for you."

"For me?" Little Kate blushed, reaching for it.

"Give it here!" Eli commanded, scanning the handwriting. He read aloud, his voice dropping to a whisper. "Hearken! 'And, sweetheart, if these lines should travel safe to thee, make thou trial of my people's hearts withal. Maybe they are somewhat turned towards me, being far away. If 'tis so they will show it to thee, since now to me they may not. Read, then, this letter! But I do strictly forbid thee to let it from thy hand; and if they still hold aloof from thee, why, then say nought, but let them think me dead. Obey me in this; for, if thou dost disrespect my judgment and my will in this, thou lovest me not.'"

A heavy silence fell upon the room as Gerard's words, copied by Margaret's hand, circulated among the listeners.

"Well," Catherine finally spoke, breaking the stillness. "That is another matter. Methinks 'tis for her to come to us, not for us to journey to her."

"Alas, Mother! What odds does that make?" little Kate exclaimed, distressed.

"Much," Eli replied, his expression grim. "Tell her we are too numerous to visit her, and bid her come hither, the sooner the better."

When Denys departed to deliver the message, Eli confessed to himself that it was a bitter pill to swallow. "When that girl crosses my threshold, all the mischief and misery she has wrought here will seem to tumble into a single heap. Yet, what could I do, wife? We must hear the news of Gerard. I saw it in thine eyes, and felt it in my own heart. She is backed by our undutiful, yet still beloved son, and thus she stands stronger than we. She drives us to the grindstone, that sly, cruel jade. But never heed. We will listen to the letter; and then, let her depart unblessed, just as she arrived unwelcome."

"Make your mind easy," Catherine assured him, though a faint tone of regret lingered in her voice. "She will not come at all."

Shortly thereafter, Richart arrived from Amsterdam. Grave and dignified in his burgher's robe, gold chain, ruff, and furred cap, he was received not merely with affection but with deep respect. For he had risen a step higher than his parents, and in medieval society, such ascents were marked as visibly as staircases.

Admitted to the family council, Richart displayed plainly, though without overt discourtesy, that his pride had been deeply wounded by his parents' willingness to treat with Margaret Brandt. "I see the temptation," he remarked. "But which of us hath not, at times, wished one thing and done another?" These words cast a chilling frost over the elderly couple.

"So little," little Kate ventured timidly, "vex not thyself, dear Richart. Mother says she will not come."

"All the better, sweetheart," Richart replied coldly. "I fear me, if she do, I shall hie me back to Amsterdam."

Just then, Denys popped his head through the doorway. "She will be here at three on the great dial."

They all stared at one another in a silence that stretched like a taut wire, waiting for the inevitable collision.

"Nay, Richard," Catherine said finally, "for Heaven's sake, let not this single wretched creature set us all by the ears. Has she not already bred enough ill blood?"

"In truth, she has," replied Gerard, his voice steady though his heart hammered. "Do not fear me, good mother. Let her enter and read the letter of the poor boy whom she has bewitched by devilish arts, and then let her depart. Give me your words to ensure she receives no countenance beyond decent, constrained civility; we cannot afford less, for we are in our own house. And I promise, I will say no more."

On this understanding, they awaited the foe. She, for her part, prepared for the interview in a spirit little less hostile. When Denys brought word that they would not come to her but would receive her, her lip curled, and she bade him observe how in them every feeling, however small, was magnified in importance compared to their love for Gerard. "Well," she said, "I have not that excuse; so why mimic the pretty burgher's pride, the pride of all unlettered folk? I will go to them for Gerard's sake. Oh, how I loathe them!"

Thus poor, good-natured Denys was unwittingly bringing into one house the materials of an explosion.

Margaret made her toilet in the same spirit that a knight of her day dressed for battle—he to parry blows, and she to parry glances—glances of contempt at her poverty or of irony at her extravagance. Her kirtle was of English cloth, dark blue, and her farthingale and hose of the same material, a glossy roan or claret color. There was not an inch of pretentious fur about her, only plain snowy linen wristbands and curiously plaited linen from the bosom of the kirtle up to the commencement of the throat; it did not encircle her throat but framed it, being square, not round. Her front hair still peeped in two waves, much after the fashion which Mary Queen of Scots revived a century later; but instead of the silver net which would have ill become her present condition, the rest of her head was covered with a very small, tight-fitting hood of dark blue cloth, hemmed with silver. Her shoes were red, but the roan petticoat and hose prepared the spectator's mind for the shock, setting off her arched instep and shapely foot. Beauty knew its business then as now.

And with all this, she kept her enemies waiting, though it was three by the dial.

At last she started, attended by her companion. And when they were halfway, she stopped and said thoughtfully, "Denys!"

"Well, she-general?"

"I must go home" (piteously).

"What? Have ye left somewhat behind?"

"What?"

"My courage. Oh! oh! oh!"

"Nay, nay, be brave, she-general. I shall be with you."

"Ay, but wilt keep close to me when I be there?"

Denys promised, and she resumed her march, but gingerly.

Meantime, they were all assembled, waiting for her with a strange mixture of feelings. Mortification, curiosity, panting affection, aversion to her who came to gratify those feelings, yet another curiosity to see what she was like, and what there was in her to bewitch Gerard and make so much mischief.

At last Denys came alone and whispered, "The she-comrade is without."

"Fetch her in," said Eli. "Now, whisht, all of ye. None speak to her but I."

They all turned their eyes to the door in dead silence. A little muttering was heard outside; Denys's rough organ and a woman's soft and mellow voice. Presently that stopped; and then the door opened slowly, and Margaret Brandt, dressed as I have described, somewhat pale but calm and lovely, stood on the threshold, looking straight before her.

They all rose but Kate, and remained mute and staring.

"Be seated, mistress," said Eli gravely, and motioned to a seat that had been set apart for her.

She inclined her head and crossed the apartment; and in so doing, her condition was very visible, not only in her shape but in her languor. Cornelis and Sybrandt hated her for it. Richard thought it spoiled her beauty. It softened the women somewhat.

She took her letter out of her bosom and kissed it as if she had been alone; then disposed herself to read it, with the air of one who knew she was there for that single purpose.

But as she began, she noticed they had seated her all by herself, like a leper. She looked at Denys, and putting her hand down by her side, made him a swift, furtive motion to come by her. He went with an obedient start, as if she had cried "March!" and stood at her shoulder like a sentinel; but this zealous manner revealed to the company that he had been ordered thither, and at that she coloured. And now she began to read her Gerard, *their* Gerard, to their eager ears, in a mellow, clear voice, so soft, so earnest, so thrilling, her very soul seemed to cling about each precious sound. It was a voice as of a woman's bosom set speaking by Heaven itself.

"I do nothing doubt, my Margaret, that long ere this shall meet thy beloved eyes, Denys, my most dear friend, will have sought thee out, and told thee the manner of our unlooked-for and most tearful parting. Therefore I will e'en begin at that most doleful day. What befell him after, poor faithful soul, fain, fain would I hear, but may not. But I pray for him day and night next after thee, dearest. Friend more stanch and loving had not David in Jonathan, than I in him. Be good to him, for poor Gerard's sake."

At these words, which came quite unexpectedly to him, Denys leaned his head on Margaret's high chair and groaned aloud. She turned quickly as she sat, and found his hand, and pressed it. And so the sweetheart and the friend held hands while the sweetheart read.

"I went forward all dizzied, like one in an ill dream; and presently a gentleman came up with his servants, all on horseback, and had liked to have ridden o'er me. And he drew rein at the brow of the hill, and sent his armed men back to rob me. They robbed me civilly enough and took my purse and the last copper, and rode gaily away. I wandered stupid on, a friendless pauper."

There was a general sigh, followed by an oath from Denys.

"Presently a strange dimness came o'er me; I lay down to sleep on the snow. 'Twas ill done, and with store of wolves hard by. Had I loved thee as thou dost deserve, I had shown more manhood. But oh, sweet love, the drowsiness that did crawl o'er me desolate, and benumb me, was more than nature. And so I slept; and but that God was better to us than I to thee or to myself, from that sleep I ne'er had waked; so all do say. I had slept an hour or two, as I suppose, but no more, when a hand did shake me rudely. I awoke to my troubles. And there stood a servant girl in her holiday suit. 'Are ye mad,' quoth she, in seeming choler, 'to sleep in snow, and under wolves' noses? Art weary o' life, and not long weaned? Come, now, said she, more kindly, 'get up like a good lad;' so I did rise up. 'Are ye rich, or are ye poor?' But I stared at her as one amazed. 'Why, 'tis easy of reply,' quoth she. 'Are ye rich, or are ye poor?' Then I gave a great, loud cry; that she did start back. 'Am I rich, or am I poor? Had ye asked me an hour ago, I had said I am rich. But now I am so poor as sure earth beareth on her bosom none poorer. An hour ago I was rich in a friend, rich in money, rich in hope and spirits of youth; but now the Bastard of Burgundy hath taken my friend, and another gentleman my purse; and I can neither go forward to Rome nor back to her I left in Holland. I am poorest of the poor.' 'Alack!' said the wench. 'Natheless, an ye had been rich ye might ha' lain down again in the snow for any use I had for ye; and then I trow ye had soon fared out o' this world as bare as ye came into it. But, being poor, you are our man: so come wi' me.' Then I went because she bade me, and because I recked not now whither I went. And she took me to a fine house hard by, and into a noble dining-hall hung with black; and there was set a table with many dishes, and but one plate and one chair. 'Fall to!' said she, in a whisper. 'What, alone?' said I. 'Alone? And which of us, think ye, would eat out of the same dish with ye? Are we robbers o' the dead?' Then she speered where I was born. 'At Tergou,' said I. Says she, 'And when a gentleman

When consciousness returned to Margaret, her head rested upon Catherine's shoulder, surrounded by the sturdy, unpretentious circle of her kin who offered their rough, heartfelt encouragement. Giles, the voice of the household, boomed that Gerard lived and wrote the finest letter humanity had ever seen, a testament to a man who, though missing from his counting house in Amsterdam, remained a beacon of kindness. "Gerard was alive," Margaret whispered, her strength failing her, yet her heart swelling with a mixture of guilt and relief. "But why did he flee to these wild shores? Why did I not keep him closer?" She gently pushed aside Catherine's arm, attempting to kneel, only to collapse back onto her sister-in-law's lap, clinging to her with a desperate, tender embrace.

"Read it, Richart," Eli commanded gently, noting the youthfulness in the boy's gaze. As Richart unfolded the parchment, his admiration for the neat, needle-point script quickly turned to exasperation regarding Gerard's absence from business. But as the reading began, the somber atmosphere lifted, replaced by the vivid warmth of Gerard's journey. He recounted his recovery in the care of a benevolent merchant, the healing of his sprained leg, and the sorrow of his broken psaltery. His voice, through the ink, traveled across the plains of Lombardy, describing a land where winter wore the colors of summer, with olive trees draped in white linen and oxen adorned like living pictures. He spoke of Venice, a city of marble and mystery, viewed from his sickbed as a dream of gold boats and palaces built on water.

Yet, it was Gerard's observations of the human soul that captivated the room. He contrasted the churlish honesty of northern Germans with the sophisticated, wine-soaked gentility of the French, noting how the latter treated their women with a reverence that bordered on idolatry, yet betrayed them with cruelty in times of strife. He described the intricate customs of the Italians—their subtlety, their obsession with cleanliness, and their peculiar morality where sin was taxed and virtue was sometimes masked as vice. Through his eyes, the family saw the breadth of the world: from the red snails of Germany to the black-fleeced sheep of France, from the brutal justice of Venice to the artistic wonders of its churches.

As the narrative reached its climax, the tone shifted abruptly. Gerard, now aboard a ship bound for Rome, described a scene at St. Mark's Square that pierced the hearts of the listeners. Amidst the grandeur of the cathedral and the treasures within, he had seen a garland of amaranths on a Dutch mast—a symbol of courtship. There, in bold letters, burned the name *Richart Eliassoens*. Tears streamed down Richart's cheeks, a surge of brotherly love crashing over him as he realized his sibling's devotion. The room fell silent, save for the soft sobs of Catherine and the gentle, tear-streaked face of little Kate, who sewed baby caps with hands that trembled.

The revelation that followed, however, shattered the fragile peace. Eli, noticing the betrayal in Gerard's account of "marriage lines," confronted Margaret. With a stinging accusation, he revealed that Martin Wittenhaagen knew of a bond between them, a fact hidden until now. The air grew thick with tension as Denys stepped forward, his voice rising in defense of the girl.
