He recalled how Margaret had bled for Gerard when he was hunted, how she had risked everything to protect him, calling her a traitor to the accusation of loving another. The truth, raw and painful, hung in the air: Gerard had loved her deeply, and she had loved him back, a secret kept in the shadows of their lives.

In the end, the storm cleared, leaving a trail of forgiveness and understanding. Eli, moved by the depth of Margaret's sacrifice, declared that a chair would always be set at their table for her, and for Gerard, wherever his path led. He urged them to write immediately, sending Gerard home to heal, assuring the family that distance would not sever the bonds forged in blood and love. As the evening settled, the room filled with a quiet hum of anticipation. Giles, the dwarf, sat upon a stool, listening intently as Margaret whispered the final, tender lines of Gerard's letter into his ear, her face inches from his, a striking contrast of beauty and grotesquery that seemed to capture the very essence of their intertwined fates.

Two months prior to the present scene, near the coast midway between Naples and Rome, the local populace gathered on the beach, their gazes fixed intently on a vessel battling a ferocious gale driven directly onto the shore. The ship appeared to defy disaster at moments, prompting cheers from the spectators; yet at others, the relentless wind and towering waves pressed her dangerously close, granting the onlookers a private, unspoken satisfaction at her precarious plight.

Though poorly engineered for open-sea navigation, the vessel was surprisingly robust for grounding, her exaggerated stern catching the gale and her hull shaped like an inverted cocked hat. To the crowd, the battering wooden frame seemed a living entity, struggling desperately against mortality with a gasping heart. Yet those standing upon the deck would have known better: the ship possessed not one heart but many, and a multitude of souls were emerging into the light amidst that terrifying hour.

The sailors stumbled wildly across the swaying timber, hauling ropes at random, alternating between curses and prayers. Passengers huddled around the mast, some seated, others kneeling or lying prostrate, clutching the railings as the ship heeled violently in the monstrous seas. Among them stood a handsome young man, his complexion ashen and lips tightly compressed, revealing an internal war between sheer terror and rigid self-respect. It was Gerard. Around him, a trembling mass of humanity raised countless vows and pleas, creating a cacophony where deities outnumbered mortals. The sailors, however, placed their faith singularly in a divine feminine figure, invoking her as "Queen of Heaven" or "Star of the Sea." In contrast, the landlubbers practiced a chaotic polytheism; an English merchant vowed a fortune to Our Lady of Walsingham, while a Genoese trader pledged silver to Our Lady of Loreto, and a Tuscan noble promised wax candles to Our Lady of Ravenna, each pledging allegiance to different local manifestations of the divine rather than the universal Truth.

Suddenly, a violent gust caught the mainsail at a fatal angle. The rotting rigging snapped with a loud crack, tearing the canvas free. It billowed away downwind, shrinking into darkness until it vanished into the sea like a scrap of paper half a mile offshore. Before the helmsman could correct the course, a massive wave slammed into the quarter, soaking the survivors to the bone and offering them a foretaste of chilling death. Panic erupted instantly. One man vowed to become a Carthusian monk if Saint Thomas rescued him; another promised pilgrimage to Compostela in rags if Saint James interceded. Others called upon Thomas, Dominic, Denis, and especially Catherine of Siena.

Two small Neapolitan traders stood shivering in the cold. One shouted loudly, "I vow a waxen statue of Saint Christopher weighing as much as myself if I reach land safely!" His companion nudged him, warning, "Brother, beware what you vow. If you auctioned everything you possess, it would not buy enough wax to cover the statue."

"Hold your tongue, you fool," retorted the first, then whispered, "Do you think I am joking? Let me survive, and I will not give him so much as a straw."

Nearby, others lay flat, pleading with the ocean itself. "Oh, most merciful sea! Oh, generous sea! Be gentle, be kind, spare us in this hour of peril!" Others wailed in pure animal terror as the ship became a mere toy tossed by the titanic waves.

Amidst this chaos sat a humble Roman woman with her infant at her breast. Her cheeks were pale, but her eyes remained strangely calm. Though her lips moved in silent prayer, she wept not, nor did she bargain with the gods. Whenever the ship threatened to sink beneath them and the men screamed, she simply kissed her child. With stoic patience, she nursed him within the jaws of death, refusing to let him lose any comfort she could offer. *Moribundo?* Yes, there, amidst the medieval panic, sat the spirit of antiquity. Sixteen centuries had not corrupted the old Roman blood in her veins, and an instinct inherited from ancestors she had scarcely heard of taught her to meet death with dignified composure.

A colossal friar stood upon the poop, legs braced wide like the Colossus of Rhodes, seemingly ignoring the surrounding peril rather than defying it. Reciting verses from the Canticles in a loud, steady voice, he invited the passengers to confession. Those who knelt before him found absolution as easily as if they were in a quiet sacristy, despite the dying ship around them. Gerard, drawn by an instinct guiding the wavering toward strength, approached him. Indeed, the courage of ordinary heroes might have paled beside the monk's monumental composure. Here, two figures maintained the dignity of their race: a woman, tender yet heroic, and a monk, hardened by faith against mortal fear.

Now, with the sail gone, the sailors lowered the useless mast a foot above the deck, causing it to crash over the side. This slight relief proved temporary. Without propulsion, the hull could not outrun the surf. The sea began pounding the stern repeatedly, delivering blows that felt more like strikes from a rocky mountain than liquid water.

The captain abandoned the wheel and moved to midships, pale as a corpse. "Lighten her!" he cried. "Throw everything overboard, or we shall founder before we strike, losing our only chance of life." As the sailors executed this order, the captain spoke with a demeanor utterly alien to an English skipper in such straits. "Friends," he declared, "last night, when conditions were too fair, a sphere of fire appeared near the ship. When twin spheres appear, it is good luck, and no doom awaits her voyage. We mariners name these Castor and Pollux. But if one arrives without the other, the ship is doomed. Therefore, like good Christians, prepare to die."

These words elicited a loud wail. When asked how long they had to prepare, the captain replied, "Perhaps half an hour, maybe less; beyond that, it is impossible. She leaks like a sieve. Hurry, men, lighten her."

Desperate passengers grabbed anything on deck and hurled it into the churning water. Soon, they seized a heavy sack containing an elderly, seasick man lying upon it. They dragged it to the edge. Suddenly, the owner sprang up with an unearthly shriek, leaping onto the sack. "Holy Moses! What are you doing? 'Tis my all! The entire fruit of my journey! Silver candlesticks, silver plates, brooches, chalices..."

"Release him, you hoary villain!" cried others. "Shall all our lives be lost for your stolen goods?" "Fling him in with it!" shouted one. "'Tis this Hebrew whom Christian men are drowning for!"

Numbers of men wrestled the sack from his grasp and heaved it over the side. It splashed into the waves. The owner uttered one final cry of anguish, stood glaring with streaming white hair, and prepared to leap after it. Had it floated, he surely would have followed. But it sank, vanishing forever. He staggered back and forth, tearing his hair, cursing the ship, the sea, and all the powers of heaven and hell.

"Now!" cried the captain. "There is a church in sight! Steer for it, mate, and you, friends, pray to the saint, whoever he may be!"

They turned the ship toward the distant spire and prayed to the unknown deity it housed. Immediately, a tremendous wave swamped the vessel, breaking the rudder and jamming it immobile while flooding the deck.

Wild with superstitious terror, some rushed to Gerard. "Here is the cause of all!" they cried. "He has never invoked a single saint! He is a heathen; there is a pagan aboard!"

"Alas, good friends, say not so!" Gerard stammered, his teeth chattering with cold. "Call these heathens those lying down praying to the sea. Friends, I honor the saints—but I dare not pray to them now. There is no time! (Oh!) Of what use to me are Dominic, Thomas, and Catherine? Saint Peter sits nearer to God's throne than they, and if I pray to Him, it seems odd, but I shall be drowned before He has time to plead my case. Oh! Oh! Oh! I must go straight to Him who made the sea, the saints, and me. Our Father, who art in heaven, save these poor souls and me who cry for bare life! Oh, sweet Jesus, pitiful Jesus, who walked on Galilee when Peter sank, and wept for Lazarus when the apostles' eyes were dry, oh, save poor Gerard—for dear Margaret's sake!"

At this moment, sailors were seen preparing to abandon the sinking ship in the small launch, a standard feature of vessels even then. A rush of self-preservation ensued, crowding thirty souls into the boat. Three remained behind, bewildered, and two paralyzed by terror. The paralyzed ones sat like heaps of wet rags; the bewildered paced frantically, watching the departing boat but making no attempt to join them, merely wringing their hands. Additionally, there was one person on his knees, praying fervently over a wooden statue of the Virgin Mary, detached reverently from the mast. As water sluiced across the deck, washing the statue about, this poor soul followed it on his knees, clasping his hands to it, while the waves played with both. Beside him sat the Jewish traveler, palsied not by fear but by grief. No longer capable of petty passions, he

Gerard secured a modest chamber on the west bank of the Tiber, embarking each morning on a pilgrimage for employment. He presented his samples to every workshop known to execute such commissions, only to be met with icy indifference. "Our letters must be somewhat thinner than this," remarked one merchant, critiquing the density of his ink. Another sneered, "How dark!" Yet the prevailing cry cut deeper: "What avail does this serve? Latin writings grow scarce here. Cannot you render Greek?"

"Aye, but not nearly so well as Latin," Gerard admitted humbly.
"Then you shall never earn your bread in Rome."

Undeterred, Gerard borrowed a magnificent Greek manuscript at a steep price, returning home with a hollow purse but an unbroken spirit. Within two weeks, he had mastered the Greek characters with remarkable speed. Eager to capitalize on this skill, he dedicated his mornings to study and his afternoons to hunting clients. When he finally circulated a superior Greek specimen, the traders delivered a crushing verdict: both Greek and Latin were unsaleable. The city was flooded with works from across Europe; he should have arrived a year prior.

Desperate, Gerard purchased a psaltery. His landlady, charmed by his demeanor, often offered words of kindness. One evening, moved by his evident despondency, she invited him to dine and gently probed the cause of his gloom. He confessed his struggles, and she offered her interpretation. "Those sly traders," she declared, "employ scribes in their pay, paying nobly for their labor while offering you a pittance. No wonder they blow cold on you. Methinks you write too well. Do you not lock your door, like the churl Pietro? Women are naturally curious. Yes, you write too beautifully for them."

Gerard sought clarification. "Pray, why?"
"Because," she replied, "your fine work might blind those who sell inferior goods."
Gerard sighed. "Alas, Dame, you read people on the wrong side, yet you are so kind and frank."
"My dear little heart," she countered, "Romans are a subtle race. I am a Siennese, blessed by the Virgin. Your error was leaving Augsburg."
"Augsburg?" she scoffed haughtily. "A place to compete with Rome? I have never heard of it."

Despite the book sellers' lies, she assured him of his future fortune. "Seeing thee a stranger, they deceive thee without sense or discretion. All the world knows our great folk are bitten by the writing spider, pouring out money like water to convert lands and houses into scribbles kept in chests. God help them from this madness! Since the advent of scribbling, there has been less cutting and stabbing among rival factions, less vendetta. Two men prove this: Eminence Cardinal Bassarion and the Pope himself. They could keep a score like thee working night and day. I spoke to Teresa; she confirms five more signori are similarly afflicted."

The following day, armed with Teresa's gossip, Gerard gathered the names of five additional patrons. He purchased parchment and prepared specimens, leaving a card with his name and address at each doorstep. Hope faded quickly. Day after day passed, leaving him heartsick.

It was a time when Margaret, far away in Rotterdam, fought valiant odds to feed her dependents, recently arrested for practicing medicine without a license rather than killing with it. Back in Rome, ruin seemed to stare Gerard in the face. To stave off starvation, he spent his afternoons transcribing canzonets and laying in playing cards to color, earning a meal a day. This ingenuity, however, bred further trouble. In the *camere locande*, the landlady dressed all meals, though lodgers supplied the provisions. Her sharp eyes soon detected Gerard's culinary contributions. When she confronted him, asking if he lacked shame, her brusque opening made him blush and tremble. Yet, her tone softened instantly, appealing to his common sense regarding adversity and hunger.

"Patience, my lad! Times will mend; meanwhile, I will feed you for the love of heaven."
"Nay, Hostess," Gerard protested. "My purse is not yet void, and it would add to my tribulation if true folk lost their due by me."
"Why, you are as mad as your neighbor Pietro, with his single bad picture."
"How do you know 'tis a bad picture?"
"Because no one buys it. There is one who lacks gift; he must don casque and glaive, carrying his panel as a shield."

Gerard listened intently. The landlady recounted Pietro's tale: a Florentine who arrived wealthy with an unfinished canvas, furnished a neat room opposite Gerard's, and displayed his completed work. Though offers were reportedly generous, Pietro had refused them disdainfully, making enemies. Since then, he had dragged the painting out repeatedly, only to return it defeated. Recently, furniture vanished from his room until he wore a single suit and slept atop a large chest. The landlady peered through the keyhole, noting how vigilantly he locked his door. "Is he afraid thieves will steal his chest or picture, that no soul in Rome is weak enough to buy?"
"No, sweet Hostess," Gerard mused. "See you not 'tis his poverty he screens from view?"
"And the more fool he! Are all our hearts as ill as his? A trial first, anyway."
"How you speak of him. Why, his case is mine; and your countryman to boot."
"Oh, Siennese love strangers. His case yours? Nay, 'tis the contrary. You are the comeliest youth ever lodged here; hair like gold. He is a dark, sour-visaged loon. Besides, you know how to charm a woman on her better side; not he. Nevertheless, I cannot bear to see a starveling die in my house and tarnish my reputation. One starving guest suffices. You are far from home, and it falls to me, the mistress, to count your meals—for me and the Dutch wife, your mother, afar off. We two women shall settle that. Mind your business, being a man, and leave cooking to us, who exist merely to roast fowl and suckle grown men, sweeping up their cobwebs."

"Dear kind Dame," Gerard said, "you truly remind me of my mother."
"All the better," she beamed. "I shall put you more in mind of her before I finish."

Gerard, neither an egotist nor blinded by female partiality, saw his own grief mirrored in poor, proud Pietro. Resolving to share his scant means, he attempted to intercept the artist, but failed. One afternoon, a groan echoed from the garret. Gerard knocked, receiving no answer, then knocked again. A gruff voice commanded him to enter. Timidly, Gerard stepped inside to find a garret furnished sparsely with a chair, a picture facing the wall, an iron basin, an easel, and a long chest. Coiled upon it sat a haggard young man with a brilliantly bright eye, resembling a cobra ready to strike.

"Good Signor Pietro," Gerard began, "forgive me that, weary of solitude, I intrude on yours. I am your nearest neighbor and, methinks, your brother in fortune. I am an artist too."
"You are a painter? Welcome, signer. Sit down on my bed."
With a magnificent gesture, Pietro leaped off and waved him onto the vacant "throne." Gerard bowed and smiled, yet hesitated. "I may not call myself a painter. I am a writer, a calligrapher. I copy Greek and Latin manuscripts when commissioned."
"And you call that an artist?"
"Without offense to your superior merit, Signor Pietro..."
"No offense, stranger. Only, I deem an artist one who thinks and paints his thought. Now a calligrapher but draws thoughts of others in black and white."
"'Tis well distinguished, signor. But a writer can chronicle the thoughts of ancient giants and matters of pure reason, inaccessible to paint—nay, the thoughts of God, which angels could not depict. Let that pass. I am a painter as well; but a sorry one."
"The better thy luck. 'They will buy thy work in Rome.'"
"But seeking favor from one of thy eminence, I deemed it wise to present myself as a capable writer rather than a scurvy painter."

Just then, footsteps sounded on the stairs. "Ah! 'tis the good dame!" cried Gerard. "Hostess, I am conversing with Signor Pietro. I trust he permits me to take my humble dinner here."
The Italian bowed gravely.
The landlady entered with Gerard's dinner, steaming and savory. She set the dish upon the bed with an expressionless face and departed.
Gerard ate, but midway, he paused. "I am an ill-mannered churl, Signor Pietro. I never eat to my satisfaction when alone. For our Lady's sake, lend me a spoon for this ragout; 'tis not unsavory, I promise."
Pietro fixed his glittering eye upon him.
"What, good youth, thou a stranger, and offerest me thy dinner?"
"Why, see, there is more than one who can eat."
"Well, I accept," said Pietro, taking the dish with feigned calm, then flinging the entire contents out the window.
Turning, trembling with mortification and wrath, he declared, "Let that teach thee to offer alms to an artist thou knowest not, master writer."

Gerard's

A week had passed since their last meeting, yet the atmosphere within the lodging remained unchanged, if not more so. Seated side by side, Pietro and Gerard worked, though their spirits diverged sharply. Pietro attacked his canvas with erratic bursts of energy; he would produce a marvel in minutes, only to abandon it in a fit of rage, muttering curses against the medium before resuming his labor with renewed fury, ending each cycle with a heavy groan. In stark contrast, the shrewd Dutchman worked with a calm, unyielding steadiness, a quiet smile playing upon his lips as he guided his brush. To be blunt, Gerard had already tamed his companion; the friendship was, in truth, a partnership where Gerard held the reins, guiding Pietro's raw talent through the harness of discipline. Their craft was simple but effective: they painted playing cards to "boil the pot."

Once finished, the indignant master seized his creation to begin his daily rounds in search of a buyer. Gerard beseeched him to take the deck as well, urging him to try selling them. At first, Pietro recoiled, his expression contorted in a look of pure disdain, resembling a rattlesnake poised to strike. Yet, the warmth of the clime and the weight of Gerard's plea softened him. Eventually, embracing Gerard in the traditional Italian fashion, he agreed, first drying the freshly completed cards in the fierce sunlight that bathed their happy corner of the world.

Left alone, Gerard sketched a few Greek letters, then repaired a tear in his hose. The landlady observed this humble industry and, with an ironic lift of her brow, inquired whether there were truly no women in the house. "When you have finished that," she declared, "descend and speak with Teresa, my friend—of whom I have spoken. She possesses a husband who claims acquaintance with the great, though he is far from being such himself."

Gerard descended, and lo, standing before him was the very Roman matron he sought. "Ah, Madam," he greeted her warmly, "you? The good lady gave me no word of your presence. And the little fair-haired boy—is he well? Did the voyage in that strange vessel harm him?"

"He is well," the matron replied simply.

"Why, what are you two discussing?" the landlady interjected, staring alternately at Gerard and Teresa with wide eyes. "And why do you tremble so, *mia Teresa*?"

"He saved my child's life," Teresa answered, struggling to compose herself.

"What!" the landlady exclaimed. "My lodger? And he never uttered a word of this to me? Are you not ashamed to look me in the face?"

"Alas, speak not harshly to him," the matron pleaded gently. Turning to her friend, she poured forth a glowing account of Gerard's conduct, describing the selfless care he had shown. As she spoke, Gerard stood crimson, blushing like a schoolgirl, scarcely recognizing his own actions until gratitude painted them anew in her retelling.

"And to think you asked me to serve your lodger," Teresa lamented, addressing the landlady, "of whom I knew naught save that you held him in high regard. That was enough for me. My dear youth, in serving you, I serve myself."

An eager dialogue ensued between the two women, outlining a strategy to breach the formidable wall of fees, commissions, and legal chicanery that separated patrons of art from unknown talents in the Eternal City. Teresa, however, smiled with a sadness that touched Gerard's heart as she reflected on his naive approach to presenting his work at the doors of the powerful.

"What!" she cried, her voice sharp with reproach. "Without promising a share to the servants? Without offering even a coin to those who might introduce your wares to the Signori? It is as if you had cast them into the Tiber!"

"Well-a-day!" sighed Gerard, his shoulders slumping. "Then how shall an artist find a patron? For artists are poor, not rich."

"By seeking a city nobler, less greedy than this," Teresa advised wisely. "'La corte Romana non vuol' pecora senza lana.'"

She fell into contemplation, declaring she would return the next day. The landlady, sensing a shift in the wind, felicitated Gerard. "Teresa has something in her mind," she whispered, "something stirring."

Scarcely had Teresa departed when Pietro returned, carrying his picture, his countenance black as thunder. Gerard exchanged a glance with the landlady and followed him upstairs to offer comfort. "What, have they refused to accept your masterpiece?" Gerard asked.

"As heretofore," Pietro grumbled.

"More fools they, then."

"That is not the worse."

"Why, what troubles you?"

"They have bought the cards!" Pietro yelped, hammering the empty air furiously with his fists. "They were enraptured with them! They tried to hide their longing, but they could not. I saw it, I feigned ignorance, and I pillaged them! Curse those boobies!"

With dramatic flair, he flung a dozen small silver coins onto the floor, leaped upon them, dancing with basilisk-like eyes, kicking them assiduously until they spun and scattered across the room. Down knelt Gerard, following the maltreated innocents directly, transferring them tenderly back into his purse.

"You should have smiled at their ignorance and put it to profit," Gerard suggested softly.

"And so I will," Pietro vowed, his face a mask of concentrated indignation. "Those brutes! We shall paint a pack a day; we shall set the whole city gambling and ruining itself, living like princes upon their vices and stupidity." He paused, his gaze softening as he pointed to a specific card. "There was one queen, though—I had fain kept back. You limned her, brother. She possessed lovely red-brown hair, sapphire eyes, and above all, a soul."

"Pietro," Gerard said softly, "I painted that one from my heart."

The quick-witted Italian nodded, his eyes twinkling with a sudden understanding. "You love her so well, yet you leave her behind."

"Pietro," Gerard continued, his voice heavy with emotion, "it is precisely because I love her so dearly that I have wandered this weary road."

This poignant conversation was abruptly cut short by the landlady's cry from below: "Come down, you are wanted." Gerard obeyed, descending to find Teresa waiting once more.

"Come with me, Ser Gerard," she invited.

Gerard walked silently beside Teresa, his artist's mind speculating on her intentions regarding his future, though he dared not ask. Finally, she revealed her plan. A friend had informed her of a goldsmith's wife seeking a writer, noting her shop was nearby.

Soon, they arrived at the goldsmith's dwelling. Teresa introduced Gerard with fervent praise, claiming he had saved her child at sea, hoping to secure favor. The goldsmith's wife, however, showed little interest in a writer despite being captivated by Gerard's handsome face. She quickly dismissed the idea, stating her gossip, Claelia the sausage-maker, required someone. Teresa bowed politely and departed.

They journeyed to Claelia's distant home, only to find her absent. Teresa waited with composed dignity, causing Gerard to feel a peculiar unease as he sketched her secretly. Upon Claelia's return, Teresa repeated her plea, recounting Gerard's maritime rescue. Claelia, equally uninterested in hiring a writer, mentioned a friend named Appia Claudia, a tailor living in the Via Lepida, who needed assistance. Teresa retreated gracefully, leaving Gerard to express his concern about the tediousness of their quest.

"Patience is the price of everything," Teresa replied gently, promising justice if they knocked on every door in Rome. Gerard, however, grew anxious, pointing out a shadow following them. Teresa admitted coldly that it was her poor servant, Lodovico. As the figure approached reluctantly, Teresa introduced Gerard to him, revealing that he had saved Lodovico's wife and child. Lodovico's demeanor shifted instantly from artificial bowing to heartfelt gratitude, embracing Gerard warmly. Yet, Gerard remained unsettled by Lodovico's previous secretive behavior, though he accepted his presence. Their path led them onward, with Teresa adopting a somber air.

Their search eventually led to Lucretia, a cheerful keeper of a glove shop, who required a writer for her confessor, Father Colonna. Despite Lucretia's initial refusal due to vows of poverty, she relented upon hearing of Gerard's heroic act, assuring him that a Colonna would never lack funds. She spoke with amusement of Father Colonna's eccentricities, describing how he once dismissed a confession of murder with a perfunctory penance, seemingly lost in a realm above mundane concerns. Lucretia warned Gerard that while the friar welcomed all, he despised incompetence, citing an incident where he angrily rejected a poorly carved crucifix. Teresa, confident in Gerard's ability, promised to answer for him, but Gerard pleaded for delay, fearing the friar's exacting standards. Reluctantly, Teresa agreed to postpone the visit until the next day.

Back at Gerard's lodging, near a shop selling vellum, he found himself drawn to a beautiful white skin in the window. Though unable to afford it immediately, he resolved to purchase it later. Parting with Teresa at his doorstep, he rushed upstairs to examine a bond from Sevenbergen. Overcoming his aversion to deciphering difficult script, he read the document aloud: it detailed a financial arrangement between Ghysbrecht Van Swieten and Floris Brandt regarding a piece of land. Realizing the importance of the evidence, Gerard decided against destroying it, vowing instead to fully understand its contents at leisure. Determined to buy the vellum, he descended the stairs.

There, he encountered the landlady and Teresa. To his astonishment, she produced the very sheet of vellum he desired, explaining that she could not bear to see him want it. Overwhelmed by gratitude, Gerard struggled to formulate a payment, but Teresa merely wished him prosperity before departing.

The following afternoon, Gerard visited Lucretia and was guided to Father Colonna's lodgings. He presented his work, trembling with anticipation. The friar's chamber was a chaotic treasure trove of antiquities and manuscripts, reflecting a mind vast and distracted. After examining Gerard's handwriting with a critical eye, the friar delivered his verdict. Instead of rejection, Gerard received a Dominican embrace—a rare honor indeed.

Meanwhile, across the sea in Holland, a bizarre procession approached Eli's cottage. A grotesque figure, a composite of man and horse, demanded the delivery of their dwarf, Giles. Eli and his wife Catherine protested fiercely, citing the dangers of sending their child to the Duke's court, where innocent lives could be sacrificed. The messenger, representing absolute authority, threatened dire consequences unless compliance was immediate. Just as the argument reached its climax, Giles emerged from the house. Defying his parents' pleas and the risks involved, he declared his willingness to join the monarch's service, dismissing the dangers as trivial compared to the opportunity. Refusing the small mule intended for him, he insisted on riding the highest horse, demonstrating his agility by scaling the animal's flank and leaping from a window onto its back. With a final, tearful farewell from his grieving parents, Giles rode away, leaving behind a household plunged into sorrow, while he ascended toward a destiny fraught with peril and intrigue.

Happy is the man who holds two chains: one of merit, the other of women. Oh, that I possessed, like Gerard, a *chaine des dames* to hoist me upward by! With such an anchor, I should become a prose laureate, a professor of the spasmodic, or some other dignitary in no time at all. Until then, let me sketch the portrait of Fra Colonna.

The true resurrectors of ancient learning and philosophy were two fictional writers—Petrarch and Boccaccio. Their labors were not crowned with immediate, vast, or public triumph; yet they sowed the good seed, which never perished but quickened in the soil, waiting for the sun. From their day forward, Italy knew no lack of a native scholar or two versed in Greek, and every learned Greek who landed there was received with fraternal warmth. Before the fourteenth century closed, it saw the births of Poggio, Valla, and the elder Guarino; and early in the fifteenth, Florence under Cosimo de' Medici became a nest of Platonists. Headed by Gemistus Pletho, a born Greek, these scholars began around 1440 to transcribe Aristotle. For few minds are large enough to render justice to Great A without becoming unjust toward Capital B.

Theodore Gaza defended that great man with moderation; George of Trebizond attacked him with acerbity, retorting sharply against Plato. Then Cardinal Bessarion, another born Greek, rose to resist George and his idol in a tract titled *"Adversus calumniatorem Platonis."* Whether wise or not, pugnacity is a form of vitality. Born without controversial bile in so zealous an epoch, Francesco Colonna, a young Florentine nobleman, lived solely for the arts. At twenty, he turned Dominican friar. His aim was quiet study. He retreated from idle company and faction fights—the humming and stinging of the human hive—to St. Dominic and the Nine Muses.

An eager student of languages, pictures, statues, chronology, coins, and monumental inscriptions, these latter studies loosened his faith in popular histories. He traveled many years in the East, returning laden with spoils: master of several choice manuscripts, versed in Greek, Latin, Hebrew, and Syriac. He found his homeland had not stood still. Besides Cosimo, other lettered princes had risen: Alfonso, King of Naples; Niccolò d'Este; Lionel d'Este. Above all, his old friend Thomas of Sarzana had been made Pope, lending a mighty impulse to letters. He had amassed five thousand manuscripts in the Vatican library, setting Poggio to translate Diodorus Siculus and Xenophon's *Cyropaedia*, Laurentius Valla to translate Herodotus and Thucydides, Theodore Gaza to translate Theophrastus, and George of Trebizond to tackle Eusebius and various treatises of Plato.

The monk found Plato and Aristotle under truce, but Poggio and Valla at loggerheads over verbs and nouns, burning with philological hatred. All this was heaven, and he settled down in his native land, his life a rosy dream. None are so happy as the versatile, provided they need not earn their bread by it. And Fra Colonna was versatility. He knew seven or eight languages and a touch of mathematics; he could write, paint, model, sing, and strum, and he could relish superior excellence in all these branches. For this last trait, he deserved to be as happy as he was. Gauge the intellects of your acquaintances, and you will find few whose minds are neither deaf, nor blind, nor dead to some great art or science—"And wisdom at one entrance quite shut out." And such of them as are conceited as well as stupid shall parade instead of blushing for the holes in their intellects.

A zealot in art, the friar was a skeptic in religion. In every age, there are a few men who hold the opinions of another age, past or future. Being a lump of simplicity, his skepticism was as naive as his enthusiasm. He affected to view the religious ceremonies of his day as mere models, while heathen philosophers regarded the worship of gods and departed heroes as "mummeries" fit only for the populace. Yet here his mind drew an unconscious, droll distinction. Whatever Christian ceremony his learning taught him was of purely pagan origin; therefore, he respected it out of reverence for antiquity, though had he, with his turn of mind, been a pagan contemporary, he would have scorned it from his philosophic heights.

Fra Colonna was charmed with his new artist. Having access to half the palaces in Rome, he sounded Gerard's praises until the young man was soon summoned to resign him. "But I am so happy with you, Father," objected Gerard. "Fiddlesticks about being happy with me!" said Fra Colonna. "You must not be happy; you must be a man of the world. The grand lesson I impress on youth is: be a man of the world. Now these Montesini can pay you three times as much as I can, and they shall too—by Jupiter!"

And the friar slapped a terrific price on Gerard's pen. It was acceded to without a murmur. Much higher prices were being paid for copying than authorship had ever obtained for centuries under the printing press. Gerard received three hundred crowns for Aristotle's treatise on rhetoric. The great are mighty sweet upon all their pets while the fancy lasts; and in the fever for Greek manuscripts, the handsome writer soon became a pet, caressed by nobles of both sexes like a lapdog.

It might have turned a vain fellow's head, but the canny Dutchman saw the steel hand beneath the velvet glove and did not presume. Nevertheless, it was a proud day when he found himself seated with Fra Colonna at the table of his present employer, Cardinal Bessarion. They were perhaps a mile below the summit of that table, but never mind—they were there, and Gerard enjoyed the advantage of seeing roast pheasants served with all their feathers intact, as if they had just flown out of a coppice rather than off the spit, alongside chickens cooked in bottles, tender as peaches. But the grand novelty was the napkins, surpassingly fine and folded into cocked hats, bird's wings, fans, and other intricate shapes, instead of lying flat. This electrified Gerard, though readers may have witnessed the dazzling phenomenon without tumbling backward, chair and all.

After dinner, the tables were splintered and carried away, revealing another table spread with sweetmeats beneath. The *signore* and *signorine* fell upon them and gorged themselves, while the *signori* eyed them with reasonable suspicion. "But, dear Father," objected Gerard, "I see not the bifurcate daggers with which men say His Excellency arms the left hand of a guest." "Nay, 'twas Cardinal Orsini who invented that peevish instrument for guests to fumble their meat withal," replied the friar. "One, being in haste, did skewer his tongue to his palate with it, I hear; *O tempora, O mores!* How would the ancients, reclining godlike at their feasts, have spurned such pedantries?"

As soon as the ladies had disported themselves among the sugar-plums, the tables were suddenly removed, and the guests sat in a row against the wall. Then entered, ducking and scraping, two ecclesiastics with lutes, kneeling at the cardinal's feet to sing the service of the day before retiring with a deep obeisance. In answer, the cardinal fingered his skullcap as our late Iron Duke did his hat; the company dispersed, and Gerard had dined with a cardinal and one who had thrice almost become pope.

But greater honor was in store. One day the cardinal sent for him, praised the beauty of his work, and took him in his coach to the Vatican. Up a private stair, they reached a luxurious little room with a great oriel window. There were inkstands, sloping frames for writing, and all the instruments of art. The cardinal whispered to a courtier, and presently the Pope's private secretary appeared with a glorious, grimy old manuscript of Plutarch's *Lives*. Soon Gerard was seated alone, copying it, awe-struck yet half-delighted at the thought that His Holiness would handle his work and read it.

The papal inkstands were glorious externally, but within the ink was vile. Gerard, however, always carried good, home-made ink in a dirty little inkhorn. Praying on his knees for a firm and skillful hand, he set to work. One side of his room was nearly occupied by a massive curtain divided in the center, but its ample folds overlapped. After a while, Gerard felt drawn to peek through it. He resisted the impulse. It returned. It overpowered him. Leaving Plutarch, he stole across the matted floor, took the folds of the curtain, and gently gathered them up with his fingers. Putting his nose through the chink, he ran it against a cold steel halbert. Two soldiers, armed cap-a-pie, held their glittering weapons crossed in a triangle. Gerard drew swiftly back, but in that instant he heard the soft murmur of voices and saw a group of persons cringing before some hidden figure.

He never again attempted to pry through the guarded curtain, but often eyed it. Every hour or so an ecclesiastic would peep in, eye him, chill him, and exit—all gloomy and mechanical. But the next

Fra Colonna held the keys to the Pope's library, a privilege that allowed him to wander the ancient streets alongside Gerard whenever the friar paused from his labors. To the happy artist, Rome was a vision of eternal beauty, a city where grandeur flowed from every ruin and palace. But Fra Colonna, the scholar, treated the splendor with a dampening skepticism.

"This place you call Rome?" the friar murmured, pointing to the subterranean remains of triumphal arches buried beneath the cobblestones. "It is merely the tomb of mighty Rome." He guided Gerard's gaze upward, revealing how modern thoroughfares trampled over the roofs of ancient palaces and the tops of shattered columns. Coupling the cramped confines of the medieval city with the colossal, fragmented ghosts of antiquity peeking through the cracks, the friar struck a striking comparison. "Consider this village, 'Rome,' as nothing more than a swallow's nest clinging to the decaying eaves of an ancient abbey."

"Old Rome must indeed have been magnificent," Gerard admitted.

"Judge for yourself, my son," the friar countered, leading him toward the great sewers—the engineering marvels of early Rome designed to outlive Mount Vesuvius—and the fragments of the Temple of Peace. "Imagine the Capitol with its twenty-five temples. Look at Monte Savello; what is it but the ruins of the ancient Theatre of Marcellus? And Testaccio, one of the highest hills today? It was once a garbage heap where the women of old Rome discarded their broken pottery. Lo and behold, a mountain formed."

*"Ex pede Herculem"*—"From the foot, judge the giant"—the friar declared.

Gerard listened with respectful deference until the holy man began to draw a moral parallel, suggesting that the pagan Romans possessed a superior morality proportional to their grand architecture. Gerard resisted firmly. "Has the world suffered a loss with the coming of Christ?" he asked, though he flushed immediately, realizing he was questioning his benefactor.

"Saints forbid!" cried the friar sharply. "'Twere heresy to suggest otherwise." Yet, having conceded this point directly, he quickly retreated via subtle circumlocutions, guiding Gerard toward a forbidden door accessed by a spiral staircase. Inside lay a church crowded with a knot of onlookers gathered around a porch where a demon was being exorcised. Fra Colonna had a peculiar habit of emitting a low, mournful sound—a curdling moan—when confronted with facts that Zeno or Epicurus would reject. This noise conveyed not only sharp dissent but a profound pity for human credulity, ignorance, and error, particularly when such blindness obscured the merits of paganism.

The friar sighed and urged departure. "Then let us withdraw."

"Nay, Father! Pray, stay! I have never witnessed a devil cast out."

Reluctantly, the friar followed Gerard inside, giving a final, heavy shrug. Within, the demoniac knelt before the altar, a scarf tightly bound around his neck, held fast like a hound on a leash by the officiating priest. Few souls were present, for rumors circulated that the last demon expelled from this church fled no further than the next burrow, like a hare driven from one hole straight into another.

When the pair arrived, the priest, sensing sufficient spectators, commenced his ritual. He faced the prostrate man, breviary in hand, beginning with the arduous task of identifying the spirit tormenting the victim.

"Come out, Ashtaroth! Oh, it is not you. Come out, Belial! Come out, Tatzi! Come out, Eza! No, he trembles not. Come out, Azymoth! Come out, Feriander! Come out, Foletho! Come out, Astyma! Come out, Nebul!"

"Aha!" the priest exclaimed, his voice rising. "Have I found thee? 'Tis thou, thou reptile, at thy old tricks! Let us pray!"

"Oh, Lord, we pray thee to drive the foul fiend Nebul from this Thy creature! From his hair, his eyes, his nose, his mouth, his ears, his gums, his teeth, his shoulders, his arms, his legs, his loins, his stomach, his bowels, his thighs, his knees, his calves, his feet, his ankles, his finger-nails, his toe-nails, and from his soul! Amen."

The priest rose, turning to the congregation with a quiet geniality. "Gentlemen, we have here a devil as obstinate as one might encounter on a summer's day." Facing the patient, he alternated between rigorous addresses to the man and the fiend. Through the same mouth, the victim now cursed the holy names the priest invoked, now whimpered complaints of internal misery.

It was the priest who first confused the victim with the culprit, physically attacking the former—he cuffed him, kicked him, spat repeatedly in his face. He lit a candle, deliberately extinguished it, burned it until his fingers singed, then dropped it swiftly. He produced the *custodia*, displaying the Holy Corpore within, before burning another candle with greater caution. Finally, speaking civilly to the demoniac in his human guise, he dismissed him and accepted the applause of the crowd.

"Good father," Gerard observed, "how you have their names committed to memory. Our northern priests possess no such exquisite knowledge of the infernal squadrons."

"Aye, young man," the friar replied, "here we know all their names and their methods; these reptiles. This Nebul is a bitter, hard one to track down."

He then shared several anecdotes with the assembly, concluding with a feat from the previous day: driving a hulking fiend from a woman by her mouth, leaving behind nails, pins, and a tuft of hair, as the creature shrieked in anguish, "It is not thou that conquerest me! Behold that stone on the windowsill! Know that the angel Gabriel, descending to earth, alighted upon it—it is that stone which performed my business!"

The friar let out another mournful sigh. "And you believed him?"

"Certainly! Who, save an infidel, would discredit a revelation so precise?"

"What? Believe the father of lies? That pushes credulity beyond endurance."

"Oh, a liar does not always lie."

"Aye? Whenever he begins with an improbable tale and produces a holy relic, he arms you against the Satanic host. If fiends existed, they would not be so simple-minded. Thou shouldst have answered him from antiquity: *Timeo Danaos et dona ferentes*—I fear the Greeks bearing gifts."

"Some blackguard chopped his wife's head off on that stone, young man! You take my word for it." And the friar hurried Gerard away.

"Alas, Father, I fear you have shamed the good priest."

"Aye, by Pollux!" chuckled the friar. "I blistered him with a single touch of 'Socratic interrogation.' What modern mind can parry the weapons of antiquity?"

Later, one afternoon, after Gerard completed his daily tasks, a fine lackey summoned him to the Palazzo Cesarini. Gerard was ushered into a noble chamber where a young girl sat working on a tapestry. She rose and departed, promising to inform her mistress. An hour passed, and Gerard began to fret, wondering if nobles valued the time of a poor craftsman. Just as he resolved to slip away, the door opened, admitting a superlative beauty followed by two maids. It was the young Princess of the House of Cesarini. Though she spoke loudly and haughtily to her attendants, her demeanor softened instantly upon seeing Gerard, lowering her voice to a feminine whisper. "Are you the writer, Messer?"

"I am, Signora."

"'Tis well."

She seated herself while Gerard and the maids remained standing. "What is your name, good youth?"

"Gerard, Signora."

"Gerard? Body of Bacchus! Is that the name of a human creature?"

"It is a Dutch name, Signora. I was born at Terghou, in Holland."

"A harsh name, girls, for so well-favored a youth," she mused, "what say you?"

The maids assented warmly.

"What did I summon you for?" the lady asked, her expression shifting to lofty languor. "Ah, I remember. Sit, Ser Gerardo, and write me a letter to Ercole Orsini, my lover—or at least, that is what he claims."

Gerard sat, taking out paper and ink, looking to the princess for instructions. She sat on a high chair, almost a throne, gazing down at him with inquisitive eyes.

"Well, Gerardo?"

"I am ready, Your Excellence."

"Write, then."

"I await the words."

"And who, pray, is to provide them?"

"Who but Your Grace, whose letter it is to be?"

"Gramercy! What, you writers, find you not the words? What avail is your art without the substance? I suspect you are an impostor, Gerardo."

"Nay, Signora, I am no impostor. I might translate your speech into grammar as readily as I commit it to parchment. But I cannot interpret your silence. Therefore, speak what is in your heart, and I will transcribe it before your eyes."

"But there is nothing in my heart. Sometimes I think I have no heart at all."

"What is in your

Princess Claelia commanded a full-length portrait of herself. Gerard suggested employing his acquaintance, Pietro Vanucci.

She rejected the notion immediately. "'Twill be time to cast a slight upon the Gerardo," she declared, "when his work displeases me." Then Gerard, acknowledging his prowess as a draughtsman but admitting his inferiority as a colorist, beseeched her to pose as a Roman statue. He demonstrated how faithfully he could imitate marble upon paper. She agreed initially, but hesitated when the enthusiastic artist explained that she must don the tunic, toga, and sandals of antiquity.

"Why, I had as lief be presented in my smock," she remarked with medieval frankness.

"Alack, signorina!" Gerard replied. "You have surely never observed ancient custom; it is so free, so ample, so simple, yet so noble. And it suits your Highness perfectly, to whom Heaven has granted Roman features, a shapely arm, and a graceful hand, albeit in modern guise."

"What?" Gerard flattered her, like the rest. "Well, grant me time to consider. Return on Saturday, and I shall decide."

During this respite, the garments were crafted and fitted in her chamber to ensure they compensated for their thousand-year obsolescence.

Hurrying toward this appointment, Gerard was suddenly halted, rooted to the spot before a shop window. His keen eye had spotted an open copy of Lactantius. "That is fairly written, at least," he thought.

He scrutinized it a moment longer.

It was not written. It was printed.

Gerard groaned inwardly. "I am undone; my enemy stands at the door. The press has arrived in Rome."

Entering the shop, he feigned nonchalance to inquire how long the printing press had been in the city. The merchant claimed there was no press there. "Oh, the Lactantius," the man corrected, "was printed atop the Apennines."

"What? Did the printing press tumble down from the moon?"

"Nay, messer," the trader laughed, pointing to the title page. "It sprang up from Germany. See!"

Gerard snatched the book eagerly, reading the imprint: *Opera et impensis Sweynheim et Pannartz... Impressum Subiacis. A.D. 1465.*

"Shall I purchase it, messer?" the seller asked, highlighting the fairness of the letters. "Few can write like that today, and it costs but a quarter of the price."

"I would gladly buy it," Gerard said sadly, "but my heart forbids it. Know that I am a calligrapher, and these disciples of Fust pursue me worldwide, stealing my bread. Yet I wish them no ill. Heaven forbid!" He hastened from the shop.

"My dear Margaret," he whispered to himself. "We must lose no time. We must gather our harvest while the sun shines. Within a month, an avalanche of printer's type shall descend upon Rome from those mountains and destroy us, those who write."

He nearly ran to the Princess Claelia.

He was led into a private apartment unfamiliar to him. Though not vast, it was sumptuous; a fountain bubbled in the center, and the floor was paved with dressed panther skins, ensuring silence underfoot. It served as an antechamber to her boudoir, separated by a magnificent tapestry curtain.

Left alone, Gerard grew uneasy, doubting if he had been misdirected. These fears vanished quickly.

A light step approached behind the curtain, which parted to reveal a figure the pagans might have worshiped. She was neither entirely Venus nor Minerva, but possessed a nobility surpassing Venus and a femininity exceeding Jove's daughter. Clad in toga, tunic, and sandals, she was wholly antique. As for beauty, she transcended time.

Gerard sprang forward, the artist within him flushing with delight.

"Oh!" he cried innocently, gazing in rapture.

This reaction added the final charm to his model. A light blush colored her cheeks, her eyes brightened, and her mouth curved in a smile of delicious self-satisfaction at his genuine tribute.

For some time, they gazed at one another until the Princess realized Gerard's eloquence consisted merely of ejaculations and staring. She spoke. "Well, Gerardo, thou seest I have fashioned an antique monster for thee."

"A monster? I doubt Fra Colonna would prostrate himself in adoration, beholding your Highness thus attired."

"Nay, I care not to be adored by an old man. I prefer to be loved by a youth of my own choosing."

Gerard set up his pencils and prepared his canvas, covered with sturdy paper. So absorbed was he that he showed no mercy to his model. After nearly an hour in one posture, she spoke faintly, "Gerardo, I can stand thus no longer, even for thee."

"Sit down and rest awhile, Signora."

"I thank thee," she said, sinking into a chair, turning pale and sighing.

Gerard was alarmed, realizing his inconsiderateness. He drew water from the fountain to moisten her brow, but she raised a white hand in protest. "Nay, hold it to my forehead with thine hand; do not fling it at me!"

Timidly, he applied the wet cloth. "Ah!" she sighed, feeling relief. "Again."

He repeated the action. She thanked him and requested a handbell on the table. A maid arrived, sent to summon Floretta with orders to bring a large fan.

Floretta appeared swiftly. As soon as she neared the Princess, the lady's aristocratic nostrils flared wide like a bloodhorse's. "Wretch!" she cried, springing to her feet with renewed vigor. Seizing Floretta by the left arm, she twisted it violently into her hair, then struck her ears three times with the right hand.

Floretta screamed and wept, receiving no mercy. The antique toga, now loose, revealed a bare arm that seemed as powerful as it was beautiful. It rose and fell like the piston of a steam engine, delivering heavy slaps that echoed against Floretta's shoulders. The final blow drove her sobbing and screaming through the tapestry curtain, where she continued to cry bitterly for some time.

"Saints of heaven!" cried Gerard. "What is amiss? What has she done?"

"She knows well enough. 'Tis not the first time. That nasty toad! I shall teach her to approach me reeking of musk-cats."

"Alas, Signora, 'twas a minor fault, I thought."

"A minor fault? Nay, 'twas a grievous offense." Her tone shifted instantly to humility and sweetness. "Art thou angry with me for beating her, Gerardo?"

"Signora, it ill becomes me to chastise thee. Yet methinks those appointed to govern others must first govern themselves."

"That is true, Gerardo. How wise thou art, being so young." Calling the other maid, she gave her a purse. "Take this to Floretta and tell her 'the Gerardo' interceded for her; therefore I must pardon her. There, Gerardo."

Gerard blushed profusely at the compliment, unable to formulate a fitting response. He asked if he should resume her portrait.

"Not yet," she replied. "Beating that hussy has somewhat enlivened me. I shall sit awhile, and thou shalt converse with me. I know thou canst speak, as rarely as thou drawest."

"That were easily done."

"Do it then, Gerardo."

Gerard was taken aback. "But, signora, I know not what to say. This is sudden."

"Speak thy true mind. Say thou wishest to be elsewhere."

"Nay, signora, that would not be truthful. I desire one thing, however."

"And what is it?" she asked gently.

"I wish I could have painted thee as thou wast beating that poor maiden. Thou wast awful, yet lovely. Oh, what a subject for a Pythoness!"

"Alas! he thinks but of his art. And why all this fuss about my beauty, Gerardo? Thou art far fairer than I. Thou resemblest Apollo more than I do Venus. Moreover, thou hast lovely hair and eyes—though I suspect thou knowest not how to wield them."

"Ay, do I. To paint thee, signora."

"Ah, yes; with them thou canst perceive my features, but thou canst not see what any Roman suitor long ago perceived in thy stead. Yet surely thou must have noticed how welcome thou art to me, Gerardo?"

"I behold that your Highness is perpetually kind to me, a poor stranger."

"No, Gerardo. I have often been cold to thee; sometimes rude. And thou art so simple thou perceivest not the cause. Alas! I feared for mine own heart. I feared to become thy slave. I, who have hitherto enslaved others. Ah! Gerardo, I am unhappy. Since thou arrived, I have lived upon thy visits. On the day thou comest, I am radiant; on other days, listless, wishing them gone. Thou art not like the Roman suitors. They make me hate them. Thou art tenfold braver in mine eyes; wise, scholarly, and never flattering or lying. I scorn a liar. Gerardo, teach me thy magic; teach me to render thee as

Gerard woke not to hope, but to a hollow, suffocating despair. By the second day, his fever burned through his skull, leaving him ravaged and delirious. Beside his bed lay the tangle of his own rich auburn hair, once long and flowing like a woman's, now discarded. His condition deteriorated with terrifying speed; the leeches retreated on the fifth day, declaring their work futile. As the sun dipped below the horizon, Gerard succumbed to a profound slumber. Whispers circulated that he would awaken only to meet his end, yet an elderly woman, a professional nurse whose counsel held sway, offered a glimmer of faith. "His youth may still save him," she murmured, "if he can endure twelve hours of sleep."

His old landlady, moved by devotion, swept away the clutter and stood vigil beside his cot. She vowed a wax candle to the Virgin Mary for every passing hour of rest. Twelve hours passed, and the good soul knelt in gratitude, thanking Heaven. Twenty-four followed, though the nurse's joy began to curdle into doubt. By the thirtieth hour, she summoned the physician. "Shall we disturb him?" she asked anxiously. The doctor, a man of science, examined Gerard closely. "His breathing is steady, his hands warm," he observed, dismissing the learned physicians who would have pricked the patient to check his tongue. "Women possess a wisdom men lack," he reasoned gently. "Sleep has never harmed a fractured mind or a shattered heart."

Forty-eight hours later, curiosity became a nuisance, and the landlady struggled to bar the nosing crowds. Eventually, only Fra Colonna and his massive companion, Fra Jerome, were permitted entry. They took over from the women, sitting in silence. Fra Colonna gazed at his youthful friend with tears welling in his eyes, while the giant friar held his beads, staring at Gerard with the same calm, unflinching gaze he had fixed upon the churning sea during their previous confrontation.

Around the sixtieth hour, the landlady nudged Fra Colonna. He glanced over. Gerard's eyelids fluttered open slowly, as if emerging from a gentle doze. He stared blankly, drawing himself upright in the straw-filled bed. A hand rose to his temples, and he gasped—the hair was gone. Yet, seeing Fra Colonna, a smile of pure relief broke across his face. Then, abruptly, that smile froze. His features twisted into a mask of unspeakable agony, a sharp cry tore from his throat, and he buried his face against the pillow.

The landlady, knowing the crushing weight of bereavement, wept silently. Fra Jerome began chanting psalms from his breviary, filling the air with holy sounds. Gerard curled into the corner of the bed, shivering. Fra Colonna approached, whispering with trembling lips that nothing was lost. "The divine Muses are immortal!" he urged, trying to comfort the broken boy. "Transfer your love to them; they will never betray you, unlike mortal flesh." Overcome by emotion, the simple priest hurried away.

Fra Jerome remained. "Young man," he intoned gravely, "the Muses dwell only in the fantasies of pagans and dreamers. The Church alone offers earthly peace and eternal bliss. If the world has deceived you, if passion has rent your heart, the Church opens her arms wide. Consecrate your talents to her!" With those solemn words, he departed before Gerard could respond.

"The Church!" Gerard screamed, springing up and shaking a fist at the retreating friar. "Curses on the Church! Had it not been for her, I would not lie broken here, while she lies cold, frozen, in Holland! Oh, Margaret! My darling! My love! And I ran from you, fleeing the few months you had left! Cruel, cruel monsters! They allowed her to die! Death always leaves a sign, but the blind, selfish fools saw nothing—or cared less. I would have seen it, I who loved her! If I had stayed, I would have saved her, I would have saved her! An idiot! A fool to leave her for a single moment!"

He wept bitterly, a sound of raw, animal grief. Suddenly, fury erupted within him again. "The Church!" he roared, his voice echoing off the walls. "For whose sake was I cast out? May its curses fall upon me and the hypocrites who wear its name to wound my broken spirit! Accursed be the world! Ghysbrecht lives, yet Margaret dies! Thieves, murderers, harlots thrive forever, while only angels perish! Cursed be life! Cursed be death! And cursed be whoever forged them into such monstrous shapes!"

The friar heard no more than the wild yell of rage that chased him from the room, and wisely so. To have heard Gerard's blasphemy would have led to immediate condemnation, perhaps even burning at the stake.

As Fra Colonna descended the stairs, the landlady lingered outside, hearing the raised voice. Returning in alarm, she found Gerard dressing, his countenance distorted by tears. "What use is this lying here?" he muttered, his voice heavy with gloom. "Will I find what I seek within these four walls?"

"Saints protect us! Is he mad again?" she stammered. "What do you seek?"

"Oblivion."

"Oh, child, how young you speak!" she cooed, trying to soothe him. "You are far too young to talk of giving up."

"Young or old, what purpose remains for me to live?" he asked, his eyes empty.

He donned his finest garments, the fabric seeming to strain under the weight of his sorrow. "My pretty Gerard," she warned, growing pale, "know that today is Tuesday, not Sunday."

"Oh, Tuesday?" he mused, his gaze distant. "I thought it were Saturday."

"Nay, you have slept far too long. Never wear your finery on a working day."

"What I did while she lived, I did. Now I shall do whatever I did not do before. The past is dust. Here lies my hair, and with it, my former life. I served one Master faithfully, and behold, this is His reward. Now I shall serve another, and grant Him a fair trial."

"Alas!" she sighed, her voice trembling, "What darkness clouds your words? And who is this new Master whose service you wish to try?"

With that terrible confession hanging on his lips, the wretched boy walked out. His cap and feather sat jauntily askew, framing a face pale as ash and drawn tight by invisible lines of age. His limbs were weak, yet he moved forward, a sinewy shadow fleeing the light of God.

A shadow, heavy and suffocating, descended upon the mind of Gerard. For years, his love for Margaret had served as his north star, guiding his actions with unwavering purity. Now, that light was extinguished. He was adrift upon a vast, gloomy ocean where hope had perished, leaving only the endless, churning waves of despair.

Yet, his torment was not merely sorrow; it was a searing rage. He felt a profound, maddening exasperation at the self-denial he had endured, at the fortitude and dangers he had risked, and at the virtue he had sacrificed—all for nothing. The thought gnawed at him with relentless cruelty: if he had remained lazy, selfish, and comfortable at home, he might have saved Margaret's life.

These twin poisons, despair and bitter regret, boiled in his young blood, driving him to a fever pitch. He plunged headlong into pleasure, embracing it with a desperate ferocity. In those days, pleasure was indistinguishable from vice. He sought wine, women, gambling, and anything capable of procuring an hour of frantic excitement or a fleeting moment of oblivion. He dove into these vices as a drowning man rushes blindly into the mouths of enemies, hoping to escape the water by falling deeper into the mud.

The substantial sum he had set aside for Margaret now fueled his debauchery. Soon, he was the acknowledged leader of a circle of loose companions, men he had once respected and avoided. His heart, already fragile, began to rot alongside his morals. He grew sullen toward his former landlady, resenting her gentle advice and warnings, until he finally packed his bags and moved to a different quarter of the town, determined to escape her reproaches and the painful memories attached to her household.

Time wore down his resolve. His hand, once steady, began to tremble, rendering the pen useless for writing letters to comfort his conscience. As his patience eroded under the weight of his new habits, he abandoned the art of drawing in ink and took up painting in colors. Yet, even this creative outlet slipped from his grasp whenever his idle associates arrived to drag him back into the fray.

Thus, he sank into foul waters, searching for a shell of oblivion that would never hold. It was not necessary to detail every scene of coarse vice in which this unhappy young man now participated. What mattered was the undeniable truth: Gerard Vanucci had become a rake, a drunkard, and one of the most wicked and reckless youths in Rome.

Those who hide the sins of the fallen, claiming they wish to protect the reputation of the virtuous, ultimately rob despairing sinners of hope. Gerard was not a man who acted by halves; neither was his fall.

News of his degradation spread quickly through the city. His humble female friends whispered of his decline. His old landlady, Teresa, spoke to her husband, Lodovico, praying him to track down the lost youth. "Do you remember his face, Lodovico?" she asked. "Lodovico mio, a man of our trade never forgets a face, especially that of a benefactor. But you know I rarely venture abroad by daylight."

"Teresa," Lodovico replied with a sigh, "how long will this last?"

"Until some cavalier drives his sword through me," Lodovico answered grimly. "They will not allow a poor fellow like me to practice any honest trade."

Gerard, however, was not alone in his ruin. Pietro Vanucci, a man who bore adversity far better than prosperity, had recently been ejected from their shared lodging by the landlady for his late-night revelries. Upon crossing paths with Gerard in the street, Vanucci greeted him warmly, and before long, they were sharing quarters once more.

Vanucci brought with him a young apprentice named Andrea, who ground his pigments and served as both pupil and model. Andrea was a youth of striking beauty, sharp-tongued and quick-witted, possessing a vitality that contrasted sharply with Gerard's decay. Though Gerard had not entirely erased the warmth of his old friendships, he offered only a cold, distant politeness to Vanucci's advances.

Together, they formed a bizarre pair: Vanucci, a libertine, half-misanthrope, and avowed atheist; Gerard, a man whose spirit had been hollowed out by grief. They ran together with little in common, a rare phenomenon in the crowded streets of Rome.

One day, having squandered most of his remaining fortune and further undermined his already frail health, Gerard joined Vanucci in organizing a wild excursion up the Tiber. The plan involved a magnificent galley drawn by twelve buffaloes—a novelty imported from Florence by Lorenzo de' Medici. The libertines gathered with their chosen companions, each required to bring a beautiful lady. The prize for presenting the fairest lady was a crown of laurel and the title of public benefactor.

Gerard was notably absent from the initial gathering. Vanucci, feigning concern, claimed Gerard had forgotten to acquire a *fardingale* (a stiffened skirt worn by Renaissance women), suggesting the young man was still searching for a partner worthy of the company. Finally, Gerard appeared, accompanied by a woman of breathtaking beauty.

She was tall and statuesque, yet possessed a fluid grace like a young panther. Her features were flawless: a perfect oval face, a pale brow, olive cheeks flushed with vibrant life, and eyes framed by long, thick lashes that seemed designed to captivate any observer. Her lips were a saucy red, her teeth the color of polished ivory.

As they approached the boat, the effect was instantaneous. The women, previously confident, were silenced and depressed by this vision. The men erupted into cheers, waving their hats. One enthusiastic fool knelt on the gunwale, declaring her of divine origin, only to be gently rebuked by his companion and the goddess herself, who then took her seat at the prow with regal indifference. Pietro Vanucci sat slumped beside her, staring with his mouth agape like a stunned fish.

"The drover speaks to the beasts," Vanucci announced, "and the ropes tighten."

The buffaloes moved upstream. Vanucci turned to his companions. "What say you of this new beef, ladies?"

"We have never seen cattle so vile!" one lady cried, pointing at the animals' massive horns and foul nostrils. "Are those holes or noses?"

Another interjected, "They are so dull. Last time, I ascended the Tiber twice as fast with just five mules and an ass."

"Nonsense," a gallant retorted. Drawing his sword, he leaped onto the muddy bank, ignoring the drivers' protests, and charged the herd, goading them forward.

The buffaloes snorted and swished their tails, moving with sluggish momentum. The crowd laughed loudly. Suddenly, the leader of the bulls, a patriarch of immense size, swung its head. Its horn pierced the spark's doublet, sending the man flying high into the air. He landed sitting in the yellow water, his glittering sword waving unconsciously, soaking wet.

The ladies shrieked and clutched their hands, except for Gerard's companion. She cursed softly and seized the helm, steering the boat away from the chaos. The soaked cavalier scrambled aboard, gripping the gunwale.

"What is this?" he spluttered, confused and peevish. "I cannot understand it. One moment I am pricking African oxen, and the next..."

Gerard's companion, with dry wit, suggested he ask the Tiber. "Perhaps you should try pricking them again, Signor, until the matter becomes clear to you, bit by bit, as lawyers ascend to heaven and buffaloes cross the Tiber."

A knight nearby declared that she surpassed the sons of Adam in wisdom as much as she exceeded the daughters of Eve in beauty. At these compliments, Pietro Vanucci's behavior shifted dramatically. No longer gaping in shock, he sat coiled like a snake, his mouth hidden, two bright eyes twinkling comically as they fixed upon the lady.

However, the lady's gaze remained calm, sweeping over him without recognition or amusement, treating him as little more than furniture.

Suddenly, the harmony of the party fractured. Mortified by the overwhelming attention paid to Gerard's companion, the other ladies began to whisper—and eventually shout—their criticisms. The beautiful stranger proved poor in feminine tact; instead of retaliating through the men, she listened and openly defended herself against her detractors.

"There is not one of you wearing Nature's colors!" she declared, pointing accusingly. "Behold," she continued, exposing the makeup beneath a rival's face. "This is *cerussa*, this is *stibium*, and this is *purpurissum*. I know these articles well—I use them daily—but never on my face."

Her words caused Vanucci's eyes to sparkle with delight. "Why, your lips are colored, and the veins on your forehead are visible!" another lady shouted. "And look at your coarse black hair, drugged to resemble tow! Your bodies are as false as your hearts. Look at your padded bosoms and your wooden-heeled chopines, used to elevate your stunted limbs and deceive the world. Skinny dwarfs, cushioned and stultified into fat giants!"

"Avaunt, vulgar toad!" the accused retorted. "Tell the men everything, will you? Your cheeks are naturally ruddy, and your hair is but a handful of African wool. But who is more padded? You are shaped like a fire-shovel!"

"You lie, impudent girl!"

"Oh, the well-educated young person!" someone

Lady Beatrice sat in a chamber draped with the warm hues of animal skins, her posture rigid, her hands half-clenched as if grasping at air invisible to others. Her countenance shifted rapidly between pallor and crimson flush, betraying a turmoil that mirrored the restless tapping of her foot. Suddenly, a servant pulled aside the heavy curtains. Beatrice's brow flared, and she motioned for entry. "Admit him," she commanded, snatching a small black mask from the table and pressing it over her face. In an instant, the blood drained from her cheeks, leaving a stark, terrifying contrast between the jet-black veil and her marble-like skin.

The intruder bowed deeply, a man cut from the common mold—short-haired, round-faced, dressed plainly yet decently. His features were unremarkable save for a pair of eyes that seemed perpetually unsettled. After a flurry of curtsies, he stood opposite her, awaiting instruction.

"Have they told you why you are summoned?" Beatrice asked.

"Yes, Signora."

"Do those who sent you concur regarding the sum you shall receive?"

"Yes, Signora. It is the full price, which secures the greater vendetta. Unless, by your benevolence, you wish to accept the lesser."

"I do not comprehend you."

"The lesser vendetta claims only the body," the man explained gently. "We observe him emerging from a church or striking him down in some unsuspecting moment. But the greater vendetta watches for him fresh from unrepented sin, slaying both his soul and his flesh. Yet, alas, such vendettas are less frequent today than they were a few years past."

"Silence his tongue," Beatrice whispered fiercely, her voice trembling with resolve. "Let his treasonous heart cease beating. But his soul... with that I bear no grudge."

"So be it, Signora," the bravo replied. "My employer knows not the man's name, nor his dwelling. From whom shall I glean these details?"

"From myself."

Anxiety flickered in the assassin's gaze. "Pray, let me entreat caution and precision in this matter."

"Fear me not," Beatrice said, leaning forward. "He is a youth of tall stature, with auburn hair and dark blue eyes, possessing a face so honest it might deceive a saint. He resides in the Via Claudia, at the corner house occupied by the glovers. Within live but three men: himself, a painter of short build and swarthy complexion, and a slender lad. My betrayer is a stranger, handsome and taller than you."

The bravo leaned in, listening intently. "Is that sufficient?"

"Hold," Beatrice interrupted. "Does he frequent any secluded haunt where I may direct your blow?"

"My spies report that recently he has wandered the banks of the Tiber after dusk," she continued, her voice rising. "Presumably to meet his light lover, who dares call herself my rival. Slay him there! Let my rival return to find nothing but a corpse—the smooth, heartless, insolent traitor!"

"Be composed, Signora. He will betray no other ladies."

"I know not that! He wears a sword and wields it well. He is young and resolute."

"Neither weapon nor spirit shall avail him."

"Do you truly trust in your aim? What manner of weapons do you employ?"

The bravo produced a steel gauntlet. "We strike with such ferocity that protection is essential. Consider this our mallet." He unbuttoned his doublet to reveal armor beneath, then laid a glittering stiletto upon the table with a flourish.

Beatrice shivered initially, then seized the blade in her pale hand, testing its edge against her finger.

"Beware, madam," the bravo warned.

"What? Is it laced with poison?"

"Saints forbid! We do not steal lives through drugs. We claim them with steel alone. However, it was freshly honed, and I hesitated to risk the Signora's fair skin."

"His skin is as white as mine," she murmured, a fleeting glimmer of pity crossing her face before vanishing. "But his heart is blacker than soot. Tell me, is it not right to remove a traitor who slandered me?"

"That judgment belongs to your confessor, Signora. I am but a tool in noble hands, much like this stiletto."

Beatrice did not heed his words. "Oh, how I once loved him! To the very end! And now I hate him. A fool! He will learn to toy with princes, to spurn them while fawning, preferring the scum of the streets to royalty, turning them into a byword." She lifted her masked face. "Why do you linger? Hasten to execute my vengeance."

"It is customary to remit half the payment beforehand, Signora."

"Ay, I forgot. Your revenge is purchased. Here is more than half," she declared, pushing a heavy leather sack across the table. "Come for the balance when the deed is done."

"You shall see me again, Signora."

With another bow, he departed.

Left alone, Beatrice remained seated, still masked, her arms stretched stiffly before her, her knuckles white as she gripped the wood. So sat the Sphinx of legend; so sat a tigress trapped by instinct. Yet, a cold chill crept over her now that the killer had vanished, giving rise to moody doubts, the harbingers of futile regret. *Gerard and Margaret* were already lost to time. This, indeed, was the true medieval world: proud, amorous, vengeful, generous, foolish, cunning, impulsive, unprincipled—and ignorant as dust.

Power was the curse of such a being. Had she been forced to commit her crimes with her own hands, her weak nerves would have tempered the fury of her passions, making her bark far worse than her bite. But power granted a feeble, furious woman access to male instruments. The result was as dreadful as the union itself unnatural. In this specific instance, it had sharpened an assassin's dagger for a poor, forlorn wretch who was merely contemplating suicide.

Two days after the turmoil that had shaken his soul, Gerard wandered lost through the labyrinthine alleys of Rome, seeking refuge from a sudden, violent tempest. The heavens opened in a fury, drenching the wretched street where he stumbled, until he sought shelter within a low, smoky tavern. There, he demanded wine, and as the downpour continued unabated outside, the liquor turned his thoughts to a sluggish haze. Soon, his limbs grew heavy, and he slumped over a rickety table, resting his weary chin in his palms, drifting toward a stupor.

Time passed, and the tavern filled with a rough, boisterous crowd whose clamor eventually jolted him back to awareness. Yet, amidst the din, his ears caught the hushed murmur of two figures standing nearby, engaged in a clandestine negotiation. One was a pardoner, a man of loose morals and brazen speech. Beside him stood another, dressed in clothes that were neat enough to pass for a respectable tradesman, though the manner in which he dragged his wide-brimmed hat low over his brow betrayed a different nature. This gesture served to conceal every feature save a scruffy beard, marking him as a man who hid his face from the gaze of honest folk and the reach of the law. Together, they were trading souls in a marketplace of sin, sealing a celestial contract before the crime itself was committed.

The man with the slouched hat grumbled about the soaring costs of indulgences. "If prices climb any further," he lamented, "we poor wretches will be locked out of heaven entirely."

The pardoner scoffed at the notion. "Indulgences have never been so affordable for the prudent householder."

"And who, pray, purchased them?" the buyer asked.

"Why, those who sin openly, behaving like reasonable creatures," the pardoner replied with a shrug. "But if you are so stubborn as to seek absolution for a specific offense the Pope has explicitly condemned, then you must blame your own conscience, not my ledger!"

To demonstrate that even grave transgressions fell within the reach of all but the destitute, the pardoner unrolled a parchment and recited the rates.
