It was a chilling catalogue, far too dark for print, yet its contents were well known to the archives of the church. What struck Gerard most was the disparity in value: the price for the life of a common layman was laughably cheap compared to the sum required to wipe away the sins of a clergyman. With a few trivial concessions, the deal was struck. Gold changed hands, and the aspiring pilgrim was absolved in advance for the murder of a layman. The word *assassination* was never spoken, yet the transaction hung heavy in the air.

Gerard heard every syllable, though he did not lift his head from the table nor stir from his drunken stupor. He merely listened, a hollow vessel for the horror unfolding around him. Only when the conspirators rose and parted ways did he force his eyes open. As the figure of the purchaser walked away, Gerard let out a guttural growl, his vision narrowing onto the retreating back.

"If Margaret were still alive," he whispered, the words thick with venom, "I would seize you by the throat and crush you, you cowardly murderer! But she is dead... utterly dead. Let the whole world perish; it matters nothing to me now. So, may I join her first in the dust."

Later, when Gerard returned to his lodging, he found a man wearing a slouched hat pacing restlessly on the sidewalk across the narrow street. "There is that cur again," Gerard thought, his heart beating faster. Yet, in the shadowy aftermath of his despair and the feverish delirium of the storm, the sight of the stranger failed to register as anything more than a fleeting, meaningless detail in the chaos of his broken mind.

Two nights following the previous evening, Pietro Vanucci and Andrea waited together for Gerard's return, expecting supper. By nine o'clock, patience had worn thin, and Pietro, growing irritable, dispatched Andrea to Gerard's chamber on a slim hope that his friend might have slipped inside unnoticed. Andrea, shrugging off the uncertainty, ventured forth.

He returned empty-handed regarding Gerard, yet carrying a crumpled scrap of paper. Though illiterate in the scholarly sense, Andrea possessed a sharp eye and knew precisely how Pietro Vanucci inscribed his thoughts upon parchment.

"That is for you, I believe," Andrea declared, pride swelling in his chest at his own deduction.

Pietro seized the note and read it aloud, his voice laced with biting satire as he recited the lines: *"Dear Pietro, dear Andrea, life is too great a burden."* To which Pietro retorted, "So 'tis, my lad, yet that is no cause for wandering at supper-hour. Food is no burden."

*"Wear my habits!"* continued the voice of the letter. *"And thou, Andrea, mine amethyst ring; and me in both your hearts a month or two."* Pietro paused, asking, "Why, Andrea?"

*"For my body, ere this ye read, it will lie in Tiber,"* the note concluded. *"Trouble not to look for it. 'Tis not worth the pains. Oh unhappy day that it was born—oh happy night that rids me of it. Adieu! adieu! The broken-hearted Gerard."*

"Here is a wretched jest from a peevish rogue," Pietro muttered, though his pallid cheeks and trembling teeth betrayed a far deeper terror than his words allowed. Andrea, however, could contain himself no longer. Filling the house with his screams, he cried out, "O, miserable day! O, calamity of calamities! Gerard, my friend, my sweet patron! Help! Help! He is destroying himself! Oh, good people, aid me to save him!"

His frantic alarm drew a crowd. Yet, despite Andrea's desperate pleas, the gathered folk remained sluggish. "Go down to the river?" someone suggested doubtfully. "Nay, 'twas not their affair." "What section of the river?" another queried. "A wild-goose chase," whispered others. Indeed, venturing near the Tiber after sunset was ill-advised; too many spirits haunted those banks through the long, quiet hours of night.

However, a lackey who had lingered opposite the house stepped forward, offering to accompany Andrea. This offer swayed three or four of the younger bystanders. A small band thus formed, making its way toward the river.

As they approached, the lackey questioned Andrea. Sobbing, the boy recounted the letter and Gerard's recent brooding. Unbeknownst to most, this lackey served as a spy for Princess Claelia. As they drew nearer, their rapid exchange of Italian fell silent when they caught the cool breath of the stream and the faint, earthy scent of the water under the calm spring sky. They froze.

Above them, the moon hung bright and unclouded in a vast expanse. Their footsteps echoed loudly and ominously against the silence. Their tongues were stilled. Turning a corner, they encountered a solitary figure who halted, hesitating at their sudden presence.

The man wore no hat, his hair matted and dripping with moisture that gleamed in the silver light. At the next stride, they saw his garments were soaked through.

"He is here!" cried one of the youths, unfamiliar with Gerard's visage.

The stranger spun instantly and bolted. They pursued him with furious speed, Andrea leading, the Princess's lackey close behind. Andrea closed the gap briefly, but the fugitive twisted abruptly into a narrow alleyway. Andrea, unable to brake his momentum, shot past, leaving the pursuers in a labyrinthine maze where chasing a swift-footed ghost-knight who knew every stone was futile. They could follow him only by sound.

Returning to their companions, they found them standing exactly where the man had stood, utterly bewildered. "Are ye surely certain?" they asked. "He must have entered the river. In God's name, why did he flee at our approach?"

Then Vanucci spoke softly, "Friends, I think this has naught to do with whom we seek. What shall we do, Andrea?"

The lackey interjected, "Let us track him to the waterside to confirm. Observe, he came dripping all the way." This counsel was accepted, and with ease, they traced the path of the wet footprints.

Yet, soon a fresh puzzle arose. After traveling barely fifty paces, the trail of droplets veered sharply away from the riverbank, guiding them instead toward the heavy iron gates of a large, somber building—a monastery. They stood frozen before the dark edifice, feeling it harbor some terrible secret.

Suddenly, Andrea let out a cry. "Behold, the drops return!" he exclaimed. "And this path leads to the river."

Resuming the chase, it quickly became evident that the moisture was once again pointing home. The trail grew wetter and heavier until they reached the edge of the Tiber. There, on the muddy bank, lay what appeared to be a bucketful of water spilled directly from the stream.

At first, shouts rang out, convinced they had solved the riddle. But reflection set in, revealing the truth: it meant nothing. A man had indeed entered the water and emerged safe, but he was not Gerard. One suggested knowing a fisherman nearby who owned nets and drags. They located the man, paying him generously to cast nets below the spot and drag them upstream and downstream, promising gold if he found the body.

Thus, they ran uselessly up and down the silent, voiceless river, guarding its strange secrets. Suddenly, Andrea returned to the house with a cry of hope. He arrived within half an hour, only to groan and wring his hands.

"What hour is it?" asked the lackey.

"It is four hours past midnight," Andrea replied.

"My pretty lad," the lackey said solemnly, "pray a mass for thy friend's soul, for he is no longer among the living."

Morning broke, weary and cold. Exhausted by fatigue, heartbroken by failure, Andrea and Pietro returned home, their spirits crushed. The days that followed rolled on, as mute and still as the Tiber itself, speaking no word of Gerard's fate.

If the men who stood upon the river's edge had hoped to decipher the secret written in ink upon the sand, they were doomed to failure. Their knowledge was too scant, their vision too narrow to read the tragedy unfolding before them. For at that precise hour, a singular event transpired—one so devoid of precedent in the annals of humanity that it might well stand alone until the final day. Yet, the tale must be told, though briefly, woven from the threads of a narrator's observation and the testimony of a participant in the dark drama.

Gerard, having penned his final farewell to Pietro and Andrea, slipped silently into the shadows as twilight deepened. His resolve was ironclad, the stubborn determination common to souls bent on ending their own lives. With a frantic urgency, he emptied his pockets, stuffing every coin of silver and copper within his grasp, ensuring his weight would drag him swiftly beneath the surface. He hastened to a secluded bend of the stream, a place known for its silence and lack of foot traffic.

Yet, solitude, Gerard soon discovered, was a luxury rarely granted to the desperate. Some, particularly the timid, glance around nervously to ensure a witness stands nearby; but this man, hardened by despair, scanned the gloom solely for absence. And yet, there, perched like a vulture upon the corner of a narrow alley, sat a solitary figure. Annoyance prickled through Gerard's feverish mind. He feigned a careless stroll away, only to circle back immediately. Lo! The shadow had risen from the side street and lingered, waiting.

*Does he watch me?* Gerard wondered, his heart hammering against his ribs. *Can he know the purpose of this night?* Impossible, surely. A stranger could not possibly know the depths of his misery.

Driven by a frantic need to prove his isolation, Gerard marched briskly down the street, took a long detour, and returned. The figure had vanished, swallowed by the darkness. But when Gerard cast his eyes wide to confirm the clearing, there stood the man again, merely a dozen paces behind, ostensibly tying his shoe.

Gerard realized instantly that he was being hunted. In the pale, silvery light of the moon, he caught a glint of steel—a gauntlet clenched in the watcher's fist. An assassin.

Strangely, the thought never crossed his mind that this was the life targeted for destruction. Indeed, he remained unaware that any enemy existed in the world. Overcome by a sudden, delirious impulse, he turned and approached the bravo. "Good friend!" he cried, his voice trembling with a mixture of madness and pleading, "buy me out! Sell me this arm! One stroke is enough! Look, here is everything I possess!" He thrust the handful of coins into the assassin's grip.

"Oh, pray thee! Pray thee! Do one kind deed, and free me from this hateful existence!" As he spoke, Gerard tore open his doublet, exposing his bare chest to the cold air.

The assassin stared, unmoved.

"Why do you hesitate?" Gerard shrieked, tears streaming down his face. "Do you have no mercy? Is it so great a pain to raise your arm and let me fall? Is it because I am poor and cannot pay you gold? Wretched fool, you can only strike a man from behind, refusing to look him in the eye. Well then, turn your head, keep your tongue shut, and do it!"

With a snarl of utter contempt, the bravo turned away. Gerard, spurning the offer, leaped forward and threw himself into the water.

"Margaret!"

The heavy splash of his body breaking the surface seemed to rouse the assassin from a trance-like stupor. The bravo rushed to the bank, and with a strange, guttural cry, he dove after the drowning man. What followed next would be revealed by the hands of the killer.

As surely as a man carries his burdens, so too does a woman bear hers, though the chroniclers of history, being predominantly male, often sketch these sorrows only with faint, cold lines, leaving the female heart to fill in the gaps with its own intuition. Thus, I shall merely point toward the storm that swept over Margaret Brandt until that fateful hour when she stepped into Eli's home as an adversary, yet emerged, having read her lover's letter, as a friend.

Now, however, a trial greater than any woman is fated to endure approached, while Gerard remained a ghost across the sea.

Margaret made scant use of Eli's newfound favor. For this reserve, she possessed a thousand plausible excuses, though the truth lay hidden deep within her: two men haunted that household, figures before whom she shrank with an instinctive, primal dread. In their countenances, she had seen wickedness and hate, along with the silent, conspiratorial glances of enemies bound by secret intelligence. Consequently, she preferred the sanctuary of her own hearth. There, Catherine arrived daily, consumed by the feverish anticipation of the expected birth.

Catherine belonged to that order of women whose sole divine mandate seems to be multiplication—the bearing forth, then hovering anxiously over the "fair scourges" still in motion, clucking and fussing as if she herself were multiplying by proxy. It was in this spirit that she beseeched Eli to remain in Rotterdam while she ventured back to Tergou.

"The poor lass stands alone," Catherine argued, her voice thick with worry. "What good are a pair of soldiers? Such beasts ought to be cast out upon the first breath of trouble."

Needless to say, these words offered Margaret profound relief.

Alas, the soul within her was a vessel brimming with anxiety as the appointed day neared. She feared death, and worse, she feared solitude without Gerard. Yet, the scales of fate balanced themselves. The poverty that had once nearly crushed her, alongside her father's frailty, now became her shield. Adversity's iron grip had forged in her a strength denied to many of her sex, forcing her to combat the natural lassitude that overtakes the wealthy, keeping her perpetually on her feet, laboring. She maintained this vigilance, guided by Catherine's counsel.

So it came to pass that on a fine evening, just as the sun dipped below the horizon, Margaret lay weak as water, yet secure. Beside her rested a tiny face, and above them opened the vast, gentle heavens of maternity.

"Why dost thou weep, sweetheart? All of a sudden?" Catherine asked gently.

"He is not here to behold it," Margaret whispered.

"Ay, well, lass, he shall return ere the babe is weaned. Meanwhile, consider how kindly God hath dealt with thee compared to any woman born. Had it not been for the threat my own Kate made against me, we might never have secured this bonny boy, a sturdy lad indeed—for which the Saints give thanks!"

"Yes, Mother. I am but a sorry, ungrateful wretch to weep. If only Gerard were here to witness it. 'Tis strange; I bore him well enough to feel no sorrow in his absence, but oh, it does seem so cruel that he may not share this joy. Prithee, prithee, come to me, Gerard! Dear, dear Gerard!" She stretched out her feeble arms toward the empty air.

Catherine bustled about, deliberately averting her gaze from Margaret's tear-stained face, for she could not restrain her own tears at hearing her own lost son invoked with such earnest love.

Soon, however, turning back, she found Margaret regarding her with a singular, piercing expression. "Didst thou hear nothing?"

"No, my lamb. What?"

"I did cry out for Gerard, but now..."

"Ay, ay, sure I heard that."

"Well, he answered me."

"Tush, girl: say not that."

"Mother, as sure as I lie here with his boy by my side, his voice returned to me. 'Margaret!' So. Yet methought 'twas not his happy voice. Perhaps that is because of the distance; all voices grow sad like when carried afar. Why art thou not happy, sweetheart? And I am so joyful this night?" Margaret's sweet eyes turned, gazing with rapt attention upon the sleeping infant in silence.

It was that same night that Gerard threw himself into the Tiber. And it was within that very hour that she heard him speaking her name, crying aloud amidst the jaws of death and despair.

"Margaret!"

Let those who can account for such mysteries try their hand; I cannot.

Within the quiet seclusion of a Dominican convent, a solitary stranger lay in a guest chamber, his strength nearly spent by waves of violent nausea that had finally receded. His condition mirrored the fragility of Margaret in Holland, leaving him as weak as dust. A roaring wood fire crackled on the hearth, casting warm light upon the patient's hanging garments, while a towering friar knelt nearby, his voice resonating softly as he read sacred collects from his breviary.

At intervals, the dying man opened weary eyes to gaze upon the monk, listening intently, only to close them again in a moan of pain. The friar prostrated himself, pressing his face to the earth in fervent prayer, before rising to bid farewell. "Day breaks," the monk announced gently. "I must prepare for Matins."

"Good Father Jerome," the stranger gasped, his voice thin. "Before you depart, tell me—how did I arrive here?"

"By the hand of Heaven," the friar replied, his tone grave yet kind. "You cast aside God's gift, and He has returned it to you. Reflect on this! You sought the bitterness of the world and found only gall. Now turn to the Church! Here lies peace. *Pax vobiscum*."

With those words, the friar departed. Gerard lay back, his mind reeling in meditation, until exhaustion dragged him into a heavy slumber.

When consciousness returned, a new figure sat by his side—a layman whose eyes were small, darting, and far less tranquil than the majestic stillness of Friar Jerome.

"How do you feel?" the man inquired with genuine concern.

"Extremely weak," Gerard murmured. "Messer, where have I encountered you before?"

"None the worse for my gauntlet?" the man asked, his anxiety evident. "I intended to strike you down, or both of us might well have perished at the bottom of the Tiber."

Gerard stared, bewildered. "Did you save me? How?"

"At your service, signor," the man explained. "I was by the banks of the Tiber on some errand of my own. You approached me, pleading desperately for a dagger's stroke. Yet, even as you spoke, I recognized you—the very gentleman who rescued my wife and child upon the sea."

"It is Teresa's husband!" Gerard exclaimed, horror dawning. "And an assassin?!"

"At your disposal," the man continued calmly. "Next, you threw yourself into the river and commanded me to stand aside. If anyone else had done so, I would have obeyed instantly, neither stirring a finger. My enemies are many enough to warrant such caution. But when you sank in the Tiber and cried out 'Margaret!'"

"Ah!" Gerard interrupted.

"My heart screamed 'Teresa!'" the man confessed, his voice trembling slightly. "How could I return home and face her, knowing I had allowed you to perish—the same man who had saved her life? Thus, I entered the water. Fortune favored us both, for I swim with the ease of a duck. Seeing me approach, intent on destruction, you grasped at me, seeking to drag us both under. But I swam around you—and forgive me, Sir Gerard—and struck the back of your neck with my steel-gloved fist. You lost consciousness, and with great effort, battling the strong current, I drew your body to shore. You were insensible, drowned. I carried you on my back to my home. I reasoned, 'Teresa will nurse him, and she will approve of my deed.'"

"But a holy friar, the largest I have ever beheld, intercepted us just outside the monastery," the man recounted. "He questioned me. I told him everything. He gazed at you sharply. 'I know this face,' he declared. 'It is Gerard, the fair youth from Holland.' 'The same,' I confirmed. Then his Reverence spoke, 'He possesses friends within our order. Leave him with us! This is charity, our divine office.'"

"He further advised me that the convent possessed superior means to tend to you than I did," the man concluded. "His counsel proved sound. Consequently, I negotiated permission to visit you daily, and behold—you are here."

As he spoke, the man cast a peculiar glance upon Gerard, blending affection with intense interest.

Gerard offered no response. A cold chill ran through him, as though a serpent coiled within the room. He shut his eyes tight.

"Ay, you wish to sleep," the visitor pressed eagerly. "I withdraw." He retreated silently on tiptoe, promising his daily return.

Gerard remained motionless, his eyes closed, yet his mind raced with profound turmoil. Saved from death by a murderer? Was this not the work of Heaven itself? Of that Heaven he had once insulted, cursed, and defied?

Shuddering at the weight of his blasphemies, Gerard attempted to pray. Words formed on his lips, yet they failed to carry the spirit of devotion.

"I am damned eternally!" he cried out, his voice filled with dread. "Doomed, doomed!"

Suddenly, the organ of the convent church erupted in a rich, solemn harmony, filling the air. Above the swelling chorus of the choir, a single voice soared—a sweet, boyish tenor, pure and angelic, seeming to hover toward the heavens.

Gerard closed his eyes and listened. In those ethereal harmonies, the days of his childhood flooded back, untainted by earthly corruption, devoid of foul and raging passions that had rent his soul.

"Peace," he whispered, tears streaming down his cheeks. "Sweet, balmy peace. Until I abandoned her bosom, I knew no sorrow, no sin."

And the broken, weary creature wept bitterly.

Just as his grief overwhelmed him, the soft, reverend visage of one he believed lost forever appeared before him. It was Father Anselm. The good father had arrived at the convent only the previous night. Gerard recognized him instantly and called out, "Oh, Father Anselm! You healed my wounded body in Juliers; now heal my shattered soul in Rome! Alas, you cannot."

Anselm sat beside the bed, placing a gentle hand upon Gerard's head. First, he soothed the agitated youth with kind words, then, having learned the truth of Gerard's arrival, he spoke to him with solemn gravity. He laid bare the magnitude of Gerard's crime, urging him toward repentance and gratitude for the Divine Power that had frustrated his will to damn his soul.

"Come, my son," Anselm said warmly. "First, cleanse your breast of its burden."

"Oh, Father," Gerard sobbed. "In Juliers, I could do so; then I was innocent. But now, an impious monster that I am, I dare not confess to you."

"Why not, my son?" Anselm pressed gently. "Do you think I have not sinned against Heaven in my time? Oh, how deeply! Come, poor, laden soul, pour forth your grief, unburden yourself of your faults, hide nothing! Do not lie crushed beneath the weight of secret sins."

Moved by the father's compassion, Gerard eventually fell to his knees, confessing every sin with deep sighs and groans of penitence.

"Your sins are grievous," Anselm acknowledged. "Yet your temptation was equally terrible. I must consult our Prior regarding your penance."

Kissing Gerard's brow, the good father departed to confer with the superior. And lo! Gerard could now pray. He prayed with all the fervor of his heart.

The extraordinary journey of this mind may be summarized in a single word: Penitence. With terror and revulsion, Gerard turned away from the world, begging passionately to remain within the convent walls. To him, the monastery was like a wounded bird returning to its gentle nest. There, he spent his novitiate in prayer, self-mortification, pious reading, and deep meditation.

Meanwhile, far away, Claelia's spy hastened home, reporting that Gerard was certainly dead, the circumstances of his passing still unknown. She stood utterly stunned. When she finally found breath to speak, her words were laced with despair. "So swiftly," she sighed, "these monsters commit evil. They arrive in our hour of passion, first tempting us with venal daggers, then enacting the deadly deeds we never imagined without their instigation."

Hours passed, and her pity for Gerard mingled with a burning hatred for his killer, rising to a fever pitch, fueled by the fool's bloodlust. "Poor soul!" she lamented. "I cannot call thee back to life. But he who treacherously slew thee shall never live."

She immediately dispatched armed men into ambush, keeping them in vigil throughout the day. Her plan was set: when Lodovico arrived to collect his payment, they would spring from concealment in a specific antechamber and hack him to pieces.

"Strive for his head!" she commanded fiercely. "For he wears a private coat of mail. Should he escape alive, your own heads shall answer for it."

Thus, she sat weeping over her fallen victim, pulling the strings of machinery designed to spill the blood of a second life, having used it merely as a tool to take the first.

Gerard had undertaken a peculiar penance: to welcome Lodovico with kindness, though he harbored inward thoughts akin to those directed toward a slippery serpent. Yet, in the economy of self-denial, there is rarely true suffering; what begins as a rigorous trial often dissolves into mere habit. Initially, the assassin's gaze upon the single life he had spared was a grotesque mixture of arrogance and pathos, bordering on the ridiculous. However, once a virtuous opening is made within a hardened heart, a river of grace tends to flow through it. Having discovered the sweetness of preservation, Lodovico gradually grew repulsed by the act of killing. From this aversion sprang remorse, and from remorse, a shadow resembling repentance. Here, Teresa played her part, issuing threats—not for the first time—to depart should he refuse to live honestly. Supported by the benevolent Fathers of the convent, Lodovico and Teresa were dispatched by sea to Leghorn, where Teresa possessed acquaintances. There, the former murderer settled into the mundane trade of a porter.

At first, the labor seemed intolerably drab to him, and he voiced his discontent. "It is a miserable, dull existence," he would complain. Yet, reflection suggests that this quiet, grinding toil served him far better than the fleeting, bloody thrill of being torn apart by Princess Claelia's minions. His exile preserved him from that fate, leaving the Princess frustrated and bereft of vengeance. She descended into a profound, brooding melancholy, dismissing her former spiritual guide and seeking out a new confessor renowned for his sanctity—a man named Fra Jerome. It proved to be providential timing, for she could scarcely have landed in kinder hands.

Fra Jerome listened to her harrowing tale with grim composure before turning upon her, shaking the illusions from her mind with a roughness reserved for a scullery maid. To give credit to this stern monk, he feared neither the wrath of kings nor the fury of the seas when standing in the path of divine duty. In a few moments, delivered with the voice of thunder and lightning, he laid bare her guilt, declaring her the criminal among criminals.

"You are the Devil," he proclaimed, "who, through your wealth, tempted a man to slaughter his brother. Blinded now by selfish love, instead of condemning and punishing yourself, you thirst for the blood of others, yet they are not half so guilty as you!"

Initially, she resisted, protesting that she was unaccustomed to such severe rebuke from her confessors. But Jerome overpowered her arguments. He invoked the Church's curses, both temporal and eternal, tearing away the scales from her eyes until she sank to the floor, groveling in terror and remorse before the towering figure of this Boanerges-like friar.

"Oh, holy Father," she cried, "have pity on this poor, weak woman and aid me to save my soul. I was lost in darkness, lacking guidance like yours, good Father. I awake now as from a nightmare."

"Doff thy jewels," commanded Fra Jerome sternly.

"I shall," she replied quickly.

"Strip off thy silks and velvets. Go instantly to Loretto clad in garments humbler than those worn by thy lowest servant."

"I will," the Princess whispered, her strength failing.

"No shoes, but a single bare sandal."

"No, Father!"

"Wash the feet of every pilgrim, both arriving and departing. Confess thy sins to any holy friar who hears thee, and await his judgment."

"Oh, holy Father, allow me to keep my mask!"

"Hmph!"

"Oh, mercy! Consider! My face is known throughout Italy."

"Aye. Beauty is a curse to many of your kind. Very well, thou mayst veil thy eyes, but no further."

With this concession, she lunged forward to seize his hand, intending to kiss it. He snatched it away with a rude, abrupt motion.

"What would you do?" he asked sharply. "That hand touched the Eucharist but an hour ago. Is it fit for one of thy condition to lay it upon such holiness?"

"Oh, no. But pray, do not depart without blessing your penitent child."

"There is ample time to seek that blessing when thou returnest from Loretto."

Thus, that miraculous event by the banks of the Tiber left an indelible mark upon all involved, much like the legends surrounding ancient prodigies. The assassin, softened by the act of saving the life he was hired to end, abandoned the stiletto for the knot of porters. The Princess journeyed barefoot to Loretto, weeping her crimes and washing the feet of men born to humble stations. As for Gerard, having been brought from the Tiber into the convent as a suicide, he now passed within those walls for a young saint.

Under the watchful gaze of loving, experienced eyes, he underwent a probation shorter than usual before being admitted to Holy Orders. Soon thereafter, he took the monastic vows, becoming a friar of St. Dominic. Dying to the world, the monk severed ties with his past, discarding even the name by which he had lived, thereby breaking the final chain linking him to earthly affections.

Here ended Gerard, and Brother Clement began.

"As frail as the leaf upon the bough, so fleeting is the span of mortal men." Thus spoke the voice of time over Rotterdam, where in this same year, a giant silently budded within a humble tailor's shop, while a colossus of power fell with a thunderous crash.

At Bruges, Philip, the Duke of Burgundy, lay dying. For the poor, sickness came and went as nature whimsically decreed; but for the wealthy, a cruel law held sway: if one physician failed, three might perish under his care. The Duke's ailment, though now known by the clinical name of diphtheria, was then a silent, wasting evil. He was old, and his blood grew thin. So Dr. Remedy, the royal surgeon, turned to the ancient arts of leeching, draining the life from his veins until the great man turned icy cold.

When bleeding proved insufficient, the doctor called upon the arcane secrets of science. "Behold!" cried he, his voice trembling with anticipation. "Flay an ape without delay and bind its skin to the Duke's heart!"

Panic swept through the chambers like a storm. Officers of high estate scrambled in every direction, their voices rising in a frantic chorus as they sought the elusive primate. Yet, perdition awaited them. No ape remained. Buffaloes, lizards, Turks, and leopards filled the halls—beasts wild and unreasoning, save for the specific simian required.

"Why, surely there was an ape here just moments ago!" cried one officer, pointing a shaking finger. "I saw it standing by the door!"

Indeed, it had been there, lively and spirited. But fate, ever jealous, had intervened. A mastiff, hungry for the stolen scraps of dinner, had torn the creature limb from limb, fulfilling the ancient human adage: *Be of your generation*, and face the consequences.

Despair settled heavily upon the shoulders of the seneschal. He gazed around the room, searching for hope, when suddenly, a glimmer of light pierced the gloom. He leaned forward, speaking softly to the chancellor. "Look here. This garment... it bears a resemblance. Doublet and hose aside, the pattern is distinctly apelike."

"Nonsense!" snapped the chancellor, his voice sharp with irritation. "Princess Marie would have us hanged for such a grotesque substitute. She cherishes these things above all else."

It was true; the princess, our friend Giles, strutted through the hall in robes of gold, utterly unaware of the tragedy unfolding, her mind fixed on vanity rather than the dying king.

Impatient with the delays, Dr. Remedy issued a final, desperate command. "Flay a dog!"

"A dog is the nearest kin to the ape," the doctor insisted, "but it must be of a single hue."

A liver-colored canine was seized. With terrible efficiency, it was skinned and pinned to the Duke's chest, still beating wildly against the cold flesh. Within moments, the great Philip the Good ceased to breathe.

Thus ended the reign of a prince who, according to Flemish chronicles, traced his lineage back through Thierry of Aquitaine and the ancient kings of Franconia to the Trojan throne. Yet, in truth, such genealogy meant little. Every ruler of that age, from the mighty to the lowly, claimed descent from Priam, for medieval history often treated Adam himself as merely another Trojan king.

His body was borne back to Burgundy, interred beneath a tomb of black marble in Dijon. The bells of Holland tolled for his passing, unaware that within their borders, in a modest dwelling, a destiny far greater than any duke's was being forged. As the song goes, no one knows where the wise man dies, and few care. Dukes come and go, leaving the world no richer, no wiser, no kinder.

Yet, when Heaven chooses to kindle a torch after centuries of darkness, the light shines from pole to pole, proclaiming *Let there be Light*.

The Dominicans, once the towering giants of European piety, were now fading shadows, overshadowed by the rapid rise of their fiercest rivals, the Franciscans. Even in England, a land steeped in faith where the "Black Friars" had formerly ruled supreme, their influence waned. Consequently, the Order's wise leaders grew desperate to recruit and dispatch men of rare ability and burning zeal to reclaim the lost world.

Among these hopefuls, Clement stood out. His mastery of languages—fluent in Latin, Italian, French, and both high and low Dutch—promised a future of wide travels and powerful preaching in England. Yet, Jerome, the superior's trusted confidant, blocked this path. With a fatherly, albeit stern, caution, Jerome warned Clement: "Has the milk of the world not still flowed in your veins? Let not your new-found zeal and humility blind us to the need for temperance. Test him first, lest we one day lean on a reed for a staff." The Prior, agreeing, handed Clement over to Jerome for a rigorous probation.

Jerome began his trials immediately. First, he led Clement to a field where young men played, knowing it to be a haunt of Clement's former friends. There, Pietro Vanucci and Andrea passed by, casting casual glances at the monks. Though they failed to recognize their deceased companion in the shaven head, Clement felt a sharp pang. When Jerome pressed him to speak with them, Clement refused, bowing his head to recite a *Pater Noster*. "They remind me of sins I can never fully repent," he murmured. Jerome noted this restraint as a sign of maturity.

Next came the test of suffering. Jerome dragged Clement into death-chambers and dungeons, places of appalling darkness and loathsome stench, where men resembling wild beasts lay coiled in filth and despair. Clement's body suffered greatly, but his soul remained unbroken, tirelessly offering comfort to the hopeless. Jerome reported this to the leadership with the verdict: "His spirit is willing, but his flesh is weak." The Superior nodded in approval.

However, the ultimate test awaited. One morning, the streets of Rome were choked with a massive crowd gathered for a brutal execution. The Governor himself had been murdered in his sleep, his hand severed at the wrist and his throat cut. To ensure justice, the criminal, a servant, was strapped to a wall, had his left hand hacked off, and was bound with a raw chicken before being marched to the scaffold. A young friar, presumably Clement, prayed earnestly beside him, holding a crucifix close to the dying man's eyes. Suddenly, the executioner shoved the friar aside and, with a heavy mallet, struck the criminal down, hacking his throat from ear to ear. The crowd screamed in horror, and the young friar swooned, collapsing like a child until a giant monk carried him away.

Sad and discouraged, Clement returned to the convent, confessing his failure to the Prior. "Courage, son Clement," the Prior urged. "A Dominican is not forged in a day. You shall face another trial, and I forbid you to approach it fasting." Clement bowed, accepting the command.

Soon, a notorious robber, a monster of cruelty who had slain men for sheer wantonness, was brought to the scaffold. Clement was tasked with passing the night in prison with him and accompanying him to the execution. Despite his natural repugnance, Clement prayed with such fervor that the hardened ruffiant wept and embraced him. As the man was garrotted, the crowd, having watched the killing with indifferent satisfaction, suddenly erupted in piteous cries as the axe struck the corpse, feeling the loss of life anew. Clement stood firm amidst the chaos, vibrating like a rock that cannot be toppled.

Suddenly, Jerome's voice rang out: "Brother Clement, mount the cart! Preach to the people! Strike while the iron is hot!"

Clement's face drained of color, his breath coming in short gasps. Trembling, he climbed onto the cart and faced the sea of upturned faces. His throat parched, his heart pounding, his voice initially shook. But as he recounted the robber's tragic history, fresh from his lips in the dungeon, something shifted. He saw the gradations of folly and crime, warning the crowd not to step onto the first rung of that ladder. Thundering against bloodshed, moving them to charity, his tremors vanished. He became a master of the crowd, playing sacred melodies on their hearts as on a psaltery, drawing forth sobs and groans. Jerome stared in stunned awe, realizing he had lit a rocket rather than merely warming a stick.

Later, Jerome reported the success to headquarters, though reluctantly, calling Clement an "orator born."

The trials continued. Julio Antonelli, accused of sacrilege despite conflicting testimonies, was subjected to the *ordeals* of hot or cold water. Choosing the cold, Antonelli was stripped naked and lowered into the Tiber. A roar from the banks declared him guilty as he sank beneath the surface, but moments later, wet ropes caused him to resurface, proving his innocence. While Clement prayed on the bank, he afterward questioned Jerome regarding compensation for the terror Antonelli endured. Jerome, stern, replied that earthly suffering was actually a reward in the eyes of God, counting as a hundredfold merit. Clement submitted, asking that Jerome's words take root in his heart.

Yet, the severest trial was unpremeditated and intellectual. Jerome visited Fra Colonna, a proud scholar, where *The Dream of Polifilo* lay on the table. Colonna, boasting of his masterpiece, defended his use of classical imagery and pagan references, arguing that true wisdom and beauty transcended Christian boundaries. Jerome, however, seized the manuscript and condemned it as obscene, accusing Colonna of producing a heretical work. Colonna, indignant, retorted that "pure things remain pure," launching into a torrent of arguments.

He challenged Jerome on the origins of Christianity itself, claiming that many supposed Christian inventions were merely adaptations of pagan customs. He argued that the worship of jewels, the concept of monotheism, the veneration of saints, and even the miracles attributed to them were borrowed from the ancients. He cited the "speaking statues" of antiquity, the "kissing of images," the Persian roots of infant baptism, and the Egyptian origins of funeral rites. He claimed that the Catholic calendar was a patchwork of Roman festivals, from the Feast of Juno to Candlemas, and that the doctrine of Purgatory derived directly from Virgil and Plato.

Most scurrilously, Colonna attacked the sanctity of the Church's relics and figures. He mocked the "Virgin of Bolsena," suggesting it was a revival of Numa's shield, and ridiculed the "Mouth of Truth" as a statue of the goddess Rhea. He listed a parade of "fake saints"—St. Baccho, St. Quirina, St. Amphibolus—and reduced the legendary eleven thousand virgins of Cologne to a mere couple, attributing the number to a linguistic error by a foolish chronicler. He scoffed at the idea of saints performing impossible feats, like joining a decapitated head to its body or causing a city to be eaten by rats, labeling them absurd fictions compared to the logical lies of antiquity.

Finally, he attacked the physical symbols of the priesthood, tracing the tonsure and the vestments back to pagan and Brahminical sources, declaring the monastic habit a relic of the "rude times" when priests wore beast skins. "Strip off thy black gown, Jerome," he taunted, "for it comes from the Pagan ladies!"

Jerome, red with fury, rose abruptly. "Come forth, Clement!" he shouted, pointing at Colonna. "Know that but for the powerful house that upholds thee, thy accursed heresy should go no farther, for I would have thee burned at the stake!" He stormed out, slamming the door behind him.

Outside, Colonna, emboldened by Jerome's departure, ran shouting after him, chanting verses about Christian superstition and pagan parallels, celebrating his victory over the "blind monk." However, in his triumph, he tripped over an Egyptian stork and tumbled into a pile of Armenian gods, rolling about in the dust.

Back inside, Jerome forbade Clement from seeing Fra Colonna again. "You are forbidden to consort with him," Jerome ordered, listing the duties Clement must fulfill instead: confess, absolve, exorcise, comfort, terrify, and preach wherever men gathered. "Go alone through the city!"

Though Clement bowed in submission, the spy sent to watch him reported that the friar had lingered near Fra Colonna's lodging, his hand to his eyes, moving slowly. Jerome interpreted this as childish repining, a sign of weakness. "Fly him, then, with a string," Jerome advised the departing Anselm, predicting that Clement would achieve nothing great for the Order.

On that same day, however, an announcement shattered Jerome's gloomy prognosis: Clement was to depart immediately for England, traveling with Brother Jerome. Clement folded his hands, bowed his head in calm submission, and accepted his destiny.

A woman named Catherine proves a hazardous guest within a stranger's home, for the spirit of governance resides heavily within her. Before she has fully stepped across the threshold, the utensils gleam with a sudden polish, the hearth sweeps itself clean, the windows flood with light, and the soul of a massive cricket animates the dwelling. Yet, this cricket is a "Busy Body"—a formidable entity lacking discernment. It seeks to right all things and set all people straight. While many things improve under such correction, not everything does; indeed, everything resists having its order imposed upon it, save for the grave where silence reigns eternal.

Consequently, Catherine rearranged every chair and table in Margaret's house, perhaps improving the layout, though she felt compelled to disturb the living furniture as well. As Margaret's confinement approached, Catherine treacherously enlisted the aid of Denys and Martin. When these poor, simple men earnestly asked what service they might render, she feigned benevolence, suggesting a short walk. To extend this stroll into the following week, however, she hinted, was ungracious. Under the influence of a weak master, the strong men behaved with a servile timidity that might have appeased a she-bear with three sickly cubs. They slipped out at dawn like thieves and returned at night with the stealth of burglars; if caught at home, they moved like cats traversing walls of broken glass, wearing expressions of awe and a general aura of subjugation.

Yet, this arrangement failed entirely. Their mere presence jarred Catherine's nerves. Did instinct whisper that a pair of depopulators had no place in a house housing two multipliers? The breastplate offers no defense against a woman's tongue, and Catherine inflicted infinite pricks and needles of speech upon them. By the time Margaret descended the stairs, she found the kitchen cleared of its heroic defenders. Old Martin, stiff and weary, had retreated no further than the street, returning with the honors of war: he carried off his baggage, a single stool, and sat upon it suspended in the air.

Margaret, seeing him bask in the sun, was unaware he had become a fixture in that luminary. Seeking Denys, she spoke tenderly, expecting him to greet her warmly after three years apart. "Good, kind Denys; he will be right pleased to see me about again."

Catherine, wiping a bowl with vigorous, now superfluous force, lied smoothly. "Denys is gone to his friends in Burgundy. And high time, hasn't he? He hasn't been near them for three years, by all accounts."

"What?" Margaret exclaimed, lifting eyes like full-blown violets. "Gone without bidding me farewell?"

Catherine flushed. This new angle pricked her conscience. With a dismissive toss, she offered a falsehood: "Oh, you were asleep at the time, and I would not have you woken."

"Poor Denys," Margaret sighed, and visible dew gathered on her violet eyes.

Watching from the corner of her eye, Catherine, without overt acknowledgment, turned her attention to the surviving depopulator. Suddenly, Martin, old and stiff in many ways, stood before her.

"No, thank you, dame," he murmured. "I have grown accustomed to being outdoors. And I love not changing and changing. I meddle with nobody here, and nobody meddles with me."

"Oh, you nasty, cross old wretch!" Catherine shrieked, shifting instantly from honey to sharpest vinegar, and flounced back into the house.

On calm reflection, she let out a small cry. Half-reconciling herself to her conduct, she vowed that Margaret should never miss her "plagues of soldiers" again. Still feeling uneasy, she dispelled her regrets with a process both simple and sovereign. She took the child and washed him.

From head to foot, she bathed him in tepid water; heroes, their grievances, and wrongs dissolved like dust in an ocean of soap and suds. As this celestial ceremony unfolded, Margaret could not remain silent. Hovering around the fortunate performer, she sought an apparent hand in the act, if not a real one. Dipping her finger into the water—perhaps to pave the way for her son, though she could not deceive herself enough to believe Catherine would let her control the temperature—she knelt opposite little Gerard. Prattling with amazing fluency, yet careful not to articulate like adults lest a cherub find their pronunciation ridiculous, she addressed the infant.

"I wish you could wash out *that*," she said, fixing her gaze on the child's hand.

"What?"

"What? Have you not noticed? Upon his little finger."

Granny peered, and there lay a tiny brown mole.

"Eh, but this is wonderful!" she cried. "Nature, my lass, you are strong—and meddling to boot! Have you noticed such a mark on anyone else? Tell the truth, girl!"

"On him? Nay, mother, not I."

"Well, then he has, and on the very spot. And you never noticed that much. But, dear heart, I forgot; you haven't known him from childhood to manhood as I have. I've had him hundreds of times on my knees, just as this, and washed him from top to toe in lukewarm water." Swelling with conscious superiority, she regarded Margaret as a woman beyond competition.

Catherine looked down from her dizzy height and moralized. She differed from other busybodies in that she occasionally reflected—not deeply, nor would she dare print it.

"It is strange," she mused, "how things come round and about. Life is but a whirligig. At least, we poor women, our lives are all cut upon one pattern. Was I not, in my Gerard's young days, washing out his mole? "'Oh, fie! Here's a foul blot,'" I quoth, and scrubbed away until the poor wight cried, so then I thought 'twas time to desist. And now you say to me, 'Mother,' do try and wash out of my Gerard's finger.' Think on't!"

"Wash it out?" cried Margaret. "I would not for all the world. Why, it is the sweetest bit in his little darling body. I'll kiss it morning and night till he who owned it first returns to us three. Oh, bless you, my jewel of gold and silver, for being marked like your own daddy, to comfort me."

Kissing little Gerard's mole, she could not stop there. Soon, he sprawled on her lap, receiving kisses over his entire back again and again, seeming to worry the lamb as a wolf would, while Catherine watched with a smile. She had witnessed many such savage onslaughts in her day.

This little sketch indicates the tenor of Margaret's life for several months. A few small occurrences happened during that time which must be told, yet I reserve them, since one string serves for many glass beads. While her boy's father passed through those fearful tempests of the soul, ending in the dead calm of the monastery, her life might fairly be summed in one great, blissful word: Maternity.

You, who know what lies within that word, may enlarge my little sketch, seeing the young mother nursing, washing, dressing, undressing, crowing, and gamboling with her first-born. Then, swift as lightning, dart your eye into Italy and behold the cold cloister; see the monks passing like ghosts, eyes lowered, hands meekly crossed over breasts dead to earthly feeling.

One of these cowled ghosts is he whose return, filled with love, youth, and joy, that radiant young mother awaits.

In the valley of Grindelwald, the traveler finds on one side the perpendicular Alps—all rock, ice, and everlasting snow, towering above the clouds and piercing to the sky. On the other hand lie little everyday slopes, green as emeralds, studded with cows and pretty cottages, teeming with life. Whereas those lofty neighbors stand leafless, lifeless, inhuman, and sublime. Elsewhere, sweet commonplaces of nature often pass unnoticed; but facing the grim Alps, they soothe, and even gently strike, the mind by contrast with their tremendous opposites. Such, in their way, are the two halves of this story, rightly viewed: on the Italian side, rugged adventure, strong passion, blasphemy, vice, penitence, pure ice, holy snow, soaring directly at heaven. On the Dutch side, all on a humble scale and womanish, yet ever green. And as a pathway parts the ice towers of Grindelwald, aspiring to the sky, from its little sunny braes, so here is but a page between "The Cloister and the Hearth."

The new Pope held the Dominican Order in high esteem. From the Vatican came a summons requiring a friar of proven capability to instruct at the University of Basel. Father Clement, well-versed in multiple tongues and having once attended the lectures of Guarini the Younger in his secular youth, was deemed the ideal candidate. Consequently, his planned return to England was deferred, though not abandoned. Instead, he was dispatched to Basel with the proviso that, needing no specific labor there for three months, he must preach on his journey.

Stepping out through the northern gate, Clement lowered his gaze, his entire figure enshrouded in a silence of devout contemplation. If one could render a mind and its history in paint, this barefoot friar would be a living fresco. Within him resided a tumultuous narrative: hopeful, happy love; bereavement; despair; impiety; vice; suicide; remorse; religious despondency; penitence; a death to the world; and resignation. And all of this, compressed into merely twelve short months.

Now the traveler was once again on foot. Yet everything had altered. The perilous adventures of the past were gone. Even thieves and robbers, bowing before him, refused to harm him; instead, they proffered stolen coins and begged for his prayers. Thus, this journey offered few picturesque or dramatic incidents. However, for those readers who prefer a quiet peep into the workings of a human soul rather than mere melodrama, the undramatic moments are often the most vocal, revealing the steady progress of a mind.

On the first Sunday of Clement's pilgrimage, he knelt to pray for the soul of Margaret. It was a novel act for him; her eternal welfare had always been dearer to him than anything earthly. Yet, humility now governed his spirit. The terrible impieties that had once burst from his lips following news of her death—words that had horrified his contemporaries and eventually horrified him upon reflection—were rooted in a long period of religious despair during his novitiate. He had believed he had committed the unforgivable sin against the Holy Spirit, damning his soul forever. Though Anselmo had gradually helped to clear away that dark cloud, a profound self-abasement remained. Clement felt his own salvation insecure and reasoned that it would mock Heaven for him, a soul so deeply stained, to pray for one as innocent as Margaret's. Henceforth, he coaxed good Anselm and another kindly monk to intercede for her. These good old monks, possessing a pure and tender affection for their younger brethren that transcended worldly bounds, did not refuse, nor did they offer their aid perfunctorily.

Having preached on Sunday morning in a small Italian town and finding his seriocara carrying him onward, Clement was greatly encouraged. That day, a balmy sense of God's forgiveness and love descended upon him. He prayed for the welfare of Margaret's soul. From that hour, this became his daily habit, the single purified tie connecting his heart to the earth. For his family, now, existed for him as if they had never been. The Church, in its divine economy, would not share with earth, nor could it cure a great love without annihilating the lesser ones.

Throughout most of this journey, Clement felt no spring within him until he stood in the pulpit, or when he had the chance to relieve a fellow creature. A young man, either tarantula-bitten or merely suffering from delirium, had lain awake for two days in extraordinary convulsions, leaping, twisting, and battering the walls. Village musicians, attempting to soothe him, had only worsened his condition. Exhaustion and death seemed imminent. Clement intervened, learning the boy's plight. He summoned a psaltery and attempted to soothe the patient with gentle melodies. While other tunes mad the boy frantic, Clement's strains seemed to crush him. The friar observed that at intervals the boy's lips moved, whispering a tune. Applying his ear, Clement discovered it was a melody with no existence outside the boy's mind—a strange creation born of his fever. Learning the tune, Clement played it. The boy's face brightened remarkably. He danced lightly, marching about the room, and when Clement's fingers ached from the repetitive playing, he was rewarded by seeing the youth sink complacently into sleep to this lullaby, saving his life. Clement taught the tune to another traveler as a safeguard, leaving forward with his heart a little warmer.

On another occasion, Clement encountered a mob dragging a decently dressed man who struggled and vociferated in a strange tongue. The man had entered the town sprightly, waving a mulberry branch overhead. The locals, initially stupefied, quickly pounced, dragging him before the *poderà*. Clement approached quietly, speaking to the prisoner first in Italian, then French, German, and Dutch, finding no response. Finally, the man answered in tolerable Latin, albeit with a sharp accent. He revealed himself to be an Englishman, oppressed by the heat of Italy, who had plucked the branch from the nearest tree because "in my country, anyone may take what grows on the highway." He expressed frustration at the local uproar over a mere twig.

The stubborn *poderà* intended to imprison the "dogged islander," but Clement mediated. With some difficulty, he explained that silkworms and their mulberry leaves were sacred, falling under the protection of the Sovereign and constituting the source of local income. He argued that ignorance of such specific laws was natural in a distant land where the tree itself might be unknown. The opinionated islander, however, tipped the scales by producing a long purse and reiterating his point with mercantile logic: "What damage?" (*Quid damni?*), "Tell me, and I will pay quickly." The *poderà*, smelling the gold, fined the man a ducat for the Duke—roughly the value of the entire tree—and pocketed the coin.

The Englishman shook off his ire instantly, laughing heartily at the absurdity, yet remaining deeply grateful to Clement. "You are too good for this hole of a country, father," the man declared. "Come to England! That is the only place in the world. I was an uneasy fool to leave it and wander among mulberries and their idiots. I am a Kentish squire, educated at Cambridge. My name is Rolfe, my place Betshanger. Both the man and the house are at your service. Come over and stay until Domesday. Forty sit down to dinner every day; one more or one less makes no difference. You shall end your days with me and my heirs if you will. Come now! What an Englishman says, he means." He gave Clement a hearty grip of the hand.

"I will visit thee some day, my son," Clement replied, "but not to weary thy hospitality."

The Englishman then beseeched Clement to shrive him, confessing, "I know not what will become of my soul; I have lived like a heathen since I left England." Clement consented gladly. Soon, the islander was on his knees by the roadside, confessing the sins of the last month. Finding him so pious, Clement let the man know he was indeed returning to England. He then inquired if the country was overrun with Lollards and Wickliffites. The Englishman flushed slightly. "There be black sheep in every land," he admitted. After reflection, he spoke gravely: "Holy father, hear the truth about these heretics. None are better disposed toward the Holy Church than we English. But we are ourselves, and by ourselves. We love our own ways, and above all, our own tongue. The Normans conquered our bill-hooks, but not our tongues, and they tried hard by law and proclamation for many a long year. Our good foreign priests speak God to plain English folk in Latin, or some French or Italian lingo, like the bleating of a sheep. Then comes the fox Wickliffe and his crew, reading Scripture out of its own book in plain English, warming all men's hearts. Who can withstand this? God forgive me, I believe the English would turn deaf ears to Saint Peter himself, if he spoke not to them in the tongue their mothers sowed in their ears and their hearts along with mothers' kisses." He hastened to add, "I say not this for myself; I am Cambridge-bred, and good words come not amiss to me in Latin. But for the people in general, the key to the hearts of the English is the mother tongue."

"My son," said Clement, "blessed be the hour I met thee; for thy words are sober and wise. But alas! How shall I learn your English tongue? I have no book."

"I would give you my Book of Hours, father," the islander offered. "'Tis in English and Latin, cheek by jowl. But then, what would become of my poor soul, wanting my 'hours' in a strange land? Stay, you are a holy man, and I am an honest one; let us make a bargain: you pray for me every day for two months, and I give you my Book of Hours. Here it is. What say you to that?" His eyes sparkled, burning with mercantile fervor.

Clement smiled gently at this trait. Quietly detaching a manuscript from his girdle, he showed the islander that it was written in Latin and Italian. "See, my son," Clement said, "Heaven hath foreseen our several needs and given us the means to satisfy them. Let us change books. And, my dear son,

Brother Clement exhaled a long, weary breath, a sound that betrayed a flicker of doubt regarding the path he had chosen with a soul so fiercely impassioned. Yet, in the quiet corridors of ecclesiastical wisdom, youth had already begun its steep education. He recalled the ancient precept: when faced with tangled difficulties, silence was the vessel that allowed truth to settle before action could be taken. To speak or act rashly was to risk shipwreck. Thus, Clement chose stillness. He withdrew his foot gently from beneath the heavy embrace of the kneeling woman, offering instead a silent prayer for illumination.

The penitent, however, reacted with a convulsive intensity. Her arms flew around his withdrawn limb, clutching it with desperate force, and a piteous cry tore from her throat. She wept openly over the bare skin, her body trembling with the dual agony of profound shame and burning contrition. A flush of crimson, stark against her pale complexion, raced up her neck, marking the heat of her humiliation.

"My daughter," Clement spoke softly, his voice steady despite the storm before him. "Take courage. Stop tormenting yourself over Gerard, who, in the sight of God, does not exist." He paused, allowing the weight of his identity to anchor the moment. "I am Brother Clement, whom Heaven has sent to comfort thee and aid thee in saving thy soul. Since thou hast made me thy confessor, I claim thy obedience."

"Oh, yes," she sobbed, the waterworks of grief momentarily stalling under the authority of his gaze.

"Leave this pilgrimage immediately and return to Rome," Clement instructed, his tone shifting from gentle to firm. "Penitence sought abroad holds little value. Wherever we dwell lies the battlefield of temptation; we must defeat it there or perish, rather than fleeing to escape to other, perhaps more gilded, arenas. It is easy to wash the feet of strangers masked in anonymity; far harder to remain meek and charitable toward those who surround us."

"I promise, I will never touch her again!" she gasped.

"Nay," Clement corrected gently. "I speak not of servants alone, but of dependents, kin, and friends. This is thy penance: at the last hour of the night and the first after matins, recall thy sin and God's boundless goodness. Let humility and gentleness govern thy dealings with human frailty. The world courteth the rich; seek thou the poor—not the beggar, for he is often neither honest nor truly destitute—but those who blush to approach thee, yet stand in direst need. To give unto them is to lend unto Heaven. And further, thou shalt wed a good son of the Church."

"Me?" she whispered, horror dawning in her eyes. "I will never marry."

"Thou shalt marry within the year," Clement declared, his voice resonating with spiritual gravity. "I entreat and command thee to choose one who feareth God. For thou art but clay; mated ill, thou shalt become naught. But wedded to a worthy husband, by the grace of God, thou mayest live as a pious princess and die a saint."

"I?" she stammered, unable to comprehend the transformation.

"Thou."

With these final instructions laid down, Clement bid her rise to perform the good works he had assigned. She knelt, removed her mask, and cast upon him a look fraught with unspoken emotion, before lowering her eyes in submissive reverence.

"I will obey thee as I would an angel," she whispered, her voice thick with tears. "How happy I am, yet how wretched; for oh, my heart whispers that I shall never behold thy face again. I cannot depart until I have dried thy feet."

"It needs not," Clement replied, shaking his head. "I have absolved thee of this bootless penance."

"'Tis no penance to me," she insisted, her hands hovering near him. "Ah! If thou dost not grant me leave to dry thy poor feet, then thou forgivest me not."

"So be it then," Clement said, resigning himself with a soft sigh. *Femina leviora sunt,* he thought internally, *lighter things.* Yet, even these fragile vessels, drawn inevitably toward the smallest earthly anchors as celestial bodies turn to the massive spheres, possess flashes of angelic intelligence.

Having finally dried the friar's feet, the princess gazed up at him through tear-filled eyes, murmuring with a singular tenderness that seemed to transcend her station. "Shall I have masses said for her soul? May I?" she asked timidly.

A faint blush colored Clement's cheeks, and his cold blue eyes softened. Such devotion from one he had just judged so lowly struck him with unexpected force. "It is a gracious thought," he admitted. "Do as thou wilt; often such acts fall back upon the doer like blessed dew. Remember, I am thy confessor, not hers; it is thy soul I must now strive to save, lest woe befall mine own. My daughter, my dearest child, I see both good and ill angels battling for thy soul this day, right now. Fight for thy own side. Dost thou remember all I bade thee?"

"Remember!" she exclaimed, her voice ringing true. "Sweet Saint, every syllable of thine is carved into my heart."

"But one word more," Clement urged. "Pray much to Christ, and pray little to His saints."

"I will."

"And that is the finest counsel I have to offer thee. With that, we part. Go to the place where thou art most needed—thy father's house—and I return to my Holy Mother's work."

"Adieu," she faltered, her voice breaking. "Adieu, thou whom I have loved too deeply, hated too poorly, known and revered only too late. Forgiving Angel, adieu—for ever."

The monk caught the word, though it lingered only in a heavy sigh. "For ever?" he cried aloud, his voice suddenly ardent, glowing with a sacred fire. "Christians live 'for ever,' and love 'for ever,' but they never part 'for ever.' They separate, as earth and sun do, only to meet more brightly in a little while. You and I part here for life, but what is life? It is but a single line in the grand narrative of the Church, a handful in time's vast sands, a drop in the ocean of eternity. Adieu—for the fleeting moment called 'life.' We part in trouble, we shall meet in peace; we part as creatures of clay, we shall meet as immortal spirits; we part in a world of sin and sorrow, we shall meet where all is purity and divine love, where no ill passions exist, but only Christ and His saints arrayed in white. There, in the turning of an hourglass, in the shattering of a bubble, in the drifting of a cloud, she, thou, and I shall meet again. And we shall sit at the feet of angels, archangels, apostles, and saints, beaming with joy unspeakable in the shadow of God upon His throne, FOR EVER—AND EVER—AND EVER."

Thus they parted. The monk stood erect, his eyes turned heavenward, glowing with the fervor of zeal; the princess retreated slowly, turning repeatedly to cast lingering glances of awe and tender regret upon that inspired figure. She departed home subdued and purified. Clement, in due course, arrived in Basel and immersed himself in his duties, teaching in the university and preaching throughout the town and its environs. His life became a study in two words: deep scholarship and severe mortification. The brethren of his order soon began to perceive in him a descendant of the saints and martyrs, a man whose spirit burned with a light that illuminated the darkest corners of the cloister.

The Hearth

It was a winter that clung to the bones, when little Gerard, barely three months past his birth, brought a messenger scurrying through the snow from Tergou. The boy arrived breathless, bearing news for Catherine.

"Go back," she commanded, her voice sharp with the weary resolve of a woman trapped by circumstance. "Tell them I cannot come, and I will not. They have taken Kate." The messenger vanished, leaving Catherine alone in the quiet of her cell. "There, child," she murmured, turning to her own sorrow, "I must return to them. They are all at sixes and sevens; the house is a storm. This is the third time they ask, and tomorrow my man will drag me home by the ear, flea and all."

She paused, reciting the trials of infancy, instructing Margaret with the gravity of a teacher preparing a student for war. She pressed money into Margaret's hands, a desperate measure. Margaret refused, bowing her head with a polite, if strained, gratitude. Catherine, however, grew angry at the refusal, her contempt cool and unyielding. To her mind, Margaret's reasons, though reasonable, lacked the necessary spirit of feminine fortitude.

"Out with your heart," Catherine demanded, her eyes glistening. "Speak it now, before we part, and perhaps never see one another's face again."

"Oh, Mother!" Margaret cried, her voice breaking. "Say not so."

"Alas, girl, I have seen it often. It comes to me now, at every parting. When I was your age, we believed in eternity, that we should live forever. Now, out with it."

"Well, then, Mother..." Margaret hesitated, then whispered, "I would rather not have told you... your Cornelis said, 'So you are come to share with us, eh, mistress?' Those were his words. I told him I would be sorry."

"Beshrew his ill tongue!" Catherine snapped. "What does it signify? He will never know."

"Most likely he would, sooner or later." Catherine stood firm. "Whether or no, I will take no grudged bounty from any family. Unless I saw my child starving—and Heaven only knows what I might do—nay, Mother, give me but thy love. I prize that above silver, and they grudge me not that, by all I can find. Not a stiver of money will I take from your house."

"You are a foolish lass," Catherine chided gently. "Were it me, I'd take it just to spite him."

"No, you would not. You and I are apples off one tree."

Finally, Catherine yielded with a grace that softened her stern features. When the moment of departure truly arrived, embraces tangled together, and tears flowed freely until both women were undone.

Once Catherine was gone, the child wailed, a sound that tore at the heart. All attempts to pacify him failed until Margaret, suspecting a hidden object, searched beneath his clothes and against his skin. There, buried close to his spine, lay a gold angel, irritating his small backbone.

"There, now, Gerard," she cooed to the weeping babe. "I thought Granny gave in rather suddenly."

She took the coin, wrapping it carefully in a scrap of linen, and placed it at the bottom of her wooden chest. She bid the infant watch closely, teaching him that sometimes, as resolute as his grandmother, one must stand fast.

Later, Catherine recounted Margaret's proud defiance to Eli, marveling at how the girl had baffled her mother-in-law. Eli agreed, stating plainly that Margaret was right and Catherine wrong. Catherine tossed her head in disbelief, while Eli pondered the shift in their fortunes.

Yet, Margaret's peace was fragile. Her burdens doubled: she had two men to feed, yet could not labor as fiercely as before. Keeping the house, caring for the child, and cooking for all left her exhausted. Though she had a little money saved, she lived by a delusion, telling her son that his father would return before the funds ran dry. Clinging to these bright hopes and the living memory of her husband, she spent several happy months.

But time, relentless, wore on. No Gerard returned. Stranger still, no word of him reached them.

Disquiet settled over Margaret, overriding her practical nature. Often lost in sad reverie, she sighed in silence. As her heart troubled, her purse melted away. One day, she found the cupboard empty. Looking into the faces of her dependents, her bosom swelled with pity. Could she let Grandfather Eli and poor old Martin go hungry? She reached for Catherine's angel. As she unfolded the linen, a tear of gentle mortification fell onto the sacred coin. She sent Martin outside to exchange it.

While he was gone, a Frenchman arrived, accompanied by a dealer in illuminated works who had previously offered her a pittance. This man claimed employment from his sovereign to collect masterpieces for a Book of Hours. Margaret showed him her finest treasures. He was charmed, particularly by a panel of flowers, raspberries, and creeping vines that Margaret Van Eyck had shaded with delicate skill. He offered a price unheard of.

"Nay, flout not my need, good stranger," she pleaded. "Three mouths breathe in this house, and none to fill them but me."

Curious arithmetic! Number One was left out.

"I would not outdo thee, fair mistress," the stranger assured her. "My Princess charged me strictly: 'Seek the best craftsmen.' I make no hard bargains; I wish to content both them and myself."

In a heartbeat, Margaret knelt beside the cradle, kissing little Gerard and showering four gold pieces upon him, recounting the miracle in broken Dutch. "And oh, what a good Princess! Wasn't she? We will pray for her, won't we, my lambkin?"

Martin returned, furious. "They will not change it! I trow they think I stole it."

"I am beholden to thee," Margaret hastened, snatching the coin from him. She wrapped it again and hid it, restoring it to its sanctuary.

Before these unexpected funds were spent, she returned to her ironing and starching. Midway through, Martin fell ill. After nine days of suffering, he died.

Nearly all her money vanished in his burial, ensuring a decent send-off. He was gone, leaving an empty chair by her fireside. For as soon as the Busy Body had left, Martin had preferred the warmth of the hearth to the sun. Margaret forbade anyone else to sit there. Yet, whenever her gaze lingered too long on the vacancy, a tear inevitably followed.

Now, there was no one to carry her linens home. Doing it herself meant leaving little Gerard in a neighbor's care, a trust she dared not place in mortal hands, especially not for hours at a stretch. She set inquiries afoot for a boy to transport her baskets on Saturdays and Mondays.

A plump, fresh-complexioned youth named Luke Peterson entered. He looked fifteen, though likely eighteen, blushing as he twiddled his bonnet. "Mistress," he stammered, "would not a man serve your turn as well as a boy?"

Before he finished speaking, Margaret thought, *This boy will just do.* But taking the cue, she replied, "Nay; but a man may seek more than I can well pay."

"Not I," Luke said warmly. "Why, Mistress Margaret, I am your neighbor. I do very well at coopering. I can carry your basket before or after my day's work. Welcome! You need pay me nothing. 'Tis not as if we were strangers, ye know."

"Why, Master Luke," she admitted, "I know your face, but I cannot recall a word passing between us."

"Oh yes, you did, Mistress Margaret!" Luke exclaimed. "Have you forgotten? One day you tried to carry your baby and your pitcher full of water. I said, 'Give me the baby to carry.' 'Nay,' you said, 'I'll give you the pitcher, and keep the bairn myself.' I carried the pitcher home, and you took it from me at this door, saying, 'I am muckle obliged to you, young man,' in such a sweet voice—not like the folk in this street speak to a body."

"I do mind now, Master Luke," she said softly. "And methinks it was the least I could say."

"Well, Mistress Margaret, if you will say as much every time I carry your basket, I care not how often I bear it, nor how far."

"Nay, nay," Margaret blushed faintly. "I would not put upon good nature. You are young, Master Luke, and kindly. Shall I give you your supper on Saturday night, when you bring the linen home, and your dawn-meat on Monday? Would that make us even?"

"As you please," Luke smiled. "Only say not I sought a couple of diets for such a trifle."

With Luke's timely aid and the health Heaven bestowed upon this poor young woman to balance her many ills, the household moved smoothly for a while. Yet, the heart remained troubled by Gerard's long, now most mysterious, silence.

Suspense, that mental torturer, began to tear her heavy heart with hot pincers, driving her to cry often and vehemently, "Oh, that I could know the worst!"

One day, amidst this agony, she heard a heavy step mounting the stairs. She started, trembling. "That is no step I know

Six days later, the household at Tergou gathered around their simple meal when Luke Peterson crashed through the door, his boots caked in the dust of the road. "God save you!" he gasped, breathless. "Mistress Catherine is needed at once in Rotterdam."

"My name is Catherine, sir," the young girl corrected gently, though her heart hammered. "Please, call me Kate."

"Ay, Dame," Luke nodded solemnly, wiping his brow. "She told me, 'Luke, hasten to Tergou. Find Eli the hosier and beg his wife, Catherine, to come to me, for God's love.' I rode without waiting for dawn."

"Holy Saints!" Cornelis and Sybrandt exchanged a quick, furtive glance, relief washing over them. "She has returned! What could possibly bring her back?"

Kate hesitated, then ventured, "Perhaps Master Luke can explain."

"I can," Luke said gravely. "Her baby is dying. She is frantic with worry."

Catherine sprang from her seat. "What is wrong with him?"

"Nay, I know not the ailment," Luke admitted, shaking his head. "But it has been wasting away steadily for some time."

Cornelis and Sybrandt smiled again, a silent understanding passing between them. Catherine, however, saw only fear in her husband's eyes. "Eli," she cried, her voice trembling with fury, "if you speak a single word against it, we shall quarrel, after all these years!"

"Who dares question thee, foolish woman?" Eli stood, clapping his hands together. "Quarrel with thy own shadow while I fetch Peter's mule for thee."

"Bless thee, my good man!" she exclaimed, tears welling. "Didst thou ever fail me? Eat thy fill, whoever may, while I prepare."

She took Luke back to the cart, questioning him relentlessly on the journey, turning his thoughts inside out until he confessed everything he knew.

At the door, Margaret awaited them, pale and shaken. She flung her arms around Catherine's neck, her face a mask of terror.

"He lives!" Catherine declared, scanning the child's fragile form. "Thank God."

She gazed first at the failing infant, then at the hollow-eyed mother. "Fortunate you summoned me," she said softly. "The child is poisoned."

"Poisoned!" Margaret gasped. "By whom?"

"By thee. Thy fretting."

"Nay, Mother!" Margaret wept. "How can I avoid sorrow?"

"Do not deceive me. A nursing mother must turn her mind from grief to the comfort in her lap. Dost thou not know that a child wastes if the mother vexes herself? This misery stems from thy reading and writing. Those are man's arts; they keep vital wisdom from a woman. The child must be weaned."

"Oh, cruel woman!" Margaret cried, clutching Catherine's arm. "I am sorry I called. Wilt thou rob me of the last comfort in a world where I am wretched?"

"That thou dost not," Catherine retorted sternly, "or he would not lie thus."

"Mother!" Margaret implored.

"'Tis hard," Catherine relented, her tone softening. "But consider: would it not be far harder to gaze down upon his sweet face looking up at thee from a little coffin?"

"Oh, Jesus!"

"And how couldst thou bear thy other sorrows with a heavy heart and an empty lap?"

"Oh, Mother, I consent to anything. Save my boy."

"A virtuous lass," Catherine assured her. "Trust me. I see clearly what thou dost not."

Yet another consent was required—the babe's—and he proved more stubborn than his mother.

"There!" Margaret cooed, feigning regret at his disobedience. "He loves me too dearly."

But Catherine was a master of persuasion. En route, she had spotted a robust young woman seated outside her own door, tending an infant nearby. Catherine approached her, explaining the desperate situation. Would she nurse the starving child briefly while preparations were made for weaning?

The stranger agreed with a warm smile, placing her own child in its cradle and entering Margaret's home. With a polite curtsy, Catherine handed the frail infant to her. The stranger examined the child, sighed with pity, and immediately began to nurse it as if it were her own.

Margaret, stunned by the stranger's composure, stared blankly before bursting into tears.

"What troubles thee?" the nurse asked kindly. "Thou told me the mother was reluctant."

"She is not," Catherine interjected sharply. "She is merely a fool. Ignore her, Margaret. And thou, Margaret, I am ashamed of thee."

"You are a hard-hearted, cruel woman!" Margaret sobbed.
