"Those who undertake to guide the weak must possess a certain hardness," Catherine explained bluntly. "And forgive me, but thou art not my flesh and blood, whereas this boy is."

Allowing the words to settle, Catherine softened. "Hark, now. She sacrifices her own to save thine. Yet thou canst think of naught but to burst into tears before her face. Away with thee, for shame!"

"Nay, wife," the nurse replied gently. "Thanks be to God, I have enough for myself and hers besides. And pray, do not blame her! Perhaps life's troubles have soured her milk."

"And her heart, too," Catherine observed mercilessly.

Margaret fixed her gaze upon Catherine, then lowered her eyes.

"I know I ought to be grateful," Margaret whispered to the nurse, then turned away, leaning over a chair to hide from the unbearable sight of another woman nursing her Gerard, who showed no distinction between this stranger and his exiled mother.

The nurse responded, "Thou art welcome, my poor woman. And so art thou, Mistress Catherine, a townsfolk of mine, though I know not thy name."

"What, art thou of Tergou?" Catherine asked. "All the better. But I cannot recall thy visage."

"Oh, thou knowest me not. My husband and I are humble folk compared to thee. Yet true Eli and his wife are known and respected throughout the town. So, I am at thy call, dame; at thy call, wife; and at thy call, my pretty poppet—night or day."

"There is a woman of the right old sort," Catherine murmured as the door closed behind her.

"I HATE HER!" Margaret screamed, the words tearing from her throat with fierce fervor. "I HATE HER! I HATE HER!"

Catherine merely chuckled at the outburst.

"That is proper," she said. "Better to speak it openly and hold it within than to brood silently. It is quite natural, truly. Here, take thy bundle of comfort. Thou mayest hate it, if thou canst," and she placed the child in Margaret's lap.

"No, no!" Margaret protested, turning her head half-way from the baby; she could not bring herself to look fully at him. "He is not mine anymore; he is hers. I know not why she left him here, for my part. It was kind of her not to take him to her home, with cradle and all... Oh! Oh! Oh!"

"Well, one solace remains," Catherine said soothingly. "He liveth. This gives me hope: some other hath taken him from me. Like father, like son..."

Catherine felt a pang of sorrow for Margaret and allowed her to weep undisturbed. From then on, whenever Catherine needed assistance, she would take Gerard outdoors for fresh air. Margaret never objected, expressing no disbelief, yet returning indoors always in tears.

This tacit agreement lasted only briefly. Margaret grew equally eager to wean little Gerard. The process was completed, and the child regained his health and vigor. Soon, however, a new affliction struck him during teething. Catherine's experience proved invaluable here; amidst her grief regarding Eli, Margaret found fleeting moments of joy watching the tiny teeth emerge. "Teeth, Mother?" she would exclaim, calling them "pearls of pearls." Each sparkling gem on his red gums seemed to Margaret the greatest miracle nature could achieve.

Her companion shared the delusion. If we had told them that standing corn was equally magnificent, Margaret might have transformed into a reproachful gazelle, and Catherine would have driven us from the room; so, each new tooth was heralded with a shriek of triumph by whichever of them discovered it first.

Catherine chatted with Joan and learned she was the wife of Jorian Ketel of Tergou, formerly a servant to Ghysbrecht Van Swieten. Having fallen out of favor, Jorian had returned to Rotterdam, securing the post of sexton to the parish alongside carpentry work.

In return, Catherine confided in Joan about the child she had nursed, detailing Margaret and Gerard, and the profound anxiety caused by Gerard's silence. "Ay," Joan remarked, "the world is filled with trouble." On one occasion, she suggested to Catherine, "I believe my husband knows more about thy Gerard than anyone here. Yet he grows increasingly secretive lately. Visit him sometime before sunset, and coax him to talk. For our Lady's sake, do not tell me thou wert sent. The only concealment he ever granted me was for whispering his business, and I seek no further."

Catherine visited. Jorian readily spoke of how he had befriended her son and perhaps spared his life, but this offered no new information. The moment Catherine pressed him to speculate

"A lie is not like a blow with a curtal axe," muttered a coarse voice, perhaps Sybrandt's. "With steel, the wound bleeds, and the blood tells the tale. But a lie? It spreads like ivy, choking roots unseen, growing far beyond the reach of any sword or shield."

Indeed, truth is bound by physics, but falsehood knows no limits. In the rough trade of the streets, a single untruth has toppled kings and buried innocents beneath piles of stone. Here, within the walls of this cloister, one cruel fabrication had already begun to unravel lives with terrifying speed.

Yet, strangely, the architects of the deceit reaped little reward. The brothers, who concocted the story solely to diminish their share of their father's estate, found themselves displaced in their mother's affections by the ghost of little Gerard. Worse still, Eli, emboldened by the fiction, declared that since Gerard was lost, perhaps dead, he had already secured provisions by will for both the orphan and his widowed mother.

Between the conspirators, a warning glance passed—a silent pact of silence. Cornelis, standing on the brink of speaking the truth—that Gerard might yet live—caught his mother's gaze and swallowed his words. Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, the other partner in this treachery, felt the weight of his crimes pressing down. He was a man failing in body, aware that soon his gold would leave him, leaving only his sins to haunt his soul.

Though his conscience gnawed at him, Ghysbrecht had long ago bargained with himself. "I cannot surrender my lands and coin," his greed had whispered, while a smaller voice replied, "I will cause her no further pain." Thus, having tampered with the seal of Gerard's letter, discovering it contained no clause regarding the deed, Ghysbrecht delivered it to Margaret himself. He rationalized this betrayal as a necessary compromise, a self-righteous balance sheet where he was both the sinner and the judge.

But time, that relentless healer, had brought a shock to his system: the birth of Margaret's child. Suddenly, the implications of his forgery loomed large. If his forged letter were to take effect, it would strip Margaret of her inheritance, branding her a thief, and disgrace the lineage of friends he had once sworn to protect—the granddaughter of his friend, the grandchild of another. As his strength faded, memories of old loyalties resurfaced, filling him with gloomy terrors. Yet, confession was impossible; his doctor was a man of honor who would demand restitution, and Ghysbrecht's penance was but a fleeting sentiment, easily drowned by ingrained habits of avarice.

One afternoon, returning from the town hall, Ghysbrecht found a figure waiting in his vestibule. A woman, her face hidden beneath a veil, cradled a child. Assuming shame, he greeted her with stiff, magisterial formality. But as he spoke, she lowered the veil, revealing a countenance etched with sorrow and resolve.

It was Margaret Brandt.

Her sudden presence jarred him, exposing a flash of confusion he could not hide.

"Where is my Gerard?" she cried, her chest heaving with emotion. "Is he alive?"

"For all I know," Ghysbrecht stammered, hoping to deflect, "I pray for your sake that he is. Pray, step inside. Let the servants attend!"

"Not a step," Margaret commanded, seizing his shoulder with the fierce energy of a desperate woman. "You hold the key to what breaks my heart. Why does Gerard not come? Why no word for these many months? Speak, or I will tear this house apart, let alone the ears of your servants. My misery is too vast to be treated as a game."

Ghysbrecht insisted he knew nothing of Gerard. But Margaret's gaze burned with certainty. "Those who sent you to me told a different story," she pressed. "You know why he neither returns nor writes."

Ghysbrecht paled, his dignity crumbling. "Would you believe the twain knaves against a man of worship?" he gasped.

"Which two knaves?" she demanded sharply.

He faltered, his voice trembling. "Did I not say...? I am a broken old man, my mind fogged. You confuse me, I know not what words escape me."

"Yes, sir, your mind is shaken, or you would not stand as my enemy," Margaret countered, her tone cutting. "My father rescued you from the plague when others fled; your friend Floris aided you in poverty, loved you then and now. Three generations of friendship have you known. Here stands the fourth. Look down, look at this innocent face. Think of them!"

"Woman, you torment me," Ghysbrecht sighed, collapsing onto a nearby bench.

Seeing her opportunity, Margaret knelt, placing the boy upon his knees. "This fatherless babe belongs to poor Margaret Brandt, who has never harmed you, coming from a lineage that loved you. Look at his face! It will melt you more than any plea. What crime could a desolate girl and her infant commit to erase the memory of your own youth, when you were as blameless as he now gazes up at you, begging you to restore his father?"

Holding the child aloft with her arms, smiling faintly beneath the old man's horrified stare, she intensified the vision. Ghysbrecht gazed wildly at the child, then at the kneeling mother, and sprang up, screaming, "Away with you, you pair of adders!"

The sting of her words sparked a final burst of vitality in his aged frame. He stumbled forward, wringing his hands, clutching at his white hair. "Forget those days? I forget everything! Oh, woman, whether I sleep or wake, I see only the faces of the dead, hear only their voices. Soon I shall join them! Here, here, what is done is done. I am in hell. I am in hell!"

His unnatural exertion led swiftly to collapse. He staggered, and without Margaret's support, he would have fallen. She caught him with one arm, crying aloud for help. Servants rushed in, carrying him away in a state bordering on syncope. The last sight Margaret had of him was his ancient, furrowed face, pale and helpless, his white hair hanging limp over a servant's elbow.

"Heaven forgive me," she whispered, tears streaming down. "I fear I may have killed the poor old man."

Yet, this desperate attempt to pierce the mystery only deepened the shadows. Her suspicions hardened: Ghysbrecht knew something about Gerard. "And who were the two knaves he claimed did a good deed and lied to me? Oh, my Gerard, my abandoned child, we are wading in deep waters."

The journey to Tergou drained her resources, and a customer absconded with her funds. Forced once again to reveal Catherine's angelic token, she descended the stairs only to find loose silver coins scattered on the table, accompanied by a scrawled note: *For Gerard, his wife.*

She collapsed over the paper, a cry of surprise rising instantly into a shout of joy.

"He is alive! Some kind hand delivers this!"

She kissed the note repeatedly, clutching it to her breast. Time passed, bringing no further news of Gerard. Yet, roughly every two months, small sums of silver appeared in the household. Sometimes they lay on the table; once, a purse was thrown through the bedroom window; another time, it was discovered at the bottom of Luke's basket. The giver remained elusive, never caught. Catherine disavowed knowledge of it; Margaret Van Eyck denied involvement; even Eli refused to acknowledge a role. Whenever the money arrived, Margaret would whisper to little Gerard, "Oh, my poor abandoned child, you and I are wading in deep waters."

She devoted at least half of this meager supply to dressing the boy beyond his station, insisting, "If it truly comes from Gerard, he shall see his son neat." Neighboring mothers began to sneer, particularly those with children ragged at the elbows.

Months bled into years, and a deadly sickness of the heart replaced the sharper torments of uncertainty. Margaret returned to her initial conviction: Gerard must be dead. She believed she would never see her husband again, nor secure her marriage lines. This grief, previously soothed by Eli and Catherine's recognition of her betrothal, flared anew. Others viewed her story with suspicion, and she often mourned over her son's illegitimacy.

"Is suffering enough for us, bereaved as we are? Must we bear dishonor as well? Oh, that we had never been born!"

A shift occurred in Peter Brandt. After nearly two years of mental clouding, his mind seemed to clear, granting moments of lucidity. During these times, he and Margaret spoke of Gerard until his condition darkened again. One day, returning after hours away, Margaret found him conversing with Catherine in a manner unseen since his paralytic stroke.

"Eh, girl, why must you be out?" he asked.

"But indeed, I have told him all," Catherine replied softly. "We have been weeping together over your troubles."

Margaret stood silent, her eyes lighting with joy as she looked from one to the other. Peter smiled, reaching out. "Come, let me bless thee."

She knelt at his feet, and he bestowed a blessing so eloquent it warmed her soul. He told her she had been the lovingest, truest, and most

Catherine, who had shed so many tears for poor old Martin, could not endure this latest blow with the stoicism required for the passing of an elder. Her attempts to soothe Margaret with trite consolations—that it was a blessed release, that the tree was fully ripened—fell flat. Even her pragmatic argument, urging that there were now but two mouths to feed and thus one burden less, struck a dissonant chord.

"Such cares are all the joys I possess," Margaret whispered, her voice trembling. "They fill this desolate heart, leaving it void and barren. Look at that empty chair! My bosom aches to see it. I loved him as if he were my own child, though he was my father. He, once so wise and learned, has become helpless as my infant self. Oh, empty chair! Oh, empty heart! Well-a-day!"

Her pious grief refused to be stifled. Catherine, humbled, fell silent, lowering her gaze. Days later, she finally confessed, speaking from her mind rather than her soul, "Do not listen to me, for I know not what I say. Yet, were I in thy place, I would mourn the old man exactly as thou dost."

Moved by this rare, genuine sympathy, Margaret clutched Catherine's arm, finding some comfort therein, whereas the hollow platitudes had offered none. Catherine's heart swelled with compassion. "My poor girl," she murmured, "you were not born to live alone. Consider me thy mother indeed. Do not squander thy youth on my son Gerard; either he lies dead or acts as a traitor. It breaks my heart to utter such words, yet what else can I see? Thy father is gone, and I cannot stand beside thee forever. Here stands an honest lad who has loved thee faithfully for many days. I would take both him and Comfort. Heaven sends us creatures to torment and comfort alike; without them, we are naught."

Seeing Margaret's face cloud with perplexity, Catherine pressed on, "Pray, art thou so blind as not to perceive it?"

"What? Who?"

"Why, surely, but Luke Peterson."

"Oh, Mother, our Luke? The boy who carries my basket?"

"Nay, he is past nineteen, a sturdy, hale young man. I have made inquiries, and all attest to his capability as a worker and his temperance. That is rare in a Rotterdam lad, who are often but half-man, half-sponge."

Margaret's lips curved into a smile for the first time in weeks. "Luke loves dried puddings deeply," she admitted softly. "'Tis because of them that he comes to court me here." Suddenly, her cheeks flushed crimson. "Yet, if I believed he sought the hand of thy son's wife—or should be—the moment I knew, I would show him the door."

"Nay, nay! For Heaven's sake, let me not stir up mischief. Poor lad! Folly cannot be bridled, bless thee, I wormed it out of him nearly a year ago."

"Oh, Mother, and you allowed it?"

"Well, I thought of thee. I reasoned to myself, 'If he is foolish enough to be her slave for naught, all the better for her. A solitary woman is lost without a man to fetch and carry her small burdens.'" Catherine paused, her expression shifting. "But now my mind is altered. I believe the best use thou mayest put him to is to marry him."

"So," Margaret protested, "his own mother opposes him, wishing to wed me to the first comer? Ah, Gerard, thou hast but me. I will not believe thee dead until I behold thy tomb, nor false until I see thee with another lover in thy hand. Foolish boy, I shall never be civil to him again."

Burdened by Catherine's meddlesome protection, Luke Peterson encountered a chill reception in the household where he had previously found kindness. Soon, finding himself largely ignored and then sharply rebuked, the gentle giant began to whimper. He approached Margaret, asking bluntly if he had done something to offend her.

"None at all," Catherine replied. "It is my fault. I am cursed. If thou wilt heed my counsel, keep out of my sight for a while."

"It is all because of me, Mistress Catherine," Luke stammered. "What have I done to turn her against me?"

"Nay, I meant all for the best," Catherine explained. "I told her I saw thou wast gazing toward her through a wedding ring, but she would not hear of it."

"There was no need to whisper that, wife; she knows I have courted her for this whole twelve months."

"Not I," Margaret interjected, shaking her head. "Or I should never have opened the street door to thee."

"But why not?" Luke argued gently. "I come here every Saturday night. That is precisely how the lads in Rotterdam court. If we sup with a lass on a Saturday, that is the wooing."

"Oh, that is the custom of Rotterdam, is it?" Margaret said, her tone sharpening. "Then next time thou comest, let it be on a Thursday or Friday. As for me, I thought thou camest only for my puddings, boy."

"I like thy puddings well enough," Luke conceded tenderly. "Thou maketh them better than mother does. But I like thee still better than the puddings."

"And therefore, thou hast seen the last of them," Catherine declared, her voice firm despite its gentleness. "How darest thou speak so to another man's wife, especially when that husband lies far away?" She concluded, "Thou needest not trouble thyself to visit here again, Luke. I can carry my basket myself."

"Oh, very well," Luke muttered. After a silence heavy with stupidity, he rose and said sorrowfully to Catherine, "Dame, I suppose I have lost my employment;" before departing.

However, the following Saturday, Catherine found him seated on the doorstep, sobbing openly. He confessed that he had grown accustomed to coming there, and every other place seemed alien to him. Catherine entered and informed Margaret. Margaret sighed, remarking, "Poor Luke; he might have earned her hand if he could but know his place and treat her as a wife should."

When this advice was passed to Luke, he hesitated. "Pshaw!" Catherine scoffed. "Promises are merely pie-crusts. Promise her the whole world, sooner than sit there like a fool. If thou but humoured her in everything, and then, if poor Gerard did not return to claim her, thou wouldest surely have her—in time. A lonely woman is ever to be wearied out, thou foolish boy."

For over a year, Brother Clement had labored in Basel, weaving his voice through the markets and chapels, when one day Jerome appeared. Dust clung to his robes, but his eyes burned with a triumphant light. "Glory be to God, Brother Clement," Jerome declared, "for now you may journey with me to England."

"I am prepared, Brother Jerome," Clement replied, his tone steady and warm. "In the quiet hours between my lessons and prayers, I have devoted myself to mastering the English tongue."

"A wise foresight," Jerome acknowledged. He explained that his delay had stemmed from securing special papal authority to gather funds for the English Church and to administer sacraments within the secular monasteries—a privilege denied to ordinary friars. "Now, gird up your loins," Jerome urged, "and let us strike a mighty blow for the Faith, and specifically against the Franciscans."

Thus, the two brothers descended the Rhine, a mobile beacon of Dominican order. In the great cities, they shared the single pulpit; in the lesser towns, they split apart, preaching from opposite banks before converging at a pre-arranged meeting point. Though both were men of considerable eloquence, their styles diverged sharply. Jerome spoke with noble gravity, his delivery impressive yet occasionally stiff, his topics somewhat narrow and his cadence monotonous.

Clement, however, possessed a fluidity that seemed to breathe life into his words. His speech was rich with variation, often dropping effortlessly into the vernacular of the common folk. Like a gentle current, his narrative curled around the listener's mind and heart, drawing them in. Yet, when his thoughts ascended to higher spiritual planes, Clement soared with a fervor that eclipsed Jerome. On such occasions, he became a tempest of prophecy—vehement as a storm, roused like a torrent, inflamed like lightning, thundering and flashing, sweeping away all resistance with the wild surges of his rhetoric.

Jerome, ever the pragmatist, believed it unnecessary to detail every nuance of Clement's genius here; suffice it to say that Clement had a habit of secretly investigating the local vices of every town before addressing them. Jerome, conversely, held to a rigid principle: he refused to tailor his message to specific sins unless they were matters of universal doctrine.

At a bustling city some leagues from the Rhine, they preached consecutively from the same platform. Jerome attacked the sin of vain attire, a subject dear to his heart. Satirical and sharp, he moved the crowd with a complacent ease, for vanity was not a prevalent vice among them.

Clement, however, chose a different path. He targeted drunkenness, a scourge that plagued the region and even infected the clergy themselves, shielded by deep-seated popular prejudice. Clement attacked the vice mercilessly, wielding Scripture and stark reality alike. He painted a grim picture: a recent parricide committed by a man lost to liquor, and a band of drunkards who had roasted one of their own comrades alive. He recalled the death of their last prince, misdiagnosed as apoplexy but truly the result of excess, and the miserable end of an alderman who had suffocated in a puddle while drunk.

"You feed your families with poison," Clement thundered, his voice rising. "You make your children bare-backed so you may gorge on beasts, leaving your souls to rot in hell. If your women drank as you do, this town—and indeed this nation—would sink beneath the mud of humanity. How long shall they hold back, defying nature and the examples of their husbands?"

The hall erupted. The medieval audience, unaccustomed to polite indifference, reacted with visceral horror. A shout of disbelief rose from the crowd. "It strikes home!" cried Clement, seizing the moment. "Do you imagine it shocks the All-Pure God less to see a man, His noblest creation, reduced to a swine, than to see a woman, pure and innocent, degraded to your level?"

He concluded with two vivid tableaux: the chaotic ruin of a drunkard's household versus the peace of a sober man's home. The descriptions were so precise and dramatic that the women in the gallery fell silent, overcome by tears and gasps of recognition. "Oh, that is true!" they whispered. "That is exactly what happens."

The effect was immediate and explosive. The sermon sparked a riot. Men gathered in knots, indignant and defensive. Their wives, sensing the danger to their honor, openly sided with the men, flattering their husbands and turning against the preacher. A husband, they argued, needed a drop to endure the labor of providing for his family. They refused to trade their sturdy, if flawed, mates for "milksops."

Yet, the reaction proved a double-edged sword. That same night, a group of men intercepted Clement, not to harm him, but to honor him. They filled his bag with coins, plucked fresh roses from their own heads to offer him, kissed his robe, and blessed him for undertaking to reform their "sots."

Jerome watched the scene with a furrowed brow. To him, the sermon felt too grounded, too earthly. "Drunkenness is not a heresy, Clement," he remarked quietly later. "Why preach an entire sermon against it?"

As their journey continued, Jerome found himself increasingly perplexed. He realized with growing surprise that Clement's words sank deeper into the hearts of the people than his own. While Jerome opened minds, Clement seemed to unlock them, making listeners weep, reflect, and occasionally repent. "He possesses the art of finding the peg upon which to hang his tale," Jerome mused, watching the crowds sway with emotion. "Yet, he can soar high enough when the spirit demands it."

On the whole, the situation troubled Jerome, who harbored a secret sense of superiority over his softer brother. After traveling nearly two hundred miles, this sentiment began to grate upon him, though he struggled to suppress it as petty and unworthy. "Souls differ like locks," he reasoned to himself, trying to reconcile the disparity. "Preachers must differ like keys; otherwise, the Church remains shut tight to God's entry. And certainly, this novice holds the key to these northern souls, for he himself is a man of the North."

Slowly, they drifted further down the Rhine, alternating between sailing a few miles downstream and walking the banks, preaching and hearing confessions in the scattered towns and villages. Eventually, they reached Düsseldorf.

They stepped onto the small quay where Gerard and Denys had once boarded their vessel upstream. The familiar streets stretched out before them, leading inevitably to "The Silver Lion." Everything remained unchanged—the stones, the buildings, the air—but Jerome himself was altered. He walked through the town barefoot, his heart a fortress of cold calm, his hands clasped tightly across his chest, his eyes bowed meekly to the earth. He was, in appearance and spirit, a true son of Saint Dominic and the Holy Church, standing apart from the world he sought to save.

"Eli," Catherine commanded, her voice cutting through the quiet of the room, "give me a straight answer now, like a man, and I promise to trouble you no further today. Tell me plainly: what is wormwood?"

Eli hesitated, looking suddenly at sea with this abrupt summons to his wits, though he quickly steadied himself. "It is a herb, Mistress," he stammered, "with a taste so sharp and bitter it makes the tongue ache."

"That is a fair description, my man, yet not the truth I seek."

"Then perhaps you must define it yourself."

"And so I shall!" Eli groaned, the weight of the word settling heavily on his heart. "Wormwood is having two men under the same roof who sit idle, waiting for your shoes and mine, doing naught but breathing air." The barb had struck deep.

"Methinks," Catherine mused, pacing the floor, "that idleness is poison enough to curdle blood and turn souls sour, far worse than any natural ailment. If you will not act, why not set them up somewhere else? Give them a chance to earn their bread."

Eli nodded slowly, expressing his willingness but voicing a lingering fear. "I am loath to part with them, but I dread the thought. They may easily squander their earnings on ale and dice games until they have gambled away their very shelves."

"Nay," Catherine replied firmly, her eyes flashing with resolve. "Do you take me for a fool? Of course, I intend to keep a close watch upon them at first, guiding them with a loose rein, as the saying goes, until they find their footing."

"But where did you conceive such a scheme?" Eli asked, suspicion coloring his tone. "Not here, to split our own trade, surely?"

"Not likely," Catherine dismissed. "I speak of Rotterdam. There, amidst the bustling markets, I could launch them."

Ah, there lay the deception! To the surface, it was charity; beneath, it was a desperate desire to draw near to little Gerard again.

After days of debate and earnest vows of reform from Cornelis and Sybrandt, Catherine ventured to Rotterdam, taking Kate with her. Their search proved fruitful; they secured a shop in a respectable street, though it was woefully disordered. Yet, necessity dictated price, and they purchased it cheaply. Immediately, they set to work, scrubbing floors, fitting sturdy shelves for merchandise, and rendering the dwelling fit for habitation.

Elsewhere, in a quieter corner of the city, Luke Peterson remained a constant shadow, ever seeking a purpose for his life. He frequently approached Margaret, asking, "What can I do for you, Miss?" Her response usually fell flat, delivered in a heavy, sorrowful whisper: "Nothing, Luke. Nothing."

"What, then?" he persisted, hopeful. "You are so clever. Can you think of nothing for me to create for you?"

"Nothing, Luke. Nothing."

Yet, time and gentle persistence wore down her resistance. Finally, her tone shifted, softening into a plea. "If you could craft something... something to aid my dear sister Kate, that would be kind."

The slave of love, bound by duty to the woman he adored, consented with joyful alacrity. Soon, he presented a small, wooden cart, carefully cushioned within. With his own shoulders harnessed to it, he became its engine. At twilight, he drew Kate gently out of the town gates, rolling her along the pleasant boulevard. Margaret and Catherine walked beside them, their steps light. To the outside eye, it appeared a scene of pure happiness, though the shadows of their pasts lingered unseen.

Kate, however, relished every moment. Little Gerard sat nestled in her lap, and her affection for the child knew no bounds. It was as if a cherub were borne aloft by a little angel, or a fragile rosebud cradled safely within the chalice of a lily.

Still, the vulgarity of the townspeople followed. Jeers rang out, mocking the sight: "How does a thistle taste, I wonder? Does his mistress lack the coin for a beast with four legs?"

Luke paid little heed to the ridicule, his focus fixed solely on Kate. But Kate, tender-hearted, felt the sting for him.

"You have made this cart for me, good Luke," she whispered, her eyes filling. "'Twas enough. I acted poorly in asking you to pull me myself; we can afford to hire a poor soul for that labor. I love my rides, and I love carrying little Gerard, but I would rather never ride again than see you mocked for it."

"My mistress's command is law to me," Luke declared, his face hardening with protective pride. "Little do I care for their tongues. If I did, I would knock their heads together before they spoke another word. I shall draw you until my mistress commands me to stop."

"Luke," Margaret interjected softly, her gaze searching his, "if you truly wish to honor me, you must follow Kate's lead. Obey her."

"Then I will obey Kate."

An honorable exception to the general mockery was Jorian Ketel's wife, Joan. She watched the procession with a knowing, weary smile. "That is strength well spent, drawing the weak," she murmured to those nearby. "And her prayers shall be your reward, though she smiles through pain. She is not long for this world."

Single-minded Luke offered a curt denial to the sentiment. "I do not need the prayers of the dying lass," he stated plainly. "I do this solely to please my mistress, Margaret."

In the weeks that followed, Luke continued to press Margaret for tasks, his spirit unyielding in its devotion, though she remained unable to assign him anything substantial. Until one day, seemingly worn down by his relentless importuning, Margaret turned upon him. Her expression held a gravity he had never seen before, her accent thick with an emotion he struggled to name.

"Find me my boy's father," she said, the words hanging in the air like a sudden storm.

"Mistress, the tongues of men say he is dead."

"Not so," Margaret replied, her voice trembling. "They feed me with hope, but they speak otherwise behind my back."

"Aye, to your face, yes," the servant echoed, watching the tears begin to fall from Margaret's cheeks. "Ay, to your face, but behind your back, they all whisper the death knell."

Luke whimpered, seeking comfort in her sorrow. Though he possessed the frame of a man, his heart beat with the timidity of a girl.

"Prithee, weep not so bitterly, sweet mistress," he cooed, pressing close. "If I could conjure him back to life, I would sooner than behold thee weeding thy heart so sore."

Margaret wiped her eyes, blaming the cruelty of the double-tongued world. Recovering her composure, she laid a heavy hand upon his shoulder and spoke with solemn intensity. "Luke, he lives not in death. A dying man possesses a sight strange unto others. Listen to me, Luke! When my poor father lay upon his bed, nearing the end, and I, a simple fool, was so happy believing he would recover entirely, he sat in that very chair where thou standest now—Mother was beside him, and I was yonder sewing a sleeve—and he looked up and said, 'I see him!' I see him!" She paused, her gaze distant. "Not like a failing man, drained and gray, but all on fire. 'Sore disfigured,' he cried, 'on a great river, coming this way.'"

"Oh, Luke," she cried, clutching his arm, "if thou wert a woman, possessing the depth of feeling thou feignest, thou wouldst pity me and find him for me. Take a thought! He is the father of my child!"

"Alack," Luke murmured, his brow furrowing, "if I knew how, I would. But how?"

"Nay, of course thou cannotst. I am mad to think it. But oh, if anyone truly cared for me, they would; that is all I know."

Luke fell silent, his mind racing. "The old folk say dying men see more than living wights. Let me think: for my mind cannot gallop like thine. On a great river... well, the Maas is a great river." He paced, weighing the possibilities. "Coming this way? Then if it were the Maas, he would have arrived by now. Therefore, it is not the Maas. The Rhine is a greater river, vast and long. I think it must be the Rhine."

"And so do I, Luke," Margaret interjected, hope flickering. "Denys bade him come down the Rhine. But even if it is, he may turn off before reaching his birthplace. He does not pine for me as I for him; that is clear. Luke, dost thou not think he has deserted me?" She sought contradiction, but he offered only a grim reflection. "It looks very like it. What a fool he must be!"

"What do we know?" Margaret pleaded, imploring him to deny the worst.

"Let me think again," Luke said, pacing slowly. "My mind cannot gallop."

The result of this meditation was a plan. He knew a station roughly sixty miles up the Rhine where all public boats docked. He would travel there and intercept the truant. Though he did not know Gerard by sight, he would mingle with the passengers on every incoming boat and inquire if a man named Gerard was aboard.

"And, mistress," Luke suggested, looking at her with newfound gravity, "if thou wouldst give me a token, a letter to him? For, being strangers, he may not believe a word I say without proof."

"Good, kind, thoughtful Luke," she breathed, gratitude flooding her features. "How have I undervalued thee! Give me till supper-time to write it."

At supper, she handed him a long letter, tied with silk in the fashion of the age and sealed over the knot. Her cheeks burned with a deep blush.

Luke weighed the parchment in his hand, a shadow of discontent crossing his face. "Say your father was not dreaming," he demanded gravely, "and say I have the luck to fall in with this man, and say he should prove a better bit of stuff than I think him, and come home to thee then and there... what becomes of me?"

Margaret flushed to her brow, panic seizing her. "Oh, Luke, Heaven will reward thee! I shall fall on my knees and bless thee; I shall love thee all my days, sweet Luke, as a mother loves her son. I am so old by thee; trouble ages the heart. Thou shalt not go 'tis not fair of me. Love maketh us to be all self."

"Humph!" Luke grunted. "And if," he resumed, his tone hardening, "that scapegrace reads thy letter, hears me tell him how thou pinest for him, and yet, being a traitor or a mere idiot, refuses to turn to thee... what shall become of me? Must I die a bachelor, and thou fare lonely to thy grave, neither maid, wife, nor widow?"

Margaret panted with fear and emotion at this terrible piece of good sense, the plain question hanging in the air. Finally, she faltered, "If, which our Lady be merciful to me and forbid—"

"Well, mistress?"

"If he should read my letter, hear thy words—and, sweet Luke, be just and tell him what a lovely babe he hath, fatherless, fatherless. Oh, Luke, can he be so cruel?"

"I trow not but if?"

"Then he will give thee up my marriage lines, and I shall be an honest woman, and a wretched one, and my boy will not be a bastard. Of course, then we could both go into any honest man's house troubled by us. And for thy goodness this day, I will—I will—ne'er be so ungrateful as to pass thy door to another man."

"Ay, but wilt thou come in at mine? Answer me that!"

"Oh, ask me not! Some day, perhaps, when my wounds cease bleeding. Alas, I'll try. If I don't fling myself and my child into the Maas. Do not go, Luke! Do not think of going! 'Tis all madness from first to last."

But Luke was as slow to forego an idea as to form one. His reply revealed how swiftly love was forging a man within him. "Well," he said, "madness is something, anyway. And I am tired of doing nothing for thee. I am no great talker. To-morrow, at peep of day, I start."

"But hold!" He paused, his expression darkening. "I have no money. My mother keeps all mine, and I ne'er see it again."

Then Margaret produced Catherine's gold angel, which had escaped scrutiny so often, and pressed it into Luke's hand. With the coin, he set out on his mad errand.

To Luke, it did not seem so mad as it did to us. It was a superstitious age, and he acted upon the dying man's dream, vision, or illusion with the same reverence we apply to respectable information.

Yet Catherine was downright angry when she heard of it. "To send the poor lad on such a wild-goose chase!" she fumed. "But you are like many more girls; mark my words: by the time you have worn that Luke fairly out, and made him as sick of you as a dog, you will turn as fond on him as a cow on a calf, and 'Too late' will be the cry."

***

Two friars reached Holland from the south merely twelve hours after Luke had departed up the Rhine. Thus, whether wild-goose chase or not, the parties were rapidly nearing one another. For Jerome, unable to preach in Low Dutch, pushed on toward the coast, anxious to reach England as soon as possible. Had the stream borne them along, the friars would have missed Luke entirely, passing him in full stream below his intended station, but fate intervened.

About twenty miles above Luke's destination, Clement landed to preach in a large village. Towards the end of his sermon, he noticed a grey nun weeping. He approached her kindly, asking the cause of her grief.

"Nay," she replied, "it is not for myself these tears flow. 'Tis for my lost friend. Thy words remind me of what she was, and what she is, poor wretch." She confessed her distinction as a Franciscan, noting his status as a Dominican, but he reassured her, "It matters little, my sister, if we are both Christians, and if I can aid thee."

Moved by his eloquence, she revealed a terrible secret: a young nun, the darling of the convent and her bosom friend, had been lured away from her vows. After various gradations of sin, she was now living in a small inn as a chambermaid, effectively a decoy, selling her favors to wealthier customers. "Anywhere else," she lamented, "we might, by kindly violence, force her away from perdition. But this innkeeper serves the fierce baron on the heights, holds his ear, and would burn our convent to the ground were we to take her by force."

"Moreover, souls will not be saved by brute force," Clement observed wisely.

Jerome arrived shortly after. Persuaded by Clement to

To sit in wait for a letter that carries weight upon it is to allow the mind to shift from stillness into a restless current. Margaret, having sent her earnest missive, poured out the full reservoir of her spirit and her faith—whether in her father's dying vision or merely a desperate illusion. Once the ink was dry and Luke departed, a sudden chill settled over her. She marveled at her own gullibility, and her conscience, sharp as a needle, pricked her regarding Luke. Soon enough, Catherine arrived to scold her, and Margaret paid the heavy toll of shattered illusions and lifted spirits by sinking back into a profound despondency.

In this low state, she was found by a sturdy friend she had recently acquired, Joan Ketel. Joan entered radiating a bright idea.

"Margaret, I know the remedy for thy sickness!" Joan declared. "There is a hermit at Gouda, a wondrous holy man. When he is in the right frame of mind, he can tell thee what cometh."

"Ay, I have heard tales of him," Margaret murmured, hope draining from her eyes. Yet, with some difficulty, Joan persuaded her to walk out to Gouda to seek counsel. They carried a basket with butter and eggs and made their way to the hermitage.

How had these two women become such close companions? Six weeks prior, Jorian had fallen violently ill with a bowel malady. It began with raging agony; when that subsided, leaving him frail, a terrible symptom followed. Nothing, liquid or solid, could remain in his stomach for a moment. The physician, despairing, declared, "He must die if this continues; therefore, boil thou now a chicken with a golden angel floating in the water, and let him drink that!" Alas, the gilded broth shared the fate of the humblest fare. Next, the healer soaked the thumb of Saint Sergius in beef stock, yielding the same grim result. Desperate, Joan ran weeping to Margaret to borrow linen for a shroud.

"Let me see him," Margaret implored. She entered, laid a hand upon his wrist, and checked his pulse. "Ah!" she cried. "I suspect they have not touched the root. Open the window! Thou art stifling him. Now, change all his linens."

"Alack, woman, what need?" objected the medieval wife. "Why soil more linen for a dying man?"

"Do as I bid thee," said Margaret, her voice dull with resolve.

Joan, though bewildered, obeyed. Margaret returned with her apron heavy with a flowering herb. She brewed a decoction and brought it to the bedside. Before offering it to the patient, she took a spoonful herself and smiled with feigned delight. "That is fair," Jorian whispered, making a feeble effort at humor. "Why, 'tis sweet, and now 'tis bitter."

She engaged him in conversation immediately after he drank. That bitter-sweet mixture stayed with him. Observing his reaction, she played her cards carefully: she mixed a trace of strong Schiedam in the third spoonful and a beaten yolk of egg in the seventh. With the gentle patience characteristic of her sex, she coaxed his body back from Death's clutches. Finally, Nature, patted rather than kicked, set Jorian Ketel upon his feet again. However, the healer made them both swear a solemn oath never to reveal her guilty deed to a soul, lest she be cast into prison, separated from her child.

The simple remedy that saved Jorian was known as sweet feverfew. She had gathered it from his own garden, her keen eye having spotted it growing through the window pane.

Thus, Margaret and Joan arrived at the hermit's cave and placed their offerings—a modest basket of butter and eggs—upon the small stone platform. Margaret leaned forward to the aperture designed for communication and spoke: "Holy hermit, we bring thee butter and eggs of the finest sort. And I, a poor deserted girl, a wife though not truly wed, and mother of the sweetest babe, come to pray thee tell me whether he lives or dies, and if he remains true to his vows or has faltered."

A faint, weary voice drifted from within the dark cavern: "Trouble me not with the trifles of earth. Send me a holy friar; I am dying."

"Oh, Alas!" cried Margaret, tears welling instantly. "Is it even so, poor soul? Then let us enter to aid thee."

"Saints forbid! Thy voice is that of a woman. Send me a holy friar."

They turned back as they had come. Joan, unable to contain herself, asked, "Must women then be demons of darkness, barred from approaching a dying bed?"

But Margaret was too deeply bowed by grief to answer. Joan offered rough consolation, which remained unheard until she spoke of Jorian. "And Jorian will speak out ere long; he is just on the boil. He is very grateful to thee, believe it."

"Seeing is believing," Margaret replied, her tone quiet and bitter.

"Not that he does not think thou might have saved him with something far beyond the common, yonder feverfew." Joan continued, lowering her voice. "'A man of my stature to be cured with a weed?' says he. 'If there is a sorry herb,' says he. 'Why, I was thinking of pulling up all mine,' says he. I told him remedies are naught the better for being far-fetched; thou and feverfew cured him when the grand medicines failed utterly. So I say, 'Thou mayst go down on thy four bones to feverfew.' But indeed, at bottom, he is grateful. Thou art all his thought and all his talk. But he senses Gerard's kin circling about thee, good friends, and last night—"

"Well?"

"He made me vow not to tell thee."

"Prithee, tell me."

"Well, he said: 'If I utter what little I know, it shall not bring him back, and it will set all by the ears. I wish I had more sense,' said he. 'I am sore perplexed. But least said is soonest mended.' That is his favorite phrase; he returns to it from a mile away."

Margaret shook her head slowly. "Ay, we are wading in deep waters, my poor babe and I."

It was Saturday night, and Luke was nowhere to be seen.

"Poor Luke!" Margaret whispered. "It was very kind of him to undertake such an errand."

"He is one out of a hundred," Catherine answered warmly.

"Mother, dost thou think he would be kind to little Gerard?"

"I am certain he would. So, be kinder to him when he returns! Wilt thou promise?"

"Ay."

***

Brother Clement, guided by the nuns, bypassed a bend in the river and strode vigorously toward a station several miles closer to the coast than where Luke lay in ambush for Gerard Eliassoen. Early the following morning, he departed and arrived in Rotterdam by noon. Making straight for the port, he intended not to keep Jerome waiting.

Upon observing several monks of his order along the quay, he approached them. Yet, Jerome was absent. He asked one if Jerome had arrived. "Surely, brother," the monk replied.

"Prithee, where is he?"

"Where? Why, there!" the monk pointed, directing Clement's gaze to a ship cutting swiftly across the water in full sail. Immediately, Clement noticed that every monk present was staring seaward.

"What? Gone without me! Oh, Jerome! Jerome!" Clement cried, his voice cracking with anguish. Several friars turned, staring at him.

"You must be Brother Clement," one said after a pause. On this, they kissed him and greeted him with warm brotherhood, handing him a letter Jerome had entrusted to them. It was a hasty scrawl. The writer conveyed a cold message: a ship bound for England was imminent, and he loathed to lose time. Though Clement might follow if he wished, Jerome urged him to stay and preach to his own countrymen. "Give glory to God, brother; thou hast a wonderful power over Dutch hearts, but thou art no match for those haughty islanders; thou art too tender."

"And know thou," the letter concluded, "that on the voyage I encountered one who asked for thee under the name thou didst bear in the flesh. Beware! Let not the world catch thee again in any silken net. Remember, Solitude, Fasting, and Prayer are the sword, spear, and shield of the soul. Farewell."

Clement was deeply shocked and mortified by this contemptuous desertion and the icy tone of the missive. He promised the benevolent monks to sleep at the convent and preach wherever the prior appointed, for Jerome had once raised him to the skies as a preacher. Yet, wounded to the quick, he withdrew abruptly, needing solitude.

He questioned himself: Was there some incurable fault in him, repulsive to so true a son of Dominic? Or was Jerome himself devoid of that Christian Love which St. Paul held above Faith itself? Shipwrecked together, saved upon the same fragment of wreckage, pupil, penitent, son in the Church—and now, for four hundred miles, a fellow traveler in Christ—to be shaken off like dirt at the first chance, with harsh, cold disdain. "Why are worldly hearts no colder nor less trustworthy than this?" he pondered. "The only one who ever truly loved me lies in a

The following day, a Sunday marked by the bustle of creation, saw Catherine's household in the Hoog Straet abuzz with activity. The shop stood ready, a testament to weeks of labor, with the names "Cornelis and Sybrandt" freshly painted above the entrance and a sign depicting a white lamb suckling a gilt sheep fluttering gently in the breeze. Eli had arrived laden with fresh stock from his store, ensuring they began with ample provision. Within the home, the atmosphere was electric; the parents' hearts burned with a fervent glow, driven by the resolve to embrace commerce over idleness. They strolled side by side through the garden, their spirits lifted by the prospect of earning rather than wasting time, vowing to adhere to business with the tenacity of wax.

Through an open window, Catherine's mother, sharp-eyed and ever-watchful, caught the drift of their conversation. She called out to Eli, announcing that evening supper would be an inaugural feast, welcoming Margaret and her son. It was to be a gathering where wisdom regarding trade would flow freely from the elders, accompanied by wine raised to the health of those converting from agriculture to commerce—a shift away from wild oats and unremunerative toil. Margaret, eager to shake off a lingering malaise, had joined her mother-in-law. Both women wore complexions flushed as if by the very fires they tended. Soon, Joan entered carrying a crisp salad harvested from Jorian's garden.

"He sliced it especially for you, Margaret," Joan remarked, her tone laced with playful jealousy. "He said you were to feast today. But tell me, lass, what sermons were delivered in the new church? I have never heard preaching before."

"I wish I had been there," Margaret murmured, her voice hollow with longing. "I feel parched, drained of the heavenly dew."

"Why, he preaches again this afternoon," Joan noted. "Perhaps you are needed here, though?"

"Not yet," Catherine interjected warmly. "Go on, if your heart is set."

"But I fear I might dampen the spirits at table," Margaret hesitated. "Unless I return warmed by a fine sermon."

"Then you must move swiftly," Joan advised, her gaze fixed on the street. "Look! The people are heading that way. By my soul, here comes the holy friar!" The energetic woman bounded from the room, crossed the road, and intercepted the monk. Returning moments later, her face alight, she declared, "I was determined to see him close up. 'My daughter,' says he, 'I shall visit him ere sunset, God willing.' His voice was sweet, but look at those thin cheeks, Margaret, and those large eyes—not unlike yours."

"I long to hear him," Margaret confessed, though her brow furrowed. "Yet my cap is not quite clean, and all will be wearing their snow-white veils."

"There, take my handkerchief from the basket," Catherine instructed kindly. "You cannot keep the child; I need him for my poor Kate. She is having one of her difficult days."

Margaret ascended the stairs with the boy, finding Kate lying in bed. "How fares you, darling? I need not ask; you are in sore pain, yet you smile. Look, I have brought you something you cherish."

"Two, by my count," Kate whispered, offering an angelic smile. A sudden spasm seized her, causing the air in the room to seem heavy.

"In your lap?" Margaret asked, interpreting Kate's gesture. "No, he is too heavy, and you are in such distress."

"I love him too dearly to care for his weight," Kate replied softly.

Seizing the moment, Margaret attended to her own toilette. "I am bound for the church," she announced, "to listen to a beautiful preacher." Kate let out a soft sigh. "A minute ago, Kate, I was eager to go. That has been my state for the past month—up and down, like the rolling waves of the Zuyder Zee. I would much rather stay with you. Say the word!"

"Nay," Kate insisted, her eyes filling with tears. "Pray, go. Bring me back every word. Alas, I cannot go myself." These words broke Margaret's hesitation. She leaned forward, kissed Kate tenderly, glanced lovingly at the sleeping infant, and let out a small sigh. "I think I dare not," she whispered. "I could never bear to part with him, even briefly. My father forbade waking a child by day or night. If it pleases thee to rouse him, call upon Aunt Kate for the two new words thou hast learned." With a final, affectionate look over her shoulder, she closed the door soundlessly.

Downstairs, Catherine turned to Joan. "Will you lend a hand peeling these, Joan?"

"With pleasure, mistress." The kitchen fell into a rhythm of silent, vigorous preparation. "Now, Joan, remember: those who assist me in cooking and serving shall share in the eating. That is a rule."

"There are harsher laws in Holland than that," Joan laughed. "Your will is my delight, mistress. For my Luke has already missed his supper, thanks to his digging today by good fortune." Just then, Margaret descended.

"Eh, woman, that is no pleasant trade," Catherine observed, gesturing to Margaret's refreshed appearance. "She has just washed her face and coiffed her hair, and now who is fit to compare? Rotterdam, indeed, for you!" She snapped her fingers playfully. "Give me a kiss, hussy! Mind, Eli will not wait for the Duke to join us. Do not linger once the service ends."

Together, Catherine and Joan watched Margaret depart down the street. Among homely wives stepping out of doors, the departure itself became half an event. Catherine paused, reflecting aloud. "Joan, it feels as though I am sending off my own daughter to church, standing watch behind her door—a scene repeated countless times. Joan, lass, she refuses to hear a word spoken against our Gerard. And since he lives, he has treated her cruelly. This is why my heart aches for the poor wretch. I am older and wiser than she; therefore, I shall wed her to that simple Luke, and thus shall the matter conclude. What is one grandchild, after all?"

The service had commenced just as Margaret stepped across the threshold of the vast, unfinished cathedral of St. Laurence. It was a monumental undertaking, clearly not destined for completion within a single year. While the side aisles bore roofs, the central nave and the sanctuary remained open to the elements; yet the stone pillars and soaring arches stood with a majestic perfection, some already washed in bright white lime. Of the windows, only one held glass; the remaining apertures were mere jagged wounds in the masonry.

And today, these raw imperfections transformed the space into something transcendent. A glorious summer afternoon bathed the interior, casting sunlight through the irregular gaps in fragments of miraculous geometry. The beams danced playful, bewitching patterns across the rough surfaces, streaming through the gaping walls to carve the dark, cool side aisles with rivers of radiance. They dazzled upon the white columns, making them glow with an inner life, while the great central aisle became a checkerboard of shifting light and shadow. These shades offered a coolness more profound than any artificial chill, and the lights appeared as patches of amber diamonds ignited with heavenly fire. Above, the azure sky vaulted the lofty aisle, pressing down intimately from west to east.

A sea of white bonnets contrasted exquisitely with this vivid canopy of blue. Though the massive central aisle was packed to capacity, a hushed silence reigned. The words and the mellifluous, earnest cadence of the preacher held the multitude mute.

Margaret stood entranced by the sheer beauty, the depth of devotion, and "the great calm" that permeated the air. Seeking shelter, she slipped behind a pillar in the north aisle. Even though the distance rendered the words indistinct, a sweet, languid peace settled over her, stirred by the sanctity of the place and the musician quality of the voice. Like warm oil trickling over troubled waves, it smoothed the turmoil within her heart. Leaning wearily against the stone, her eyes half-closed, everything around her softened into a dreamlike haze. It felt, simply, good to be there.

Soon, she observed a lady rising from a prime seat opposite to escape the scorching rays pouring directly onto her head from the high window. Seizing the opportunity, Margaret moved swiftly and quietly, securing the spot. Now positioned beside a pillar in the south aisle, less than fifty feet from the rostrum, she sat at his side, slightly behind, able to catch every syllable.

However, her focus fractured moments later by the sight of a man's head and shoulders bobbing up and down with a peculiar, comical rhythm, tempting a smile despite herself. Yet, beneath the surface, it was no longer essentially droll.

It was the sexton, diligently digging.

She realized this instantly by peering backward through the window gap, tracing the shadow to its source. Just then, a specific inflection of the preacher's voice struck her ear and mind with such distinct force that it seemed to literally vibrate her very bones. Her hand flew instinctively to her bosom, startled by the sudden, strange thrill. Turning sharply, she sought the speaker. His back was to her, revealing nothing but the shaved crown of his tonsure. She sighed, feeling a contradiction; the physical reality of the tonsure effectively undermined the profound emotional resonance of the tone.

Still, she leaned forward slightly, eyes cast downward, praying for that accent to return. It did not. Yet the entire voice grew strangely potent within her, rising and falling as the preacher warmed to his subject. It seemed to awaken faint echoes of a thousand happy, long-lost memories. She refused to look away, unwilling to dispel the melancholy, intoxicating pleasure this voice evoked.

Suddenly, midway through an eloquent passage, the preacher halted.

An involuntary sigh escaped her lips; a soothing melody had abruptly ceased. Was the sermon finished? No. Glancing around, she saw the congregation remained seated, unmoving.

Then, a number of faces began to turn toward her. She whipped her gaze backward, finding nothing there. Startled expressions nearby fixed themselves upon the preacher. Following their stares, she froze. In the pulpit, a face stared back, pale and rigid as a corpse. The friar's eyes, naturally large and accentuated by the hollows of his cheeks, were dilated to a supernatural size, blazing outward from a bloodless visage, locked directly upon her.

Margaret cringed, spinning about in terror, convinced some dreadful entity stood beside her. But there was nothing; she was merely an external figure within the listening throng.

In that instant, the church erupted into commotion. Figures surged upward, craning necks forward; agitated countenances swayed from the friar to Margaret, and back again. The rapid turning of so many caps created a deafening rustle. Shrieks from nervous women and the buzzing murmur of men filled the air. Seeing hundreds of eyes leveled at her, Margaret shrank terrified behind the pillar, stealing one frantic, hurried glance at the speaker before burying herself in the shadows.

That fleeting glimpse captured an expression on the stricken face that sent a shiver racing down her spine.

She swooned, collapsing onto a pile of woodchips left by the laborers, burying her face in her hands. The sermon resumed, and she heard the sound of it, but the sense eluded her. Her mind spun in a vortex, unable to coalesce into any form other than this terrifying truth: *upon that prodigy-struck face, I had seen a look.* And the memory of that look now made her tremble from head to toe.

For that look was "RECOGNITION."

After wavering uncertainly, the sermon concluded in a strain of exalted, feverish eloquence, driving the crowd to forget the preacher's strange pause and ghastly stare. Margaret merged hastily with the departing throng and exited the church.

Outside, they dispersed toward their homes, but she paused at the door, turning instead into the churchyard to Peter's grave. Though poor, she had secured him a slab and a headstone. Sitting upon the stone, she pressed a kiss to it, then drew her apron over her head to conceal her hair from any passersby.

"Father," she whispered, "you have often heard me say I am wading in deep waters; but now I begin to think God alone knows the bottom of them. I will follow that friar round the world, but I shall keep him at arm's length. And he shall tell me why he looked toward me like a dead man awakened, with no soul standing behind me. Oh, Father; you often praised me here: speak a word for me there. For I am wading in deep waters."

Her father's tomb commanded a commanding view of the church door. There, with her face veiled, she sat upon the stone, waiting to intercept the holy preacher.

The sanctuary, dappled with shafts of sunlight and draped in a canopy of celestial purple, soothed Father Clement's weary soul. It lifted his thoughts directly from the dust of the road to the presence of the Divine Architect. Yet, as his gaze swept across the vast nave, it was seized by the sea of humanity filling the aisle—a thousand heads bowed beneath snowy caps, intricately filigreed with gold. Though he had traversed many hundreds of leagues since leaving home, nothing had ever struck him with such profound majesty save perhaps the silent expanse of winter snow. Earlier, in the morning, his voice had cracked with thunderous denunciations; yet now, under the soft, golden light of afternoon, those stern threats rang hollow, discordant with the peace surrounding him. A deep, aching tenderness welled within his chest for this multitude. These were poor souls, hearing far too little of divine charity from the pulpit in their day, drowned instead by the clamor of sin and worldly need. Today, however, he spoke only of the glad tidings of salvation. The congregation leaned forward, captivated by the gentle, earnest cadence of his tongue.

Clement was not among those preachers who spun like weathercocks atop steeples, rattling their bodies to distract the ear. His influence worked through the quiet movement of hearts rather than the agitation of flesh. Warm with this thought—that not a single soul in that throng should miss the joyous news of Christ's love—he turned slowly toward the south aisle.

And there, bathed in a river of sunlight pouring through the window, stood the radiant countenance of Margaret Brandt. He fixed his gaze upon her, and the sensation that followed was akin to physical numbness, freezing both his soul and his limbs.

Soon, the words lodged in his throat, dissolving into silence. Trembling, he stared at her. There, with her auburn hair catching the beams like the halo of a saint, her face aglow with an inner fire doubled by the sun, stood the woman he loved until death. She leaned lightly against a white marble column, her lashes cast low in a posture of tender, humble listening. He recalled seeing her in that same pose a hundred times, back in the days when life had been bright and unburdened. Nothing had changed. If anything, she was a shade more mature, a depth richer in loveliness.

Then, with a start that made his eyes widen in horror and his cheeks drain of color, he realized the impossibility of it. He gazed around, expecting the crowd to fade away, to hear a rustle of rising voices, yet he could not tear his prodigy-struck eyes from that face—all vitality, all bloom, all beauty, framed by that glorious, shimmering hair. He watched her, convinced she must vanish like a phantom, yet she remained solid and real.

Suddenly, she lifted her head. Her violet eyes met his, full and piercing. At the shock of that encounter, Clement was beside himself. His lips parted to scream her name, but she turned swiftly, slipping away into the shadows before he could utter a sound. As she vanished, she offered him one final, fleeting glance. It was swift as lightning, yet it held a weight that sent his mind reeling in a dizzy whirl, leaving him panting and clutching the pulpit convulsively. That look, though devoid of clear recognition, was the startled, inquiring, indescribable expression that always precedes it. It froze him in a trance. With a mighty, desperate effort, he stammered out a few incoherent syllables that no one understood, then forced himself to resume the discourse. He faltered and babbled for a moment, struggling to anchor himself, until gradually, with her out of sight, he managed to view the apparition as a vision from the other world. Rising into a state of unnatural, electric excitement, he delivered his conclusion with an eloquence that bordered on rhapsody, electrifying the simple hearts of his listeners.

When the sermon finally ended, Clement sat heavily upon the pulpit stool, terribly shaken. But quickly, an idea peculiar to the age took hold of him. He reasoned that since he had sought her grave at Sevenbergen in vain, she must now be permitted to reveal herself to him in this new place. Perhaps she was buried right here, close to that same pillar where her spirit had appeared. Once this notion took root, it settled upon his mind with the absolute certainty of fact. He felt compelled to speak to the sexton—the same poor lad whom he had once saved—who was currently working outside, minding the yard.

The church was now empty. Climbing down from the pulpit, Clement stepped through an opening in the south wall onto the grass and approached the sexton. He recognized the boy instantly, though Jorian could not possibly suspect the shapely-bearded knight he had rescued was now hidden beneath the guise of a holy friar. Time had altered him profoundly: the loss of his beard had reshaped his features, while his tonsure, short gray hair, and pale, gaunt cheeks—marked by long fasts and vigils—had completed his transformation.

"My son," said Friar Clement softly, "if you retain any memory of those whom you have laid in the earth, pray tell me, is any Christian buried within the church, near one of the pillars?"

"Nay, father," replied Jorian, "here in the churchyard lie buried all who rest here. Why ask?"

"No matter. Pray tell me, then, where lieth Margaret Brandt."

"Margaret Brandt?" Jorian stared at the speaker with a look of stupefaction. "She died about three years ago, and was buried here."

"Oh, that is another matter," said Jorian, his tone shifting. "That was before my time. The vicar might tell you, likely. If she was a gentlewoman, or at least wealthy enough to pay him his fee."

"Alas, my son, she was poor—and paid a heavy penalty for it—but she was born of decent folk. Her father, Peter, was a learned physician. She came hither from Sevenbergen—to die."

As Clement uttered these words, his head sank upon his breast. He seemed to lose all power or desire to press Jorian further. Doubtfully, he wondered if he truly knew where he stood; he was utterly lost in the past.

Jorian lowered his spade and stood upright in the fresh earth, arms akimbo. "Are you making a fool of me, holy sir," he said sulkily, "or has some wag been making a fool of you!" Having vented his irritation, he resumed digging with a vigor that betrayed a ruffled temper.

Clement looked at him with a puzzled, gently reproachful eye. The tone was rude, the words unintelligible. Good-natured though crusty, Jorian had not thrown up three spadefuls before shame washed over him. "Why, what a base churl am I to speak thus to thee, holy father," he blurted out, "and thou standest there looking at me like a lamb!" A sudden realization struck him. "Aha! I have it! 'Tis Peter Brandt's grave you would fain see, not Margaret's. He does lie here, hard by the west door. There; I'll show you."

He laid down his spade and donned his doublet and jerkin to accompany the friar. He did not know that anyone was sitting upon Peter's tomb, still less that she was waiting specifically for this holy friar. Nor did Pietro Vanucci or Andrea recognize him without his beard. Indeed, a beard that has never known a razor grows in a picturesque, characteristic form, becoming a defining feature of the face; its removal can sometimes serve as an effective disguise, obscuring identity behind the familiar lines of youth and vigor.

As Jorian adjusted his doublet and jerkin, preparing to depart for Peter Brandt's grave, his thoughts raced ahead of his steps. "They used to whisper of him as a sorcerer in Sevenbergen," he mused aloud, pacing the churchyard. "And it is said he bestowed a final token of his craft upon them. He claimed to have glimpsed Margaret's son descending the Rhine in splendid attire, laden with wealth, though his visage bore the scars of a foreign blade and his limbs were incomplete. Yet, alas, nothing materialized from such visions. Margaret still mourns her lost boy, and Peter rests beneath his neighbors, silent and still, save for the stone slab she commissioned to seal his fate."

He placed both hands upon the cold edge of the mound, poised to lift himself from the earth, when the Friar's hand, trembling with a sudden, strange intensity, settled heavily upon his shoulder. A hushed, urgent whisper cut through the air.

"How long since Peter Brandt passed?"

"Two months past, Father. Pray, why do you ask?"

"And was it his daughter who laid him to rest?"

"Nay, I interred him, though she funded the stone and oversaw the work."

"Then... but he had but one daughter, did he not? Margaret?"

"No fewer, at least, that he acknowledged."

"Do you believe, then, that Margaret lives?"

"B Believe? Good heavens, I would be dead without hope!" Jorian laughed, the sound harsh in the quiet air. "Riddle me that. You speak of love, Father?"

"Not more than reason dictates, considering I am a husband and father of four sturdy lads. No, the truth is far stranger. She saved my life scarcely six weeks hence. Had she perished, I surely would have followed. I could not swallow food; physicians could do naught for me. My wife, Joan, muttered, 'Time to buy thee a shroud.' I replied, 'Ay, perhaps, but let us borrow one first.' Then in stepped this Lady Margaret—though she is dead, by your account—and she opened the windows, shifted me from my bed, and healed me in the blink of an eye. With what? With the scurviest herbs, plucked from my own garden, sweet feverfew. An herb? Nay, 'tis a weed, until it cures me. Now, whenever I pass my humble cottage, I doff my bonnet and cry, 'Fly service to thee!' Why, Father, you grow pale, then red, then white again? What troubles you?"

"The surprise—the joy—the wonder—the terror," gasped Clement, clutching at his throat.

"Why, what concern is it to thee? Art thou of kin to Margaret Brandt?"

"Nay, but I knew one who loved her deeply, so profoundly that her death nearly shattered his spirit, body and soul. And yet, thou speakest of her living. And I believe thee."

Jorian stared, the silence stretching taut between them. After a long pause, he spoke with solemn gravity. "Father, I have answered your inquiries truly. Now, for the sake of our Lady, answer me but two. Did you truly know one who loved this poor maiden? And where?"

Clement stood on the brink of confession, yet Jerome's letter haunted him, shrinking him from uttering the name he had worn in the world.

"I knew him in Italy," he murmured.

"If thou knewest him, thou canst tell me his name," Jorian pressed, his tone cautious.

"Gerard Eliassoen."

"Oh, but this is strange. Stay, what caused thee to claim Margaret Brandt was dead?"

"A letter arrived from Margaret Van Eyck," Clement confessed, sinking onto a nearby bench. His strength seemed to fail him. "It told Gerard that the woman he loved lay buried. Let me sit, for my spirit falters. Foul play! Foul play!"

"Father," Jorian said, kneeling beside him, "I thank Heaven for sending thee. Sit, thou looketh like a specter; thou hast fasted too much. Methinks I hold the key to this riddle. If I do, there be two knaves in this town whose heads I would gladly crush against the soil like this very dust." He ground his teeth and lifted his heavy spade high above his head, bringing it down furiously upon the loose earth. "Foul play? Thou hast spoken truer words than any man in his life. If thou knowest where Gerard dwells now, lose no time in showing him the trap. Mine is a dull brain, yet sometimes the slow hound discerns the scent. Go, and I believe we have seized two ends of the same twisted rope, and a most malignant one."

Jorian then stripped away the pleasantries customary to his station and delivered the core of his tale. Employed by the Burgomaster of Tergou to mend an upper floor, he had grown curious during the removal of his tools. Loud voices below had drawn him down; lying prone, he had overheard the Burgomaster discussing a letter Cornelis and Sybrandt intended to swap for one destined for Gerard by Hans Memling. "Their will was noble, but their stomachs were small," the old man had boasted, displaying a drawer brimming with silver to embolden the thieves. "If they performed the deed, they might take as much as they could grasp."

"Well, Father," Jorian continued, his voice dropping, "I paid little heed then, save for the profit that kept me sleepless. Next dawn, ere the sun peeked, who should I encounter emerging from their homes but Cornelis and Sybrandt, each sporting a blackened eye. 'Oho,' I muttered, 'what mark hath Hans left upon thee? I trust that is all thou hast gained.' Did they not hasten to the Burgomaster's residence? I hid."

At this juncture, the monk's nostrils flared, his restless eyes betraying a suspended breath.

"Well, Father," Jorian persisted, "the Burgomaster summoned them to that same chamber. He held a letter, though I am no scholar; yet I possess as many eyes as the Pope, and I saw the drawer open. Those two rogues plunged their hands in, drawing out the silver. Saints preserve us, how they strove to hoard more, and more, and more! Sybrandt, moreover, had smeared his hand in some sticky substance, likely glue, carrying away one or two pieces adhered to his palm. 'Tis a sin to laugh. Luck, as usual, sided against them. They completed the trick, yet how they did it remains unknown until Judgment Day. They left their eternal treasures in that drawer. A mere handful of silver for their trouble; the Devil won the bargain. There, Father, that weighs heavily on my mind. Often I longed to confide, but dared not speak to the women; otherwise, Margaret would have found no friend left in this world. Those two black-hearted villains are the favorites of fortune. Behold, they have just acquired a grand new shop in Hoog Straet. Their sign is a gilt sheep and a lambkin; a pair of wolves suckling their dam would be nearer the truth. There, the entire family feasts today. Oh, 'tis a fine world. What, not a word, holy Father? Thou sittest like stone, unable to cast even a curse upon them, the stony-hearted miscreants. Was it not enough that the poor lad stood alone in a strange land? Must his own flesh and blood conspire to bury the sole blessing those enemies left him? Consider her pining, yearning for years, gazing down the street for Gerard! So constant, so tender, so true: my wife swears no woman ever loved a man with greater devotion than she does the boy whom these villains have torn from her. Not a day passes without her weeping salt tears. And when I reflect that, but for those two greedy, lying knaves, that winsome youth, whom I rescued, might walk beside her today, the happiest soul in Holland; and that sweet lass, who saved my life, might rest her cheek upon her sweetheart's shoulder, the happiest in Holland instead of the saddest; oh, I thirst for their blood! The nasty, sneaking, lying, cunning, cowardly, heartless, bowelless—"

How now?"

The monk leaped upward, livid with fury and despair, rushing headlong from the spot, both hands clenched and raised heavenward. So terrible was this inarticulate eruption of wrath that Jorian's petty anger died instantly, leaving him standing in stunned dismay behind the tempest he had unleashed.

Absorbed in his shock, Jorian felt a small, tremulous hand seize his arm. It was Margaret Brandt.

He startle d; her presence there at that moment seemed utterly inexplicable. She had waited long upon Peter's tombstone, but the friar had not returned. Seeking him within the church, she had failed to find him.

Suddenly, a gigantic shadow, belonging to a friar, swept rapidly across the floor, passing through her as if she were invisible. Fear seized her lips, threatening a scream, but she recalled Jorian's spectral form emerging from the churchyard and managed to scramble out the nearest exit with difficulty. Just as she fled, Clement vanished down the street, yet her gaze caught the wild agitation etched upon his features. Jorian and she locked eyes, both watching the frantic figure of the distant monk.

"Well?" she whispered, trembling.

"Well," he

Supper time had fallen, and the household gathered round the board, save for one. Margaret was absent, and though Catherine expressed surprise, Eli declared he would wait. "I told her you'd not wait for the Duke," Catherine remarked. Eli shook his head gently. "She is no Duke; she is a poor, gentle lass who has waited not minutes, but many long years, for a graceless son of mine. Lay the meats forth, then, Mistress Joan; we shall eat without further loss when she arrives."

The savory scent of the smoking dishes proved irresistible, and Eli relented. "She will come if we begin," he assured them. "They always do." He beckoned Mistress Joan to sit, assuring her she was a guest, not a servant. As the covers were drawn away and the knives raised, a frantic footstep echoed across the threshold—not Margaret's return, but the entrance of a Dominican friar, his countenance drained of all color, twisted in a mask of livid fury.

In a heartbeat, the monk stood between the table and his enemies, looming over Cornelis and Sybrandt like an eagle over trembling prey. With both hands hovering above their shrinking forms, he unleashed a torrent of invective, cursing them by name, soul, and body in this life and the next. His words were a blight, a devastation, terrible enough to freeze the blood. "Cursed be the lips!" he shrieked, his voice cracking with agony, "that spoke the lie of Margaret's death! May they rot before the grave and kiss white-hot iron in hell thereafter! Doubly cursed be the hands that forged those deceitful letters, may they be severed by the hangman's blade and handle the fires of damnation forever! Thrice accursed be the cruel hearts that conceived this damnable falsehood to rend true love asunder! May they sicken and wither on earth, joyless, loveless, hopeless, and turn to dust before their time, burning in eternal flame!"
