His wrath descended upon the meat before them and every atom of their existence, from hair to sole. Turning from the cowering pair, who had hidden beneath the table, he plucked a letter from his bosom and hurled it before his father. "Read this, thou hard old man, that imprisoned thy son! Read, and behold the monsters thou hast spawned! Let the memory of these wrongs dwell with thee forever! I shall meet thee again at the Judgment Day; on earth, thou shalt never see me more."

With the speed of lightning, he vanished, leaving the room silent, the figures round the smoking board stiff, cold, and white as statues.

Just then, the scene unfolded before Margaret and Jorian. They saw the petrified men and women frozen around the untouched feast, a tableau akin to those feigned by Eastern poets. Margaret's gaze swept the room, and she gasped, "Oh, joy! All are here; no blood hath been spilled. Oh, you cruel, cruel men! I thank God he hath not slain you."

At the sight of her, Catherine let out an eloquent scream before burying her face away. But Eli, who had just scanned the false letter and begun to comprehend its horror, saw the other victim enter with her sorrows mirrored in her pale, sweet face. A transport of rage seized him. He sprang to his feet, shouting, "Stand clear, and let me lay hands on the traitors! I will hang for them!" In an instant, he drew his short sword and lunged at them.

"Fly!" Margaret screamed. "Fly!"

Cornelis and Sybrandt slipped howling under the table, crawling out the far side. Before they could reach the door, the furious old man circled back to intercept them. Catherine merely screamed and wrung her hands; her notables were useless in such a crisis, and surely blood would have flowed. Yet, Margaret and Jorian seized the fiery old man's arms, holding him fast with all their might until the fugitives gained the street. Only then did they release him, and he went vainly raging after them into the night.

They fled a furlong, running like hares. Behind them, Eli hacked down the board bearing their names, dragged it within, and threw it into the chimney. Catherine sat rocking herself, her apron pulled over her head. Joan had rushed to her husband. Margaret clung to Catherine, her arms tight around the weeping woman, panting yet striving to offer comfort.

"It cannot be," Eli murmured, breaking the spell. "Oh, my poor children! Oh, miserable mother! 'Tis a mercy Kate was ill upstairs. There, I have lived to thank God for that!" A fresh burst of sobs shook her. "It would have killed her. He had better have remained in Italy than return to curse his own flesh and blood and set us all by the ears."

"Oh, hold your chatter, woman," Eli cried angrily. "Thou art still on the side of the evildoer. Thou art cheaply served; thy weakness made the rogues what they are. I sought to correct them in their youth with sore ills requiring sharp remedies, yet thou sidedst with their faults and undermined me, baffling wise severity." He turned to Margaret. "And thou, Margaret, leave comforting her who ought rather to comfort thee. For what is her hurt to thine? She never possessed a grain of justice under her skin, nor will she."

He extended his hand. "So come thou to me, that am thy father from this hour."

It was a command. She kissed Catherine farewell and tottered to him. He seated her beside him, and she laid her feeble head upon his honest breast. Yet not a tear fell; the sorrow was too deep for tears.

"Poor lamb," he whispered. After a pause, his voice broke, "Come, good folks," he addressed Jorian and Joan. "We are in a little trouble, as ye see; but that is no cause for starvation. For our Lady's sake, fall to, and add not to my grief the reputation of a churl. What the dickens!" He forced a sudden, ghastly attempt at stout-heartedness. "The more knaves I have the luck to shake off, the greater my need of true men and women to help me clear the dish and cheer mine eye with honest faces where else there are gaps. Eat, I do entreat ye."

Catherine, sobbing, backed his request. Poor, simple, antique, hospitable souls! Jorian, whose appetite, especially following his illness, was keen, moved to accept this invitation. But Joan whispered a word in his ear, and he instantly recoiled. "Nay, I'll touch no meat that Holy Church hath cursed."

"In sooth, I forgot," Eli said apologetically. "My son, who was reared at my table, hath cursed my victuals. That seems strange. Well, what God wills, man must bow to."

The supper was flung out into the yard.

Jorian took his wife home, and a heavy sadness reigned in Eli's house that night.

Meanwhile, where was Clement?

He lay prostrate upon the floor of the convent church, his lips pressed against the lowest step of the altar. He existed in a state of indescribable terror, misery, penitence, and self-abasement, through which glimmered fleeting rays of joy that Margaret lived. Night fell, finding him there weeping and praying; dawn would have found him there still. But suddenly, absorbed in his own wrongs and Margaret's, he realized he had committed another sin besides intemperate rage. He had neglected a dying man.

He rose instantly, groaning under the weight of his accumulated wickedness, and set out to make amends. The weather had shifted; it rained hard. When he cleared the town, he heard the wolves bay, close upon his heels. Yet Clement was himself again, or nearly so. He thought little of danger or discomfort, driven by a shameful omission of religious duty to repair. He strode stoutly forward through rain and darkness, beating his breast and crying, "ME A CULPA! ME A CULPA!"

To describe the inner turmoil of a mind so sensitive, a conscience so tender, and a heart so devoted to God within such a brief span of time following such a sudden and terrible shock would require a vocabulary beyond human reach. Imagine yourself in his shoes, bearing the weight of that specific burden. If I were to chronicle every thought, the book would indeed need to end here. Instead, let us focus on his next two decisive actions, which reveal the state of his spirit once the initial storm of anguish had finally calmed.

After spending a night beside the dying hermit, exchanging mutual spiritual consolations, Clement did not head back to Rotterdam. His destination was Tergou. There, he intended to face his mortal foe, the burgomaster, armed with a parchment—a document whose history was, in its own right, a tale of romance—to force the return of what rightfully belonged to Margaret.

By the time he reached Tergou, Clement was heated and covered in dust. He paused at a public fountain, unwrapping black bread and drinking deeply from the cool water. Mid-meal, however, a female servant burst forward, breathless, begging him to accompany her immediately to shrive her dying master. With a swift motion, Clement returned his bread to his wallet and followed her without a single word spoken.

She led him directly to the Stadthouse. As they crossed the threshold, Clement drew back slightly, a faint shudder passing over him at the sight of where she was leading. Yet, he quickly composed himself and ascended the stairs. At the top, propped high by pillows, lay his deadly enemy. To all outward appearance, the old man looked already like a corpse.

From the doorway, Clement watched the scene for a moment, recalling the tower, the forest, and the letter. "Peace be with you," he whispered softly, his voice trembling just enough to betray the effort it took.

The sick man received him with eager gratitude, as best his failing strength allowed. "Thank Heaven, Father," he cried, "you have arrived in time to absolve me from my sins. Pray for my soul, both you and your brothers."

"My son," Clement replied, "absolution comes only after confession. In this act, there must be no concealment, for the salvation of your soul depends upon it. Consider carefully wherein you have most grievously offended God and the Church, while I pray for the wisdom necessary to guide you."

Clement knelt and offered a prayer. Rising, he addressed Ghysbrecht with a facade of calm, "Now, my son, confess your sins."

"Oh, Father!" the sick man exclaimed, "They are many and great."

"Then great must be your penitence, my son, so that you may find God's mercy equally vast."

Clasping his hands, Ghysbrecht began to speak, displaying every sign of contrition. He admitted to eating meat during Lent, frequently missing Mass on Sundays and feast days, and casually neglecting other religious observances, listing them with scrupulous detail.

Upon finishing, the friar spoke quietly, "'Tis well, my son. Those are faults. Now, to your crimes. You ought to have begun with those."

"Why, Father?" Ghysbrecht asked, confused. "If these are not crimes, what else lies to my account?"

"Am I confessing to you, or are you confessing to me?" Clement asked, his tone sharpening with severity.

"Forgive me, Father! I meant to confess to you. But I do not know what you mean by 'crimes.'"

"Do you claim to be free of the Seven Deadly Sins?"

"Heaven forbid I should be guilty of them. I know them not by name."

"Many who commit them cannot name them. Begin with the sin that leads to lying, theft, and murder."

"I am clean of that, any way. What do you call it?"

"Avarice, my son."

"Avarice?" Ghysbrecht stammered. "I have been a thrifty man all my life, yet I have kept a generous table and never forgot the poor. But alas, I am a great sinner! Perhaps the next one will catch me. What is the next?"

"We have not finished with this one. Remember, the Church cannot be trifled with."

"But Father, am I in a fit state to trifle with Her now? Avarice? Avarice?" The old man looked utterly puzzled and innocent.

"Hast thou ever robbed the fatherless?" the friar inquired.

"Me? Robbed the fatherless?" Ghysbrecht gasped, recoiling. "Not that I mind."

"Once more, my son," Clement warned, his voice hardening, "I am forced to tell you that you are trifling with the Church. Wretched man! Another evasion, and I shall depart. Let demons gather round your bed and drag you down to the bottomless pit."

"Oh, do not leave me! Do not leave me!" shrieked the terrified old man. "The Church knows everything. I must have robbed the fatherless. I will confess. Where do I start? My memory for names is shattered."

His defense was skilled, but in this instance, it failed.

"Hast thou forgotten Floris Brandt?" Clement asked, his voice like stone.

The sick man threw himself up in bed, seized by a pitiable terror. "How did you know that?" he cried.

"The Church knows many things," Clement said coldly, "by many paths dark to you. Wretched impenitent soul, you called her to your side, hoping to deceive her. You said, 'I will not confess to the cure, but to some friar who knows not my misdeeds. Thus I will cheat the Church on my deathbed and die as I have lived.' But God, kinder to you than you are to yourself, has sent you one whom you could not deceive. He has tested you; He was patient, warning you not to mock Holy Church. Yet all is in vain. You cannot confess because you remain as stubborn as stone. Die, then, as you have lived."

Turning his back, Clement prepared to leave. But Ghysbrecht, driven to the brink of despair, lunged forward and grabbed the friar's robe. "Oh, holy man, mercy! Stay! I will confess all, everything. I robbed my friend Floris. Alas, if it had ended there, for he lost little by me. But I withheld the land from Peter, his son, and from Margaret, Peter's daughter. Yes, I always intended to return it, but I could not, I simply could not."

"Avarice, my son, avarice," Clement repeated gently. "Happy for you, it is not too late."

"No!" Ghysbrecht protested. "I will leave it to her by will. She will not have long to wait; perhaps a month or two at most."

"For a month's possession, thou wouldst damn thy soul forever, thou fool!"

Ghysbrecht groaned, pleading with the friar to be reasonable. Clement, however, remained firm yet gentle, persisting with infinite patience until he loosened the dying man's grip on property that did not belong to him. There were moments when Clement's patience was severely tested, standing on the verge of drawing forth the deed he carried specifically for this purpose. But having weathered yesterday's emotional outbreak, he was now on guard against his own anger. Furthermore, he had conquered a deep-seated personal revulsion toward the man, strong enough to make his own skin crawl throughout this struggle for the miser's soul. Finally, without uttering a single word regarding the deed, he succeeded in winning the man to make immediate and full restitution.

How exactly this restitution was effected will be recounted briefly elsewhere, along with certain peculiar effects produced upon Ghysbrecht by the act, and the terms under which Ghysbrecht and Clement eventually parted ways.

I promised to relate two acts of the latter, indicative of his mind. This is the first. The second requires only two words.

As soon as Clement was absolutely certain that Margaret had regained her inheritance and stood as a wealthy woman—

He vanished.

The silence that followed the tragedy at the Hoog Straet was heavier than the storm that had battered the house. For Catherine, usually a whirlwind of activity, the quiet felt like a tomb. With eyes swollen from weeping, she ventured to the convent the following afternoon, hoping to find Gerard and begin the arduous path toward reconciliation. Yet, the gatekeeper offered only a grim report: Gerard had departed the previous night under the cover of darkness and showed no sign of returning soon.

Defeated, Catherine returned home to find Margaret sitting in a stupor, clutching their young son Elias so tightly it seemed she feared losing him to the world itself. Life had lost its flavor for Margaret, who sat like a statue carved from grief. Persuading her weary companion to visit Gerard proved difficult, but eventually, Margaret yielded. "If you think I can do any good," she murmured, "I will come."

Upon reaching the carpenter's yard, they discovered a new name board being installed where Cornelis and Sybrandt's had stood. To their shock, the name read *Margaret Brandt*. Though Catherine harbored deep love for her stepmother, the sight cut her deeply. "The bane of one is another's meat," she whispered bitterly. Margaret, however, remained cold and defensive, asking if Catherine believed she squandered funds unjustly. "You are a good soul," Catherine admitted, noting how the truth often favored the strongest.

Later, Giles arrived, eager to champion their side. He listened to Catherine's tale of woe—how they were anathematized by their brother and cast adrift by their father—but his own prejudices ran counter to hers. He scoffed at the idea that the culprits had escaped punishment lightly, crowing about his influence at court and promising to use it to bring justice back to the Brandts. "I'll show him what it means to have a brother with a heart to serve a friend," Giles declared, his spirit buoyed by the prospect of revenge.

Just as the mood settled back into somber silence, a well-dressed stranger entered, asking quietly for Margaret Brandt. Catherine, her gossip instincts instantly ignited, announced loudly that Margaret was wanted. A pale, frail elderly man was ushered inside: Ghysbrecht Van Swieten. At the sight of him, Catherine shrieked and covered her face, while Margaret turned away in horror, trembling violently.

From the shadows, a weak voice emerged. "Good people, a dying man hath come to ask your forgiveness."

"It is to look upon your work," Catherine retorted, dropping her apron as fresh sobs racked her frame. "She is fainting!" Eli stepped forward, his voice shaking with a mix of anger and shame. "Burgomaster, 'tis not our wont to leave a visitor standing... but man, man, you have wrought us too much ill."

Amidst the tension, little Kate stepped forward. "Father, mother," she pleaded gently, "Death squares all accounts. See you not death in his face? I shall not live long, and his time is shorter still." Her wisdom softened the atmosphere. Eli quickly arranged a chair, and Ghysbrecht's attendants helped him sit. Two wooden boxes were brought forward, leaving the intruder alone in the midst of those he had cruelly injured.

With unsteady fingers, Ghysbrecht opened the lids. From the first, he produced the title deeds to property in Tergou. "These lands and houses belonged to Floris Brandt," he confessed, tears streaming down his cheeks. "They were usurped for a debt long since paid with interest. I restore them to their rightful owner, his granddaughter, with penitent tears." He then revealed a second box containing three hundred and forty golden angels—the rents and fines he had collected, which he had meticulously accounted for, intending justice despite his nature. "Avarice withheld me," he sobbed, "against temptation! I was not born dishonest, yet you see."

"Well, to be sure!" Catherine exclaimed, her greed momentarily overriding her sorrow. "And you the burgomaster! Whipped many a thief in your day. 'Tis better late than never. What, Margaret, art thou deaf? The good man hath brought thee back thine own. Thou art a rich woman!"

"But bid him keep the land and gold," Margaret interjected, her voice hollow, "and give me back my Gerard, whom he stole from me with treason."

"Alas!" Ghysbrecht cried, "Would I could! What I can I have done. It cost me a sore struggle; I rose from my last bed to do this myself, lest some mischance prevent her from receiving her rights."

"Old man," Margaret said, her gaze fixed on the floor, "since thou, whose idol is wealth, hast done this, may God and the saints forgive thee. As for me, I am neither saint nor angel, but a poor woman whose heart thou hast broken. Speak to him, Kate, for I am like the dead."

Kate, the family oracle, meditated silently before speaking. Her soft, silvery voice flowed like a soothing melody. "My poor sister hath a sorrow that riches cannot heal. Give her time, Ghysbrecht. 'Tis not in nature she should forgive thee all. Her boy is fatherless, and she is neither maid, wife, nor widow. The blow fell but two days since, laying her heart a-bleeding."

A single, heavy sob echoed from Margaret, acknowledging the truth. Kate continued, "Therefore, give her time! Ere thou diest, she will forgive thee all, yea, even to please me, who may not long survive thee. Meanwhile, we, whose wounds are sore but not so deep as hers, do pardon thee, a penitent and a dying man. I, for one, will pray for thee henceforth. Go in peace!"

Her words were sufficient. Even Eli, usually stern, invited Ghysbrecht to share bread and wine to strengthen him for his journey. But Ghysbrecht declined. "What I have done is a cordial to me," he explained, his eyes clear with relief. "This land I clung to so dearly was a bed of nettles all along. 'Tis gone, and I feel happier and livelier for the loss."

As Ghysbrecht was carried away on a litter, Catherine gazed upon the pile of gold with undisguised joy. She had never seen so much wealth at once and chided Margaret for sitting like an image, refusing to acknowledge the treasure. Despite Kate's gentle plea that Margaret's heart was like lead, Catherine persisted until Margaret finally approached the money.

Margaret stared down at the wealth with wide, wondering eyes. Suddenly, she wrung her hands, her composure shattering into piercing anguish. "Too late! Too late!" she cried, collapsing into hysterics. The gold and land, though rightfully hers, arrived far too late to alter the course of her life. If only a fraction of this fortune, or the land itself, had been hers a year or two prior, Gerard might never have abandoned her side to seek adventure in Italy. Instead, the riches stood as a monument to a love lost forever, echoing the tragic refrain: "Too late! Too late!"

News spread swiftly that Margaret Van Eyck had passed, and within days, her funeral rites were concluded. By a will she had drawn up a year prior, she bequeathed her entire estate to her beloved daughter, Margaret Brandt, following the costs of interment and specific gifts to Reicht Heynes. She requested that Reicht remain under Margaret Brandt's roof until he wed, a testament to the bond they shared. Through this inheritance, Margaret acquired a fully furnished dwelling, alongside a collection of sketches and paintings that, in modern times, would constitute a vast fortune. Among these treasures hung a portrait she cherished above all others—a depiction titled "A Betrothal." The canvas captured a solemnity etched into the groom's grave visage and a demure, composed grace in the bride. Though largely executed by Margaret Van Eyck herself, the remainder was the hand of Jan. The details surrounding the couple were rendered with exquisite mastery, remaining a marvel of artistic skill to the present day. Before her passing, Margaret sent word to Reicht to remain in the house until she could summon the courage to re-enter it, asking him to guard the painting carefully, noting it was hidden in a small cupboard—referring to the diptych.

Shortly thereafter, Luke Peterson returned home and learned that Gerard was now a monk. Joy surged within him, threatening to overwhelm his senses. Rushing to Margaret, he declared, "Take heed, mistress. If he cannot marry you, I can."

"You?" Margaret asked, surprised. "I have seen him."

"But he is a friar!" Luke insisted.

"He was my husband and the father of my boy long before he took vows," Margaret replied firmly. "I have seen him, I have seen him."

Luke grew thoroughly puzzled. "Tell you what," he suggested, "I have a cousin who is a lawyer. I will go and ask him whether you are married or single."

"Nay," Margaret chided, "I shall ask my own heart, not a lawyer. Such inquiries would turn me into town gossip, oh, fie!"

"That rumor is already spreading without a word from me."

"But not by anyone seeking my respect. If you pursue this, never approach me again."

"Ay," Luke sighed, "you are like a dove to everyone else, but a hard-hearted tyrant to me."

"'Tis your own fault, dear Luke, for courting me. That prevents me from being kinder to you than I wish, Luke, my bonny lad, listen. I am wealthy now; I can make friends happy, though not myself. Look around the street, look around the parish. There are many fairer wenches than I, twice told, and not ruined by weeping. Look higher; choose wisely. Speak to the lass yourself, and I will speak to her mother; they shall not refuse you. Take my word for it."

"I understand what you mean," Luke said, turning a deep shade of crimson. "But if I cannot have your affection, I want none of your money. I served you when you were poor as I, and poorer. If you prefer to be a friar's mistress rather than an honest man's wife, you are not the woman I took you for. Let us part without malice; seek your comfort on that road where no woman has ever found it, and for me, I will live and die a bachelor. Good evening, mistress."

"Farewell, dear Luke; and may God forgive you for speaking thus to me."

For several days, Margaret awaited the inevitable meeting with Gerard with a mixture of dread and longing. She reasoned that perhaps he would keep away briefly, as she might, but eventually, the truth of his paternity would surface, and his desire to see their son would compel him. "If," she thought, "this meeting does not kill me, I shall feel better afterward than I do now."

However, as day succeeded day without his appearance, a chilling suspicion began to gnaw at her mind. Was his absence intentional? Had he fled to some ruthless monastic order where they forbade contact with former lovers? The convent, she recalled, had proven as merciless to true love as the grave itself. At the thought, her vitality seemed to drain away.

Deep indignation began to mingle with her grief. "Could he have ever truly loved me?" she wondered, anger rising in her chest. "To flee from me and his child without a single word! Why does this poor Luke think more of me than he does?"

Suddenly, Giles erupted in a roar. "I have hit the mark! Our Gerard is the Vicar of Gouda."

A brief sketch of Giles's transformation at court prepares the reader for his account. Months prior, his intelligence had begun to bud. He dated this awakening to a specific eighth of June, when, swinging by one hand along with a week's washing on a tightrope in the drying yard, something cracked inside his head. Lo! Intellectual powers were unchained. At court, his shrewdness, blunt speech, gigantic voice, and diminutive stature made him a power; without his small size, he feared he would have been condemned to the gallows. Both the Young Duchess of Burgundy and Marie, the heiress apparent, doted on him as great ladies have always favored dwarfs. The court poet melted butter by the six-foot rule, pouring enough down Giles's back to stew Goliath. Even Giles's rough dictations were amplified, versified, and softened by the poet.

The resulting prolixity reached Eli via letter, titled: "The high and puissant Princess Marie of Bourgogne her lytel jantilman hys complaynt of y' Coort, and praise of a rusticall lyfe, versificated, and empapyred by me the lytel jantilman's right lovynge and obsequious servitor, etc."

Yet Giles reached his apex through a fortunate mixture of mind and muscle. The day before a grand joust, he challenged the Duke's giant to a contest of strength. The challenge provoked a grim grin and raised expectations. Giles erected a lofty pole, ascended it like a squirrel at the appointed hour, used his immense arm strength to form a right angle with his body, and remained suspended there. Then, sliding down with terrifying speed, he caused the high and puissant princess to squeak and bury her face in her hands, terrified to witness the demise of her pocket-Hercules. The giant managed only about ten feet, then looked ruefully upward and downward, descending bathed in sweat to justify his failure.

"It was not the dwarf's greater strength, but his smaller body," the giant offered as an excuse.

The spectators received this justification with loud derision. The fact stood clear: the dwarf was mighty at climbing poles; the giant was merely mighty at excuses. In short, Giles had gauged their intellects—with his own body, no doubt.

"Come," Giles said, "if you go that route, I will wrestle you, my lad, if you will allow me to blindfold your eyes."

Smarting from defeat, the giant, believing he could recover honor through this means, readily agreed.

"Madam," Giles addressed the court, "see yonder blind Samson? At a signal from me, he shall make me a low obeisance and unbend his hat."

"How may that be, being blinded?" a maid of honor inquired.

"I'll wager on Giles for one," the princess declared.

"That is my affair."

As wagers were placed on both sides, Giles struck the giant squarely in the bread-basket. The giant doubled over—the obeisance—and his bonnet fell off. The company yelled with delight at this delicate stroke of wit, and Giles fled on his heels. The giant pursued as soon as he could regain his breath and remove his bandages, but it was too late. Giles had prepared a small door in the wall, through which he could pass, but not a giant, and had colored it so artfully it appeared to be solid masonry. He tore it open and plunged through, leaving no trace save a poem written boldly upon the reverse of his trick door:

Long limbs, big body, panting wit
By wee and wise is bet and bit.

After this feat, Giles became a force.

He now spoke for himself. Finding Margaret unable to believe the news, skeptical of the Holy Church being administered by dwarfs, he narrated as follows:

"When the princess summoned me to her chamber, as per custom, to keep her from lethargy, I arrived not merry nor filled with country ditties, as is my wont, but dull as lead.

"Why, what aileth thee?" she asked. "Art sick?" "At heart," I replied. "Alas, he is in love," she remarked. Whereupon five brazen hussies, whom they call maids of honor, laughed loudly. "Not so mad as that," I said, "seeing what I see at court regarding women folk."

"There, ladies," the princess said, "let him be. 'Tis a liberal mannikin, and still giveth more than he taketh of saucy words.'

"In all seriousness," she asked, "what is the matter?"

I told her I was meditating, and what perplexed me was that common folk sometimes kept their word, but princes never did.

"Hayday," she exclaimed, "thy shafts fly high this morn." I told her, "Ay, for they hit the Truth."

She said I was as keen as keen; but it became not me to put riddles to her, nor her to answer them. "Stand aloof a bit, mesdames,"

Indeed, the hermit of Gouda was the vicar of Gouda, yet he remained oblivious to this duality, bound by the absolute walls of his self-imposed exile. My readers know that when the fever of his passion finally broke, it was replaced immediately by a crushing remorse. Yet, few who have not climbed the highest peaks of spiritual aspiration and tumbled down its steep slopes can truly comprehend the terror that seized him. He felt as though a treacherous guide had led him right to the brink of a cliff, where the ground vanished beneath his feet.

"Ah, good Jerome!" he cried, tears streaming down his face. "How much better you knew me than I knew myself! How bitter, yet how necessary, was your warning!"

Accustomed to the rigorous audit of his own soul, Clement instantly identified the root of his fury: it was Margaret. "I love her," he whispered, the confession tearing from his lips in despair, "more than God; more than the Church. From a love like this, what good can spring for me, or for her?" He shuddered violently at the thought. "Let the strong man engage temptation in combat; it is for the weak to flee. And who, I ask you, is weaker than I have proven myself to be? What remains of my penance, my religion? It is a house of cards, carefully built degree by degree into a facade of piety, and behold—here comes a breath of earthly love, and the whole structure crumbles into dust."

He resolved to abandon Holland immediately, to spend years in some distant convent before ever returning. By that time, the allure of worldly passion would be doubly baffled; grown older, a wiser monk, he would master his affections. And Margaret, seeing him gone, would surely marry another. The sheer agony this final thought inflicted upon him proved, paradoxically, that he was indeed walking the narrow path of religious duty.

Yet, in forsaking her for his eternal salvation—and ostensibly for hers—he could not neglect her temporal welfare. The comforting thought that he could secure her comfort for life, enriching her with worldly goods which she was not bound to despise, sustained him through the bitter struggle of turning his back on her without a single kind word or look. "Oh, what will she think of me?" he groaned, pacing his cell. "Shall I not appear to her, of all creatures, the most heartless, inhuman? But so it must be; yes, better she should hate me, miserable wretch that I am. Heaven is merciful, giving my broken heart this comfort: I can make that villain restore her dowry, and she shall never lose another true lover to poverty. Another? Ah me! Ah me! God and the Saints, lend me your aid!"

How he fared on this errand has been chronicled elsewhere. First, however, as you may recall, he went at night to confess the dying hermit of Gouda. He found him on the verge of death and stayed until he had closed his eyes and interred him beneath the floor of the tiny oratory attached to his cell. It was the peaceful conclusion of a stormy life. The hermit had been a soldier, carrying a steel corselet next to his skin even in death, declaring himself now Christ's soldier, as he once had been Satan's. When Clement had heard his confession and prayed with him, the dying man, seeking counsel from one nearing the end in such a pious frame, advised Clement to become his successor in this peaceful retreat. "His fight against the world, the flesh, and the devil was hard," the hermit murmured, "and he never thoroughly mastered them until he retired into the citadel of Solitude."

These words, coupled with the hermit's serene passing, which seemed to seal the truth of his life's work, made a profound impression on Clement. In Clement's case, there was no prejudice to overcome; the solitary recluse was still profoundly revered in the Church, whether immured as an anchorite in a cave belonging to a monastery or hiding in the wilder, more lax seclusion of an independent hermitage. Clement knew more about hermits than most theologians of his time. Having studied extensively at the monastery near Tergou, devouring their lives with wonder in the Vatican manuscripts, and engaging earnestly with mendicant friars of various nations, he possessed a deep reservoir of knowledge. Before the printing press, these friars were the great circulators of local annals and biographies accumulated in convents across lands.

Moreover, his mentor Jerome had spent three years as an anchorite on the heights of Camaldoli, where the tradition of the Thebaid had been revived for over four centuries. Jerome, usually cold and curt on most religious topics, burned with enthusiasm regarding hermits. He had pored over the annals of St. John Baptist's Abbey, recounting the names of nobles and warriors who ended their days there, bishops and archbishops who moved from the see to the hermitage and back again. He spoke with grim delight of their multifarious austerities, describing how each hermit attacked his own weaknesses without mercy until he became strongest even there. He detailed how seven times daily, regardless of thunder, rain, or snow, solitaries flocked from distant points over rugged paths to worship in the convent church, attending matins, prime, tierce, sext, nones, vespers, and compline. Under eager questioning, Clement learned of the famous anchorites he had sung the Psalter with: Moncada, Duke of Moncada and Cardova; Hidalgo of Spain; Father John Baptist of Novara, who had led armies but was now a private soldier of Christ; Cornelius, Samuel, and Sylvanus. Of Sylvanus, Clement recalled how the Great Duchess de' Medici, despite obtaining the Pope's reluctant leave to visit, was warned by the hermit at the first wooden cross. Surrounded by courtiers and flatterers, she retreated with all her retinue, awed by the holiness of the man and the mountain.

At Basel, Clement found fresh materials, particularly concerning German and English anchorites, eventually preparing a "Catena Eremitarum" spanning from the year 250, when Paul of Thebes began his ninety years of solitude, down to 1470. He called them *Angelorum amici et animalium*—"Friends of Angels and Animals."

Though Clement never intended to become a recluse, the path was paved. When the dying hermit of Gouda blessed the citadel of Solitude, where he had fought the good fight, and invited Clement to take up the breastplate of faith now falling from his own shrunken body, Clement thought within himself: "Heaven itself led my foot hither to this end." It struck him as no small coincidence that his patron, St. Bavon, was also a hermit, an austere one, a cuirassier of the solitary cell.

Eager to reconcile with Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, Clement hastened to his abode, praying that Heaven would not have already occupied the famous den. His fears were not unfounded; these sacred spaces rarely waited long for a human tenant. He found the rude stone door ajar, entered softly, and confirmed that he was not too late. The cell was vacant, holding the hermit's great ivory crucifix, pens, ink, seeds, and a *memento mori* skull. There lay his cilices of hair and bristles, his well-worn sheepskin pelisse and hood, and his tools: hammer, chisel, and psaltery. Men and women had passed that way, yet none had dared intrude, far less steal. Faith and simplicity had guarded this keyless door more securely than the houses of the laity, defended by gates like modern jails, iron bars, moats, bastions, and portcullises.

As soon as Clement stepped inside, a loud flap and flutter echoed, and down came a great brown owl from a corner, wheeling out the window and driving the cold air onto Clement's face. He started and shuddered. Was this seemingly ordinary owl something diabolical, attempting to deter him from his soul's good? On second thought, might it not be some good spirit the hermit had employed to keep the cell for him, perhaps the hermit himself? Finally, he concluded it was merely an owl, deciding to try and make friends with it.

Kneeling down, Clement inaugurated his new life with prayer.

Beyond the earthly passion he needed to quell—a power that made him tremble for his eternal welfare—Clement bore a penance for having yielded to ire, his besetting sin, and for cursing his own brothers. He looked around the roomy cell, furnished with such comforts, comparing it to the hideous places in his mind's eye: the *eremus in eremo*, a desert in a desert, where St. Jerome had wrestled with sickness, temptation, and despair for four mortal years; the inaccessible, thorny niche where the boy hermit Benedict hid away, surviving three years on a pittance shared with a raven; the hollow tree of his patron St. Bavon; the earthly purgatory at Fribourg, where a nameless saint endured perpetual gloom and the sound of an eternal waterfall; the pillar of St. Simeon Stylites, existing for forty-five years; the stone box (*destina*) of St. Dunstan, where he could scarce sit, stand, or lie; and the living tombs sealed with lead of Thais, Christina, and others. These dismal dens, where true hermits wore out

For days, Clement lay in his cavern of despair, a prayerless, tuneless, hopeless wreck, until a singular thought pierced the fog of his misery. *What I have given freely to the poor and the weary traveler,* he reasoned, *why should I not give it to myself?* He resolved to fill his den of ruin with the Name of God, with the sacred words of Scripture, and with the comforting piety of the Church.

Like the pilgrim Christian standing before the gates of Hell, he thrust out his hand—not for a sword, which he lacked—but for the humble tool of peaceful labor, his chisel. With a vigor born of necessity, he worked as if the salvation of his soul hung by a thread from his arm.

History tells of Michelangelo, who, unlike timid sculptors who model in clay and delegate the carving to mechanics, would seize the block himself. Conceiving the image instantly, he would strike with hammer and steel, sending marble chips flying wildly, causing the rough stone to sprout into form. Thus did Clement proceed. He drew no guiding lines; instead, he mined his memory for gracious words and dashed them into the soft stone with frantic energy, chipping away at his work between the labors of living and the battles of faith.

He begged visitors for candle stubs and rancid oil. "Anything will serve," he declared, "so long as it burns." Consequently, at night, his cave glowed like a blacksmith's forge, visible through the window and the cracks of his crude stone door. The local villagers, beholding the eerie light, crossed themselves, suspecting devilries. Yet, within, the holy talismans—verses and prayers—appeared one by one upon the walls, while sparks and stone chips flew ceaselessly, day and night. This was the soldier of Solitude and the Church, wielding his bloodless weapon, sighing and groaning, as he carved between the grind of labor and the fight for grace.

Beneath the great crucifix fixed to the wall, he inscribed a passage from Augustine: *"O Christian soul, behold the wounds of the suffering One, the blood of the dying One, the price of our redemption! Consider how immense these things are, and weigh them in the balance of your mind, so that He who was nailed entirely to the Cross for you may become entirely nailed to your heart. For if you recall the Passion of Christ, there is nothing on earth too hard to bear with composure."*

Soothed, albeit slightly, by these sweet and pious words surrounding him, and crushed by the endless watch and toil, Clement sank prostrate one morning, overcome by profound fatigue. A deep sleep seized him for many hours. When he awoke quietly, a tiny cheeping sound caught his ear. Opening his eyes, he saw a robin redbreast perched upon his breviary, resting on a low stool near his feet. The bird ruffled its feathers with a single pull, smoothed them instantly, and cocked its bill with a display of cunning that was purely imaginary.

Clement held his breath. He half-closed his eyes, terrified lest he frighten this airy guest. Down the bird hopped onto the floor. There, it enacted a pantomime of astuteness; then, with three stiff little hops, like a ball of worsted on vertical wires, it landed squarely on the hermit's bare foot. Swelling and contracting with the ebb and flow of its plumage, the robin remained there. Clement closed his eyes completely, scarcely drawing breath, fearing to lose his visitor. Content merely to feel the minute claw upon his foot—he could barely feel it, relying on knowledge rather than sensation—the game continued.

Soon, the fluttering wings signaled the bird's return to the breviary. Determined not to frighten this fidget away, Clement attempted to feed it. He held a piece of bread within reach, but moving his hand proved impossible. After five minutes of creeping motionless toward the loaf, he managed to slip a crumb between his finger and thumb. Like children shooting marbles, he flicked the crumb forward, keeping his hand utterly still.

"Cock-robin" observed the falling morsel, displayed sagacity, and did not move. When another crumb followed, then another, the bird finally popped down, snatched one, and fled to a higher eminence—the hermit's knee—for greater security. The game persisted until a unusually large fragment rolled along. Cock-robin pounced, lifted the prize aloft, and fled swiftly into the world, the cave resonating with the buffeting air.

"Now, bless thee, sweet bird," sighed the solitary sufferer. "Thy wings are music, and thou, a feathered ray, hast come to lighten my darkened soul."

From that moment, his devotions gained a spark of courage, and this became his daily work:

*"Come, Creator Spirit, visit the minds of men, fill with heavenly grace those hearts Thou hast created. Kindle the light in our senses, visit the minds of Thy people, strengthen the weakness of our bodies with perpetual virtue."*

Thus, the days rolled on. The weather grew colder, yet Clement's heart grew warmer, and despondency, having arrived like a cloud, departed like one. Soon, everything reversed. His cell seemed bathed in joy. His work brought pleasure; his prayers flowed with unction; his psalms became hymns of praise. Flocks of small birds followed their crimson leader, fleeing the snow, making friendship with the "parish full of Cains"—transforming enemies into Abels.

On one keen, frosty night, as he sang praises to God upon his tuneful psaltery, causing his hollow cave to ring with holy psalmody—as if the cave itself were Tubal's surrounding shell or David's harp—he heard a clear, melodic whine. It grew louder, though slightly untuned. Peering through the cracks of his rude door, he saw a great red wolf moaning melodiously, its nose held high in the air.

Clement was overjoyed. "My sins depart!" he cried. "The creatures of God acknowledge me, one after another!" In a burst of enthusiasm, he struck up the Laud: "Praise Him, all ye creatures of His! Let everything that hath breath praise the Lord!" All the while, the wolf bayed at intervals.

Above all, he felt drawn nearer to that celestial intercourse, the sign and bliss of the true hermit. His dreams of saints and angels became so vivid they resembled actual visions. He saw bright figures clad in woven snow, bending upon him eyes lovelier than those of the antelope he had seen in Rome, fanning him with broad wings hued like rainbows, their gentle voices bidding him speed on his course.

However, not long after enjoying this felicity, his dreams took on a strange and ominous complexion. He wandered with Fra Colonna across the relics of ancient nations. The friar was lame, leaning on a staff which he waved over mighty ruins. Wherever the staff pointed—whether Egyptian, Greek, or Roman—the temples and palaces, previously mere wrecks, rose again like an exhalation, thronged with the famous dead. Songsters capable of eclipsing Apollo poured forth their lays; women, godlike in form and draped like Minerva, danced voluptuously yet gracefully around marble courts. Sculptors carved amidst admiring pupils, and forms of supernatural beauty emerged from Parian marble in moments; grave philosophers discoursed on high and subtle matters while youths listened reverently. It was a realm of eternity, long past.

Yet, beneath this wonderful panorama, a lurking suspicion gnawed at the dreamer. "This is a prologue, a flourish," he thought. "There is something behind; something that means me no good, something mysterious, awful."

One night, when the wizard Colonna had transcended himself, he pointed with his staff. Many great ancient cities were swallowed up, and the pair stood on a vast sandy plain under a huge crimson sun sinking to rest. Great palm trees and bulrush hives, scarce a man's height, dotted the horizon.

"These are the anchorites of the Theban desert," Colonna said calmly. "Followers not of Christ and His apostles, nor of the great Fathers, but of the Greek pupils of the Egyptian pupils of the Brachmans and Gymnosophists."

Clement felt a burning desire to embrace these holy men, to tell them his troubles, and seek their counsel. But he was tied by invisible fetters, unable to move. The crimson sun sank, dusk fell, and the hives became scarce visible. Colonna's figure grew shadowy and shapeless, yet his eyes glowed ten times brighter. Something in all eyes spoke and said: *"Nay, let them be. A pack of fools. I see how dismal it all is."* Then, with sudden sprightliness, *"But I hear one of them possesses a manuscript of Petronius on papyrus! I shall go to buy it

Her presence was meant to stir compassion, not dread, in eyes unclouded by prejudice. Her chest heaved like a trapped bird, her cheeks flushed with alternating waves of crimson and pallor, and her hand clutched the stone wall—the instinctive grip of a frightened creature. She trembled violently, her gaze a bewildered mixture of adoration, reverent awe, pity, and tender remembrance fixed upon the gaunt, glaring figure of the hermit. Such a scene was, in itself, a marvel of nature.

"Aha!" he cried, his voice jagged. "At last thou comest in flesh and blood; come to me as thou camest to Saint Anthony. Yet I am wary. I suspected thy wiles were not yet spent. I am armed." Snatching up his small crucifix, he thrust it outward at her, his expression one of stunned disbelief, while holding a flickering candle in his other hand. Both objects shook with the violence of his agitation. "Exorcizo te."

"Ah, no!" she cried, her voice a pitiful plea, raising two delicate, supplicating palms. "Alas, do me no harm! It is Margaret."

"Liar!" the hermit shouted, his face contorted. "Margaret was beautiful, yes, but not with a supernatural radiance like thine. Thou didst shrink at that sacred name, thou subtle hypocrite!" He raised the candle higher, invoking the Divine Name with fervor. "In Nomine Dei exorcizo vos."

"Oh, Jesus!" Margaret gasped, the terror absolute in her extremity. "Curse me not! I will return home. I thought I might have visited. By manhood, Latinize me not! Oh, Gerard—is this how we meet after all? After all?"

She cowered almost to her knees, weeping with a superstitious fear mingled with wounded affection.

Though steeped in a deep-seated hatred of Satan, Clement might have remained doubtful whether this distressed creature, so wholly human and natural, was not merely artifice and a fiend. But her spontaneous invocation of that holy name dissolved his illusion. Let him see with his eyes, let him hear with his ears.

A cry of self-reproach escaped his lips. He attempted to lift her, but fasts and the overwhelming shock of his prolonged solitude being broken rendered him unable. "What," he gasped, shaking over her, "is it thou? And have I met thee with harsh words? Alas!" They were both choked by emotion, speechless for a moment.

"I heed it not," Margaret said bravely, fighting back tears. "Thou took me for another—for a devil! Oh! Oh! Oh!"

"Forgive me, sweet soul!" As soon as he could form more than a fragment of a sentence, he continued, "I have been plagued by the Evil One since I arrived here."

Margaret glanced around with a shudder. "Likely enough. Then, take my hand, and let me lead thee from this filthy place."

He stared at her, astounded.

"What? Desert my cell and return to the world? Is that why thou hast come to me?" he asked, his tone heavy with sadness and reproach.

"Yes, Gerard. I am come to take thee to thy pretty vicarage. Thanks to Heaven and thy good brother Giles, thou art Vicar of Gouda. Mother and I have made it so neat for thee, Gerard. 'Tis quite comfortable in winter, I promise thee. But wait until the hawthorn blooms, and anon thy walls shall don their robe of brave roses and sweet woodbine. Hast we forgotten thee, and the foolish things thou lovest? And, dear Gerard, thy mother awaits; 'tis late for her to leave her bed: pray, pray, come! And the moment we escape this foul hole, I will show thee a treasure thou hast acquired, of which thou knowest naught—or surely thou wouldst never have fled from us so. Alas, what have I done? What have I ignorantly said to warrant this treatment?"

For he had drawn himself up into a rigid heap, regarding her with a gaze that blended fear and suspicion.

"Unhappy girl," he said solemnly, yet deeply agitated, "wouldst thou have me risk my soul and thine for a miserable vicarage and the flowers growing upon it? But this is not thy doing: the bowels-less fiend sends thee, poor simple girl, to me with this lure. But oh, cunning fiend, I will unmask thee, even unto this thine instrument, and she shall see thee and abhor thee as I do. Margaret, my lost love, why am I here? Because I love thee."

"Oh! No, Gerard, thou lovest me not, or thou wouldst not have hidden from me; there was no need."

"Let there be no deceit between us twain, who have loved so truly; and after this night, let us meet no more on earth."

"Now God forbid!" she exclaimed.

"I love thee, and thou hast not forgotten me, or thou wouldst have married ere this, and wouldst not be the one to find me, buried here from sight of man. I am a priest, a monk: what but folly or sin can ensue from thee and me living neighbors, feeding a passion innocent once, but now (so Heaven wills it) impious and unholy? No, though my heart breaks, I must be firm. 'Tis I that am the man, 'tis I that am the priest. We must meet no more, till I am schooled by solitude, and thou art wedded to another."

"I consent to my doom, but not to thine. I would ten times liefer die; yet I will marry, ay, wed misery itself, sooner than let thee lie in this foul, dismal place, with yon sweet manse awaiting thee." Clement groaned; with every word she spoke, two things stood clearer and clearer—his duty, and the agony it must cost.

"My beloved," he said, with a strange mixture of tenderness and dogged resolution, "I bless thee for granting me one more sight of thy sweet face. May God forgive thee, and bless thee, for destroying in a minute the holy peace it hath taken six months of solitude to build. No matter. A year of penance will, Dei gratia, restore me to my calm. My poor Margaret, I seem cruel: yet I am kind. 'Tis best we part; ay, this moment."

"Part, Gerard? Never! We have seen what comes of parting. Part? Why, thou hast not heard half my story; no, nor the tithe. 'Tis not for thy mere comfort I take thee to the Gouda manse. Hear me!"

"I may not. Thy very voice is a temptation with its music, memory's delight."

"But I say thou shalt hear me, Gerard, for henceforth I go not unheard."

"Then must we part by other means," said Clement sadly.

"Alack! what other means? Wouldest thou put me to thine own door, being the stronger?"

"Nay, Margaret, well thou knowest I would suffer many deaths rather than use force upon thee; thy sweet body is dearer to me than my own; but a million times dearer to me are our immortal souls, both thine and mine. I have withstood this direst temptation long enough. Now I must flee it: farewell! Farewell!"

He moved toward the door, having actually opened it and stepped halfway out, when she darted after him, catching hold of his arm.

"Nay, then another must speak for me. I thought to reward thee for yielding to me; but unkind that thou art, I find I need help; turn then this way, one moment."

"Nay, nay."

"But I say ay! And then turn thy back on us, if thou canst." She relaxed her grasp slightly, thinking he would never deny her so small a favor. But at this, he saw his opportunity and seized it.

"Flee, Clement, flee!" he almost shrieked. Religious enthusiasm momentarily restoring his old strength, he burst wildly away from her. After a few steps, he bounded over the little stream and ran alongside it, but finding he was not pursued, he stopped and looked back.

She lay upon her face, her hands spread out.

Yes, without meaning it, he had thrown her down and injured her.

When he saw this, he groaned and turned back a step; but suddenly, by another impulse, he flung himself into the icy water.

"There!" he cried, standing there up to his throat in liquid ice, "kill my body, but save my soul!"

While he stood thus, Margaret uttered one long, piteous moan and rose to her knees.

He saw her plainly, almost as clearly as in midday sun. He beheld her pale face and eyes glistening, and then, in the still night, heard these words:

"Oh, God! Thou that knowest all, Thou seest how I am used. Forgive me then! For I will not live another day." With this, she suddenly started to her feet and fled like some wild creature, wounded to death, close by his miserable hiding-place, shrieking:

What manifold anguish may burst from a human heart in a single syllable. There were wounded love, wounded pride, despair, and coming madness all in that piteous cry. Clement

The hermit, startled from his reverie, gazed first at his nursling, then at Margaret, and back again, his eyes wide with a tumultuous agitation. In his left arm lay the child, sound asleep, while Margaret knelt beside him at his right knee. Gone was the pale, panting, terrified girl he had so effortlessly overpowered hours prior; standing before him now was an imperial beauty, her cheeks flushed, her eyes sparkling with a triumphant radiance, her lips parted in a sweet, silent joy. Her entire countenance glowed with a look he had never witnessed before—a profound, radiant maternal pride.

He stared, transfixed, his gaze oscillating between the slumbering infant and the woman who held him, his heart pounding with a wonder that bordered on disbelief.

"Us?" he gasped finally, the word barely escaping his lips as his eyes darted back and forth.

Margaret, equally surprised, struggled for words in that moment where impression outweighed fact. "What!" she cried, her voice rising. "Does not a father know his own child? And a man of God, too? Fie, Gerard, to pretend! Nay, thou art too wise, too virtuous not to know—look, I watched thee closely, and now behold you both! 'Tis thine own flesh and blood thou holdest to thy heart."

Clement trembled violently. "What words are these?" he stammered, clutching at air. "This angel of mine?"

"Whose else?" Margaret pressed gently. "Since he is mine."

Turning his gaze upon the sleeping child, Clement beheld a vision beyond the power of pen to describe. As his eyes seemed to drink in the little life, he trembled from head to foot, as if absorbing the essence of pure love itself.

Margaret's eyes followed his. "He bears not a single trace of me," she declared proudly, though her tone softened. "Yet, oh, at intervals, he is thy very image, shrunk into miniature form. See this golden hair? Thine was exactly this hue when thou wast his age; ask any mother else. And observe this mole upon his little finger. Now look at thine own! There! 'Twas thy mother who let me know thou wast marked so before him. And oh, Gerard, 'twas this very child who found thee for me. By that tiny mark upon thy finger, I knew thee for his father when I watched from above thy window and saw thee feeding the birds."

Seizing the child's hand, she kissed it with eager fervor, pressing half of it into her mouth, Heaven knew how, before whispering, "Ah! Bless thee, thou didst find thy poor daddy for her. And now thou hast made us friends again after our little quarrel—the first, and the last. Thou wast very cruel to me but now, my poor Gerard, yet I forgive thee; for I love thy child."

"Ay! Ay! Ay! Ay! Ay!" sobbed Clement, choking back tears. Overcome by prolonged fasts and unnerved by solitude, the once-strong man fell into hysteria, teetering on the brink of fainting.

Margaret, alarmed yet seasoned by experience, let her pity outweigh her fear. "Nay, take not on so," she murmured soothingly, placing a gentle hand upon his brow. "Be brave! So, so. Dear heart, thou art not the first man to journey afar and return richer by a lovely little self than when he departed. Being a man of God, take courage. Say that He sends thee this to comfort thee for what thou hast lost in me—and that is not so very much, my lamb. For surely the better part of love shall ne'er cool here to thee; though it may in thine, and ought, being a priest, and parson of Gouda."

"I? Priest of Gouda? Never!" murmured Clement in a faint voice. "I am a friar of St. Dominic: yet speak on, sweet music. Tell me all that hath happened to thee before we are parted again."

Now, some might have exclaimed against parting at all, raising the true question in dispute. But women like Margaret do not repeat their mistakes. It is exceedingly difficult to defeat them twice where their hearts are truly set.

She assented, turning her back on the Gouda manse as if it were a forgotten dream, and began her tale. She dwelt above all upon the kindness shown to her by his parents. As she recounted her troubles, his hand stole to hers, and often she felt him wince and tremble with righteous ire, pressing her hand in sympathy with every vein of her sorrow.

"Oh, piteous tale of a true heart battling alone against such bitter odds," he said.

"It all seems small, when I see thee here again, nursing my boy," she replied. "We have had a warning, Gerard. True friends like you and me are rare, and they are mad to part ere death divideth them."

"And that is true," said Clement, momentarily off his guard.

Then she urged him to speak of his sufferings for her sake. He begged her to excuse him, and she consented, yet by questions quietly revoked, she elicited everything. Many a sigh she heaved for him, hiding her face in her hands with terror at the perils he had faced, though now past. To console him, she knelt, sliding her arms beneath the little boy, lifting him gently. "Kiss him softly," she whispered. "Again, again, kiss thy fill if thou canst; he is sound. 'Tis all I can do to comfort thee till thou art out of this foul den and in thy sweet manse yonder."

Clement shook his head.

"Well," she said, "let that pass. Know that I have been sore affronted for want of my lines."

"Who hath dared affront thee?"

"No matter; those that will do it again if thou hast lost them, which the Saints forbid."

"I lose them? Nay, there they lie, close to thy hand."

Clement hung his head. "Look in the Vulgate. Heaven forgive me: I thought thou wert dead, a saint in heaven."

She looked, and on the blank leaves of the poor soul's Bible, she found her marriage lines.

"Thank God!" she cried, "Thank God! Oh, bless thee, Gerard, bless thee! Why, what is here, Gerard?"

On the other leaves were pinned every scrap of paper she had ever sent him, their two names written together in sport, the lock of her hair she had given him, half a silver coin she had broken with him, and a straw she had sucked from her soup the first day he ever saw her.

When Margaret saw these proofs of love and signs of a gentle heart bereaved, even her exultation at recovering her lines was overwhelmed by gushing tenderness. She almost staggered, her hand going to her bosom, leaning her brow against the stone cell wall. She wept so silently he did not see, for she felt that to befriend him now she must be the stronger, lest emotion weaken her resolve.

"Gerard," she said, "I know thou art wise and good. Thou must have a reason for what thou art doing, let it seem ever so unreasonable. Let us talk like old friends. Why art thou buried alive?"

"To escape temptation," Gerard answered. "My impious ire against those two had its root in the heart; that heart I must deaden, and, Dei gratia, I shall. Shall I, a servant of Christ and of the Church, court temptation? Shall I pray daily to be led out onto it, and walk into it with open eyes?"

"That is good sense, anyway," Margaret said, with a consummate affectation of candor.

"'Tis unanswerable," Clement sighed.

"We shall see," she countered gently. "Tell me, hast thou escaped temptation here? Why I ask is, when I am alone, my thoughts are far more wild and foolish than in company. Nay, speak sooth!"

"I must needs own I have been worse tempted here with evil imaginations than in the world."

"There now."

"Ay, but so were Anthony and Jerome, Macarius and Hilarion, Benedict, Bernard, and all the saints. 'Twill wear off."

"How dost thou know?"

"I feel sure it will."

"Guessing against knowledge. Here 'tis men-folk are sillier than us, who are but women. Wise in their own conceits, they will not let themselves see; their stomachs are too high to be taught by their eyes. A woman, if she went into a hole in a bank to escape temptation, and there found it, would just lift her farthingale and be out on't, and not even know how wise she was, till she watched a man in like plight."

"Nay, I grant humility and a teachable spirit are the roads to wisdom; but when all is said, here I wrestle but with imagination. At Gouda, she I love as no priest or monk must love any but the angels, she will tempt a weak soul, unwilling, yet not loth to be tempted."

"Ay, that is another matter; I should tempt thee then? To what, i' God's name?"

"Who knows? The flesh is weak."

"Speak for yourself, my lad. Why, thou art thinking of some other Margaret, not Margaret a Peter. Was ever my mind turned to folly and frailty? Stay, is it because thou wast my husband

Rotterdam faded from view long before Gerard stirred from his slumber. Margaret, driven by a resolve that felt both desperate and final, had already abandoned her boy, leaving him in the care of Reicht, a man of sturdy loyalty. She dispatched him immediately to Gouda, entrusting the faithful steward with a vicar's grey frock, a broad felt hat, and a set of precise instructions regarding the governance of his new charge.

Once Reicht was gone, Margaret sought out Jorian Ketel. Guided by an internal conviction that he was "the closest" man she had ever encountered, she engaged in two acts of profound cunning with him—deeds she deemed necessary, though perhaps not virtuous, and which she intended to keep hidden until her dying breath. Together, they orchestrated a withdrawal from the Gouda parsonage. Though Margaret was innocent in nature, a sudden, fierce pride mingled with her modesty, whispering a command to sever ties with the manse entirely. She resolved to let little Gerard remain there, believing her departure would allow him to complete the spiritual conquest his mother's heart attributed to him, thereby anchoring his father permanently to the path of humanity.

Yet, this act of policy, though generous in intent, tore at her heart. Having never been parted from her son for an hour, the solitude became unbearable within a single day. Driven by a grief that felt like desolation, she crept to the Gouda parsonage at twilight, lurking like a thief until she spotted Reicht Heynes alone in the kitchen. Softly tapping at the window, she pleaded for him to bring the boy to her unseen. To Margaret, the object of her longing ceased to have a name, sinking simply into the role of "him." Reicht soon found a pretext to escort little Gerard out, resulting in a scene of mutual rapture followed by fresh tears when they were forced apart again.

An arrangement was struck: Reicht would transport the boy halfway to Rotterdam daily at a set hour, meeting Margaret on the way. During these brief reunions, after the initial joy and playful gambols, the boy would occasionally amuse himself at their feet, creating a private space for the two women to speak seriously of Luke Peterson.

"Reicht," Margaret began, her voice heavy with regret, "I essentially promised him to marry Luke Peterson. 'Say the word,' I told him, 'and I will wed.'"

"Poor Luke!" Reicht replied with sympathy.

"Prithee, why 'poor Luke'?" Margaret asked.

"To be tossed back and forth between yes and no."

"Why, Reicht, have you never been so simple as to cast an affectionate eye on the boy?"

"I?" Reicht tossed his head, feigning innocence. "Oh, I ask your pardon. Well, then, you can do me a good turn."

"Whisht! Whisper!" Margaret hushed him sharply, pointing to the boy, who sat listening with eyes wide like saucers. Two women conspiring against a child? A thought that would have made either woman hide beneath a cap if caught.

For about five days, these stolen meetings continued. But eventually, Margaret felt the injustice of the deception, irritation rising alongside her unhappiness. She found herself crying over the situation when a cart pulled up to her door. Seated within, clean as a new coin, with a closely shaved beard and hands white as snow, sat the Vicar of Gouda—the man she had sent away. He wore the grey frock and large felt hat she had purchased for him.

Margaret ran upstairs, washing away the evidence of her tears, and changed into a cap, theoretically cleaner than the one she wore, before descending to greet him. He seized her hands, kissing them as a tear fell upon them. She turned her head away, hiding the start of her own tears.

"My sweet Margaret," he cried, "why hold you aloof from your own good deed? We have waited for you every day."

"You said things," she murmured.

"What! When I was a hermit, and a donkey?"

"Ay! No matter, you said things. And you had no reason."

"Forget all I said there. Who hearkens the ravings of a maniac? For I see now that in a few months I should have been a gibbering idiot; yet no mortal could have persuaded me away but you." Gerard poured out his gratitude, speaking not of the manse, but of the home he had found among the sheep, whom he now loved dearly. "So now I say to you as erst you said to me, come to Gouda manse."

"Humph! We will see about that," Margaret replied coolly, though inwardly burning with desire.

"Why, Margaret, think you I ever kept the dear child so long but that I made sure you would be back to him from day to day?" Gerard argued gently. "Oh, he curls round my very heartstrings, but what is my title to him compared to thine? Confess now, thou hast had hard thoughts of me for this."

"Nay, nay, not I. Ah! thou art thyself again... I have half a mind to go to Gouda manse, for your saying that."

"Come then, with half thy mind, 'tis worth the whole of other folk's."

"Well, I dare say I will; but there is no such mighty hurry," she said, masking her eagerness. "Tell me first how you agree with your folk."

"Already my poor have taken root in my heart," Gerard answered proudly. "Simple, rough, and superstitious, yet wonderfully good."

"Oh, I leave you alone for seeing a grain of good among a bushel of ill," Margaret teased ironically.

"And Margaret," Gerard lowered his voice, "two of them have been ill friends for four years, coming to the manse each to get on my blind side. But give the glory to God, I got on their bright side, made them friends, and made them laugh at their folly."

"But are you in very deed their vicar? Answer me that."

"Certes! Have I not been to the bishop, taken the oath, rung the church bell, and touched the altar, the missal, and the holy cup before the church-wardens? They have handed me the parish seal." He produced it. "Nay, 'tis a real vicar inviting a true friend to Gouda manse."

"Then my mind is at ease. Tell me oceans more."

Gerard spoke of the poor cripple, Sybrandt, whom his guardian angel had placed beneath his roof, and how Sybrandt and Gerard had become brothers in forgiveness. He spoke of Reicht Heynes, describing her voice as a diamond that pierced him like a knife, yet contrasting it with Margaret's mellow sweetness. He recounted tales of local idiosyncrasies—a man sweating to pass for ill-tempered, or the story of the hermit whose cave had collapsed, burying him alive.

"The strange thing is," Gerard continued, "the cave has verily fallen in. Had I been so perverse as to resist thee, it had assuredly buried me dead there where I had buried myself alive. Therefore in this I see the finger of Providence, condemning my late, approving my present, way of life."

"Somewhat more, methinks," Margaret observed. "This very tale proves thee my guardian angel..."

"Well, go you on, I'll follow."

"Nay, in the cart with me."

"Not so."

"Why?"

"Can I tell why and wherefore, being a woman? All I know is I seem—to feel—to wish—to come alone."

"So be it then. I leave thee the cart, being, as thou sayest, a woman, and I'll go a-foot, being a man again, with the joyful tidings of thy coming."

When Margaret finally reached the manse, the first thing she saw was the two Gerards together. The son performed his *capriccios* on the garden plot, while the father slouched on a chair, pencil and paper in hand, trying patiently to sketch him. After a warm welcome, Gerard showed her his failed attempts, lamenting that he could not fix the boy, who moved with the fluidity of quicksilver. "How he melteth from one posture to another!" he exclaimed, throwing the paper away in despair. Margaret, however, secretly conveyed the paper into her bosom.

That night, sitting by the peat fire, Gerard extolled the beauty of the brass candlesticks glowing in the hearth's light. Catherine's eyes sparkled at his observation. Gerard turned his gaze to Margaret, declaring that each age held its beauty. He pointed to her mild grey eye, noting that the motherly love it had shown left a shadow of beauty that defied time, and praised her delicate lip and pure white teeth. "Art beautiful in my eyes, mother dear," he said.

"And that is enough for me, my darling," Margaret replied. "'Tis time you were in bed, child. Ye have to preach the morn."

Reicht Heynes and Catherine exchanged a knowing look, acknowledging they had an amiable maniac to superintend, one who called everything beautiful.

The following Sunday, Gerard preached in his own church. The building was crammed with curious people who remained devout. In a single sermon lasting two hours, he declared the whole scripture, terrifying the impenitent, confirming the wavering, and uplifting the poor. When he ended, the multitude stood rapt, unwilling to believe the divine music of his voice had ceased. In a corner,

In the quiet sanctum of the dying, a priest finds his soul stripped bare, reduced to its essential office. Gerard performed the final rites for the departed woman with a mechanical, detached precision, treating her as if she were the humblest of his flock, though the truth gnawed at him. Once the candles burned low and the prayers ceased, a stupor, heavy and sudden, descended upon him. He sat amidst the ashes of her life, numb to the terrible shock of loss, unable to comprehend the depth of his grief until a wave of religious fervor seized him. In a trance of gratitude, he knelt, thanking Heaven for her Christian end, as if her passing were a gift rather than a wound.

Then, a singular thought consumed him: to shield her from the gaze of strangers, to return her dust to the soil of his own churchyard. That same evening, a solitary cart, drawn by one weary horse, moved slowly toward Gouda. Inside rested a simple coffin, and beside it lay a man utterly broken, his chin propped upon the lid, his arms wrapped around it as if holding back the world.

The brief, frantic energy required for these preparations had drained Gerard completely. Now, he lay crushed, clutching the cold iron latch that secured the coffin, refusing to let go. The driver, a silent figure who had guided the beast, walked ahead in respectful silence, offering only a whisper when he halted the horse, mindful of the mute agony behind him. When they sought shelter for the night, the innkeeper and his fellow travelers, driven by kindness, urged the mourner to rest and eat. Gerard, however, repelled them with a ferocity that bordered on madness, snarling like a loyal hound guarding a treasure. He would not be parted from her, clinging to the cold metal through the long hours. Finally, the others, seeing the futility of their concern, draped a heavy cloak over him and left him alone in the dark.

By noon, the sorrow-laden cart crested the hill to the manse, where nearly thirty parishioners had gathered, murmuring over trivial matters until the absence of their pastor became apparent. Upon seeing the scene—their beloved healer so utterly stricken, leaning against the cart—a collective wail rose from the crowd. This cry shattered Gerard's lethargy. His eyes opened, focusing on the familiar faces surrounding him. "Oh, my children!" he cried, his voice cracking with raw anguish, "my children!" Yet the words died in his throat, choked by a pain too vast for speech.

Despite the gentle protests of those around him, Gerard insisted on burying her himself the following day. He read the burial service, his voice trembling only at the breaking points, tears streaming freely onto the fresh earth. When the final Amen faded, he remained standing before the open grave, a statue of stillness, while the villagers retreated, unwilling to disturb his solitude.

Time seemed to suspend around him. He stood there, lost in a waking dream, until the sexton, a man of simple wit, began to shovel the earth back into the hole without offering any warning. The sound of the spade striking the wood, followed by the first layer of dirt falling softly upon her, caused Gerard to utter a scream that pierced the air, sharp and human.

The sexton paused, his hands hovering.

Gerard swayed violently, pressing a hand to his heaving chest. The sexton stepped forward, catching the faltering priest. Neighbors hurried to assist, calling for aid as they lifted Gerard from the ground. Jorian Ketel, who had lingered nearby, grieving for the benefactress who had shown him such kindness, broke from the crowd and rushed into the yard. Together, the two men supported the weakened Gerard back to the safety of the manse.

"Ah, Jorian! Good Jorian!" Gerard gasped, collapsing into a chair, his fingers clawing at his ribs. "Something has snapped within me; I felt it, I heard it snap... Here, Jorian, right here," he whispered, pointing frantically to his heart, where the silence of the grave had finally echoed too loudly.

Two weeks later, a pale, bowed form slipped through the gates of the Dominican convent in the suburbs of Gouda, seeking audience with Brother Ambrose. As the temporary governor following the recent death of the prior, Ambrose received the visitor with a warmth that belied his official duties. This stranger was Gerard, a man who had returned not to begin anew, but to close the circle of a life already weary. He had entered years ago as a mere novice, a boy on probation, yet deep down he had known himself to be failing. Now, driven by a desperate need for peace, he sought sanctuary within the gentle walls where his heart and home had once been so freely given.

Inside, Gerard found no solace. His cell, once a refuge, now felt like a prison of memory; every corner echoed with the presence of her, making his dwelling far heavier than his broken spirit could bear. Yet Ambrose, perceiving both the worth of his friend and the depth of his sorrow, harbored a tender hope. He believed that kindness alone might heal Gerard, restore him to health, and return him to his parish. To this end, Ambrose placed him in a comfortable chamber above the gateway, strictly forbidding fasting or any penitential hardships.

However, fate intervened swiftly. A few days after Gerard's arrival, the newly appointed Prior took up residence, bringing with him the iron discipline of a "very Tartar." At first, he was consumed by a zeal to purge abuses and tighten the monastic rules, but soon his attention turned to the matter at hand. Hearing that the Vicar of Gouda had taken the habit of a novice, the Prior's countenance darkened. "It is well," he declared sharply, "that we address this immediately. Let him resign his vicarage forthwith, or depart; I shall have no fat parsons in my house."

With swift authority, the Prior summoned Gerard. The friar obeyed and presented himself before the stern superior. But the moment their eyes met across the distance, time seemed to suspend. Startled by the recognition, they stood frozen.

"Clement!" cried the newcomer.

"Jerome!" replied the other, his voice breaking with emotion.

Jerome remained as stern and gloomy as ever in his nature, yet his regard for Gerard had softened considerably. Throughout his time in England, Jerome had missed his brother far more than he had anticipated, frequently wondering where Gerard had gone. The news he received in Gouda elevated Jerome's esteem for him further, particularly the account of Gerard standing firm against the Pope and the Minorites regarding "the infernal heresy of the immaculate conception," a phrase Jerome used with vehemence. However, when a young novice tearfully recounted the cause of Gerard's sickness, Jerome's lingering contempt flared anew. "Dying for a woman?" he exclaimed.

Driven by a desire to prevent such a scandal, Jerome visited Brother Clement twice daily in his cell. He deployed all his former influence and eloquence, urging Clement to shed this unspiritual despondency and not to deprive the Church of his piety and gift at such a critical juncture. Gerard listened, acknowledging the validity of Jerome's reasoning and admiring his fortitude. He confessed his own frailty, appearing visibly to waste away toward the land of the faithful. On one occasion, Jerome declared that he had heard Gerard's story and heard it with pride. "But now," Jerome argued, "you ruin everything, Clement. This is the victory of earthly passion. It would have been better to yield to it and repent, rather than resisting it while she lived and succumbing to it now, both body and soul."

"Dearest Jerome," Clement replied, his tone so gentle that it stripped Jerome's warning of its harsh edge, "perhaps you misunderstand me. There is no passion here, but a profound affection for which I need not blush now, nor will I blush in heaven. Consider, Jerome, does the poor hound that dies of grief beside his master's grave commit sin through passion? Nor do I. Passion would have saved my life, but it cost me my soul. She was my guardian angel; she upheld me in my duties and acts of charity. Her countenance encouraged me in the pulpit, and her lips soothed me amidst ingratitude.
